“We all know that you’ve got beautiful arms,” said Bruin angrily. “There’s no need for you to make an exhibition of them in the street, at midnight. Let’s get out of this.” He laid his hand on her shoulder and made as if to draw her away. “We’d better be going. Goodness knows what’s happening behind us.” He indicated with a little movement of the head the loiterers round the coffee-stall. “Some disturbance among the *canaille*.”

Mrs. Viveash looked round. The cabdrivers and the other consumers of midnight coffee had gathered in an interested circle, curious and sympathetic, round the figure of a woman who was sitting, like a limp bundle tied up in black cotton and mackintosh, on the stall-keeper’s high stool, leaning wearily against the wall of the booth. A man stood beside her drinking tea out of a thick white cup. Everyone was talking at once.

“Mayn’t the poor wretches talk?” asked Mrs. Viveash, turning back to Bruin. “I never knew anyone who had the lower classes on the brain as much as you have.”

“I loathe them,” said Bruin. “I hate everyone poor, or ill, or old. Can’t abide them; they make me positively sick.”
===Chinese===
「你的胳膊漂亮，这我们都知道，」布鲁因怒气冲冲地说，「可你犯不着半夜三更在街上展览它们。离开这儿吧。」他把手搭在她肩上，作势要把她拉走。「我们还是走的好。天知道我们背后正出什么事。」他头微微一动，指了指咖啡摊四周晃荡的人。「*canaille*（贱民）里头起了点骚动。」

维维什夫人回过头去。马车夫们和其余喝午夜咖啡的人围成了一个兴致勃勃的圈子，又好奇又同情，围着一个女人——她像一捆用黑棉布和胶布雨衣裹扎起来的软塌塌的包裹，坐在摊主的高凳上，疲惫地倚着棚子的板壁。一个男人站在她旁边，端着一只厚白瓷杯喝着茶。人人都抢着说话。

「这些可怜虫连说说话也不行么？」维维什夫人回过脸来问布鲁因。「我从没见过谁像你这样，满脑子都是下等阶级。」

「我讨厌他们，」布鲁因说。「凡是穷人、病人、老人，我一概恨。没法忍受；他们简直叫我恶心。」

===Original===

“*Quelle âme bien-née*,” piped Mr. Mercaptan. “And how well and frankly you express what we all feel and lack the courage to say.”

Lypiatt gave vent to indignant laughter.

“I remember when I was a little boy,” Bruin went on, “my old grandfather used to tell me stories about his childhood. He told me that when he was about five or six, just before the passing of the Reform Bill of ’thirty-two, there was a song which all right-thinking people used to sing, with a chorus that went like this: ‘Rot the People, blast the People, damn the Lower Classes.’ I wish I knew the rest of the words and the tune. It must have been a good song.”

Coleman was enraptured with the song. He shouldered his walking-stick and began marching round and round the nearest lamppost chanting the words to a stirring march tune. “Rot the People, blast the People.﻿ ﻿…” He marked the rhythm with heavy stamps of his feet.

“Ah, if only they’d invent servants with internal combustion engines,” said Bruin, almost pathetically. “However well trained they are, they always betray their humanity occasionally. And that is really intolerable.”
===Chinese===
「*Quelle âme bien-née*（多么高贵的灵魂），」默卡普坦先生尖声赞道。「你把我们人人都感到、却没胆量说出来的话，说得又透彻又坦白。」

利皮亚特发出一声愤慨的大笑。

「我记得我还是个小男孩的时候，」布鲁因接着说，「我老祖父常给我讲他童年的故事。他告诉我，他大约五六岁那年、就在『三二年改革法案』通过之前不久，有一支所有正派人都爱唱的歌，副歌是这么唱的：『烂掉人民，炸掉人民，咒死下等阶级。』可惜我不知道其余的词和调子。那一定是一支好歌。」

科尔曼对这支歌心醉神迷。他把文明棍扛上肩头，绕着最近的一根路灯柱操练起来，把歌词按一支激昂的进行曲调子高声唱起来。「烂掉人民，炸掉人民。……」他用重重的跺脚打着拍子。

「唉，但愿他们赶紧发明出装内燃机的仆人，」布鲁因说，那口气简直近乎可怜。「不管调教得多好，他们总免不了时不时露一露他们的人性。那才真正让人没法容忍。」

===Original===

“How tedious is a guilty conscience!” Gumbril murmured the quotation.

“But Mr. Shearwater,” said Myra, bringing back the conversation to more congenial themes, “hasn’t told us yet what he thinks of arms.”

“Nothing at all,” said Shearwater. “I’m occupied with the regulation of the blood at the moment.”

“But is it true what he says, Theodore?” She appealed to Gumbril.

“I should think so.” Gumbril’s answer was rather dim and remote. He was straining to hear the talk of Bruin’s *canaille*, and Mrs. Viveash’s question seemed a little irrelevant.
===Chinese===
「负疚的良心是多么磨人！」冈布里尔低声念出这句引文。

「不过希尔沃特先生，」迈拉说着，把谈话引回较合脾胃的题目，「还没告诉我们他觉得胳膊怎么样呢。」

「毫无想法。」希尔沃特说。「我这阵子忙着血液调节的问题。」

「可他说的是真的吗，西奥多？」她向冈布里尔求证。

「我想是真的。」冈布里尔的回答有几分恍恍惚惚、心不在焉。他正使劲去听布鲁因那些贱民在谈什么，维维什夫人的这个问题显得有点不相干。

===Original===

“I used to do cartin’ jobs,” the man with the teacup was saying. “ ’Ad a van and a nold pony of me own. And didn’t do so badly neither. The only trouble was me lifting furniture and ’eavy weights about the place. Because I ’ad malaria out in India, in the war.﻿ ﻿…”

“Nor even﻿—you compel me to violate the laws of modesty﻿—nor even,” Mrs. Viveash went on, smiling painfully, speaking huskily, expiringly, “of legs?”

A spring of blasphemy was touched in Coleman’s brain. “Neither delighteth He in any man’s legs,” he shouted, and with an extravagant show of affection he embraced Zoe, who caught hold of his hand and bit it.

“It comes back on you when you get tired like, malaria does.” The man’s face was sallow and there was an air of peculiar listlessness and hopelessness about his misery. “It comes back on you, and then you go down with fever and you’re as weak as a child.”

Shearwater shook his head.
===Chinese===
「我从前是干赶车运货营生的，」端茶杯的男人正说着。「自个儿有辆车，有匹老马。日子过得也不赖。只有一样麻烦，就是在人家家里搬家具、抬重物。因为打仗那阵儿，我在印度得了疟疾。……」

「那么——你这是逼得我违犯羞怯的律法了——那么连腿，」维维什夫人接着说，笑得费力，嗓音沙哑，气若游丝，「也不感兴趣？」

科尔曼脑子里渎神的泉眼给捅开了。「祂也不喜悦任何人的腿，」他喊道，随即以一种大张旗鼓的亲热搂住佐伊；佐伊一把抓住他的手，咬了一口。

「人一乏，疟疾就回来了，就是这样。」那男人的脸蜡黄，他的苦楚里透着一股异样的委顿与绝望。「它一回来，你就害热病躺倒，虚弱得像个孩子。」

希尔沃特摇了摇头。

===Original===

“Nor even of the heart?” Mrs. Viveash lifted her eyebrows. “Ah, now the inevitable word has been pronounced, the real subject of every conversation has appeared on the scene. Love, Mr. Shearwater!”

“But as I says,” recapitulated the man with the teacup, “we didn’t do so badly after all. We ’ad nothing to complain about. ’Ad we, Florrie?”

The black bundle made an affirmative movement with its upper extremity.

“That’s one of the subjects,” said Shearwater, “like the Great Wall of China and the habits of Trematodes, I don’t allow myself to be interested in.”

Mrs. Viveash laughed, breathed out a little “Good God!” of incredulity and astonishment, and asked, “Why not?”

“No time,” he explained. “You people of leisure have nothing else to do or think about. I’m busy and so naturally less interested in the subject than you; and I take care, what’s more, to limit such interest as I have.”
===Chinese===
「连心也不感兴趣？」维维什夫人扬起了眉毛。「啊，现在那个不可避免的字眼说出来了，每一场谈话真正的题目都到场了。爱情，希尔沃特先生！」

「不过像我说的，」端茶杯的男人总结道，「我们到底混得不算坏。没什么可抱怨的。对吧，弗洛丽（Florrie）？」

那团黑包裹用它上端的部分做了一个表示同意的动作。

「那也是题目之一，」希尔沃特说，「跟万里长城和吸虫的习性一样，我不容许自己对它发生兴趣。」

维维什夫人笑了，轻轻呼出一声「天哪！」，一半不信，一半惊奇，然后问：「为什么不呢？」

「没有时间。」他解释道。「你们这些有闲阶级，别的事没有，专有工夫想这个。我忙，对这个题目自然不如你们上心；何况我还小心在意，把仅有的那一点儿兴趣限制起来。」

===Original===

“I was goin’ up Ludgate ’Ill one day with a vanload of stuff for a chap in Clerkenwell. I was leadin’ Jerry up the ’ill﻿—Jerry’s the name of our ole pony.﻿ ﻿…”

“One can’t have everything,” Shearwater was explaining, “not all at the same time, in any case. I’ve arranged my life for work now. I’m quietly married, I simmer away domestically.”

“*Quelle horreur!*” said Mr. Mercaptan. All the Louis Quinze Abbé in him was shocked and revolted by the thought.

“But love?” questioned Mrs. Viveash. “Love?”

“Love!” Lypiatt echoed. He was looking up at the Milky Way.

“All of a sudden out jumps a copper at me. ‘ ’Ow old is that ’orse?’ ’e says. ‘It ain’t fit to drawr a load, it limps in all four feet,’ ’e says. ‘No, it doesn’t,’ I says. ‘None of your answerin’ back,’ ’e says. ‘Take it outer the shafts at once.’ ”
===Chinese===
「有一天我拉着一车货上卢德门山（Ludgate Hill），是给克勒肯维尔（Clerkenwell）一个伙计送的。我牵着杰瑞上坡——杰瑞就是我们那匹老马的名字。……」

「人不能样样都有，」希尔沃特解释着，「无论如何，不能同时样样都有。我现在已经把生活安排成干活的日子了。我安安静静地结了婚，在家里温吞吞地过日子。」

「*Quelle horreur*（多可怕呀）！」默卡普坦先生说。他身上那位路易十五时代的风流神父，被这个念头惊得骇然、激起反感。

「可是爱情呢？」维维什夫人追问。「爱情呢？」

「爱情！」利皮亚特应声道。他正仰望着银河。

「冷不丁一个警察蹿出来截住我。『那匹马多大了？』他说。『它拉不动车了，四条腿全瘸。』他说。『才没瘸呢。』我说。『不许还嘴，』他说，『立刻把它从辕里卸出来。』」

===Original===

“But I know all about love already. I know precious little still about kidneys.”

“But, my good Shearwater, how can you know all about love before you’ve made it with all women?”

“Off we goes, me and the cop and the ’orse, up in front of the police court magistrate.﻿ ﻿…”

“Or are you one of those imbeciles,” Mrs. Viveash went on, “who speak of women with a large *W* and pretend we’re all the same? Poor Theodore here might possibly think so in his feebler moments.” Gumbril smiled vaguely from a distance. He was following the man with the teacup into the magistrate’s stuffy court. “And Mercaptan certainly does, because all the women who ever sat on his *dix-huitième* sofa certainly were exactly like one another. And perhaps Casimir does too; all women look like his absurd ideal. But you, Shearwater, you’re intelligent. Surely you don’t believe anything so stupid?”
===Chinese===
「可爱情我已经全知道了。肾脏呢，我知道得还少得可怜。」

「可是，我的好希尔沃特，还没同天下所有的女人做过爱，你怎么能算把爱情全知道了呢？」

「于是我们就去了，我、警察、马，一起站到治安法庭的法官面前。……」

「再不然，您就是那种笨蛋，」维维什夫人接着说，「说起女人（Women）来要大写 W、硬说我们全都一个样？可怜的西奥多，他在自己软弱的时刻说不定真这么想。」冈布里尔远远地、茫然地笑了笑。他正跟着那个端茶杯的男人，走进法官那间闷热的法庭。「默卡普坦肯定是这么想的，因为在他那张十八世纪（*dix-huitième*）沙发上坐过的所有女人，的确一模一样。卡齐米尔或许也是；天下的女人都长得像他那个荒唐的理想。可是您，希尔沃特，您是有头脑的。您总不会相信这种蠢话吧？」

===Original===

Shearwater shook his head.

“The cop, ’e gave evidence against me. ‘Limping in all four feet,’ ’e says. ‘It wasn’t,’ I says, and the police court vet, ’e bore me out. ‘The ’orse ’as been very well treated,’ ’e says. ‘But ’e’s old, ’e’s very old.’ ‘I know ’e’s old,’ I says. ‘But where am I goin’ to find the price for a young one?’ ”

“x² − y²,” Shearwater was saying, “= (x + y)(x − y). And the equation holds good whatever the values of x and y.﻿ ﻿… It’s the same with your love business, Mrs. Viveash. The relation is still fundamentally the same, whatever the value of the unknown personal quantities concerned. Little individual tics and peculiarities﻿—after all, what do they matter?”

“What indeed!” said Coleman. “Tics, mere tics. Sheep ticks, horse ticks, bed bugs, tape worms, taint worms, guinea worms, liver flukes.﻿ ﻿…”

“ ‘The ’orse must be destroyed,’ says the beak. ‘ ’E’s too old for work.’ ‘But I’m not,’ I says. ‘I can’t get a old age pension at thirty-two, can I? ’Ow am I to earn my living if you take away what I earns my living by?’ ”
===Chinese===
希尔沃特摇了摇头。

「警察出来作证告我。『四条腿全瘸。』他说。『没瘸。』我说，治安法庭的兽医也替我作证。『这马一直被照料得很好，』他说，『可是它老了，非常老了。』『我知道它老，』我说，『可叫我上哪儿弄钱买匹年轻的？』」

「x² − y²，」希尔沃特正说着，「＝ (x + y)(x − y)。而且不管 x 和 y 取什么值，这个等式都成立。……您那套爱情的事也一样，维维什夫人。无论有关的那些未知的人身量取什么值，那个关系从根本上说始终是一样的。小小的个人小动作和怪癖——说到底，它们顶什么用？」

「可不是嘛！」科尔曼说。「小抽动，纯粹的小抽动。羊蜱、马蜱、臭虫、绦虫、蛲虫、龙线虫、肝吸虫。……」

「『这匹马必须处理掉，』法官说。『它老得干不动活了。』『可我没老，』我说。『我三十二岁，总领不上养老金吧？你们把我吃饭的家伙拿走，叫我拿什么挣饭吃？』」

===Original===

Mrs. Viveash smiled agonizingly. “Here’s a man who thinks personal peculiarities are trivial and unimportant,” she said. “You’re not even interested in people, then?”

“ ‘I don’t know what you can do,’ ’e says. ‘I’m only ’ere to administer the law.’ ‘Seems a queer sort of law,’ I says. ‘What law is it?’ ”

Shearwater scratched his head. Under his formidable black moustache he smiled at last his ingenuous, childish smile. “No,” he said. “No, I suppose I’m not. It hadn’t occurred to me, until you said it. But I suppose I’m not. No.” He laughed, quite delighted, it seemed, by this discovery about himself.

“ ‘What law is it?’ ’e says. ‘The Croolty to Animals law. That’s what it is,’ ’e says.”

The smile of mockery and suffering appeared and faded. “One of these days,” said Mrs. Viveash, “you may find them more absorbing than you do now.”
===Chinese===
维维什夫人笑得苦楚万分。「瞧这位先生，认为个人的怪癖都是琐碎小事，」她说。「这么说，您连人也不感兴趣？」

「『我有什么办法，』他说。『我不过是在这儿执行法律。』『这法律可真新鲜，』我说。『这是哪门子法律？』」

希尔沃特挠了挠头。在那部令人生畏的黑髭须底下，他终于露出他那副憨直的、孩子气的笑。「不，」他说，「不，我想我是没兴趣。经您这么一说，我才头一回想到。不过我想我是没有。没有。」他笑了，似乎对自己身上的这个发现乐不可支。

「『哪门子法律？』他说。『《虐待动物法》。就是这部。』他说。」

嘲弄而痛苦的微笑一现即逝。「将来有一天，」维维什夫人说，「您也许会发现他们比眼下叫您放不下得多。」

===Original===

“Meanwhile,” said Shearwater.﻿ ﻿…

“I couldn’t find a job ’ere, and ’aving been workin’ on my own, my own master like, couldn’t get unemployment pay. So when we ’eard of jobs at Portsmouth, we thought we’d try to get one, even if it did mean walkin’ there.”

“Meanwhile, I have my kidneys.”

“ ‘ ’Opeless,’ ’e says to me, ‘quite ’opeless. More than two hundred come for three vacancies.’ So there was nothing for it but to walk back again. Took us four days it did, this time. She was very bad on the way, very bad. Being nearly six months gone. Our first it is. Things will be ’arder still, when it comes.”

From the black bundle there issued a sound of quiet sobbing.
===Chinese===
「不过，眼下——」希尔沃特说。……

「这儿我找不着活儿，又因为一直自己单干、自己当自己的老板，领不上失业救济。后来听说朴次茅斯（Portsmouth）有活儿，我们想，哪怕走着去呢，也去试一试。」

「不过，眼下我还有我的肾脏。」

「『没指望，』他对我说，『全没指望。三个空缺，来了两百多人。』于是毫无办法，只好再走回去。这回走了我们四天。她一路上很难受，很难受。怀胎快六个月了。这是我们头一个。等它生下来，日子还要更难。」

那团黑包裹里发出一阵低低的抽泣。

===Original===

“Look here,” said Gumbril, making a sudden irruption into the conversation. “This is really too awful.” He was consumed with indignation and pity; he felt like a prophet in Nineveh.

“There are two wretched people here,” and Gumbril told them breathlessly, what he had overheard. It was terrible, terrible. “All the way to Portsmouth and back again; on foot; without proper food; and the woman’s with child.”

Coleman exploded with delight. “Gravid,” he kept repeating, “gravid, gravid. The laws of gravidy, first formulated by Newton, now recodified by the immortal Einstein. God said, Let Newstein be, and there was light. And God said, Let there be Light; and there was darkness o’er the face of the earth.” He roared with laughter.

Between them they raised five pounds. Mrs. Viveash undertook to give them to the black bundle. The cabmen made way for her as she advanced; there was an uncomfortable silence. The black bundle lifted a face that was old and worn, like the face of a statue in the portal of a cathedral; an old face, but one was aware somehow, that it belonged to a woman still young by the reckoning of years. Her hands trembled as she took the notes, and when she opened her mouth to speak her hardly articulate whisper of gratitude, one saw that she had lost several of her teeth.
===Chinese===
「我说，」冈布里尔说，突然杀进谈话里。「这实在太过分了。」他被义愤和怜悯煎熬着；他觉得自己像尼尼微（Nineveh）城里的一位先知。

「这儿有两个可怜的人，」冈布里尔气喘吁吁地把他偷听来的话讲给他们听。可怕，可怕。「一路走到朴次茅斯，再一路走回来；全凭两条腿；没有一口像样的饭；那女人还怀着孕。」

科尔曼乐得炸了开来。「怀胎（gravid），」他反复念叨，「怀胎，怀胎。怀力定律（laws of gravidy），由牛顿首创，如今由不朽的爱因斯坦重新编定。上帝说，要有牛斯坦（Newstein），就有了光。上帝又说，要有光；地面上却是一片黑暗。」他放声大笑。

他们几个凑出了五镑。维维什夫人担当起把钱递给那团黑包裹的差事。她走近时，马车夫们给她让路；一阵不自在的寂静。黑包裹抬起一张苍老憔悴的脸，像大教堂门廊里一座雕像的脸；一张苍老的脸，可不知怎么，人能觉出它属于一个按年头数还年轻的女人。她接钞票时双手发抖，开口说出那几乎不成句的感激的低语时，人看得出她掉了几颗牙。

===Original===

The party disintegrated. All went their ways: Mr. Mercaptan to his rococo boudoir, his sweet barocco bedroom in Sloane Street; Coleman and Zoe towards goodness only knew what scenes of intimate life in Pimlico; Lypiatt to his studio off the Tottenham Court Road, alone, silently brooding and perhaps too consciously bowed with unhappiness. But the unhappiness, poor Titan! was real enough, for had he not seen Mrs. Viveash and the insufferable, the stupid and loutish Opps driving off in one taxi? “Must finish up with a little dancing,” Myra had huskily uttered from that deathbed on which her restless spirit forever and wearily exerted itself. Obediently, Bruin had given an address and they had driven off. But after the dancing? Oh, was it possible that that odious, bad-blooded young cad was her lover? And that she should like him? It was no wonder that Lypiatt should have walked, bent like Atlas under the weight of a world. And when, in Piccadilly, a belated and still unsuccessful prostitute sidled out of the darkness, as he strode by unseeing in his misery when she squeaked up at him a despairing “Cheer up, duckie,” Lypiatt suddenly threw up his head and laughed titanically, with the terrible bitterness of a noble soul in pain. Even the poor drabs at the street corners were affected by the unhappiness that radiated out from him, wave after throbbing wave, like music, he liked to fancy, into the night. Even the wretched drabs. He walked on, more desperately bowed than ever; but met no further adventure on his way.

Gumbril and Shearwater both lived in Paddington; they set off in company up Park Lane, walking in silence. Gumbril gave a little skip to get himself into step with his companion. To be out of step, when steps so loudly and flat-footedly flapped on empty pavements, was disagreeable, he found, was embarrassing, was somehow dangerous. Stepping, like this, out of time, one gave oneself away, so to speak, one made the night aware of two presences, when there might, if steps sounded in unison, be only one, heavier, more formidable, more secure than either of the separate two. In unison, then, they flapped up Park Lane. A policeman and the three poets, sulking back to back on their fountain, were the only human things besides themselves under the mauve electric moons.

“It’s appalling, it’s horrible,” said Gumbril at last, after a long, long silence, during which he had, indeed, been relishing to the full the horror of it all. Life, don’t you know.
===Chinese===
这伙人散了伙。各走各的路：默卡普坦先生回他斯隆街（Sloane Street）那间洛可可的闺房、那间甜美的巴罗克卧室去；科尔曼和佐伊朝皮姆利科（Pimlico）去——只有天知道去演什么样的私生活场景；利皮亚特回托特纳姆宫廷路旁他的画室，独自一人，默默出神，被愁苦压弯了腰——也许对这份愁苦自觉得过了头。可那愁苦，可怜的泰坦！却是千真万确的，因为他不是亲眼看见维维什夫人和那个叫人受不了的、愚蠢粗鄙的奥普斯同乘一辆出租汽车走了吗？「收尾总得跳一小会儿舞。」迈拉从她那张病榻上沙哑地说出这句话——她那颗不得安生的魂灵，就在那张榻上永远地、疲惫不倦地使劲。布鲁因乖乖报了个地址，两人便驱车而去。可是跳舞之后呢？哦，难道那个可憎的、血统卑劣的年轻无赖真是她的情夫？而且她居然还喜欢他？难怪利皮亚特一路行走，弯着腰，像驮着整个世界重量的阿特拉斯（Atlas）。在皮卡迪利（Piccadilly），一个出来晚了、仍未开张的妓女从黑暗里侧身蹭出来；他正满腹愁苦、视而不见地大步走过，她朝他尖声叫出一句绝望的「开心点儿，小哥儿（duckie）」——利皮亚特猛地昂起头，泰坦式地大笑，笑声里带着一个高贵灵魂受苦时的可怕辛酸。连街角那些可怜的站街女，都感染到了从他身上辐射出来的愁苦，一阵接一阵悸动的波，像音乐——他喜欢这样想——涌进夜色里。连那些可怜的站街女。他继续走下去，腰弯得比先前更绝望；但一路上再没有遇到别的奇遇。

冈布里尔和希尔沃特都住在帕丁顿；两人结伴沿公园巷（Park Lane）出发，一路无言。冈布里尔轻轻一蹦，蹦进同伴的步子里。他发觉，在空荡荡的人行道上、脚步平脚掌啪嗒啪嗒作响得这么响的时候，两个人步子不合拍，是件别扭事，是件难堪事，不知怎么还是件危险事。像这样不合拍子地迈步，可以说，人就露了馅，就让黑夜察觉有两个存在；而倘若步声齐一，本来可以只有一个——比分开的两个都更重、更威严、更安稳的一个。于是他们步调一致地啪嗒啪嗒走上公园巷。淡紫色的电月底下，除他们之外，人形的只有一名警察，和喷泉上那三个背对背生闷气的诗人。

「这真骇人，真可怕，」冈布里尔终于开口，在这长长的、长长的沉默里，他其实一直把这满心的恐怖细细品了个够。人生嘛，你懂的。

===Original===

“What’s appalling?” Shearwater inquired. He walked with his big head bowed, his hands clasped behind his back and clutching his hat; walked clumsily, with sudden lurches of his whole massive anatomy. Wherever he was, Shearwater always seemed to take up the space that two or three ordinary people would normally occupy. Cool fingers of wind passed refreshingly through his hair. He was thinking of the experiment he meant to try, in the next few days, down at the physiological laboratory. You’d put a man on an ergometer in a heated chamber and set him to work﻿—hours at a time. He’d sweat, of course, prodigiously. You’d make arrangements for collecting the sweat, weighing it, analysing it and so on. The interesting thing would be to see what happened at the end of a few days. The man would have got rid of so much of his salts, that the blood composition might be altered and all sorts of delightful consequences might follow. It ought to be a capital experiment. Gumbril’s exclamation disturbed him. “What’s appalling?” he asked rather irritably.

“Those people at the coffee-stall,” Gumbril answered. “It’s appalling that human beings should have to live like that. Worse than dogs.”

“Dogs have nothing to complain of.” Shearwater went off at a tangent. “Nor guinea-pigs, nor rats. It’s these blasted anti-vivisection maniacs who make all the fuss.”

“But think,” cried Gumbril, “what these wretched people have had to suffer! Walking all the way to Portsmouth in search of work; and the woman with child. It’s horrifying. And then, the way people of that class are habitually treated. One has no idea of it until one has actually been treated that way oneself. In the war, for example, when one went to have one’s mitral murmurs listened to by the medical board﻿—they treated one then as though one belonged to the lower orders, like all the rest of the poor wretches. It was a real eye-opener. One felt like a cow being got into a train. And to think that the majority of one’s fellow-beings pass their whole lives being shoved about like maltreated animals!”

“H’m,” said Shearwater. If you went on sweating indefinitely, he supposed, you would end by dying.
===Chinese===
「什么骇人？」希尔沃特问。他走路时低着大脑袋，双手背在身后扣着帽子；走得笨拙，庞大身架时不时突然一个趔趄。不论在哪儿，希尔沃特似乎总要占掉两三个普通人正常占的那么大地方。风那凉爽的手指舒畅地穿过他的头发。他正想着过几天要到生理学实验室去做的那个实验。把一个人放在热烘烘的小室里的测功计（ergometer）上，叫他干活——一干好几个钟头。他当然会出汗，出得铺天盖地。你安排好收集汗液、称重、分析等等。有意思的是看几天过后会发生什么。那人会失掉那么多的盐分，血液成分说不定就此改变，随之而来的是种种可喜的后果。这该是一流的实验。冈布里尔的感叹打扰了他。「什么骇人？」他颇不耐烦地问。

「咖啡摊边的那两个人，」冈布里尔回答。「人竟得活成那个样子，真骇人。比狗还不如。」

「狗没什么可抱怨的。」希尔沃特把话岔了开去。「豚鼠没有，耗子也没有。瞎起哄的就是那帮该死的反活体解剖的疯子。」

「可是想想，」冈布里尔喊道，「这些可怜人受了多少苦！一路走到朴次茅斯找活儿干；女人还怀着孕。骇人听闻。再说，那个阶级的人平日受的待遇。不到自己也亲身受过那样的对待，人是想象不到的。比如打仗的时候，你去让体检委员会听你的二尖瓣杂音——他们那时待你，就仿佛你也属于下等阶级，同其余一切可怜虫一样。真是叫人大开眼界。人觉得自己像一头被塞进火车的母牛。再想想，自己的同类大多数，竟一辈子像受虐待的牲口一样被人推来搡着过日子！」

「哼。」希尔沃特应了一声。人要是没完没了地出汗，他想，到头来是会死的。

===Original===

Gumbril looked through the railings at the profound darkness of the park. Vast it was and melancholy, with a string, here and there, of receding lights. “Terrible,” he said, and repeated the word several times. “Terrible, terrible.” All the legless soldiers grinding barrel-organs, all the hawkers of toys stamping their leaky boots in the gutters of the Strand; at the corner of Cursitor Street and Chancery Lane, the old woman with matches, forever holding to her left eye a handkerchief as yellow and dirty as the winter fog. What was wrong with the eye? He had never dared to look, but hurried past as though she were not there, or sometimes, when the fog was more than ordinarily cold and stifling, paused for an instant with averted eyes to drop a brown coin into her tray of matches. And then there were the murderers hanged at eight o’clock, while one was savouring, almost with voluptuous consciousness, the final dream-haunted doze. There was the phthisical charwoman who used to work at his father’s house, until she got too weak and died. There were the lovers who turned on the gas and the ruined shopkeepers jumping in front of trains. Had one a right to be contented and well-fed, had one a right to one’s education and good taste, a right to knowledge and conversation and the leisurely complexities of love?

He looked once more through the railings at the park’s impenetrable, rustic night, at the lines of beaded lamps. He looked, and remembered another night, years ago, during the war, when there were no lights in the park and the electric moons above the roadway were in almost total eclipse. He had walked up this street alone, full of melancholy emotions which, though the cause of them was different, were in themselves much the same as the melancholy emotions which swelled windily up within him tonight. He had been most horribly in love.

“What did you think,” he asked abruptly, “of Myra Viveash?”

“Think?” said Shearwater. “I don’t know that I thought very much about her. Not a case for ratiocination exactly, is she? She seemed to me entertaining enough, as women go. I said I’d lunch with her on Thursday.”
===Chinese===
冈布里尔透过栏杆，望进公园幽深的黑暗里。它浩大而忧郁，四处牵着一串串渐渐远去的灯光。「可怕，」他说，又把这个词重复了几遍。「可怕，可怕。」所有摇着手摇风琴的无腿士兵，所有在河岸街（the Strand）阴沟里跺着漏湿的靴子的卖玩具的小贩；在柯西特街（Cursitor Street）和大法官巷（Chancery Lane）的拐角上，那个卖火柴的老太婆，永远朝左眼按着一块同冬雾一样又黄又脏的手绢。那只眼睛出了什么毛病？他从来不敢看，只匆匆走过，仿佛她不在那里；或者有时候，逢着雾冷得、呛得异乎寻常，便侧过目光停上一瞬，往她那盘火柴里丢下一枚棕色硬币。还有那些八点钟被绞死的杀人犯——那时人正带着近乎淫乐的知觉，品味最后一觉缠绵着梦的瞌睡。还有从前在他父亲家里帮佣的那个患痨病的清洁女工，直到虚弱得干不动、死去。还有那些拧开煤气开关的恋人，和扑到火车前的破产店主。人有权心满意足、足食丰衣吗？人有权享有自己的教育和好趣味，享有知识、谈话和爱情从容的种种曲折吗？

他再一次透过栏杆，望公园那无法看透的、乡野一般的夜，望那一行行珠串似的灯。他望着，记起另一个夜晚，多年前，打仗的时候，公园里没有灯火，大路上空的电月几乎全蚀。那晚他独自走上这条街，满怀忧郁的情绪——虽然起因不同，本身却同今夜在他胸中鼓胀如风的忧郁情绪大致无二。他那时正爱得可怕，爱得一塌糊涂。

「你觉得，」他冷不丁地问，「迈拉·维维什这个人怎么样？」

「觉得？」希尔沃特说。「我不知道我对她想了多少。她算不上一个正好用来推理的病例，对吧？照女人的行情看，我觉得她够有趣的了。我答应星期四跟她吃午饭。」

===Original===

Gumbril felt, all of a sudden, the need to speak confidentially. “There was a time,” he said in a tone that was quite unreally airy, offhand and disengaged, “years ago, when I totally lost my head about her. Totally.” Those tear-wet patches on his pillow, cold against his cheek in the darkness; and oh, the horrible pain of weeping, vainly, for something that was nothing, that was everything in the world! “Towards the end of the war it was. I remember walking up this dismal street one night, in the pitch darkness, writhing with jealousy.” He was silent. Spectrally, like a dim, haunting ghost, he had hung about her; dumbly, dumbly imploring, appealing. “The weak, silent man,” she used to call him. And once for two or three days, out of pity, out of affection, out of a mere desire, perhaps, to lay the tiresome ghost, she had given him what his mournful silence implored﻿—only to take it back, almost as soon as accorded. That other night, when he had walked up this street before, desire had eaten out his vitals and his body seemed empty, sickeningly and achingly void; jealousy was busily reminding him, with an unflagging malice, of her beauty﻿—of her beauty and the hateful, ruffian hands which now caressed, the eyes which looked on it. That was all long ago.

“She is certainly handsome,” said Shearwater, commenting, at one or two removes, on Gumbril’s last remark. “I can see that she might make anyone who got involved in her decidedly uncomfortable.” After a day or two’s continuous sweating, it suddenly occurred to him, one might perhaps find seawater more refreshing than fresh water. That would be queer.

Gumbril burst out ferociously laughing. “But there were other times,” he went on jauntily, “when other people were jealous of me.” Ah, revenge, revenge. In the better world of the imagination it was possible to get one’s own back. What fiendish vendettas were there carried to successful ends! “I remember once writing her a quatrain in French.” (He had written it years after the whole thing was over, he had never sent it to anyone at all; but that was all one.) “How did it go? Ah, yes.” And he recited, with suitable gestures:
===Chinese===
冈布里尔突然感到一种倾吐心事的必要。「有一阵子，」他用一种全然不真实地轻快、随便、置身事外的声调说，「多年前，我曾为她彻底昏了头。彻底。」枕头上那些泪湿的斑块，黑暗里贴着他的脸颊，冰凉；啊，为一件什么也不是、却是全世界的一切的东西，白白地哭，那种哭多么疼！「那是战争快结束的时候。我记得有天夜里，就在这条凄凉的街上，漆黑里，我妒火中烧，浑身扭动。」他沉默了。幽灵似的，像一缕昏昏的、缠人的鬼魂，他曾围绕着她徘徊不出；喑哑地、喑哑地恳求着、吁告着。「软弱沉默的男人」——她从前这样叫他。有一次，两三天工夫，出于怜悯，出于柔情，或者只是想打发掉那个讨厌的鬼魂，她把他那哀伤的沉默所恳求的东西给了他——只是几乎刚一给予，就收了回去。先前那个夜里，他头一回走上这条街时，欲望已经蛀空了他的脏腑，他的身体仿佛是空的，令人作呕地、疼痛地空着；妒忌正以一种不倦的恶意，不住地向他提醒她的美——提醒她的美，和此刻爱抚着那美的可恨的、流氓的手，和凝视着那美的眼睛。那都是很久以前的事了。

「她确实漂亮，」希尔沃特说，隔着一两层地对冈布里尔最后那句话发表评论。「我看得出，谁要跟她缠上，她准能把人家弄得相当不好受。」连出了一两天的汗之后，他突然想到，人也许会觉得海水比淡水更解乏提神。那就怪了。

冈布里尔凶猛地放声大笑起来。「不过也有另外一些时候，」他兴致勃勃地接着说，「是别人妒忌我。」啊，复仇，复仇。在想象的那个更美好的世界里，人是有可能把本捞回来的。多少恶魔般的世仇血账，都在那里清算得大功告成！「我记得有一回，用法文给她写过一首四行诗。」（那是整件事过去多年之后写的，他从没寄给过任何人；不过那无关紧要。）「怎么说的来着？啊，对。」于是他配上相应的手势，朗诵道：

===Original===

> “ ‘Puisque nous sommes là, je dois,
>
> Vous avertir, sans trop de honte,
>
> Que je n’égale pas le Comte
>
> Casanovesque de Sixfois.’

Rather prettily turned, I flatter myself. Rather elegantly gross.”

Gumbril’s laughter went hooting past the Marble Arch. It stopped rather suddenly, however, at the corner of the Edgware Road. He had suddenly remembered Mr. Mercaptan, and the thought depressed him.
===Chinese===
> 「既然我们到了这里，我就得
>
> 不大害臊地预先奉告：
>
> 我比不上那位卡萨诺瓦式的
>
> 『六次伯爵』。」

转得怪俏皮的，恕我自夸。下流也下得相当风雅。

冈布里尔的笑声一路呼啸着掠过大理石拱门（Marble Arch）。然而到了埃奇韦尔路（Edgware Road）的街角，它却戛然而止。他猛然想起了默卡普坦先生，这个念头叫他沮丧起来。

## VI / VI

===Original===
It was between Whitefield Street and the Tottenham Court Road, in a “heavenly Mews,” as he liked to call it (for he had a characteristic weakness for philosophical paronomasia), that Casimir Lypiatt lived and worked. You passed under an archway of bald and sooty brick—and at night, when the green gas-lamp underneath the arch threw livid lights and enormous architectural shadows, you could fancy yourself at the entrance of one of Piranesi’s prisons—and you found yourself in a long cul-de-sac, flanked on either side by low buildings, having stabling for horses below and, less commodiously, stabling for human beings in the attics above. An old-fashioned smell of animals mingled with the more progressive stink of burnt oil. The air was a little thicker here, it seemed, than in the streets outside; looking down the mews on even the clearest day, you could see the forms of things dimming and softening, the colours growing richer and deeper with every yard of distance. It was the best place in the world, Lypiatt used to say, for studying aerial perspective; that was why he lived there. But you always felt about poor Lypiatt that he was facing misfortune with a jest a little too self-consciously.

Mrs. Viveash’s taxi drove in under the Piranesian arch, drove in slowly and as though with a gingerly reluctance to soil its white wheels on pavements so sordid. The cabman looked round inquiringly.

“This right?” he asked.

With a white-gloved finger Mrs. Viveash prodded the air two or three times, indicating that he was to drive straight on. Halfway down the mews she rapped the glass; the man drew up.

“Never been down *’ere* before,” he said, for the sake of making a little conversation, while Mrs. Viveash fumbled for her money. He looked at her with a polite and slightly ironic curiosity that was frankly mingled with admiration.

===Chinese===
卡齐米尔·利皮亚特（Casimir Lypiatt）生活与工作的地方，在怀特菲尔德街（Whitefield Street）与托特纳姆宫廷路（Tottenham Court Road）之间，一条他喜欢称作「天上马厩街」（heavenly Mews）的巷子里——因为他有个标志性的老毛病，偏爱哲学的双关（Mews 谐音 Muse，「缪斯」）。你从一道砖砌的拱门底下走过——那砖光秃秃、黑黢黢——到了夜里，拱门底下那盏绿灯把青灰的光和巨厦般的阴影投在四下，你会恍然觉得自己正站在皮拉内西（Piranesi）某座监狱画的入口——接着便置身一条长长的死巷，两侧排列着低矮的房屋：楼下是圈马的厩，楼上不那么宽敞的阁楼里，圈着做人。一股老派的牲口味儿，混着更有进步气象的烧机油臭。这里的空气似乎比外面的街上稠了一分；哪怕在最晴朗的日子朝巷子深处望去，你也能看见万物的形影渐渐昏昏、渐渐柔化，颜色随着一码又一码的纵深，愈积愈浓、愈沉。全世界研究空气透视的最佳去处，利皮亚特常说，就在此地；他之所以住在这儿，为的就是这个。可是对于可怜的利皮亚特，你心里总觉得：他拿玩笑去抵挡厄运，未免自觉得过了几分。

维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash）的出租汽车从皮拉内西式的拱门底下开了进来，开得慢慢吞吞，仿佛带着几分小心翼翼的不情愿，生怕在这样腌臜的地面上弄脏了它的白轮子。车夫探询地回过头来。

「是这儿吗？」他问。

维维什夫人用一根戴白手套的指头，朝空中戳了两三下，示意他只管径直往前开。走到巷子半当中，她敲了敲玻璃；车夫把车停了。

「俺从前可没到过这*地界儿*。」他说，为的是寻点儿话说，一面维维什夫人摸索着掏车钱。他打量着她，那目光里礼貌而略带讥诮的好奇，分明掺着倾慕。

===Original===
“You’re lucky,” said Mrs. Viveash. “We poor decayed gentlewomen—you see what we’re reduced to.” And she handed him a florin.

Slowly the taxi-man unbuttoned his coat and put the coin away in an inner pocket. He watched her as she crossed the dirty street, placing her feet with a meticulous precision one after the other in the same straight line, as though she were treading a knife edge between goodness only knew what invisible gulfs. Floating she seemed to go, with a little spring at every step and the skirt of her summery dress—white it was, with a florid pattern printed in black all over it—blowing airily out around her swaying march. Decayed gentlewomen indeed! The driver started his machine with an unnecessary violence; he felt, for some reason, positively indignant.

Between the broad double-doors through which the horses passed to their fodder and repose were little narrow human doors—for the Yahoos, Lypiatt used to say in his large allusive way; and when he said it he laughed with the loud and bell-mouthed cynicism of one who sees himself as a misunderstood and embittered Prometheus. At one of these little Yahoo doors Mrs. Viveash halted and rapped as loudly as a small and stiff-hinged knocker would permit. Patiently she waited; several small and dirty children collected to stare at her. She knocked again and again waited. More children came running up from the farther end of the mews; two young girls of fifteen or sixteen appeared at a neighbouring doorway and immediately gave tongue in whoops of mirthless, hyena-like laughter.

“Have you ever read about the pied piper of Hamelin?” Mrs. Viveash asked the nearest child. Terrified, it shrank away. “I thought not,” she said, and knocked again.

There was a sound, at last, of heavy feet slowly descending steep stairs; the door opened.

“Welcome to the palazzo!” It was Lypiatt’s heroic formula of hospitality.

===Chinese===
「您算走运的。」维维什夫人说。「我们这些穷途潦倒的大家闺秀——瞧瞧我们沦落到了什么田地。」说着，她递给他一枚弗罗林（florin，两先令银币）。

车夫慢条斯理地解开外衣，把硬币收进一只内袋。他望着她穿过肮脏的街面，一双脚前一脚、后一脚，分毫不差地落在同一条直线上，仿佛正踩着一道刀刃，两边是天晓得什么样的无形深渊。她像是漂着走的，每一步带一点弹跳，那条夏装裙子——白的，通身印满繁丽的黑色花样——在她摇曳行进的四围轻飘飘地鼓荡。好一位穷途潦倒的大家闺秀！车夫发动车子，带着一股不必要的狠劲；不知怎么，他心里竟实实在在有了几分义愤。

供马匹进去吃料歇息的那几扇宽大双扇门之间，开着窄窄的小门，供人进出——留给野胡（Yahoo，斯威夫特笔下的畜生人）走的，利皮亚特惯用他那掉书袋的大口气说；说这话时他还要纵声大笑，笑口大开如钟，一腔愤世的腔调，俨然自视为一位遭人误解、怨愤满腹的普罗米修斯（Prometheus）。维维什夫人就在其中一扇野胡小门前站定，把门敲得要多响有多响——在那副小小的、铰链发僵的门环许可的范围之内。她耐心等着；几个小脏孩聚拢过来盯着她看。她又敲了一回，又等着。更多的孩子从巷子远头奔跑而来；两个十五六岁的小妞儿出现在隔壁门洞里，立刻扯开喉咙，爆出一阵阵不带笑意、鬣狗似的尖叫。

「你读到过哈默尔恩的花衣魔笛手的故事吗？」维维什夫人问离她最近的孩子。那孩子吓坏了，缩了回去。「我就知道没有。」她说，又敲了敲门。

终于，一阵沉重的脚步声顺着陡梯缓缓下来了；门开了。

「欢迎莅临本府（palazzo）！」这是利皮亚特英雄气派的待客套话。

===Original===
“Welcome at last,” Mrs. Viveash corrected, and followed him up a narrow, dark staircase that was as steep as a ladder. He was dressed in a velveteen jacket and linen trousers that should have been white, but needed washing. He was dishevelled and his hands were dirty.

“Did you knock more than once?” he asked, looking back over his shoulder.

“More than twenty times,” Mrs. Viveash justifiably exaggerated.

“I’m infinitely sorry,” protested Lypiatt. “I get so deeply absorbed in my work, you know. Did you wait long?”

“The children enjoyed it, at any rate.” Mrs. Viveash was irritated by a suspicion, which was probably, after all, quite unjustified, that Casimir had been rather consciously absorbed in his work; that he had heard her first knock and plunged the more profoundly into those depths of absorption where the true artist always dwells, or at any rate ought to dwell; to rise at her third appeal with a slow, pained reluctance, cursing, perhaps, at the importunity of a world which thus noisily interrupted the flow of his inspiration. “Queer, the way they stare at one,” she went on, with a note in her dying voice of a petulance that the children had not inspired. “Does one look such a guy?”

===Chinese===
「总算受欢迎了。」维维什夫人纠正道，跟着他登上一道又窄又黑的楼梯，陡得像一架梯子。他身穿一件棉绒上衣、一条本该是白色的亚麻裤——只是早该洗了。他头发蓬乱，两手脏污。

「您敲门不止一回吧？」他回头望了一眼，问道。

「不止二十回。」维维什夫人夸大了事实，倒也情有可原。

「真是抱歉之至。」利皮亚特申辩道。「我一头扎进工作就太深了，您是知道的。等了很久吧？」

「反正孩子们看得挺乐。」维维什夫人被一桩疑心惹恼了——这疑心到头来多半全无根据——她疑心卡齐米尔（Casimir）那副沉酣于工作的模样，未免做得太自觉；疑心他听见了她的头一遍敲门，反而愈发深深地潜入那沉酣的渊底——真正的艺术家素来栖居之地，至少也该栖居之地——直到她第三次叫门，才慢吞吞、苦着脸站起来，心里说不定还在咒骂这世界纠缠不清，竟这样吵吵嚷嚷地打断他灵感的涌流。「怪得很，他们那么盯着人瞧。」她接着说，那副渐渐咽气的嗓音里带着一星烦躁——倒不是孩子们招惹出来的。「难道人家看上去就这么一副怪模样？」

===Original===
Lypiatt threw open the door at the head of the stairs and stood there on the threshold, waiting for her. “Queer?” he repeated. “Not a bit.” And as she moved past him into the room, he laid his hand on her shoulder and fell into step with her, leaving the door to slam behind them. “Merely an example of the mob’s instinctive dislike of the aristocratic individual. That’s all. ‘Oh, why was I born with a different face?’ Thank God I was, though. And so were you. But the difference has its disadvantages; the children throw stones.”

“They didn’t throw stones.” Mrs. Viveash was too truthful, this time.

They halted in the middle of the studio. It was not a very large room and there were too many things in it. The easel stood near the centre of the studio; round it Lypiatt kept a space permanently cleared. There was a broad fairway leading to the door, and another, narrower and tortuously winding between boxes and piled-up furniture and tumbled books, gave access to his bed. There was a piano and a table permanently set with dirty plates and strewed with the relics of two or three meals. Bookshelves stood on either side of the fireplace and lying on the floor were still more books, piles on dusty piles. Mrs. Viveash stood looking at the picture on the easel (abstract again—she didn’t like it), and Lypiatt, who had dropped his hand from her shoulder, had stepped back the better to see her, stood earnestly looking at Mrs. Viveash.

“May I kiss you?” he asked after a silence.

Mrs. Viveash turned towards him, smiling agonizingly, her eyebrows ironically lifted, her eyes steady and calm and palely, brightly inexpressive. “If it really gives you any pleasure,” she said. “It won’t, I may say, to me.”

===Chinese===
利皮亚特推开楼梯顶上的房门，站在门槛上等她。「怪？」他重复道。「一点儿也不。」她从他身旁走进屋去，他把手搭上她的肩头，与她并肩而行，任凭那扇门在他们身后砰地关上。「这不过是乌合之众对贵族气的个人本能嫌恶的一例。仅此而已。『哦，为什么我生来便长着一张与众人不同的脸？』不过，感谢上帝，我生来如此。你也一样。只是这份不同自有它的坏处：孩子们会扔石头。」

「他们倒没扔石头。」维维什夫人这一回实在得很。

两人在画室中央站定。屋子不算很大，塞的东西却太多了。画架立在画室近中央处；画架四周，利皮亚特常年清出一圈空地。一条宽阔的通道直通房门，另一条窄些，在木箱、堆叠的家具与散乱的书本之间七弯八绕，通向他的床。屋里有一架钢琴，还有一张桌子，长年摆着脏盘子，散落着两三顿饭的残迹。壁炉两侧立着书架，地板上还摊着更多的书，积尘的书堆上又摞着书堆。维维什夫人站着端详画架上那幅画（又是抽象的——她不喜欢），而利皮亚特——手已从她肩上放了下来——退后几步好把她瞧得更真切，站在那里一脸郑重地端详着维维什夫人。

「我可以吻你吗？」沉默半晌之后，他问。

维维什夫人朝他转过身来，挂着一副令人苦痛的微笑，眉毛讥诮地扬着，一双眼睛安稳、平静，淡淡地、亮亮地，不带一丝表情。「倘若这真能给您什么乐趣的话。」她说。「恕我直言，给我可是不会的。」

===Original===
“You make me suffer a great deal,” said Lypiatt, and said it so quietly and unaffectedly, that Myra was almost startled; she was accustomed, with Casimir, to noisier and more magniloquent protestations.

“I’m very sorry,” she said; and, really, she felt sorry. “But I can’t help it, can I?”

“I suppose you can’t,” he said. “You can’t,” he repeated and his voice had now become the voice of Prometheus in his bitterness. “Nor can tigresses.” He had begun to pace up and down the unobstructed fairway between his easel and the door; Lypiatt liked pacing while he talked. “You like playing with the victim,” he went on; “he must die slowly.”

Reassured, Mrs. Viveash faintly smiled. This was the familiar Casimir. So long as he could talk like this, could talk like an old-fashioned French novel, it was all right; he couldn’t really be so very unhappy. She sat down on the nearest unencumbered chair. Lypiatt continued to walk back and forth, waving his arms as he walked.

“But perhaps it’s good for one to suffer,” he went on, “perhaps it’s unavoidable and necessary. Perhaps I ought to thank you. Can an artist do anything if he’s happy? Would he ever want to do anything? What is art, after all, but a protest against the horrible inclemency of life?” He halted in front of her, with arms extended in a questioning gesture. Mrs. Viveash slightly shrugged her shoulders. She really didn’t know; she couldn’t answer. “Ah, but that’s all nonsense,” he burst out again, “all rot. I want to be happy and contented and successful; and of course I should work better if I were. And I want, oh, above everything, everything, I want you: to possess you completely and exclusively and jealously and forever. And the desire is like rust corroding my heart, it’s like moth eating holes in the fabric of my mind. And you merely laugh.” He threw up his hands and let them limply fall again.

===Chinese===
「你叫我吃了好些苦头。」利皮亚特说。他说得那么平静、那么不做作，迈拉（Myra）几乎吓了一跳；跟卡齐米尔打交道，她听惯的是更喧哗、更浮夸的倾诉。

「我很抱歉。」她说；而且她是真觉得抱歉。「可我也没有法子，对不对？」

「我想你是没有法子。」他说。「你是没有法子。」他又说了一遍，这时他的嗓音已变成普罗米修斯满腔悲苦时的嗓音。「母虎也没有法子。」他已在画架与房门之间那条畅通无阻的通道上来回踱起了步；利皮亚特就爱边说话边踱步。「你们喜欢玩弄到手的猎物，」他接着说，「非叫它慢慢死不可。」

维维什夫人放了心，淡淡一笑。这才是她熟识的卡齐米尔。只要他还能这样说话，还能说得像一本旧派法国小说，就不妨事；他就不见得真是那么不幸。她在最近的一把没堆东西的椅子上坐了下来。利皮亚特继续走来走去，一路挥着胳膊。

「可是话说回来，吃苦也许于人有益，」他接着说，「也许难免，也许必需。说不定我还该谢谢你呢。艺术家若是幸福的，他还干得成什么？他还想干成什么吗？说到底，艺术是什么？不就是对人生那可怕的风霜凌厉的一声抗议吗？」他在她面前停下，双臂一摊，摆出质问的姿势。维维什夫人轻轻耸了耸肩。她实在不知道；她答不上来。「啊，可这全是胡说，」他又迸出一句，「全是扯淡。我要幸福，要满足，要成功；我若是果真如此，活儿当然干得更好。我还要——哦，高于一切、高于一切——我要你：完完全全、独占独享、醋意深浓、直到永远地占有你。这欲望像锈，在腐蚀我的心；像蛀虫，在我心念的织物上蛀出一个个窟窿。而你只管笑。」他双手向上一扬，又任它们软塌塌地垂落。

===Original===
“But I don’t laugh,” said Mrs. Viveash. On the contrary, she was very sorry for him; and, what was more, he rather bored her. For a few days, once, she had thought she might be in love with him. His impetuosity had seemed a torrent strong enough to carry her away. She had found out her mistake very soon. After that he had rather amused her: and now he rather bored her. No, decidedly, she never laughed. She wondered why she still went on seeing him. Simply because one must see someone? or why? “Are you going to go on with my portrait?” she asked.

Lypiatt sighed. “Yes,” he said, “I suppose I’d better be getting on with my work. Work—it’s the only thing. ‘Portrait of a Tigress.’ ” The cynical Titan spoke again. “Or shall I call it, ‘Portrait of a Woman who has never been in Love?’ ”

“That would be a very stupid title,” said Mrs. Viveash.

“Or, ‘Portrait of the Artist’s Heart Disease’? That would be good, that would be damned good!” Lypiatt laughed very loudly and slapped his thighs. He looked, Mrs. Viveash thought, peculiarly ugly when he laughed. His face seemed to go all to pieces; not a corner of it but was wrinkled and distorted by the violent grimace of mirth. Even the forehead was ruined when he laughed. Foreheads are generally the human part of people’s faces. Let the nose twitch and the mouth grin and the eyes twinkle as monkeyishly as you like; the forehead can still be calm and serene, the forehead still knows how to be human. But when Casimir laughed, his forehead joined in the general disintegrating grimace. And sometimes even when he wasn’t laughing, when he was just vivaciously talking, his forehead seemed to lose its calm and would twitch and wrinkle itself in a dreadful kind of agitation. “Portrait of the Artist’s Heart Disease”—she didn’t find it so very funny.

“The critics would think it was a problem picture,” Lypiatt went on. “And so it would be, by God, so it would be. You *are* a problem. You’re the Sphinx. I wish I were Oedipus and could kill you.”

===Chinese===
「可我并不笑。」维维什夫人说。恰恰相反，她很为他难过；而且更糟的是，他颇叫她腻烦。有一回，有那么几天工夫，她曾以为自己也许爱上了他。他那股子火爆的冲劲，看上去像一道足以把她卷走的急流。她很快就发觉自己想错了。那以后，他一度还颇叫她觉得有趣；如今却颇叫她腻烦了。不，千真万确，她从不笑。她纳闷，自己为什么还在同他来往。仅仅因为人总得见什么人？还是为了什么？「我的肖像你还要画下去吗？」她问。

利皮亚特叹了口气。「画吧，」他说，「我看我还是抓紧干活的好。工作——唯有工作。《母虎肖像》。」愤世的泰坦巨人又开口了。「要不，就叫它《从未恋爱过的女人肖像》？」

「那可是个蠢透了的题目。」维维什夫人说。

「要不，《艺术家的心病肖像》（Portrait of the Artist’s Heart Disease）？这个题目好，这个题目真是好极了！」利皮亚特放声大笑，拍起大腿。他笑起来的模样，维维什夫人想，真是丑得出奇。他的脸仿佛整个儿散了架：没有一处角落不在欢笑的剧烈做怪里起皱、走形。连额头也在大笑里毁了。额头，通常是人脸上最有人性的部分。任凭鼻子抽动、嘴巴咧开、眼睛猴儿似的多眨巴；额头依旧能保持安详平静，额头依旧懂得怎样做人。可卡齐米尔一笑，他的额头也加入那一场全面瓦解的做怪。有时甚至他并不笑、只是起劲地说话，他的额头仿佛也会失了安详，在一种可怕的激动里抽搐、起皱。《艺术家的心病肖像》——她可觉得这话没有多么可笑。

「批评家们会当它是幅问题画。」利皮亚特接着说。「它也确确实实是，上帝作证，确确实实是。你*确实*是个难题。你是斯芬克斯（Sphinx）。但愿我是俄狄浦斯（Oedipus），好杀了你。」

===Original===
All this mythology! Mrs. Viveash shook her head.

He made his way through the intervening litter and picked up a canvas that was leaning with averted face against the wall near the window. He held it out at arm’s length and examined it, his head critically cocked on one side. “Oh, it’s good,” he said softly. “It’s good. Look at it.” And, stepping out once more into the open, he propped it up against the table so that Mrs. Viveash could see it without moving from her chair.

It was a stormy vision of her; it was Myra seen, so to speak, through a tornado. He had distorted her in the portrait, had made her longer and thinner than she really was, had turned her arms into sleek tubes and put a bright, metallic polish on the curve of her cheek. The figure in the portrait seemed to be leaning backwards a little from the surface of the canvas, leaning sideways too, with the twist of an ivory statuette carved out of the curving tip of a great tusk. Only somehow in Lypiatt’s portrait the curve seemed to lack grace, it was without point, it had no sense.

“You’ve made me look,” said Mrs. Viveash at last, “as though I were being blown out of shape by the wind.” All this show of violence—what was the point of it? She didn’t like it, she didn’t like it at all. But Casimir was delighted with her comment. He slapped his thighs and once more laughed his restless, sharp-featured face to pieces.

“Yes, by God,” he shouted, “by God, that’s right! Blown out of shape by the wind. That’s it: you’ve said it.” He began stamping up and down the room again, gesticulating. “The wind, the great wind that’s in me.” He struck his forehead. “The wind of life, the wild west wind. I feel it inside me, blowing, blowing. It carries me along with it; for though it’s inside me, it’s more than I am, it’s a force that comes from somewhere else, it’s Life itself, it’s God. It blows me along in the teeth of opposing fate, it makes me work on, fight on.” He was like a man who walks along a sinister road at night and sings to keep up his own spirits, to emphasize and magnify his own existence. “And when I paint, when I write or improvise my music, it bends the things I have in my mind, it pushes them in one direction, so that everything I do has the look of a tree that streams northeast with all its branches and all its trunk from the root upwards, as though it were trying to run from before the Atlantic gale.”

===Chinese===
这一套神话连篇！维维什夫人摇了摇头。

他从横七竖八的杂物中间挤过去，拾起一幅脸冲着墙斜倚在窗边的画布。他伸直胳膊把画举远端详，头以行家的姿态歪向一侧。「啊，画得好。」他轻声说。「画得好。你瞧。」随后他重新迈回空地里，把画支在桌旁，好让维维什夫人不挪座椅就能瞧见。

那是一幅她的暴风骤雨式的幻象；可以说，那是透过一场龙卷风看到的迈拉。他在肖像里扭曲了她，把她画得比本人更颀长、更瘦削，把她的双臂变成溜光的圆筒，又给她面颊的曲线罩上一层发亮的金属光泽。画中人仿佛从画布表面微微后仰，还带着侧倾，拧着一股象牙小雕像的拧劲——那雕像雕自一根巨牙弯翘的尖端。只是不知怎么，在利皮亚特的画里，那曲线似乎缺了风致，没有着落，也不成个道理。

「你把我画得，」维维什夫人终于开口，「好像正叫风吹得走了形似的。」这一通暴力的铺陈——图的是什么？她不喜欢，她一点儿也不喜欢。卡齐米尔却对这句评语大喜过望。他拍着大腿，那张不安分的、线条锋利的脸又一次笑得散了架。

「对，上帝作证，」他喊道，「上帝作证，正是这样！叫风吹得走了形。就是它：你说着了。」他又咚咚地满屋踱起步来，一路打手势。「风，我胸中的大风。」他捶了捶额头。「生命之风，狂野的西风。我觉出它在我体内，吹着，吹着。它挟着我走；因为尽管它在我体内，它却比我大，它是一股从别处来的力量，它是生命本身，它是上帝。它顶着逆运的锋头把我一路吹送，叫我干下去，斗下去。」他像一个人半夜走在险恶的路上，唱着歌给自己壮胆，把自己的存在加重、放大。「我作画的时候，我写作、即兴奏乐的时候，它便把我心里的东西吹弯，把它们往一个方向推，于是我做的每一样东西都带着一棵树的模样——连同全部枝桠、连同从根往上的整条树干，一齐向东北披靡，仿佛要躲避一场大西洋的飓风。」

===Original===
Lypiatt stretched out his two hands and, with fingers splayed out to the widest and trembling in the excessive tension of the muscles, moved them slowly upwards and sideways, as though he were running his palms up the stem of a little wind-wizened tree on a hilltop above the ocean.

Mrs. Viveash continued to look at the unfinished portrait. It was as noisy and easy and immediately effective as a Vermouth advertisement in the streets of Padua. Cinzano, Bonomelli, Campari—illustrious names. Giotto and Mantegna mouldered meanwhile in their respective chapels.

“And look at this,” Lypiatt went on. He took down the canvas that was clamped to the easel and held it out for her inspection. It was one of Casimir’s abstract paintings: a procession of machine-like forms rushing up diagonally from right to left across the canvas, with as it were a spray of energy blowing back from the crest of the wave towards the top right-hand corner. “In this painting,” he said, “I symbolize the Artist’s conquering spirit—rushing on the universe, making it its own.” He began to declaim:

> “Look down, Conquistador,

There on the valley’s broad green floor,

There lies the lake, the jewelled cities gleam,

Chalco and Tlacopan

Awaiting the coming Man;

Look down on Mexico, Conquistador,

Land of your golden dream.

Or the same idea in terms of music—” and Lypiatt dashed to the piano and evoked a distorted ghost of Scriabin. “You see?” he asked feverishly, when the ghost was laid again and the sad cheap jangling had faded again into silence. “You *feel*? The artist rushes on the world, conquers it, gives it beauty, imposes a moral significance.” He returned to the picture. “This will be fine when it’s finished,” he said. “Tremendous. You feel the wind blowing there, too.” And with a pointing finger he followed up the onrush of the forms. “The great southwester driving them on. ‘Like leaves from an enchanter fleeing.’ Only not chaotically, not in disorder. They’re blown, so to speak, in column of four—by a conscious wind.” He leaned the canvas against the table and was free again to march and brandish his conquering fists.

===Chinese===
利皮亚特伸出双手，五指张到最开，在肌肉过度的绷紧里微微发颤，缓缓地向上、向两侧移动，仿佛正把两只手掌顺着树干往上摩挲——摩挲一株小小的、叫风抽瘦了的树，长在一座俯临大洋的山顶上。

维维什夫人继续端详那幅未完成的肖像。它像帕多瓦（Padua）街头的一幅味美思（Vermouth）广告：喧闹，浅易，立竿见影。辛扎诺（Cinzano）、博诺梅利（Bonomelli）、金巴利（Campari）——显赫的名字。与此同时，乔托（Giotto）和曼特尼亚（Mantegna）却在各自的礼拜堂里发着霉。

「再瞧瞧这幅。」利皮亚特接着说。他取下夹在画架上的画布，递到她眼前供她查验。那是卡齐米尔的一幅抽象画：一列机器模样的形体，自右向左斜冲上画布，仿佛有一蓬能量的浪花从浪峰向后吹掠，扑向右上角。「在这幅画里，」他说，「我象征的是艺术家的征服精神——向宇宙猛冲过去，把它化为己有。」他开始朗诵：

> 「俯瞰吧，征服者（Conquistador），

下方，山谷那宽阔的绿茵谷底，

那里卧着湖泊，珠光宝气的城池熠熠生辉，

查尔科（Chalco）与特拉科潘（Tlacopan）

正等候着降临的那个人；

俯瞰墨西哥吧，征服者，

你黄金之梦的国土。

同一个意思，换音乐来说——」利皮亚特一个箭步冲向钢琴，召唤出斯克里亚宾（Scriabin）的一缕走了样的幽灵。「你看见了吗？」待幽灵重又被安抚下去、那阵凄凉廉价的叮当声又淡入寂静，他亢奋地问。「你*感到*了吗？艺术家向世界猛冲，征服它，赐它以美，把一种道德意义强加于它。」他回到画前。「画成之后，这幅会了不起。」他说。「惊天动地。在那里面，你也感到风吹。」他用一根指头顺着那些形体的冲势一路追过去。「西南巨风驱赶着它们前进。『仿佛被魔法师驱散逃遁的落叶』（雪莱《西风颂》句）。只是不乱，不散。可以说，它们排成四路纵队受着吹送——被一股有意识的风。」他把画布靠上桌边，又腾出身来，迈开步子，挥舞起他那双征服者的拳头。

===Original===
“Life,” he said, “life—that’s the great, essential thing. You’ve got to get life into your art, otherwise it’s nothing. And life only comes out of life, out of passion and feeling; it can’t come out of theories. That’s the stupidity of all this chatter about art for art’s sake and the aesthetic emotions and purely formal values and all that. It’s only the formal relations that matter; one subject is just as good as another—that’s the theory. You’ve only got to look at the pictures of the people who put it into practice to see that it won’t do. Life comes out of life. You must paint with passion and the passion will stimulate your intellect to create the right formal relations. And to paint with passion, you must paint things that passionately interest you, moving things, human things. Nobody, except a mystical pantheist, like Van Gogh, can seriously be as much interested in napkins, apples and bottles as in his lover’s face, or the resurrection, or the destiny of man. Could Mantegna have devised his splendid compositions if he had painted arrangements of Chianti flasks and cheeses instead of Crucifixions, martyrs and triumphs of great men? Nobody but a fool could believe it. And could I have painted that portrait if I hadn’t loved you, if you weren’t killing me?”

Ah, Bonomelli and illustrious Cinzano!

“Passionately I paint passion. I draw life out of life. And I wish them joy of their bottles and their Canadian apples and their muddy table napkins with the beastly folds in them that look like loops of tripe.” Once more Lypiatt disintegrated himself with laughter; then was silent.

===Chinese===
「生命，」他说，「生命——那才是头一等、最要紧的东西。你得把生命灌进你的艺术，不然它一文不值。而生命只能出自生命，出自激情与感受；它生不出于理论。为艺术而艺术啦、审美情感啦、纯形式价值啦，那一套呱噪的愚蠢正在于此。要紧的只有形式关系；这个题材跟那个题材一样好——理论上是这么说的。你只消看看把这套理论付诸实践的人画的那些画，就知道它行不通。生命出自生命。你必须怀着激情作画，激情自会刺激你的头脑，创造出恰当的形式关系。而要怀着激情作画，你必须画那些让你热切关注的东西，动人的东西，关乎人的东西。除了像梵·高（Van Gogh）那样神秘主义的泛神论者，谁也不可能当真对餐巾、苹果和瓶子感兴趣到那种程度——像对他的情人的脸、对复活、对人类的命运那样。倘若曼特尼亚画的不是受难、殉道和伟人们的凯旋，而是一组组基安蒂（Chianti）酒瓶配奶酪，他还构想得出他那些辉煌的构图吗？除了蠢货，谁也不会相信。倘若我不爱你，倘若你不是正在杀我，我画得出那幅肖像吗？」

啊，博诺梅利，显赫的辛扎诺！

「我以激情画激情。我从生命里汲出生命。至于他们的瓶子、他们的加拿大苹果、他们那一条条沾着污渍的餐巾——上面叠着的那一层层讨厌的褶子，活像盘成一圈圈的牛肚——就祝他们受用愉快吧。」利皮亚特又一次把自己笑得散了架；随后沉默了。

===Original===
Mrs. Viveash nodded, slowly and reflectively. “I think you’re right,” she said. Yes, he was surely right; there must be life, life was the important thing. That was precisely why his paintings were so bad—she saw now; there was no life in them. Plenty of noise there was, and gesticulation and a violent galvanized twitching; but no life, only the theatrical show of it. There was a flaw in the conduit; somewhere between the man and his work life leaked out. He protested too much. But it was no good; there was no disguising the deadness. Her portrait was a dancing mummy. He bored her now. Did she even positively dislike him? Behind her unchanging pale eyes Mrs. Viveash wondered. But in any case, she reflected, one needn’t always like the people with whom one associates. There are music halls as well as confidential boudoirs; some people are admitted to the tea-party and the tête-à-tête, others, on a stage invisible, poor things! to themselves, do their little song-and-dance, roll out their characteristic patter, and having provided you with your entertainment are dismissed with their due share of applause. But then, what if they become boring?

“Well,” said Lypiatt at last—he had stood there, motionless, for a long time, biting his nails, “I suppose we’d better begin our sitting.” He picked up the unfinished portrait and adjusted it on the easel. “I’ve wasted a lot of time,” he said, “and there isn’t, after all, so much of it to waste.” He spoke gloomily, and his whole person had become, all of a sudden, curiously shrunken and deflated. “There isn’t so much of it,” he repeated, and sighed. “I still think of myself as a young man, young and promising, don’t you know. Casimir Lypiatt—it’s a young, promising sort of name, isn’t it? But I’m not young, I’ve passed the age of promise. Every now and then I realize it, and it’s painful, it’s depressing.”

Mrs. Viveash stepped up on to the model’s dais and took her seat. “Is that right?” she asked.

Lypiatt looked first at her, then at his picture. Her beauty, his passion—were they only to meet on the canvas? Opps was her lover. Time was passing; he felt tired. “That’ll do,” he said and began painting. “How young are you?” he asked after a moment.

“Twenty-five, I should imagine,” said Mrs. Viveash.

===Chinese===
维维什夫人缓缓地、若有所思地点头。「我想你说得对。」她说。是的，他肯定对；必须有生命，生命才是要紧的。这恰恰是他的画之所以那么糟的缘故——她现在明白了；他的画里没有生命。喧闹有的是，手势有的是，还有一阵猛烈的、通了电似的抽搐；可就是没有生命，只有生命的做戏。管道上有一道裂缝；在这个人与他的作品之间的某处，生命漏掉了。他表白得太过了。可是没有用；那份死气掩藏不住。她的肖像是一具跳舞的木乃伊。他如今叫她腻烦了。她对他是不是当真有了反感？在她那双一成不变的淡色眼睛后面，维维什夫人暗自纳闷。不过话说回来，她思忖道，人跟自己来往的人犯不着个个都喜欢。世上有促膝密谈的香闺，也有杂耍剧场；有的人被让进茶会、引进一对一的知心话，另一些人则登上一座看不见的舞台——可怜见的，他们自己还瞧不见那舞台——跳上一段小小的歌舞，一套一套滚出各自拿手的套话，供完了你的消遣，便带着份内的掌声被打发下场。只是，他们要是变得无聊起来，那又怎么办呢？

「好吧，」利皮亚特终于说——他一动不动地在那儿站了老半天，咬着指甲——「我看咱们还是开始这一场写生吧。」他拾起那幅未完成的肖像，在画架上安放端正。「我已经浪费了好多时间，」他说，「说到底，能供人浪费的时间并没有那么多。」他说得阴郁，整个人忽然变得出奇地萎缩、泄了气。「并没有那么多。」他重复了一遍，叹了口气。「我至今还把自己当作个年轻人，年轻而有前程，你明白吧。卡齐米尔·利皮亚特——这名字一听就是个年轻有为的名字，对不对？可我不年轻了，我把有前程的年纪过了。隔三差五我就醒悟过来一回，一醒悟就疼，就灰心。」

维维什夫人登上模特台，坐了下来。「这样对吗？」她问。

利皮亚特先看看她，再看看自己的画。她的美，他的激情——难道它们只配在画布上相遇？奥普斯（Opps）才是她的情人。时间在流逝；他觉出疲倦。「就这样行了。」他说着动起画笔。「你有多年轻？」过了一会儿他问。

「二十五吧，我估摸。」维维什夫人说。

===Original===
“Twenty-five? Good Lord, it’s nearly fifteen years since I was twenty-five. Fifteen years, fighting all the time. God, how I hate people sometimes! Everybody. It’s not their malignity I mind; I can give them back as good as they give me. It’s their power of silence and indifference, it’s their capacity for making themselves deaf. Here am I with something to say to them, something important and essential. And I’ve been saying it for more than fifteen years, I’ve been shouting it. They pay no attention. I bring them my head and heart on a charger, and they don’t even notice that the things are there. I sometimes wonder how much longer I can manage to go on.” His voice had become very low, and it trembled. “One’s nearly forty, you know. …” The voice faded huskily away into silence. Languidly and as though the business exhausted him, he began mixing colours on his palette.

Mrs. Viveash looked at him. No, he wasn’t young; at the moment, indeed, he seemed to have become much older than he really was. An old man was standing there, peaked and sharp and worn. He had failed, he was unhappy. But the world would have been unjuster, less discriminating if it had given him success.

“Some people believe in you,” she said; there was nothing else for her to say.

Lypiatt looked up at her. “You?” he asked.

Mrs. Viveash nodded, deliberately. It was a lie. But was it possible to tell the truth? “And then there is the future,” she reassured him, and her faint deathbed voice seemed to prophesy with a perfect certainty. “You’re not forty yet; you’ve got twenty, thirty years of work in front of you. And there were others, after all, who had to wait—a long time—sometimes till after they were dead. Great men; Blake, for instance. …” She felt positively ashamed; it was like a little talk by Doctor Frank Crane. But she felt still more ashamed, when she saw that Casimir had begun to cry and that the tears were rolling, one after another, slowly down his face.

===Chinese===
「二十五？天哪，我二十五岁到现在，都快十五年了。十五年，一直都在战斗。上帝，有时候我多恨这些人啊！恨所有的人。我在意的还不是他们的歹毒；他们怎么来我怎么回敬就是。我在意的是他们那不发一言、无动于衷的本事，是他们把自己弄聋的那份能耐。我站在这儿，有话要对他们说，重要而不可少的话。我说了十五年还不止，我一直在呐喊。他们置若罔闻。我把我的头颅和心脏盛在银盘里捧给他们，他们甚至没看见盘子里有东西。我有时纳闷，自己还能撑得多久。」他的嗓音变得极低，而且发颤。「眼看就四十了，你知道。……」那嗓音沙哑地淡去，归于沉寂。他懒洋洋地、仿佛这差事已把他累坏似的，开始在调色板上调起颜色来。

维维什夫人望着他。不，他不年轻了；此时此刻，他确实仿佛变得比他的实际年龄老得多。站在那儿的是个老头儿，尖削、干枯、磨损。他失败了，他很不幸。可是，倘若这个世界竟让他成功，那它未免更不公正、更没有眼力了。

「有些人是相信你的。」她说；此外再没有别的可说了。

利皮亚特抬眼望她。「你呢？」他问。

维维什夫人郑重地点了点头。那是谎话。可是，难道能说实话吗？「再说还有将来呢。」她安慰他，她那气若游丝的临终嗓音，带着十足的确信，俨然在预言。「你还不到四十；你前面还有二十年、三十年的工作好做。况且从前也有过别的人，不得不等——等上好久——有时要等到死后。了不起的人；比如布莱克（Blake）。……」她简直觉得羞愧；这活脱是一篇弗兰克·克兰博士（Dr. Frank Crane）式的小谈话。可是她更觉羞愧的是，她看见卡齐米尔哭了起来，眼泪一颗接一颗，慢慢滚下他的脸。

===Original===
He put down his palette, he stepped on to the dais, he came and knelt at Mrs. Viveash’s feet. He took one of her hands between his own and he bent over it, pressing it to his forehead, as though it were a charm against unhappy thoughts, sometimes kissing it; soon it was wet with tears. He wept almost in silence.

“It’s all right,” Mrs. Viveash kept repeating, “it’s all right,” and she laid her free hand on his bowed head, she patted it comfortingly as one might pat the head of a large dog that comes and thrusts its muzzle between one’s knees. She felt, even as she made it, how meaningless and unintimate the gesture was. If she had liked him, she would have run her fingers through his hair; but somehow his hair rather disgusted her. “It’s all right, all right.” But, of course, it wasn’t all right; and she was comforting him under false pretences and he was kneeling at the feet of somebody who simply wasn’t there—so utterly detached, so far away she was from all this scene and all his misery.

“You’re the only person,” he said at last, “who cares or understands.”

Mrs. Viveash could almost have laughed.

He began once more to kiss her hand.

“Beautiful and enchanting Myra—you were always that. But now you’re good and dear as well, now I know you’re kind.”

===Chinese===
他放下调色板，跨上模特台，走过来跪在维维什夫人的脚边。他把她的一只手拢在自己双手中间，俯下身去，把它抵在自己的额头上，仿佛那是一枚驱散愁绪的护符，时不时亲它一下；不多时它就被泪水浸湿了。他几乎无声地哭着。

「不要紧的。」维维什夫人一迭声地重复，「不要紧的。」她把空着的那只手搁在他低垂的头上，安慰地拍着，像人拍一条把鼻子拱进你两膝之间的大狗。她一边做这个动作，一边就觉出它多么空洞、多么缺乏亲昵。她若是喜欢他，早把手指插进他的头发里去了；可不知怎么，他的头发颇叫她生厌。「不要紧的，不要紧的。」可是，当然，事情并非不要紧；她在假托名义地安慰他，而他跪拜在其脚下的人，根本就不在场——她离这一切场面、离他的全部苦楚，脱身得那样干净，隔得那样遥远。

「你是唯一，」他终于说，「唯一在乎我、懂得我的人。」

维维什夫人几乎要笑出来。

他又开始吻她的手。

「美丽而迷人的迈拉——你从来如此。可如今你又善良又可亲，如今我知道你是好心肠的了。」

===Original===
“Poor Casimir!” she said. Why was it that people always got involved in one’s life? If only one could manage things on the principle of the railways! Parallel tracks—that was the thing. For a few miles you’d be running at the same speed. There’d be delightful conversation out of the windows; you’d exchange the omelette in your restaurant car for the vol-au-vent in theirs. And when you’d said all there was to say, you’d put on a little more steam, wave your hand, blow a kiss and away you’d go, forging ahead along the smooth, polished rails. But instead of that, there were these dreadful accidents; the points were wrongly set, the trains came crashing together; or people jumped on as you were passing through the stations and made a nuisance of themselves and wouldn’t allow themselves to be turned off. Poor Casimir! But he irritated her, he was a horrible bore. She ought to have stopped seeing him.

“You can’t wholly dislike me, then?”

“But of course not, my poor Casimir!”

“If you knew how horribly I loved you!” He looked up at her despairingly.

“But what’s the good?” said Mrs. Viveash.

“Have you ever known what it’s like to love someone so much that you feel you could die of it? So that it hurts all the time. As though there were a wound. Have you ever known that?”

===Chinese===
「可怜的卡齐米尔！」她说。为什么人们总要缠进人家的生活里来？要是万事都能按铁路的原理来打理，那该多好！平行轨道——要的就是这个。有那么几英里路，你们并肩同速而行。车窗外有快活的攀谈；你拿你餐车里的煎蛋卷，去换他们车上的酥皮馅饼（vol-au-vent）。等要说的话说尽了，你就添一把汽，挥挥手，飞一个吻，扬长而去，沿着平滑锃亮的铁轨一路领先。可事实偏偏不是这样，偏有这些可怕的惨祸；道岔扳错了，两列车轰然相撞；要不然，就是有人趁你过站时跳上车来，惹人讨厌，说什么也不肯下去。可怜的卡齐米尔！可他惹恼她，他是个叫人吃不消的腻烦货。她早该停止同他来往了。

「这么说，你总不至于全然讨厌我？」

「当然不至于，我可怜的卡齐米尔！」

「你要是知道我爱你爱得多么苦！」他绝望地抬眼望着她。

「可这又有什么用呢？」维维什夫人说。

「你可曾尝过那样的滋味——爱一个人爱到觉得会为之死去？以至于无时无刻不在疼。仿佛身上有一处伤口。你可曾尝过？」

===Original===
Mrs. Viveash smiled her agonizing smile, nodded slowly and said, “Perhaps. And one doesn’t die, you know. One doesn’t die.”

Lypiatt was leaning back, staring fixedly up at her. The tears were dry on his face, his cheeks were flushed. “Do you know what it is,” he asked, “to love so much, that you begin to long for the anodyne of physical pain to quench the pain in the soul? You don’t know that.” And suddenly, with his clenched fist, he began to bang the wooden dais on which he was kneeling, blow after blow, with all his strength.

Mrs. Viveash leant forward and tried to arrest his hand. “You’re mad, Casimir,” she said. “You’re mad. Don’t do that.” She spoke with anger.

Lypiatt laughed till his face was all broken up with the grimace, and proffered for her inspection his bleeding knuckles. The skin hung in little white tags and tatters, and from below the blood was slowly oozing up to the surface. “Look,” he said, and laughed again. Then suddenly, with an extraordinary agility, he jumped to his feet, bounded from the dais and began once more to stride up and down the fairway between his easel and the door.

===Chinese===
维维什夫人挂出那副令人苦痛的微笑，缓缓点头，说：「也许尝过。可是人不会死的，你要知道。人不会死。」

利皮亚特仰着身子，目不转睛地死盯着她。泪水已在他脸上干涸，他的双颊烧得通红。「你可知道那是什么滋味，」他问，「爱到那步田地，以致开始渴望肉体疼痛的止痛剂，来浇灭灵魂里的疼痛？你是不知道的。」突然，他攥起拳头，朝自己跪着的木台猛砸起来，一拳接一拳，用尽了全身力气。

维维什夫人俯身向前，想按住他的手。「你疯了，卡齐米尔。」她说。「你疯了。别打了。」她说得带着怒气。

利皮亚特直笑到满脸都在做怪里碎裂开来，又伸出自己流血的指节给她验看。皮肉挂成一小绺一小绺的白边碎条，血正从底下慢慢渗上表面。「瞧。」他说，又笑了。随即，他以一种异乎寻常的矫健，一跃而起，蹦下台来，重新在画架与房门之间的通道上大步踱起方步。

===Original===
“By God,” he kept repeating, “by God, by God. I feel it in me. I can face the whole lot of you; the whole damned lot. Yes, and I shall get the better of you yet. An Artist”—he called up that traditional ghost and it comforted him; he wrapped himself with a protective gesture within the ample folds of its bright mantle—“an Artist doesn’t fail under unhappiness. He gets new strength from it. The torture makes him sweat new masterpieces. …”

He began to talk about his books, his poems and pictures; all the great things in his head, the things he had already done. He talked about his exhibition—ah, by God, that would astonish them, that would bowl them over, this time. The blood mounted to his face; there was a flush over the high projecting cheekbones. He could feel the warm blood behind his eyes. He laughed aloud; he was a laughing lion. He stretched out his arms; he was enormous, his arms reached out like the branches of a cedar. The Artist walked across the world and the mangy dogs ran yelping and snapping behind him. The great wind blew and blew, driving him on; it lifted him and he began to fly.

Mrs. Viveash listened. It didn’t look as though he would get much further with the portrait.

===Chinese===
「上帝作证，」他翻来覆去地说，「上帝作证，上帝作证。我觉出它在我身上。你们全体，我都对付得了；整个儿挨千刀的一伙。是的，我到头来还要胜过你们。一位艺术家」——他召来那尊传统的幽灵，它安慰了他；他做出一个护持的姿势，把自己裹进那幽灵灿亮披风宽大的褶裥里——「一位艺术家不会在不幸之下垮掉。他从不幸中汲取新的力量。酷刑逼他汗出新的杰作。……」

他开始谈他的书，他的诗和画；谈他脑子里所有那些伟大的东西，他已经完成的那些东西。他谈他的画展——啊，上帝作证，那一回要叫他们大吃一惊，要把他们统统震翻，这一回。血液涌上他的脸；高耸突出的颧骨上罩着一层红潮。他感觉得到眼球后面那股热血。他放声大笑；他是一头大笑的雄狮。他张开双臂；他硕大无朋，双臂伸展如雪松的枝桠。艺术家阔步走过世界，癞皮狗们在他背后狂吠着、撕咬着追逐。大风刮呀刮呀，驱赶着他向前；它把他托起来，他开始飞翔了。

维维什夫人听着。看样子，这幅肖像一时是画不下去了。

## VII / VII

===Original===
It was Press Day. The critics had begun to arrive; Mr. Albemarle circulated among them with a ducal amiability. The young assistant hovered vaguely about, straining to hear what the great men had to say and trying to pretend that he wasn’t eavesdropping. Lypiatt’s pictures hung on the walls, and Lypiatt’s catalogue, thick with its preface and its explanatory notes, was in all hands.

“Very strong,” Mr. Albemarle kept repeating, “very strong indeed!” It was his password for the day.

Little Mr. Clew, who represented the *Daily Post*, was inclined to be enthusiastic. “How well he writes!” he said to Mr. Albemarle, looking up from the catalogue. “And how well he paints! What *impasto*.”

===Chinese===
今天是新闻日（Press Day）。评论家们已陆续到场；阿尔比马尔先生（Mr. Albemarle）带着一副公爵式的和蔼在他们中间周旋。那个年轻的助理恍恍惚惚地在近旁打转，竖起耳朵去捕捉大人物们的高论，又要竭力装出并非偷听的样子。墙上挂着利皮亚特（Lypiatt）的画，利皮亚特的图录——序言加题解，厚厚一册——到了每个人手里。

「很有力量，」阿尔比马尔先生翻来覆去地说，「确实很有力量！」这是他当天的口令。

代表《每日邮报》（*Daily Post*）的小个子克卢先生（Mr. Clew）颇有慷慨激昂之意。「他文章写得多好！」他从图录上抬起眼来，对阿尔比马尔先生说。「画得又多好！好一股 *impasto*（厚涂笔触）！」

===Original===
*Impasto, impasto*—the young assistant sidled off unobtrusively to the desk and made a note of it. He would look the word up in Grubb’s *Dictionary of Art and Artists* later on. He made his way back, circuitously and as though by accident, into Mr. Clew’s neighbourhood.

Mr. Clew was one of those rare people who have a real passion for art. He loved painting, all painting, indiscriminately. In a picture gallery he was like a Turk in a harem; he adored them all. He loved Memling as much as Raphael, he loved Grünewald and Michelangelo, Holman Hunt and Manet, Romney and Tintoretto; how happy he could be with all of them! Sometimes, it is true, he hated; but that was only when familiarity had not yet bred love. At the first Post-Impressionist Exhibition, for example, in 1911, he had taken a very firm stand. “This is an obscene farce,” he had written then. Now, however, there was no more passionate admirer of Matisse’s genius. As a connoisseur and *kunstforscher*, Mr. Clew was much esteemed. People would bring him dirty old pictures to look at, and he would exclaim at once: Why, it’s an El Greco, a Piazetta, or some other suitable name. Asked how he knew, he would shrug his shoulders and say: But it’s signed all over. His certainty and his enthusiasm were infectious. Since the coming of El Greco into fashion, he had discovered dozens of early works by that great artist. For Lord Petersfield’s collection alone he had found four early El Grecos, all by pupils of Bassano. Lord Petersfield’s confidence in Mr. Clew was unbounded; not even that affair of the Primitives had shaken it. It was a sad affair: Lord Petersfield’s Duccio had shown signs of cracking; the estate carpenter was sent for to take a look at the panel; he had looked. “A worse-seasoned piece of Illinois hickory,” he said, “I’ve never seen.” After that he looked at the Simone Martini; for that, on the contrary, he was full of praise. Smooth-grained, well-seasoned—it wouldn’t crack, no, not in a hundred years. “A nicer slice of board never came out of America.” He had a hyperbolical way of speaking. Lord Petersfield was extremely angry; he dismissed the estate carpenter on the spot. After that he told Mr. Clew that he wanted a Giorgione, and Mr. Clew went out and found him one which was signed all over.

“I like this very much,” said Mr. Clew, pointing to one of the thoughts with which Lypiatt had prefaced his catalogue. “ ‘Genius,’ ” he adjusted his spectacles and began to read aloud, “ ‘is life. Genius is a force of nature. In art, nothing else counts. The modern impotents, who are afraid of genius and who are envious of it, have invented in self-defence the notion of the Artist. The Artist with his sense of form, his style, his devotion to pure beauty, et cetera, et cetera. But Genius includes the Artist; every Genius has, among very many others, the qualities attributed by the impotents to the Artist. The Artist without genius is a carver of fountains through which no water flows.’ Very true,” said Mr. Clew, “very true indeed.” He marked the passage with his pencil.

===Chinese===
*Impasto*，*impasto*——年轻助理不声不响地侧身挪到写字台边，把这个词记了下来。回头他要去查格拉布（Grubb）的《艺术与艺术家词典》（*Dictionary of Art and Artists*）。随后他兜兜转转、装作漫不经心，又踱回克卢先生近旁。

克卢先生属于那种对艺术怀着真热情的罕见人物。他爱绘画，爱一切绘画，不分青红皂白。置身画廊，他就像后宫里的土耳其人；张张都爱。他爱梅姆林（Memling），不亚于爱拉斐尔；他爱格吕内瓦尔德（Grünewald）与米开朗琪罗，爱霍尔曼·亨特（Holman Hunt）与马奈（Manet），爱罗姆尼（Romney）与丁托列托（Tintoretto）；同它们全体厮守在一起，他能多么快活！诚然，有时他也恨；但那只是在熟稔尚未孕育出爱情的时候（戏拟谚语「熟而生慢」）。比如 1911 年第一届后期印象派画展，他当时立场便极坚定。「这是一出淫猥的闹剧，」他那时写道。而如今，对马蒂斯（Matisse）的天才，再没有比他更热烈的崇拜者了。作为鉴赏家兼艺术研究者（*kunstforscher*，德语：艺术研究家），克卢先生备受推崇。人们会把又脏又旧的画拿来请他过目，他立刻惊呼：哎呀，这是幅埃尔·格列柯（El Greco），一幅皮亚泽塔（Piazetta），或者别的什么合适的名字。人家问他怎么看出来的，他便耸耸肩：可它通体上下都签着名呢。他的笃定与热忱都富于感染力。自打埃尔·格列柯时兴起来，他已发现那位伟大艺术家的早期作品几十件。单为彼得斯菲尔德勋爵（Lord Petersfield）的收藏，他就找出过四幅早期格列柯，全出自巴萨诺（Bassano）门生之手。彼得斯菲尔德勋爵对克卢先生的信服无边无际；连那桩原始派（Primitives）风波也不曾动摇它。那是桩令人痛心的事：彼得斯菲尔德勋爵的杜乔（Duccio）显出了开裂的迹象；庄园木匠被唤来看那块画板；他看了。「风干得比这更糟的伊利诺伊山核桃木，」他说，「我从没见过。」随后他看那幅西莫内·马尔蒂尼（Simone Martini）；那幅呢，正相反，他赞不绝口。纹理细密，风干透彻——它不会裂，不会，一百年也不会。「从美国出来的板材，没有比这一块更漂亮的了。」他说话惯用夸张。彼得斯菲尔德勋爵怒不可遏，当场把庄园木匠辞了。此后他告诉克卢先生，说他想要一幅乔尔乔内（Giorgione），克卢先生便出门给他寻了一幅来——通体签名的。

「这一则我很喜欢。」克卢先生指着利皮亚特缀于图录卷首的一则题识说。「『天才，』」他扶正眼镜，朗声读起来，「『即是生命。天才是自然的一股伟力。在艺术中，其余一切都不足道。现代的无能之辈畏惧天才、又嫉妒天才，为着自卫，发明了「艺术家」这个概念。艺术家有他的形式感、他的风格、他对纯粹之美的献身，等等，等等。但天才涵盖艺术家；每一个天才，都在许许多多品质之外，兼有无能之辈归于艺术家的那些品质。没有天才的艺术家，不过是雕凿无水之泉的匠人。』千真万确，」克卢先生说，「确实千真万确。」他用铅笔在那一段下面画了线。

===Original===
Mr. Albemarle produced the password. “Very strongly put,” he said.

“I have always felt that myself,” said Mr. Clew. “El Greco, for example.…”

“Good morning, what about El Greco?” said a voice, all in one breath. The thin, long, skin-covered skeleton of Mr. Mallard hung over them like a guilty conscience. Mr. Mallard wrote every week in the *Hebdomadal Digest*. He had an immense knowledge of art, and a sincere dislike of all that was beautiful. The only modern painter whom he really admired was Hodler. All others were treated by him with a merciless savagery; he tore them to pieces in his weekly articles with all the holy gusto of a Calvinist iconoclast smashing images of the Virgin.

“What about El Greco?” he repeated. He had a peculiarly passionate loathing of El Greco.

===Chinese===
阿尔比马尔先生祭出了他的口令。「表述得很有力量。」他说。

「这话我自己也向来深有同感，」克卢先生说。「比如埃尔·格列柯……」

「早安，格列柯怎么了？」一个声音一口气说道。马拉德先生（Mr. Mallard，mallard 意为「绿头鸭」）那具瘦长的、包着一层皮的骷髅，像一桩负疚的良心似的悬在他们头顶。马拉德先生每周为《每周文摘》（*Hebdomadal Digest*）撰稿。他的艺术知识浩瀚无涯，对一切美的东西则怀着真诚的厌恶。他真心钦佩的现代画家只有霍德勒（Hodler）一位。对其余诸公，他一律施以毫不容情的酷虐；他在每周的文章里把他们撕成碎片，那股神圣的兴致，俨然捣毁圣母像的加尔文宗圣像破坏者。

「格列柯怎么了？」他又问了一遍。他对格列柯怀有一种别样狂热的憎恶。

===Original===
Mr. Clew smiled up at him propitiatingly; he was afraid of Mr. Mallard. His enthusiasms were no match for Mr. Mallard’s erudite and logical disgusts. “I was merely quoting him as an example,” he said.

“An example, I hope, of incompetent drawing, baroque composition, disgusting forms, garish colouring and hysterical subject-matter.” Mr. Mallard showed his old ivory teeth in a menacing smile. “Those are the only things which El Greco’s work exemplifies.”

Mr. Clew gave a nervous little laugh. “What do you think of these?” he asked, pointing to Lypiatt’s canvases.

“They look to me very ordinarily bad,” answered Mr. Mallard.

The young assistant listened appalled. In a business like this, how was it possible to make good?

===Chinese===
克卢先生仰起脸，赔着小心朝他微笑；他是怕马拉德先生的。他那一片热忱，敌不过马拉德先生那些渊博而合乎逻辑的厌恶。「我不过引他做个例子罢了。」他说。

「但愿是拙劣的素描、巴洛克式构图、恶心的形体、刺目的敷色和歇斯底里的题材的例证。」马拉德先生狞笑着，露出满口老象牙。「格列柯的作品所能例证的，也只有这几样。」

克卢先生神经质地小小一笑。「这些您觉得如何？」他指着利皮亚特的画布问道。

「在我看来，它们坏得平平常常。」马拉德先生答道。

年轻助理听得胆战心惊。做这样一桩买卖，还怎么做得成生意？

===Original===
“All the same,” said Mr. Clew courageously, “I like that bowl of roses in the window with the landscape behind. Number twenty-nine.” He looked in the catalogue. “And there’s a really charming little verse about it:

> ‘O beauty of the rose,
> Goodness as well as perfume exhaling!
> Who gazes on these flowers,
> On this blue hill and ripening field—he knows
> Where duty leads and that the nameless Powers
> In a rose can speak their will.’

Really charming!” Mr. Clew made another mark with his pencil.

“But commonplace, commonplace.” Mr. Mallard shook his head. “And in any case a verse can’t justify a bad picture. What an unsubtle harmony of colour! And how uninteresting the composition is! That receding diagonal—it’s been worked to death.” He too made a mark in his catalogue—a cross and a little circle, arranged like the skull and crossbones on a pirate’s flag. Mr. Mallard’s catalogues were always covered with these little marks: they were his symbols of condemnation.

===Chinese===
「尽管如此，」克卢先生鼓起勇气说，「我还是喜欢那幅窗边一盆玫瑰、背后衬着风景的画。第二十九号。」他往图录里看了看。「底下还有一首真正迷人的小诗：

> 「哦，玫瑰之美，
> 吐纳芬芳，也吐纳善良！
> 谁若凝望这些花朵，
> 望这青山、这渐熟的田野——他便知晓
> 天职引向何方，知晓那无名的众神力
> 能在一朵玫瑰里言明旨意。」

真迷人！」克卢先生又用铅笔画了一道。

「可是平庸啊，平庸。」马拉德先生摇着头。「再说，一首诗终究开脱不了一幅坏画。这色彩的调和多么不含蓄！这构图又多么无趣！那条后退的对角线——早被人用滥了。」他也在图录里做了个记号——一个十字加一个小圈，排列如海盗旗上的骷髅与交叉骨。马拉德先生的图录上向来布满这类小记号：那是他的定罪符号。

===Original===
Mr. Albemarle, meanwhile, had moved away to greet the new arrivals. To the critic of the *Daily Cinema* he had to explain that there were no portraits of celebrities. The reporter from the *Evening Planet* had to be told which were the best pictures.

“Mr. Lypiatt,” he dictated, “is a poet and philosopher as well as a painter. His catalogue is a—h’m—declaration of faith.”

The reporter took it down in shorthand. “And very nice too,” he said. “I’m most grateful to you, sir, most grateful.” And he hurried away, to get to the Cattle Show before the King should arrive. Mr. Albemarle affably addressed himself to the critic of the *Morning Globe*.

“I *al*ways regard this gallery,” said a loud and cheerful voice, full of bulls and canaries in chorus, “as positively a *mauvais lieu*. Such exhibitions!” And Mr. Mercaptan shrugged his shoulders expressively. He halted to wait for his companion.

===Chinese===
这工夫，阿尔比马尔先生已挪开身子去迎新到的客人。他得向《每日电影报》（*Daily Cinema*）的评论人解释，这里没有名流的肖像。还得告诉《行星晚报》（*Evening Planet*）的记者，哪几幅是最好的画。

「『利皮亚特先生，』」他口授道，「『既是画家，又兼诗人和哲学家。他的图录是一篇——呃哼——信仰宣言。』」

记者用速记一字不落记了下来。「太好了，」他说，「太感激您了，先生，太感激。」说罢匆匆而去，要赶在国王驾临之前先到畜牧展。阿尔比马尔先生和颜悦色地应付起《环球晨报》（*Morning Globe*）的评论人来。

「我*向*来把这家画廊，」一个洪亮欢快的嗓音开了腔，那嗓音里满是公牛与金丝雀的合唱，「看成一个不折不扣的 *mauvais lieu*（法语：藏污纳垢的去处）。这样的展览！」默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan）说着，意味深长地耸了耸肩。他停住脚，等他的同伴。

===Original===
Mrs. Viveash had lagged behind, reading the catalogue as she slowly walked along. “It’s a complete book,” she said, “full of poems and essays and short stories even, so far as I can see.”

“Oh, the usual cracker mottoes.” Mr. Mercaptan laughed. “I know the sort of thing. ‘Look after the past and the future will look after itself.’ ‘God squared minus Man squared equals Art-plus-life times Art-minus-Life.’ ‘The Higher the Art the fewer the morals’—only that’s too nearly good sense to have been invented by Lypiatt. But I know the sort of thing. I could go on like that forever.” Mr. Mercaptan was delighted with himself.

“I’ll read you one of them,” said Mrs. Viveash. “ ‘A picture is a chemical combination of plastic form and spiritual significance.’ ”

“Crikey!” said Mr. Mercaptan.

“ ‘Those who think that a picture is a matter of nothing but plastic form are like those who imagine that water is made of nothing but hydrogen.’ ”

===Chinese===
维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash）落在了后头，一边缓缓前行，一边读着图录。「这简直是完整的一本书，」她说，「依我看，里头满是诗、随笔，甚至还有短篇小说。」

「哦，老一套的爆竹签格言（cracker mottoes，英国彩包爆竹里附的格言纸条）。」默卡普坦先生笑了。「这类玩意儿我知道。『照管好过去，未来自会照管自己。』『上帝的平方减去人的平方，等于「艺术加生活」乘「艺术减生活」。』『艺术越高，道德越少』——只可惜这一句太近乎常识，不会是利皮亚特想出来的。不过这一路货色我熟。这样的句子我能没完没了地编下去。」默卡普坦先生对自己得意极了。

「我念一条给你听。」维维什夫人说。「『一幅画，是造型形式与精神意蕴的化合物。』」

「老天爷！」默卡普坦先生说。

「『以为一幅画无非是造型形式的人，就像以为水无非是氢的人一样。』」

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan made a grimace. “What writing!” he exclaimed; “*le style c’est l’homme.* Lypiatt hasn’t got a style. Argal—inexorable conclusion—Lypiatt doesn’t exist. My word, though. Look at those horrible great nudes there. Like Carracis with cubical muscles.”

“Sampson and Delilah,” said Mrs. Viveash. “Would you like me to read about them?”

“Certainly not.”

Mrs. Viveash did not press the matter. Casimir, she thought, must have been thinking of her when he wrote this little poem about Poets and Women, crossed genius, torments, the sweating of masterpieces. She sighed. “Those leopards are rather nice,” she said, and looked at the catalogue again. “ ‘An animal is a symbol and its form is significant. In the long process of adaptation, evolution has refined and simplified and shaped, till every part of the animal expresses one desire, a single idea. Man, who has become what he is, not by specialization, but by generalization, symbolizes with his body no one thing. He is a symbol of everything from the most hideous and ferocious bestiality to godhead.’ ”

===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生做了个鬼脸。「好一笔文章！」他叫道；「*le style c'est l'homme*（法语：风格即人）。利皮亚特没有风格。Argal（戏拟莎剧丑角腔的『因此』）——无可辩驳的结论——利皮亚特并不存在。不过，我的天。瞧那边那几幅骇人的大裸体。像肌肉结成立方块的卡拉齐（Carracci）。」

「《参孙与大利拉》（Sampson and Delilah）。」维维什夫人说。「想不想我把写它们的文字念给你听？」

「千万不要。」

维维什夫人没有再坚持。她想，卡齐米尔（Casimir）写这首咏诗人与女人、错乱的天才、种种苦刑、挥汗铸成杰作的小诗时，心里想的一定是她。她叹了口气。「那几只豹子倒挺好。」她说着，又去看图录。「『动物是一个象征，它的形式自有深意。在漫长的适应过程中，演化一路提炼、简化、赋形，直到动物的每一部分都表达着同一个欲望、同一个意念。人之所以成为今日之人，不凭专化，而凭泛化，故而他的身体不象征任何单独的事物。他是一切的象征，从最丑恶凶残的兽性，直到神性。』」

===Original===
“Dear me,” said Mr. Mercaptan.

A canvas of mountains and enormous clouds like nascent sculptures presented itself.

“ ‘Aerial Alps’ ” Mrs. Viveash began to read.

> “ ‘Aerial Alps of amber and of snow,
> Junonian flesh, and bosomy alabaster
> Carved by the wind’s uncertain hands.…’ ”

Mr. Mercaptan stopped his ears. “Please, please,” he begged.
“Number seventeen,” said Mrs. Viveash, “is called *Woman on a Cosmic Background*,” A female figure stood leaning against a pillar on a hilltop, and beyond was a blue night with stars. “Underneath is written: ‘For one at least, she is more than the starry universe.’ ” Mrs. Viveash remembered that Lypiatt had once said very much that sort of thing to her. “So many of Casimir’s things remind me,” she said, “of those Italian vermouth advertisements. You know—Cinzano, Bonomelli and all these. I wish they didn’t. This woman in white with her head in the Great Bear.…” She shook her head. “Poor Casimir.”

===Chinese===
「我的天哪。」默卡普坦先生说。

一幅画布迎面而来：群山，还有如初生雕塑般的巨云。

「『空中阿尔卑斯』，」维维什夫人开始念。

> 「『琥珀与白雪的空中阿尔卑斯，
> 朱诺般的肌肤，隆乳的雪花石膏，
> 出自风那双游移之手的雕琢……』」

默卡普坦先生捂住了耳朵。「行行好，行行好。」他哀求道。
「第十七号，」维维什夫人说，「题为《以宇宙为背景的女人》（*Woman on a Cosmic Background*）。」一个女子的身形倚着柱子立在山顶，再往外，便是缀满星斗的蓝夜。「底下写着：『至少对一个人而言，她比繁星的宇宙更甚。』」维维什夫人记起利皮亚特有一次对她说过的，正是这一类话。「卡齐米尔的这么多东西都叫我想起那些意大利味美思酒广告。你知道的——辛扎诺（Cinzano）啦，博诺梅利（Bonomelli）啦，这一路货。我倒宁愿不想起。这个白衣女人，一颗脑袋枕在大熊座里……」她摇了摇头。「可怜的卡齐米尔。」

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan roared and squealed with laughter. “Bonomelli,” he said; “that’s precisely it. What a critic, Myra! I take off my hat.” They moved on. “And what’s this grand transformation scene?” he asked.

Mrs. Viveash looked at the catalogue. “It’s called ‘The Sermon on the Mount,’ ” she said. “And really, do you know, I rather like it. All that crowd of figures slanting up the hill and the single figure on the top—it seems to me very dramatic.”

“My *dear*,” protested Mr. Mercaptan.

“And in spite of everything,” said Mrs. Viveash, feeling suddenly and uncomfortably that she had somehow been betraying the man, “he’s really very nice, you know. Very nice, indeed.” Her expiring voice sounded very decidedly.

===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生放声大笑，笑得又吼又尖。「博诺梅利，」他说，「正是它，一丝不差。多好的批评家，迈拉（Myra）！我脱帽致敬。」他们继续前行。「那么这幕盛大的换景奇观（transformation scene，哑剧收场时的大变换布景）又是什么？」他问。

维维什夫人看了看图录。「题为《登山宝训》。」她说。「而且说真的，你知道吗，我倒相当喜欢它。那一大群身影斜斜涌上山坡，顶上孤零零一个身影——在我看来很有戏剧性。」

「我*亲爱*的，」默卡普坦先生抗议道。

「不管怎么说，」维维什夫人说，忽然觉出一种不自在，仿佛自己不知怎么已经背叛了那个人，「他真的很好，你要知道。真的很好。」她那气若游丝的嗓音，听上去斩钉截铁。

===Original===
“Ah, *ces femmes*,” exclaimed Mr. Mercaptan, “*ces femmes*! They’re all Pasiphaes and Ledas. They all in their hearts prefer beasts to men, savages to civilized beings. *Even* you, Myra, I *really* believe.” He shook his head.

Mrs. Viveash ignored the outburst. “Very nice,” she repeated thoughtfully. “Only rather a bore.…” Her voice expired altogether.

They continued their round of the gallery.

===Chinese===
「啊，*ces femmes*（法语：这些女人），」默卡普坦先生感叹道，「*ces femmes*！她们个个是帕西法厄（Pasiphae），个个是勒达（Leda）。她们心底里个个宁要兽不要人，宁要野蛮人不要文明人。连*你*也在内，迈拉，这我*真的*相信。」他摇了摇头。

维维什夫人不理会这番发作。「很好，」她若有所思地重复道。「只是未免有点儿乏味……」她的嗓音这回彻底咽了气。

他们继续绕着画廊看了下去。

## VIII / VIII

===Original===

Critically, in the glasses of Mr. Bojanus’s fitting-room, Gumbril examined his profile, his back view. Inflated, the Patent Small-Clothes bulged, bulged decidedly, though with a certain gracious opulence that might, in a person of the other sex, have seemed only deliciously natural. In him, however, Gumbril had to admit, the opulence seemed a little misplaced and paradoxical. Still, if one has to suffer in order to be beautiful, one must also expect to be ugly in order not to suffer. Practically, the trousers were a tremendous success. He sat down heavily on the hard wooden bench of the fitting-room and was received as though on a lap of bounding resiliency; the Patent Small-Clothes, there was no doubt, would be proof even against marble. And the coat, he comforted himself, would mask with its skirts the too decided bulge. Or if it didn’t, well, there was no help for it. One must resign oneself to bulging, that was all.

“Very nice,” he declared at last.

Mr. Bojanus, who had been watching his client in silence and with a polite but also, Gumbril could not help feeling, a somewhat ironical smile, coughed. “It depends,” he said, “precisely what you mean by ‘nice.’ ” He cocked his head on one side, and the fine waxed end of his moustache was like a pointer aimed up at some remote star.

Gumbril said nothing, but catching sight once more of his own side view, nodded a dubious agreement.

“If by nice,” continued Mr. Bojanus, “you mean comfortable, well and good. If, however, you mean elegant, then, Mr. Gumbril, I fear I must disagree.”

===Chinese===
