带着挑剔的眼光，冈布里尔（Gumbril）在博亚努斯先生（Mr. Bojanus）试衣间的镜子里，端详起自己的侧影和背影。充上气，专利充气坐垫裤鼓了起来，确确实实鼓了起来，只是鼓得颇有一番雍容的富态——这份富态若长在异性身上，恐怕只显得妙不可言、天然合理。然而长在他身上，冈布里尔不得不承认，未免有点儿放错了地方，有点儿自相矛盾。不过话说回来，人为了漂亮既然不得不受罪，那么为了不受罪，也就只好甘心难看。就实用而论，这条裤子是天大的成功。他一屁股沉沉坐上试衣间那张硬木长凳，竟像落在一个弹性欢腾的膝头上似的，被稳稳接住；毫无疑问，这条专利充气裤连大理石也顶得住。他安慰自己说，上衣的下摆会把那过于扎眼的鼓凸遮掩过去。倘若遮不住，唉，那也没辙。人只好认了这份鼓凸，如此而已。

「很好。」他终于宣称道。

博亚努斯先生一直默默注视着他的顾客，脸上挂着礼貌的、然而——冈布里尔忍不住这么觉得——也颇有几分揶揄的微笑；他咳嗽了一声。「那要看，」他说，「您所谓的『好』究竟作何解了。」他把头朝一侧一歪，上过蜡的髭须那细细的一翘，像一根指针，直指某颗遥远的星辰。

冈布里尔没说什么，只是又瞥见自己的侧影，便点头表示了一种将信将疑的同意。

「您要是把『好』理解为舒服，」博亚努斯先生接着说，「那自然再好不过。可您要是理解为雅致，那么，冈布里尔先生，恕我恐怕不能苟同。」

===Original===

“But elegance,” said Gumbril, feebly playing the philosopher, “is only relative, Mr. Bojanus. There are certain African negroes, among whom it is considered elegant to pierce the lips and distend them with wooden plates, until the mouth looks like a pelican’s beak.”

Mr. Bojanus placed his hand in his bosom and slightly bowed. “Very possibly, Mr. Gumbril,” he replied. “But if you’ll pardon my saying so, we are not African negroes.”

Gumbril was crushed, deservedly. He looked at himself again in the mirrors. “Do you object,” he asked after a pause, “to all eccentricities in dress, Mr. Bojanus? Would you put us all into your elegant uniform?”

“Certainly not,” replied Mr. Bojanus. “There are certain walks of life in which eccentricity in appearance is positively a sine qua non, Mr. Gumbril, and I might almost say *de rigueur*.”

“And which walks of life, Mr. Bojanus, may I ask? You refer, perhaps, to the artistic walks? Sombreros and Byronic collars and possibly velveteen trousers? Though all that sort of thing is surely a little out of date, nowadays.”

===Chinese===

「可是雅致这东西，」冈布里尔说，有气无力地充了一回哲学家，「原不过是相对的，博亚努斯先生。有那么一些非洲黑人，在他们中间，人们认为把嘴唇穿孔、拿木盘子撑大，撑得嘴巴活像一张鹈鹕的喙，那才算得上雅致。」

博亚努斯先生把一只手按进胸怀，微微一躬。「很可能，冈布里尔先生，」他答道，「不过恕我直言，我们可不是非洲黑人。」

冈布里尔被驳倒了，而且驳得应当。他又对着镜子照了照自己。「请问，」他顿了顿，又开口道，「您是不是一概反对衣着上出格的玩意儿，博亚努斯先生？您是不是想把我们统统装进您那身雅致的制服里去？」

「当然不是，」博亚努斯先生回答。「有那么一些行当，外表出格简直是不可或缺的先决条件（sine qua non），冈布里尔先生；我几乎可以说，是一种礼数上的必需（*de rigueur*）。」

「那么请问是哪些行当呢，博亚努斯先生？您指的莫非是艺术界的行当？阔边毡帽哇，拜伦式的衣领哇，兴许还有平绒裤子？不过这一类行头，如今想必都有点儿过时了吧。」

===Original===

Enigmatically Mr. Bojanus smiled, a playful Sphinx. He thrust his right hand deeper into his bosom and with his left twisted to a finer needle the point of his moustache. “Not artists, Mr. Gumbril.” He shook his head. “In practice they may show themselves a little eccentric and negleejay. But they have no need to look unusual on principle. It’s only the politicians who need do it on principle. It’s only *de rigueur*, as one might say, in the political walks, Mr. Gumbril.”

“You surprise me,” said Gumbril. “I should have thought that it was to the politician’s interest to look respectable and normal.”

“But it is still more to his interest as a leader of men to look distinguished,” Mr. Bojanus replied. “Well, not precisely distinguished,” he corrected himself, “because that implies that politicians look *distangay*, which I regret to say, Mr. Gumbril, they very often don’t. Distinguishable, is more what I mean.”

“Eccentricity is their badge of office?” suggested Gumbril. He sat down luxuriously on the Patent Small-Clothes.

“That’s more like it,” said Mr. Bojanus, tilting his moustaches. “The leader has got to look different from the other ones. In the good old days they always wore their official badges. The leader ’ad his livery, like everyone else, to show who he was. That was sensible, Mr. Gumbril. Nowadays he has no badge﻿—at least not for ordinary occasions﻿—for I don’t count Privy Councillors’ uniforms and all that sort of once-a-year fancy dress. ’E’s reduced to dressing in some eccentric way or making the most of the peculiarities of ’is personal appearance. A very ’apazard method of doing things, Mr. Gumbril, very ’apazard.”

===Chinese===

博亚努斯先生讳莫如深地笑了，一头戏谑的斯芬克斯。他把右手更深地按进胸怀，左手把髭须的尖梢捻得更细更尖。「不是艺术家，冈布里尔先生。」他摇了摇头。「实践起来，他们尽可以显得出格一点、negleejay 一点——照法国人的说法，就是有点儿不修边幅（négligé）。可他们犯不着在原则上显得与众不同。唯有政治家，才需要在原则上如此。可以说，唯有在政界的行当里，这才是礼数上的必需（*de rigueur*），冈布里尔先生。」

「您这话叫我吃惊，」冈布里尔说。「我原本以为，政治家看上去体面而像个正常人，才合乎他的利益。」

「可是，作为一位统领众人的领袖，看上去卓异出众，就更合乎他的利益了。」博亚努斯先生答道。「呃，倒也不尽然是卓异出众，」他纠正自己道，「因为那样说来，政治家们看上去就都得 *distangay*（气派出众；他把法语 distingué 念走了样）——遗憾的是，冈布里尔先生，他们往往并不如此。我想说的，毋宁是『可资辨识』。」

「出格就是他们官职的徽记？」冈布里尔提示道。他美滋滋地一屁股坐回专利充气裤上。

「这就对啦，」博亚努斯先生说，把两撇髭须向上一翘。「领袖就得看上去跟其余的人不一样。在美好的往昔，他们向来佩戴官方的徽记。领袖也同旁人一样，有一身自家的号衣，好表明他是何等人物。那才叫明白事理，冈布里尔先生。如今他可没有徽记啦——至少平日场合没有——因为枢密顾问（Privy Councillors）的制服啦、诸如此类一年才穿一回的化装行头啦，我都不算在内。他只好退而求其次：要么穿得别出心裁地出格，要么把自家仪表上的特异之处发扬光大。这个办事的路子，实在悬乎得很，冈布里尔先生，悬乎得很。」

===Original===

Gumbril agreed.

Mr. Bojanus went on, making small, neat gestures as he spoke. “Some of them,” he said, “wear ’uge collars, like Mr. Gladstone. Some wear orchids and eyeglasses, like Joe Chamberlain. Some let their ’air grow, like Lloyd George. Some wear curious ’ats, like Winston Churchill. Some put on black shirts, like this Mussolini, and some put on red ones, like Garibaldi. Some turn up their moustaches, like the German Emperor. Some turn them down, like Clemenceau. Some grow whiskers, like Tirpitz. I don’t speak of all the uniforms, orders, ornaments, ’ead-dresses, feathers, crowns, buttons, tattooings, earrings, sashes, swords, trains, tiaras, urims, thummims and whatnot, Mr. Gumbril, that ’ave been used in the past and in other parts of the world to distinguish the leader. We, ’oo know our ’istory, Mr. Gumbril, we know all about that.”

Gumbril made a deprecating gesture. “You speak for yourself, Mr. Bojanus,” he said.

Mr. Bojanus bowed.

“Pray continue,” said Gumbril.

===Chinese===

冈布里尔表示同意。

博亚努斯先生继续说下去，一面说，一面做着细小而利落的手势。「他们当中，有的戴大得吓人的衣领，像格拉德斯通（Gladstone）先生。有的佩兰花、夹单片眼镜，像乔·张伯伦（Joe Chamberlain）。有的蓄一头长发，像劳合·乔治（Lloyd George）。有的戴古里古怪的帽子，像温斯顿·丘吉尔（Winston Churchill）。有的穿黑衬衫，像这位墨索里尼（Mussolini）；有的穿红衬衫，像加里波第（Garibaldi）。有的把髭须往上卷，像德国皇帝；有的往下撇，像克列孟梭（Clemenceau）。有的蓄一把大络腮胡，像提尔皮茨（Tirpitz）。至于历朝历代、世界各地用来标识领袖的那些制服啦、勋章啦、饰物啦、头饰啦、翎羽啦、王冠啦、纽扣啦、文身啦、耳环啦、绶带啦、佩剑啦、拖裾啦、宝冠啦、乌陵啦、土明啦，等等等等，冈布里尔先生，我就更不消说了。我们这些通晓历史的人，冈布里尔先生，对这一切全都了然。」

冈布里尔做了一个辞谢的手势。「您这话只代表您自己，博亚努斯先生。」他说。

博亚努斯先生鞠了一躬。

「请讲下去。」冈布里尔说。

===Original===

Mr. Bojanus bowed again. “Well, Mr. Gumbril,” he said, “the point of all these things, as I’ve already remarked, is to make the leader look different, so that ’e can be recognized at the first *coop d’oil*, as you might say, by the ’erd ’e ’appens to be leading. For the ’uman ’erd, Mr. Gumbril, is an ’erd which can’t do without a leader. Sheep, for example: I never noticed that they ’ad a leader; nor rooks. Bees, on the other ’and, I take it, ’ave. At least when they’re swarming. Correct me, Mr. Gumbril, if I’m wrong. Natural ’istory was never, as you might say, my *forty*.”

“Nor mine,” protested Gumbril.

“As for elephants and wolves, Mr. Gumbril, I can’t pretend to speak of them with first-’and knowledge. Nor llamas, nor locusts, nor squab pigeons, nor lemmings. But ’uman beings, Mr. Gumbril, those I can claim to talk of with authority, if I may say so in all modesty, and not as the scribes. I ’ave made a special study of them, Mr. Gumbril. And my profession ’as brought me into contact with very numerous specimens.”

Gumbril could not help wondering where precisely in Mr. Bojanus’s museum he himself had his place.

“The ’uman ’erd,” Mr. Bojanus went on, “must have a leader. And a leader must have something to distinguish him from the ’erd. It’s important for ’is interests that he should be recognized easily. See a baby reaching out of a bath and you immediately think of Pears’ Soap; see the white ’air waving out behind and think of Lloyd George. That’s the secret. But in my opinion, Mr. Gumbril, the old system was much more sensible, give them regular uniforms and badges, I say; make Cabinet Ministers wear feathers in their ’air. Then the people will be looking to a real fixed symbol of leadership, not to the peculiarities of the mere individuals. Beards and ’air and funny collars change; but a good uniform is always the same. Give them feathers, that’s what I say, Mr. Gumbril. Feathers will increase the dignity of the State and lessen the importance of the individual. And that,” concluded Mr. Bojanus with emphasis, “that, Mr. Gumbril, will be all to the good.”

===Chinese===

博亚努斯先生又鞠了一躬。「是这样的，冈布里尔先生，」他说，「所有这些玩意儿的要点，正如我方才所言，是叫领袖看上去与众不同，好让他碰巧统领的那群牲口一 *coop d'oil*（一瞥；法语 coup d'œil 的讹音）就把他认出来。因为人群这个畜群，冈布里尔先生，乃是非有领袖不可的畜群。比方说羊吧：我就从没留意过它们有什么领袖；白嘴鸦也没有。反过来，蜜蜂呢，我琢磨着是有的。至少分蜂的时候有。我说错了，您只管纠正我，冈布里尔先生。博物学么，历来就算不上——如您可能会说的——我的 *forty*（拿手；法语 fort 给他念成了『四十』）。」

「也算不上我的。」冈布里尔抗议道。

「至于大象和狼，冈布里尔先生，我不敢妄称对它们有第一手的知识。美洲驼也没有，蝗虫没有，乳鸽没有，旅鼠也没有。可是人类，冈布里尔先生，唯有这一种，我却敢声称，谈论起来是有权柄的——容我怀着全部的谦逊这么说——而且不像文士那样。我对他们下过专门的功夫，冈布里尔先生。我的职业还叫我接触过为数极多的标本。」

冈布里尔不禁暗自纳闷：在博亚努斯先生那座博物馆里，他自己究竟占着哪个位置。

「人群这个畜群，」博亚努斯先生接着说，「非有领袖不可。而领袖非有一样东西、把他从畜群里标出来不可。他能轻易被人认出，这对他的利益攸关重大。看见一个娃娃从浴盆里往外探，你立刻想起皮尔斯肥皂（Pears' Soap）；看见一蓬白发在脑后飘扬，你立刻想起劳合·乔治。秘诀就在这里。不过依我的浅见，冈布里尔先生，老法子可明白得多：我说，发给他们正规的制服和徽记；叫内阁大臣们往头发里插翎羽。这样，人民仰望的将是一个真正固定的领袖符号，而不是区区个人的什么特异之处。胡子会变，头发会变，古怪的衣领会变；可一身好制服永远是一个样。给他们翎羽，这就是我的主张，冈布里尔先生。翎羽将增进国家的尊严，削减个人的分量。」博亚努斯先生加重语气，作了总结：「而那，冈布里尔先生，那将全然是好事。」

===Original===

“But you don’t mean to tell me,” said Gumbril, “that if I chose to show myself to the multitude in my inflated trousers, I could become a leader﻿—do you?”

“Ah, no,” said Mr. Bojanus. “You’d ’ave to ’ave the talent for talking and ordering people about, to begin with. Feathers wouldn’t give the genius, but they’d magnify the effect of what there was.”

Gumbril got up and began to divest himself of the Small-Clothes. He unscrewed the valve and the air whistled out, dyingly. He too sighed. “Curious,” he said pensively, “that I’ve never felt the need for a leader. I’ve never met anyone I felt I could wholeheartedly admire or believe in, never anyone I wanted to follow. It must be pleasant, I should think, to hand oneself over to somebody else. It must give you a warm, splendid, comfortable feeling.”

Mr. Bojanus smiled and shook his head. “You and I, Mr. Gumbril,” he said, “we’re not the sort of people to be impressed with feathers or even by talking and ordering about. We may not be leaders ourselves. But at any rate we aren’t the ’erd.”

===Chinese===

「您总不至于是要告诉我，」冈布里尔说，「说我只要肯穿着这条鼓鼓囊囊的裤子到大庭广众前去露面，就能当上领袖——是吗？」

「啊，那可不行，」博亚努斯先生说。「头一条，您总得有能说会道、支使人团团转的天才。翎羽给不出天才；可天才有几分，翎羽就能把那几分的效力放大几分。」

冈布里尔站起身来，动手去脱那条充气裤。他拧开气阀，空气气息奄奄地打着呼哨泄了出去。他也跟着叹了口气。「奇怪，」他若有所思地说，「我竟然从来不觉着需要一位领袖。我从没遇见过一个能让我全心全意钦佩或信奉的人，从没遇见过一个我想追随的人。把自己交到别人手里，我想，想必很惬意。想必叫人觉着暖洋洋、亮堂堂、熨熨帖帖。」

博亚努斯先生笑了笑，摇了摇头。「您和我，冈布里尔先生，」他说，「咱们可不是会被翎羽镇住的那号人，连能说会道、支使人团团转也镇不住。咱们自己未必当得上领袖。可无论如何，咱们也不是畜群。」

===Original===

“Not the main herd, perhaps.”

“Not any ’erd,” Mr. Bojanus insisted proudly.

Gumbril shook his head dubiously and buttoned up his trousers. He was not sure, now he came to think of it, that he didn’t belong to all the herds﻿—by a sort of honorary membership and temporarily, as occasion offered, as one belongs to the Union at the sister university or to the Naval and Military Club while one’s own is having its annual clean-out. Shearwater’s herd, Lypiatt’s herd, Mr. Mercaptan’s herd, Mrs. Viveash’s herd, the architectural herd of his father, the educational herd (but that, thank God! was now bleating on distant pastures), the herd of Mr. Bojanus﻿—he belonged to them all a little, to none of them completely. Nobody belonged to his herd. How could they? No chameleon can live with comfort on a tartan. He put on his coat.

“I’ll send the garments this evening,” said Mr. Bojanus. Gumbril left the shop. At the theatrical wig-maker’s in Leicester Square he ordered a blond fan-shaped beard to match his own hair and moustache. He would, at any rate, be his own leader; he would wear a badge, a symbol of authority. And Coleman had said that there were dangerous relations to be entered into by the symbol’s aid.

Ah, now he was provisionally a member of Coleman’s herd. It was all very depressing.

===Chinese===

「也许算不上主流的那群。」

「哪一群也不算，」博亚努斯先生傲然坚持道。

冈布里尔将信将疑地摇了摇头，扣上裤扣。如今细想起来，他倒拿不准，自己是不是其实哪一群都算——凭着一种名誉会员的资格、临时的、赶上机会就算一员，好比入了姊妹大学的联合会（the Union），或者自家俱乐部一年一度大扫除期间，暂且加入海陆军俱乐部（Naval and Military Club）。希尔沃特（Shearwater）的群，利皮亚特（Lypiatt）的群，默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan）的群，维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash）的群，他父亲那个建筑家的群，教育界的群（不过那一群，感谢上帝！如今正在远处的牧场上咩咩叫呢），博亚努斯先生的群——每一群他都有点儿沾边，哪一群都不全沾。却没有一个人属于他的群。怎么可能呢？没有哪只变色龙能舒舒服服地趴在苏格兰格子呢上过日子。他穿上外衣。

「衣裳今儿晚上就给您送去。」博亚努斯先生说。冈布里尔走出店门。在莱斯特广场（Leicester Square）一家戏剧假发匠的铺子里，他定做了一副淡金色的扇形假须，配上他自己的头发和髭须的颜色。不管怎么说，他要当自家的领袖；他要佩一枚徽记，一个权威的象征。而科尔曼（Coleman）说过，借着象征的助力，可以缔结种种危险的关系。

啊，这会儿他又临时算作科尔曼那群里的一员了。这一切，真叫人丧气。

## IX / IX

===Original===
Fan-shaped, blond, mounted on gauze and guaranteed undetectable, it arrived from the wig-maker, preciously packed in a stout cardboard box six times too large for it and accompanied by a quarter of a pint of the choicest spirit gum. In the privacy of his bedroom Gumbril uncoffined it, held it out for his own admiration, caressed its silkiness and finally tried it on, holding it provisionally to his chin, in front of the looking-glass. The effect, he decided immediately, was stunning, was grandiose. From melancholy and all too mild he saw himself transformed on the instant into a sort of jovial Henry the Eighth, into a massive Rabelaisian man, broad and powerful and exuberant with vitality and hair.

The proportions of his face were startlingly altered. The podium, below the mouth, had been insufficiently massive to carry the stately order of the nose; and the ratiocinative attic of the forehead, noble enough, no doubt, in itself, had been disproportionately high. The beard now supplied the deficiencies in the stylobate, and planted now on a firm basement of will, the order of the senses, the aerial attic of ideas, reared themselves with a more classical harmoniousness of proportion. It only remained for him to order from Mr. Bojanus an American coat, padded out at the shoulders as squarely and heroically as a doublet of the Cinquecento, and he would look the complete Rabelaisian man. Great eater, deep drinker, stout fighter, prodigious lover; clear thinker, creator of beauty, seeker of truth and prophet of heroic grandeurs. Fitted out with coat and beard, he could qualify for the next vacancy among the cœnobites of Thelema.

He removed his beard﻿—“put his beaver up,” as they used to say in the fine old days of chivalry; he would have to remember that little joke for Coleman’s benefit. He put his beaver up﻿—ha, ha!﻿—and stared ruefully at the far from Rabelaisian figure which now confronted him. The moustache﻿—that was genuine enough﻿—which had looked, in conjunction with the splendid work of art below, so fierce and manly, served by itself, he now perceived, only droopily to emphasize his native mildness and melancholy.

It was a dismal affair, which might have belonged to Maurice Barrès in youth; a slanting, flagging, sagging thing, such as could only grow on the lip of an assiduous Cultivator of the Me, and would become, as one grew older, ludicrously out of place on the visage of a roaring Nationalist. If it weren’t that it fitted in so splendidly with the beard, if it weren’t that it became so marvellously different in the new context he had now discovered for it, he would have shaved it off then and there.

===Chinese===
那副胡子从假发匠那里送来了：扇面形，淡金黄色，织在纱网上，保准瞧不出破绽。它被郑重其事地装在一只结实的硬纸板盒里——盒子比它本身大上六倍——随盒还附了四分之一品脱上等的贴须胶（spirit gum）。冈布里尔（Gumbril）在自家卧室四门紧闭的私密里为它开了棺，把它擎起来供自己赏鉴，抚弄那丝一般的柔滑，最后试戴起来，对着镜子先用手把它暂且按在下巴上。效果呢，他当即断定：摄人心魄，气派堂皇。一转眼，他眼看着自己从忧郁而太过温和，变成了一个快活的亨利八世（Henry the Eighth）式人物，一个魁伟的拉伯雷式巨人（Rabelaisian man）：宽阔，有力，浑身洋溢着生命力，洋溢着毛发。

他脸上的比例焕然改观，惊人得很。嘴巴以下的那层台基原先不够厚重，承不住鼻子那一身堂皇的柱式；而额头那推理性的阁楼层，本身固然无疑够高贵，却高得失了比例。如今胡子补足了柱座上的欠缺；既已坐落在意志这块坚实地基上，感官的柱式与思想的凌空阁楼，便以一种更古典的和谐比例拔立起来。剩下的只须向博亚努斯先生（Mr. Bojanus）定做一件美式大衣，两肩垫得方方正正、气概逼人，赛过十六世纪意大利的一件紧身上衣，那么他的模样便是一位十足的拉伯雷式巨人了。大吃的美食家，豪饮的酒客，善斗的壮士，了不得的情种；清醒的思想者，美的创造者，真理的寻求者，种种英雄气派的预言家。大衣与胡子齐备，泰勒玛（Thelema，拉伯雷《巨人传》中那座院规为「随心所欲」的修道院）的修士席上一旦出缺，他尽够资格递补。

他摘下胡子——照骑士时代黄金岁月的老话，就是「把河狸掀起来」（put his beaver up；beaver 古义指头盔的护面，俚语指胡须）；这个小小的玩笑他得记住，留去讲给科尔曼（Coleman）听。他把河狸掀了起来——哈，哈！——随即怏怏地瞪视着镜中当前这副远非拉伯雷式的身形。唇上的髭须倒是真货——先前它同底下那件辉煌的艺术品配在一处，显得何等剽悍、何等丈夫气——如今他看出来了，孤零零独当一面，只会蔫头蔫脑地把他天生的温和与忧郁衬托得更加分明。

那是一桩丧气的物事，年轻时的莫里斯·巴雷斯（Maurice Barrès）或许也有过这么一撇；斜斜的、蔫蔫的、往下塌的一撇，只长在一位勤勉的「自我培育者」（Cultivator of the Me，巴雷斯的主张）唇上方才顺理成章；而随着人上了年纪，它挂上一副咆哮的民族主义者的脸，便会不伦不类得可笑。若不是它同那把胡子配得天衣无缝，若不是在他新近为它觅得的这番新境地里，它变得那么奇妙地不同，他当场就把它剃了。

===Original===
Mournful appendage. But now he would transform it, he would add to it its better half. Zadig’s quatrain to his mistress, when the tablet on which it was written was broken in two, became a treasonable libel on the king. So this moustache, thought Gumbril, as gingerly he applied the spirit gum to his cheeks and chin, this moustache which by itself serves only to betray me, becomes, as soon as it is joined to its missing context, an amorous arm for the conquest of the fair sex.

A little far-fetched, he decided; a little ponderous. And besides, as so few people had read Zadig, not much use in conversation. Cautiously and with neat, meticulous fingertips he adjusted the transformation to his gummed face, pressed it firmly, held it while it stuck fast. The portals of Thelema opened before him; he was free of those rich orchards, those halls and courts, those broad staircases winding in noble spirals within the flanks of each of the fair round towers. And it was Coleman who had pointed out the way; he felt duly grateful. One last look at the Complete Man, one final and definitive constatation that the Mild and Melancholy one was, for the time at least, no more; and he was ready in all confidence to set out. He selected a loose, light greatcoat﻿—not that he needed a coat at all, for the day was bright and warm; but until Mr. Bojanus had done his labour of padding he would have to broaden himself out in this way, even if it did mean that he might be uncomfortably hot. To fall short of Complete Manhood for fear of a little inconvenience would be absurd. He slipped, therefore, into his light coat﻿—a toga, Mr. Bojanus called it, a very neat toga in real West Country whipcord. He put on his broadest and blackest felt hat, for breadth above everything was what he needed to give him completeness, breadth of stature, breadth of mind, breadth of human sympathy, breadth of smile, breadth of humour, breadth of everything. The final touch was a massive and antique Malacca cane belonging to his father. If he had possessed a bulldog, he would have taken it out on a leash. But he did not. He issued into the sunshine, unaccompanied.

But unaccompanied he did not mean to remain for long. These warm, bright May days were wonderful days for being in love on. And to be alone on such days was like a malady. It was a malady from which the Mild and Melancholy Man suffered all too frequently. And yet there were millions of superfluous women in the country; millions of them. Every day, in the streets, one saw thousands of them passing; and some were exquisite, were ravishing, the only possible soul-mates. Thousands of unique soul-mates every day. The Mild and Melancholy one allowed them to pass﻿—forever. But today﻿—today he was the complete and Rabelaisian man; he was bearded to the teeth; the imbecile game was at its height; there would be opportunities, and the Complete Man could know how to take them. No, he would not be unaccompanied for long.

===Chinese===
丧气的附生物。可是如今他要改造它，要给它添上它那更好的一半。查第格（Zadig，伏尔泰同名小说的主角）写给情妇的四行诗，刻诗的石板一断为二，剩下的半首竟成了对君王的大不敬。这撇髭须也是如此，冈布里尔一面小心翼翼往两颊和下巴上涂胶，一面想；单摆浮搁，它只会出卖我；可一旦同它失落已久的上下文接榫，立时便成了一条征服女性的多情臂膀。

有点牵强，他断定；有点笨重。况且读过《查第格》的人寥寥无几，谈话里派不上多少用场。他小心翼翼地用干净、讲究的指尖，把那件变身之物（transformation，旧时假发假须的名称）端端正正安到涂好胶的脸上，按结实，在它粘牢之前一直按着。泰勒玛的大门在他面前敞开；那些丰饶的果园、那些厅堂与庭院、那些在座座秀美圆塔的塔身里盘旋而上、绕出高贵螺旋的宽阔楼梯，都任他随意出入。而指给他这条路的，正是科尔曼；他理所应当地心怀感激。对着完人（the Complete Man）再看最后一眼，来一次最后而决定性的确认：那位温和而忧郁的先生（the Mild and Melancholy one），至少眼下是不在了；于是他满怀信心，准备出发。他拣了一件宽松的薄大衣——其实他压根用不着大衣，这天晴朗和暖；可是在博亚努斯先生的垫肩工夫完工之前，他只好用这个法子把自己放宽出去，哪怕这意味着他会热得难受。为了怕一点小小的不便，就在完人的成色上短斤缺两，未免荒唐。于是他套上那件薄大衣——博亚努斯先生管它叫托加（toga），一件地道的西部郡斜纹呢（whipcord）的漂亮托加。他戴上他那顶最宽、最黑的呢帽，因为要补足他的「完全」，头一样要紧的就是宽：身量的宽，心胸的宽，体恤人情的宽，笑容的宽，谐趣的宽，样样都要宽。收尾的一笔，是他父亲的一根粗大而古旧的马六甲手杖（Malacca）。他若养得一条斗牛犬，定会牵它同出。可惜没有。他独自一人，走进阳光里。

只是这「独自一人」，他不打算持续太久。这些晴暖的五月天，是恋爱的绝佳日子。这样的日子里还形单影只，就如同害了一场病。温和而忧郁的那位先生害这场病，害得也未免太勤了。而国内偏偏有几百万「过剩女子」；几百万啊。每天在街上，你都看得见成千的她们走过；其中有些精致绝伦，勾魂摄魄，是唯一可能的灵魂伴侣。每天成千位独一无二的灵魂伴侣。温和而忧郁的那位，任凭她们走过去了——永远走过去了。可是今天——今天他是完足的拉伯雷式巨人；他胡须披挂到了牙齿；那场白痴游戏正玩到酣处；机会总会有的，而完人自懂得如何把机会抓住。不，他不会独自一人太久的。

===Original===
Outside in the square the fourteen plane trees glowed in their young, unsullied green. At the end of every street the golden muslin of the haze hung in an unwrinkled curtain that thinned away above the sky’s gauzy horizon to transparent nothing against the intenser blue. The dim, conch-like murmur that in a city is silence seemed hazily to identify itself with the golden mistiness of summer, and against this dim, wide background the yells of the playing children detached themselves, distinct and piercing. “Beaver,” they shouted, “beaver!” and, “Is it cold up there?” Full of playful menace, the Complete Man shook at them his borrowed Malacca. He accepted their prompt hail as the most favourable of omens.

At the first tobacconist’s Gumbril bought the longest cigar he could find, and trailing behind him expiring blue wreaths of Cuban smoke, he made his way slowly and with an ample swagger towards the Park. It was there, under the elms, on the shores of the ornamental waters, that he expected to find his opportunity, that he intended﻿—how confidently behind his Gargantuan mask!﻿—to take it.

The opportunity offered itself sooner than he expected.

===Chinese===
外头，广场上十四棵悬铃木焕发着初生而无尘的绿。每条街的尽头，金色蝉翼纱似的薄霭垂着一幅不见褶皱的帘幕，往天际纱幔般的地平线上方渐渐稀薄下去，化成透明的乌有，衬出更浓的蓝。城市里那种权当寂静的低哑的、海螺般的嗡响，仿佛朦朦胧胧地同夏日的金色迷蒙融为一体；在这片朦胧而辽阔的底子上，孩子们玩耍的喊叫脱颖而出，清晰而尖锐。「河狸！」他们嚷道，「河狸！」还有，「上头冷不冷？」完人朝他们晃了晃那根借来的马六甲手杖，满含嬉戏的威吓。孩子们这声及时的欢呼，他当作最好的兆头领受了。

在头一家烟草店里，冈布里尔买了根找得到的最长的雪茄，身后拖着一缕缕渐渐咽气的古巴蓝烟，迈着从容的大摇大摆，一路朝公园走去。他指望在那儿、在榆树底下、在那片观赏水面的岸边，等到他的机会，并且打算——躲在高康大（Gargantua）式的假面背后，多么有把握！——把它抓住。

机会自己送上门来，比他指望的还快。

===Original===
He had just turned into the Queen’s Road and was sauntering past Whiteley’s with the air of one who knows that he has a right to a good place, to two or three good places even, in the sun, when he noticed just in front of him, peering intently at the New Season’s Models, a young woman whom in his mild and melancholy days he would have only hopelessly admired, but who now, to the Complete Man, seemed a destined and accessible prey. She was fairly tall, but seemed taller than she actually was, by reason of her remarkable slenderness. Not that she looked disagreeably thin, far from it. It was a rounded slenderness. The Complete Man decided to consider her as tubular﻿—flexible and tubular, like a section of boa constrictor, should one say. She was dressed in clothes that emphasized this serpentine slimness, in a close-fitting grey jacket that buttoned up to the neck and a long, narrow grey skirt that came down to her ankles. On her head was a small, sleek black hat, that looked almost as though it were made of metal. It was trimmed on one side with a bunch of dull golden foliage.

Those golden leaves were the only touch of ornament in all the severe smoothness and unbroken tubularity of her person. As for her face, that was neither strictly beautiful nor strictly ugly, but combined elements of both beauty and ugliness into a whole that was unexpected, that was oddly and somehow unnaturally attractive.

Pretending, he too, to take an interest in the New Season’s Models, Gumbril made, squinting sideways over the burning tip of his cigar, an inventory of her features. The forehead, that was mostly hidden by her hat; it might be pensively and serenely high, it might be of that degree of lowness which in men is villainous, but in women is only another﻿—a rather rustic one perhaps, rather *canaille* even, but definitely another﻿—attraction. There was no telling. As for her eyes, they were green, and limpid; set wide apart in her head they looked out from under heavy lids and through openings that slanted up towards the outer corners. Her nose was slightly aquiline. Her mouth was full-lipped, but straight and unexpectedly wide. Her chin was small, round and firm. She had a pale skin, a little flushed over the cheekbones, which were prominent.

On the left cheek, close under the corner of the slanting eye, she had a brown mole. Such hair as Gumbril could see beneath her hat was pale and inconspicuously blond. When she had finished looking at the New Season’s Models she moved slowly on, halting for a moment before the travelling trunks and the fitted picnic baskets; dwelling for a full minute over the corsets, passing the hats, for some reason, rather contemptuously, but pausing, which seemed strange, for a long pensive look at the cigars and wine. As for the tennis rackets and cricket bats, the school outfits and the gentleman’s hosiery﻿—she hadn’t so much as a look for one of them. But how lovingly she lingered before the boots and shoes! Her own feet, the Complete Man noticed with satisfaction, had an elegance of florid curves. And while other folk walked on neat’s leather she was content to be shod with nothing coarser than mottled serpent’s skin.

===Chinese===
他刚拐进皇后路（Queen's Road），正以一副深知自己在阳光下有权占一个好位置——甚至两三个好位置——的人的神气，信步走过怀特利百货（Whiteley's），忽见前头一个年轻女人正凝神端详「本季新款」。在他温和而忧郁的年月里，这样的女子他只配无可奈何地欣赏；可如今，在完人眼里，她俨然是一份天定的、唾手可得的猎物。她个子相当高，却因出奇地纤细而显得比实际更高。倒不是瘦得刺眼，远远不是。那是一种圆润的纤细。完人决意把她归入圆筒一类——柔韧而圆筒状，可以说，像一截蚺蛇（boa constrictor）。她穿的一身都在强调这蛇一般的苗条：一件扣到颈下的贴身灰色短上衣，一条直笼到脚踝的窄长灰裙。头上是一顶乌黑发亮的小帽，看上去几乎像金属打的。一侧缀着一簇暗金色的叶子。

那簇金叶，是她通身的素净平滑、一气呵成的圆筒线条里唯一的一点妆点。至于她的脸，说严格的美也罢、严格的丑也罢，都不是，而是把美与丑的成分合成一个出人意料的整体，古怪地、不知怎么非自然地动人。

冈布里尔也装出一副关心本季新款的模样，斜着眼从雪茄烧红的烟头那边觑过去，把她的五官盘点了一遍。额头，多半叫帽子遮了；也许幽然而安详地高，也许低到某种程度——那样的低，长在男人脸上是奸相，长在女人脸上却不过是另一种——兴许有点村气，甚至有点 canaille（法语：俗民）气，但确凿是另一种——魅力。无从知晓。至于那双眼睛，是绿的，清湛；在脸上分得很开，从厚重的眼睑底下、经由朝外眼角斜挑上去的缝隙里望出来。鼻子微微带鹰钩。嘴唇丰厚，嘴线却直，而且宽得出乎意料。下巴小而圆，结实。皮肤白皙，突出的颧骨上泛着一点红晕。

左颊上，紧挨着那只斜挑眼的眼角底下，有一颗棕色的痣。帽子底下露出的一绺头发，淡黄得毫不起眼。看罢本季新款，她缓缓前行，在旅行皮箱和成套野餐篮前略停了停；在束胸衣橱窗前足足流连了一分钟；帽子呢，不知何故，颇为不屑地一掠而过；可在雪茄和酒跟前却驻了足——这显得蹊跷——久久出神地端详。至于网球拍、板球棒、学生行头、绅士袜品——她连一眼也不曾施舍。可她在靴鞋橱窗前流连得多么深情啊！她自己的那双脚，完人满意地注意到，有着繁丽曲线的优雅。旁人脚下踩的是牛皮，她则非花斑蛇皮的鞋不屑穿。

===Original===
Slowly they drifted up Queen’s Road, lingering before every jeweller’s, every antiquarian’s, every milliner’s on the way. The stranger gave him no opportunity, and indeed, Gumbril reflected, how should she? For the imbecile game on which he was relying is a travelling piquet for two players, not a game of patience. No sane human being could play it in solitude. He would have to make the opportunity himself.

All that was mild in him, all that was melancholy, shrank with a sickened reluctance from the task of breaking﻿—with what consequences delicious and perilous in the future or, in the case of the deserved snub, immediately humiliating?﻿—a silence which, by the tenth or twelfth shop window, had become quite unbearably significant. The Mild and Melancholy one would have drifted to the top of the road, sharing, with that community of tastes which is the basis of every happy union, her enthusiasm for brass candlesticks and toasting-forks, imitation Chippendale furniture, gold watch-bracelets and low-waisted summer frocks; would have drifted to the top of the road and watched her, dumbly, disappearing forever into the green Park or along the blank pavements of the Bayswater Road; would have watched her forever disappear and then, if the pubs had happened to be open, would have gone and ordered a glass of port, and sitting at the bar would have savoured, still dumbly, among the other drinkers, the muddy grapes of the Douro, and his own unique loneliness.

That was what the Mild and Melancholy one would have done. But the sight, as he gazed earnestly into an antiquary’s window, of his own powerful bearded face reflected in a sham Heppelwhite mirror, reminded him that the Mild and Melancholy one was temporarily extinct, and that it was the Complete Man who now dawdled, smoking his long cigar, up the Queen’s Road towards the Abbey of Thelema.

===Chinese===
两人缓缓漂移着走上皇后路，一路在每家珠宝店、每家古玩店、每家女帽店前流连。陌生女子没有给他可乘之机；再说，冈布里尔寻思，她又怎么会给呢？他倚仗的那场白痴游戏，是双人同行的皮克牌（piquet），不是单人消遣的接龙（patience）。没有一个心智健全的人能独自玩它。机会，他得自己去制造。

他身上一切温和的东西、一切忧郁的东西，都带着一种犯恶心的不情愿，缩了回去，不敢去打破这份沉默——打破了，往后会有怎样妙不可言而又危机四伏的后果？或者，倘若换来的是那记罪有应得的抢白，又会怎样立竿见影地丢人现眼？——何况走到第十家、第十二家橱窗跟前，这份沉默早已变得意味深长得叫人无法忍受。换了温和而忧郁的那位，就会一路漂到路的尽头，本着那种构成一切美满结合之基础的趣味相投，陪着她一起热爱黄铜烛台和烤面包叉、仿奇彭代尔（Chippendale）家具、金镯表和低腰夏裙；漂到路的尽头，哑然望着她永远消失在绿意葱茏的公园里，或者沿着贝斯沃特路（Bayswater Road）空白的便道走远；哑然望着她永远消失，然后，倘若酒馆恰好开着门，就会走去要上一杯波特酒（port），坐到吧台边，在陌生的酒客中间，仍旧哑然地，品味杜罗河（Douro）浑浊的葡萄，和他自己那份独一无二的孤独。

温和而忧郁的那位，能做的也就只有这些了。可是，当他恳切地朝一家古玩店的橱窗里凝望，从一面假赫普尔怀特（Heppelwhite）镜子里照见自己那张威风凛凛的胡子脸时，他想起温和而忧郁的那位已经暂时绝种，此刻抽着长雪茄、一路闲荡上皇后路、向着泰勒玛修道院去的，是完人。

===Original===
He squared his shoulders; in that loose toga of Mr. Bojanus’s he looked as copious as François Premier. The time, he decided, had come.

It was at this moment that the reflection of the stranger’s face joined itself in the little mirror, as she made a little movement away from the Old Welsh dresser in the corner, to that of his own. She looked at the spurious Heppelwhite. Their eyes met in the hospitable glass. Gumbril smiled. The corners of the stranger’s wide mouth seemed faintly to move; like petals of the magnolia, her eyelids came slowly down over her slanting eyes. Gumbril turned from the reflection to the reality.

“If you want to say Beaver,” he said, “you may.”

The Complete Man had made his first speech.

===Chinese===
他挺了挺肩膀；裹在博亚努斯先生那件宽松的托加里，他看上去像弗朗索瓦一世（François Premier）一样丰沛充沛。时候到了，他断定。

恰在此刻，那陌生女子从墙角的老威尔士碗柜边挪动了一小步，她那张脸的倒影便在小镜子里同他自己的脸合到了一处。她望的是那件冒牌赫普尔怀特。两双眼睛在这面好客的镜子里相遇了。冈布里尔微微一笑。陌生女子那张宽嘴的嘴角仿佛轻轻动了动；木兰花瓣一般，她的眼睑缓缓罩落，掩住了那双斜挑的眼。冈布里尔从倒影转向了实体。

「您要是想说『河狸』，」他说，「请便。」

完人说出了他的第一句话。

===Original===
“I want to say nothing,” said the stranger. She spoke with a charming precision and distinctness, lingering with a pretty emphasis on the *n* of nothing. “N﻿—n﻿—nothing”﻿—it sounded rather final. She turned away, she moved on.

But the Complete Man was not one to be put off by a mere ultimatum. “There,” he said, falling into step with her, “now I’ve had it﻿—the deserved snub. Honour is saved, prestige duly upheld. Now we can get on with our conversation.”

The Mild and Melancholy one stood by, gasping with astonished admiration.

“You are v﻿—very impertinent,” said the stranger, smiling and looking up from under the magnolia petals.

“It is in my character,” said the Complete Man. “You mustn’t blame me. One cannot escape from one’s heredity; that’s one’s share of original sin.”

===Chinese===
「我想说的是：没——没什么。」陌生女子说。她说起话来带着一种讨人喜欢的精确与清亮，在那个「没」字的鼻音上娇娇地逗留了一下，加重了它。「没——没——没什么」——听上去颇有定论的意味。她转过脸去，继续前行。

可是完人岂是一纸最后通牒打发得了的。「瞧，」他同她并起肩来，说，「这下我领到了——那记罪有应得的抢白。名誉保全了，威望也适时地维护住了。现在咱们可以接着谈咱们的了。」

温和而忧郁的那位站在一旁，惊羡得直喘气。

「您真——真是无礼得可以。」陌生女子说，微笑着，从木兰花瓣底下抬眼望他。

「这是我的本性使然。」完人说。「您可不能怪我。人逃不脱自己的遗传；那是一个人名下分得的那份原罪。」

===Original===
“There is always grace,” said the stranger.

Gumbril caressed his beard. “True,” he replied.

“I advise you to pr﻿—ray for it.”

His prayer, the Mild and Melancholy one reflected, had already been answered. The original sin in him had been self-corrected.

===Chinese===
「总有恩典在。」陌生女子说。

冈布里尔摩挲着他的胡子。「不错。」他答道。

「那我劝您为它祈——祈祷。」

他的祈祷，温和而忧郁的那位思忖道，早已蒙了垂听。他身上的原罪，已经自行校正过了。

===Original===
“Here is another antique shop,” said Gumbril. “Shall we stop and have a look at it?”

The stranger glanced at him doubtfully. But he looked quite serious. They stopped.

“How revolting this sham cottage furniture is,” Gumbril remarked. The shop, he noticed, was called “Ye Olde Farme House.”

The stranger, who had been on the point of saying how much she liked those lovely Old Welsh dressers, gave him her heartiest agreement. “So v﻿—vulgar.”

“So horribly refined. So refined and artistic.”

She laughed on a descending chromatic scale. This was excitingly new. Poor Aunt Aggie with her Arts and Crafts, and her old English furniture. And to think she had taken them so seriously! She saw in a flash the fastidious lady that she now was﻿—with Louis whatever-it-was furniture at home, and jewels, and young poets to tea, and real artists. In the past, when she had imagined herself entertaining real artists, it had always been among really artistic furniture. Aunt Aggie’s furniture. But now﻿—no, oh no. This man was probably an artist. His beard; and that big black hat. But not poor; very well dressed.

===Chinese===
「这儿又是一家古玩店。」冈布里尔说。「咱们停下来瞧瞧？」

陌生女子将信将疑地瞥了他一眼。可他看上去一本正经。两人停了下来。

「这些假冒农舍的家具，真叫人恶心。」冈布里尔评道。这家铺子，他注意到，招牌叫「老——农舍」（Ye Olde Farme House，故意仿古的拼写）。

陌生女子本来正要说，她多么喜爱那些可爱的老威尔士碗柜；一听这话，赶忙全心全意地附和他。「俗——俗气透了。」

「精致得吓人。又精致，又艺术。」

她顺着一条下行半音阶笑了下去。这一套新鲜得叫人兴奋。可怜的艾吉姨妈（Aunt Aggie），抱着她那套工艺美术（Arts and Crafts），她那套老英格兰家具。想想吧，自己当年竟拿它们当了真！她霎时间看清了如今自己已是何等人物——那位挑剔的贵妇（the fastidious lady）——家里摆的是路易什么式来着的家具，有珠宝，有年轻诗人来喝茶，有真正的艺术家。从前，她想象自己款待真正的艺术家时，背景总得是真正艺术的家具。艾吉姨妈的家具。可是如今——不，哦不。眼前这位多半就是个艺术家。瞧那把胡子；还有那顶黑色大帽。而且不穷；穿得讲究极了。

===Original===
“Yes, it’s funny to think that there are people who call that sort of thing artistic. One’s quite s﻿—sorry for them,” she added, with a little hiss.

“You have a kind heart,” said Gumbril. “I’m glad to see that.”

“Not v﻿—very kind, I’m af﻿—fraid.” She looked at him sideways, and significantly as the fastidious lady would have looked at one of the poets.

“Well, kind enough, I hope,” said the Complete Man. He was delighted with his new acquaintance.

Together they disembogued into the Bayswater Road. It was here, Gumbril reflected, that the Mild and Melancholy one would dumbly have slunk away to his glass of port and his loneliness among the alien topers at the bar. But the Complete Man took his new friend by the elbow, and steered her into the traffic. Together they crossed the road, together entered the park.

===Chinese===
「可不是，想想真好笑，竟有人管那样的东西叫艺术。真叫人替他们难——难过。」她补上一句，带一点咝咝的气音。

「您心肠真好。」冈布里尔说。「我很高兴见到这一点。」

「也——也说不上多好，我——我怕。」她侧过脸瞟他，那眼色意味深长，正如挑剔的贵妇打量她那班诗人中的一位。

「呃，够好的了，我希望。」完人说。他对这位新相识欢喜得很。

两人一齐「泄」入贝斯沃特路。正是在这里，冈布里尔想，温和而忧郁的那位本会哑然地溜走，去吧台上陌生的酒客中间，消受他那杯波特酒和他的孤独。可完人却轻轻挽住新朋友的手肘，引着她切进车流。两人一同过了马路，一同进了公园。

===Original===
“I still think you are v﻿—very impertinent,” said the lady. “What induced you to follow me?”

With a single comprehensive gesture, Gumbril indicated the sun, the sky, the green trees airily glittering, the grass, the emerald lights and violet shadows of the rustic distance. “On a day like this,” he said, “how could I help it?”

“Original sin?”

“Oh,” the Complete Man modestly shook his head, “I lay no claim to originality in this.”

The stranger laughed. This was nearly as good as a young poet at the tea-table. She was very glad that she’d decided, after all, to put on her best suit this afternoon, even if it was a little stuffy for the warmth of the day. He, too, she noticed, was wearing a greatcoat; which seemed rather odd.

===Chinese===
「我还是觉得您真——真是无礼。」这位太太说。「您怎么会想起来跟着我？」

冈布里尔只做一个总揽一切的手势，指了指太阳，指了指天，指了指轻盈闪亮的绿树和青草，指了指乡野远景里翡翠的光、紫罗兰的影。「这样的一天，」他说，「我能有什么法子？」

「原罪？」

「哦，」完人谦逊地摇了摇头，「在这上头我可不敢妄称有什么独创。」

陌生女子笑了。这一手，差不多抵得上茶桌旁的年轻诗人了。她暗自庆幸，今天下午到底还是决定穿了这身最好的套装，哪怕这样的暖天穿着有点闷。她还注意到，他也穿着一件大衣；这显得相当蹊跷。

===Original===
“Is it original,” he went on, “to go and tumble stupidly like an elephant into a pitfall, head over ears, at first sight﻿ ﻿… ?”

She looked at him sideways, then closed down the magnolia petals, and smiled. This was going to be the real thing﻿—one of those long, those interminable, or, at any rate, indefinitely renewable conversations about love; witty, subtle, penetrating and bold, like the conversations in books, like the conversations across the tea-table between brilliant young poets and ladies of quality, grown fastidious through an excessive experience, fastidious and a little weary, but still, in their subtle way, insatiably curious.

“Suppose we sit down,” suggested Gumbril, and he pointed to a couple of green iron chairs, standing isolated in the middle of the grass close together and with their fronts slanting inwards a little towards one another in a position that suggested a confidential intimacy. At the prospect of the conversation that, inevitably, was about to unroll itself, he felt decidedly less elated than did his new friend. If there was anything he disliked it was conversations about love. It bored him, oh, it bored him most horribly, this minute analysis of the passion that young women always seemed to expect one, at some point or other in one’s relation with them, to make. How love alters the character for both good and bad; how physical passion need not be incompatible with the spiritual; how a hateful and tyrannous possessiveness can be allied in love with the most unselfish solicitude for the other party﻿—oh, he knew all this and much more, so well, so well. And whether one can be in love with more than one person at a time, whether love can exist without jealousy, whether pity, affection, desire can in any way replace the full and genuine passion﻿—how often he had had to thrash out these dreary questions!

And all the philosophic speculations were equally familiar, all the physiological and anthropological and psychological facts. In the theory of the subject he had ceased to take any interest. Unhappily, a discussion of the theory always seemed to be an essential preliminary to the practice of it. He sighed a little wearily as he took his seat on the green iron chair. But then, recollecting that he was now the Complete Man, and that the Complete Man must do everything with a flourish and a high hand, he leaned forward and, smiling with a charming insolence through his beard, began:

===Chinese===
「难道一见倾心，就蠢头蠢脑地像大象一头栽进陷阱、栽个倒栽葱——这也算得上独创么……？」

她侧眼看他，然后合下木兰花瓣，微笑了。这回可要来真的了——那种漫长的、没完没了的、或者至少可以无限续期的关于爱情的谈话；机智，微妙，入木三分而大胆，像书里的谈话，像茶桌两端才华横溢的年轻诗人与贵妇人之间的谈话——贵妇人因阅历过度而变得挑剔，挑剔而略带倦怠，却仍旧以她们那微妙的方式，贪得无厌地好奇着。

「坐一坐怎么样？」冈布里尔提议，指着两只绿漆铁椅。它们孤零零立在草地中央，紧紧挨着，椅面彼此微微相向内倾，一派促膝密谈的架势。想到那场不可避免、即将铺开的谈话，他心里的雀跃分明不及他的新朋友。天下若有他嫌恶的事，那就是谈情说爱的谈话。它叫他腻烦，哦，腻烦得可怕——这种对激情的细密剖析，年轻女人总好像认定，你同她们的交往到某个节骨眼上，总得来上一场。爱情如何把性格改好又改坏；肉体的激情如何未必同心灵的一面水火不容；一种可恨而专横的占有欲，如何能在爱情里同对对方最无私的体贴结成同盟——哦，这一切他都知道，还有更多的，知道得太清楚了。还有，一个人能不能同时爱上不止一个人；没有妒忌，爱情能不能存在；怜悯、好感、欲望能不能在某种程度上顶替那份圆满而真正的激情——这些阴郁的问题，他不得不一次又一次地掰扯，掰扯过多少回了！

至于种种哲学的玄思，他同样烂熟于心：生理学的、人类学的、心理学的种种事实，一概不缺。这门学问的理论，他早已兴味索然。不幸的是，理论的讨论似乎永远是对其实践的必要序曲。他在绿漆铁椅上落座时，微微叹了口气，带几分倦意。可是随即想起自己如今是完人，而完人做什么都得铺排赫赫、大刀阔斧，便俯身向前，隔着胡子挂出一副迷人的、傲慢的笑，开了口：

===Original===
“Tiresias, you may remember, was granted the singular privilege of living both as a man and a woman.”

Ah, this was the genuine young poet. Supporting an elbow on the back of her chair and leaning her cheek against her hand, she disposed herself to listen and, where necessary, brilliantly to interpellate; it was through half-closed eyes that she looked at him, and she smiled faintly in a manner which she knew, from experience, to be enigmatic, and though a shade haughty, though a tiny bit mocking and ironical, exceedingly attractive.

An hour and a half later they were driving towards an address in Bloxam Gardens, Maida Vale. The name seemed vaguely familiar to Gumbril. Bloxam Gardens﻿—perhaps one of his aunts had lived there once?

“It’s a dr﻿—dreadful little maisonnette,” she explained. “Full of awful things. We had to take it furnished. It’s so impossible to find anything now.”

===Chinese===
「忒瑞西阿斯（Tiresias），您也许记得，蒙受了独一份的恩典：一辈子既做过男人，又做过女人。」

啊，这才是如假包换的年轻诗人。她把一只手肘支在椅背上，脸颊倚着手背，摆好了听的姿势，必要时还要精彩地插话质询；她眯缝着眼睛看他，淡淡地微笑，那副神情她凭经验知道是高深莫测的，虽然带一丝倨傲，虽然含一点嘲弄和讥诮，却动人极了。

一个半钟头以后，两人坐车驶向梅达韦尔（Maida Vale）布洛克萨姆花园（Bloxam Gardens）的一处地址。这地名冈布里尔依稀觉得耳熟。布洛克萨姆花园——莫非他哪位姨妈从前在那儿住过？

「这是所糟——糟透了的小公馆（maisonnette），」她解释道。「塞满了可怕的东西。我们只得连家具一齐租下。如今想找着空房子，简直不可能。」

===Original===
Gumbril leaned back in his corner, wondering, as he studied that averted profile, who or what this young woman could be. She seemed to be in the obvious movement, to like the sort of things one would expect people to like; she seemed to be as highly civilized, in Mr. Mercaptan’s rather technical sense of the term, as free of all prejudices as the great exponent of civilization himself.

She seemed, from her coolly dropped hints, to possess all the dangerous experience, all the assurance and easy ruthlessness of a great lady whose whole life is occupied in the interminable affairs of the heart, the senses and the head. But, by a strange contradiction she seemed to find her life narrow and uninteresting. She had complained in so many words that her husband misunderstood and neglected her, had complained, by implication, that she knew very few interesting people.

The maisonnette in Bloxam Gardens was certainly not very splendid﻿—six rooms on the second and third floors of a peeling stucco house. And the furniture﻿—decidedly Hire Purchase. And the curtains and cretonnes﻿—brightly “modern,” positively “futurist.”

===Chinese===
冈布里尔靠在他那一角车座里，端详着那张侧过去的脸，心里纳罕这个年轻女人究竟是谁、是何等人物。她仿佛身处那个一望即知的摩登阵营，喜欢的尽是人家料想中体面人都喜欢的东西；论「文明」的程度——按默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan）那个相当专门的词义来说——她仿佛同文明这门学问的伟大阐发家本人一样高度文明，一样挣脱了一切偏见。

从她不动声色撒下的暗示听来，她仿佛怀有一个大贵妇的全部危险的经验、全部的自信与从容的无情——那种一生全部被心、感官与头脑的无穷事务占满的大贵妇。可是，矛盾得奇怪，她仿佛又觉得自己的生活狭窄而无趣。她明明白白抱怨过丈夫误解她、冷落她，又旁敲侧击地抱怨过，她认识的有趣人物寥寥无几。

布洛克萨姆花园的这所小公馆实在说不上气派——一座灰泥剥落的房子里，二、三两层楼上的六个房间。家具呢——一望而知是分期付款的租购货。窗帘和印花帷幔——鲜艳地「摩登」，简直「未来派」。

===Original===
“What one has to put up with in furnished flats!” The lady made a grimace as she ushered him into the sitting-room. And while she spoke the words, she really managed to persuade herself that the furniture wasn’t theirs, that they had found all this sordid stuff cluttering up the rooms, not chosen it, oh with pains! themselves, not doggedly paid for it, month by month.

“Our own things,” she murmured vaguely, “are stored. In the Riviera.” It was there, under the palms, among the gaudy melon flowers and the croupiers that the fastidious lady had last held her salon of young poets. In the Riviera﻿—that would explain, now she came to think of it, a lot of things, if explanation ever became necessary.

The Complete Man nodded sympathetically. “Other people’s tastes,” he held up his hands, they both laughed. “But why do we think of other people?” he added. And coming forward with a conquering impulsiveness he took both her long, fine hands in his and raised them to his bearded mouth.

She looked at him for a second, then dropped her eyelids, took back her hands. “I must go and make the tea,” she said. “The servants”﻿—the plural was a pardonable exaggeration﻿—“are out.”

Gallantly, the Complete Man offered to come and help her. These scenes of intimate life had a charm all their own. But she would not allow it. “No, no,” she was very firm, “I simply forbid you. You must stay here. I won’t be a moment,” and she was gone, closing the door carefully behind her.

===Chinese===
「连家具出租的公寓，什么都得将就！」太太把他让进客厅时做了个鬼脸。而说着这话的当口，她当真成功地说服了自己：这些家具不是他们的，这一堆腌臜玩意儿是房子里原有的、堵满各屋的，不是他们自己——呕，费尽心血！——一件件挑来的，也不是他们一个月一个月咬着牙分期付账的。

「我们自己的东西，」她含含糊糊地喃喃道，「都存在仓库里。在里维埃拉（Riviera）。」正是在那儿，棕榈树下，在俗艳的甜瓜花和赌台管理员之间，挑剔的贵妇上一次开设她的青年诗人沙龙。在里维埃拉——如今想起来，这倒能解释好些事情，万一什么时候需要解释的话。

完人同情地点头。「别人的趣味。」他双手一摊，两人一齐笑了。「可我们何必惦记别人？」他又补一句。随即带着征服者式的冲动抢上一步，把她那双纤长的手拢进自己手里，举向他那张生须的嘴。

她看了他一秒钟，垂下眼睑，抽回了手。「我得去沏茶了。」她说。「仆人们」——这个复数是个情有可原的夸张——「都出去了。」

完人殷勤备至，提出要跟去帮忙。这类家常生活的场景，自有一种独特的迷人处。可她不肯。「不，不行，」她非常坚决，「我绝对不许您。您务必待在这儿。我一会儿就回来。」说罢她人已去了，还仔细地带上了身后的门。

===Original===
Left to himself, Gumbril sat down and filed his nails.

As for the young lady, she hurried along to her dingy little kitchen, lit the gas, put the kettle on, set out the teapot and the cups on a tray, and from the biscuit-box, where it was stored, took out the remains of a chocolate cake, which had already seen service at the day-before-yesterday’s tea-party. When all was ready here, she tiptoed across to her bedroom and sitting down at her dressing-table, began with hands that trembled a little with excitement to powder her nose, and heighten the colour of her cheeks. Even after the last touch had been given, she still sat there, looking at her image in the glass.

The lady and the poet, she was thinking, the *grande dame* and the brilliant young man of genius. She liked young men with beards. But he was not an artist, in spite of the beard, in spite of the hat. He was a writer of sorts. So she gathered; but he was reticent, he was delightfully mysterious. She too, for that matter. The great lady slips out, masked, into the street; touches the young man’s sleeve: Come with me. She chooses, does not let herself passively be chosen. The young poet falls at her feet; she lifts him up. One is accustomed to this sort of thing.

She opened her jewel box, took out all her rings﻿—there were not many of them, alas!﻿—and put them on. Two or three of them, on second thoughts, she took off again; they were a little, she suspected with a sudden qualm, in other people’s taste.

===Chinese===
独自留下，冈布里尔坐下来，锉起了指甲。

至于那位年轻太太，她匆匆赶往自己昏暗的小厨房，点上煤气，坐上水壶，把茶壶和茶杯摆上托盘，又从饼干盒里——蛋糕就存在那儿——取出一块巧克力蛋糕的残余；前天的茶会上，它已经服过一回役了。这边一切停当，她踮着脚尖穿过屋子进了卧室，在梳妆台前坐下，用一双因兴奋而微微发抖的手，往鼻子上扑粉，往两颊添红。哪怕最后一笔已然画完，她还坐在那里，望着镜子里的自己。

贵妇与诗人，她想，大贵妇（grande dame）与光华夺目的年轻天才。她喜欢留胡子的年轻男人。可他不是艺术家，尽管有胡子，尽管有那顶帽子。他是个写点什么的一类人。她是这样揣度的；可他不多嘴，他神秘得迷人。在这上头，她也是。大贵妇戴上面罩溜上街头；碰一碰年轻人的衣袖：跟我来。她来挑，而不是被动地由人挑。年轻诗人拜倒在她脚边；她把他扶起来。这一套，人家早就惯了。

她打开首饰盒，把戒指全数取出——唉，并没有几枚！——一一套上。其中两三枚，再一转念，又褪了下来；她心头忽地一虚，疑心它们有点「别人的趣味」。

===Original===
He was very clever, very artistic﻿—only that seemed to be the wrong word to use; he seemed to know all the new things, all the interesting people. Perhaps he would introduce her to some of them. And he was so much at ease behind his knowledge, so well assured. But for her part, she felt pretty certain, she had made no stupid mistakes. She too had been, had looked at any rate﻿—which was the important thing﻿—very much at ease.

She liked young men with beards. They looked so Russian. Catherine of Russia had been one of the great ladies with caprices. Masked in the streets. Young poet, come with me. Or even, Young butcher’s boy. But that, no, that was going too far, too low. Still, life, life﻿—it was there to be lived﻿—life﻿—to be enjoyed. And now, and now? She was still wondering what would happen next, when the kettle, which was one of those funny ones which whistle when they come to the boil, began, fitfully, at first, then, under full steam, unflaggingly, to sound its mournful, otherworldly note. She sighed and bestirred herself to attend to it.

“Let me help you.” Gumbril jumped up as she came into the room. “What can I do?” He hovered rather ineptly round her.

The lady put down her tray on the little table. “N﻿—nothing,” she said.

===Chinese===
他非常聪明，非常「艺术」——只可惜这个词儿似乎用得不对；他仿佛知道所有的新事物、所有有趣的人物。说不定他还会引她结识几位呢。而且他负着满腹的见识，那样从容自在，那样成竹在胸。至于她这一边，她相当有把握，没出过什么蠢错。她也一直很自在——至少看上去很自在——而看上去才是要紧的。

她喜欢留胡子的年轻男人。看上去多有俄国味儿。俄国的叶卡捷琳娜（Catherine）便是一位任兴使气的大贵妇。戴着面罩走在街上。年轻的诗人，跟我来。或者竟至于：年轻的屠户小伙计。不过那个，不，那就太过分、太下作了。可是话说回来，生活呀，生活——生活就是拿来过的——生活——拿来享受的。眼下呢，眼下？她还在纳闷接下来会怎样，那把水壶——就是水开了会叫的那种逗趣壶——先是一阵有一阵没，随后在十足的蒸汽底下，不依不饶，呜起了它那凄凉的、来自另一世界的调子。她叹了口气，起身去照应它。

「让我来帮您。」她进屋时，冈布里尔一跃而起。「我能做什么？」他相当笨手笨脚地围着她打转。

太太把托盘搁在小桌上。「没——没什么。」她说。

===Original===
“N﻿—nothing?” he imitated her with a playful mockery. “Am I good for n﻿—nothing at all?” He took one of her hands and kissed it.

“Nothing that’s of the l﻿—least importance.” She sat down and began to pour out the tea.

The Complete Man also sat down. “So to adore at first sight,” he asked, “is not of the l﻿—least importance?”

She shook her head, smiled, raised and lowered her eyelids. One was so well accustomed to this sort of thing; it had no importance. “Sugar?” she asked. The young poet was safely there, sparkling across the tea-table. He offered love and she, with the easy heartlessness of one who is so well accustomed to this sort of thing, offered him sugar.

===Chinese===
「没——没什么？」他学着她的腔调，带点顽皮的揶揄。「难道我压根儿就一——一无是处？」他拿起她的一只手，吻了吻。

「没有一件称得上最——最要紧的。」她坐下，开始斟茶。

完人也坐下了。「那么，一见倾心，」他问，「也算不上最——最要紧的事？」

她摇头，微笑，眼睑一抬一落。这一套人家早就惯熟了；算不得要紧。「加糖吗？」她问。年轻诗人稳稳当当到了位，隔着茶桌熠熠生辉。他献上爱情，而她，怀着惯于此道者那份从容的无情，献给他糖。

===Original===
He nodded. “Please. But if it’s of no importance to you,” he went on, “then I’ll go away at once.”

The lady laughed her section of a descending chromatic scale. “Oh no, you won’t,” she said. “You can’t.” And she felt that the *grande dame* had made a very fine stroke.

“Quite right,” the Complete Man replied; “I couldn’t.” He stirred his tea. “But who are you,” he looked up at her suddenly, “you devilish female?” He was genuinely anxious to know; and besides, he was paying her a very pretty compliment. “What do you do with your dangerous existence?”

“I enjoy life,” she said. “I think one ought to enjoy life. Don’t you? I think it’s one’s first duty.” She became quite grave. “One ought to enjoy every moment of it,” she said. “Oh, passionately, adventurously, newly, excitingly, uniquely.”

===Chinese===
他点头。「请加。不过，倘若这在您算不得要紧，」他接着说，「那我这就走。」

太太笑出了下行半音阶的一节。「哦不，你走不了，」她说。「你走不成的。」她觉得，大贵妇这一记打得很漂亮。

「一点不错，」完人答道，「我走不成的。」他搅着茶。「可您究竟是谁，」他猛地抬头看她，「您这个鬼灵精的女人？您那危机四伏的日子，是怎么打发的？」他是当真心想知道；况且，这话也是在奉承她，奉承得漂亮。

「我享受生活呀。」她说。「我想，人应该享受生活。您说呢？我认为这是人的头一桩本分。」她变得十分郑重。「生活的每一刻都应该享受，」她说。「哦，热烈地、冒险地、新鲜地、刺激地、独一无二地。」

===Original===
The Complete Man laughed. “A conscientious hedonist. I see.”

She felt uncomfortably that the fastidious lady had not quite lived up to her character. She had spoken more like a young woman who finds life too dull and daily, and would like to get on to the cinema. “I am very conscientious,” she said, making significant play with the magnolia petals and smiling her riddling smile. She must retrieve the Great Catherine’s reputation.

“I could see that from the first,” mocked the Complete Man with a triumphant insolence. “Conscience doth make cowards of us all.”

The fastidious lady only contemptuously smiled. “Have a little chocolate cake,” she suggested. Her heart was beating. She wondered, she wondered.

===Chinese===
完人笑了。「一位恪尽职守的享乐主义者。明白了。」

她局促地感到，挑剔的贵妇这回演得不大称职。她方才那番话，更像一个嫌生活太沉闷、太家常，恨不得赶紧去看场电影的年轻女人。「我这人极守本分。」她说，一面拿木兰花瓣做起意味深长的文章，挂着她的谜一样的微笑。她必须把叶卡捷琳娜大帝的名声扳回来。

「这个我打头一眼就看出来了。」完人带着得胜的傲慢讥诮道。「良心使我们全都成了懦夫。」（《哈姆雷特》句）

挑剔的贵妇只轻蔑地一笑。「吃一点巧克力蛋糕吧。」她提议。她的心怦怦跳着。她纳罕着，纳罕着。

===Original===
There was a long silence. Gumbril finished his chocolate cake, gloomily drank his tea and did not speak. He found, all at once, that he had nothing to say. His jovial confidence seemed, for the moment, to have deserted him. He was only the Mild and Melancholy one foolishly disguised as a Complete Man; a sheep in beaver’s clothing. He entrenched himself behind his formidable silence and waited; waited, at first, sitting in his chair, then, when this total inactivity became unbearable, striding about the room.

She looked at him, for all her air of serene composure, with a certain disquiet. What on earth was he up to now? What could he be thinking about? Frowning like that, he looked like a young Jupiter, bearded and burly (though not, she noticed, quite so burly as he had appeared in his overcoat) making ready to throw a thunderbolt. Perhaps he was thinking of her﻿—suspecting her, seeing through the fastidious lady and feeling angry at her attempted deception. Or perhaps he was bored with her, perhaps he was wanting to go away. Well, let him go; she didn’t mind. Or perhaps he was just made like that﻿—a moody young poet; that seemed, on the whole, the most likely explanation; it was also the most pleasing and romantic. She waited. They both waited.

Gumbril looked at her and was put to shame by the spectacle of her quiet serenity. He must do something, he told himself; he must recover the Complete Man’s lost morale. Desperately he came to a halt in front of the one decent picture hanging on the walls. It was an eighteenth-century engraving of Raphael’s *Transfiguration*﻿—better, he always thought, in black and white than in its bleakly-coloured original.

===Chinese===
接下来是长长的沉默。冈布里尔吃完巧克力蛋糕，闷闷地喝了茶，一言不发。他忽然发现自己无话可说。他那份快活的自信，一时仿佛弃他而去了。他不过是温和而忧郁的那位，蠢蠢地扮成了完人；一只披着河狸毛皮的羊。他把自己关在那道咄咄逼人的沉默后面，等着；起先是坐在椅子里等，等到这份纹丝不动再也受不了，便在屋里大步踱了起来。

她望着他，尽管一派安详镇定，心里却不无忐忑。他这会儿究竟要干什么？他心里究竟在想什么？他那样皱着眉，活像一位年轻的朱庇特（Jupiter），有须而魁伟（尽管她注意到，已不及他穿着大衣时显得那么魁伟了），正要掣出雷霆。也许他想的是她——在疑心她，看穿了挑剔的贵妇，为她那番装模作样动了气。也许他腻烦她了，也许他想要走了。好啊，走就走；她不在乎。也许他天生就是那副样子——一个喜怒无常的年轻诗人；总的说来，这似乎是最可能的一种解释；也是最可人心、最有诗意的一种。她等着。两人都等着。

冈布里尔看看她，被她那副娴静安然的气象羞得无地自容。他必须做点什么，他对自己说；必须把完人失掉的士气夺回来。他孤注一掷地在墙上唯一像样的一幅画前站住了。那是一幅十八世纪的版画，拉斐尔（Raphael）的《主显圣容》（Transfiguration）——他素来认为，黑白的样子胜过原作那冷冰冰的彩色。

===Original===
“That’s a nice engraving,” he said. “Very nice.” The mere fact of having uttered at all was a great comfort to him, a real relief.

“Yes,” she said. “That belongs to me. I found it in a secondhand shop, not far from here.”

“Photography,” he pronounced, with that temporary earnestness which made him seem an enthusiast about everything, “is a mixed blessing. It has made it possible to reproduce pictures so easily and cheaply, that all the bad artists who were well occupied in the past, making engravings of good men’s paintings, are now free to do bad original work of their own.” All this was terribly impersonal, he told himself, terribly off the point. He was losing ground. He must do something drastic to win it back. But what?

She came to his rescue. “I bought another at the same time,” she said. “*The Last Communion of St. Jerome*, by﻿—who is it? I forget.”

===Chinese===
「这幅版画真好。」他说。「非常好。」单是总算开了口这一桩，对他就已是极大的安慰，实实在在的解脱。

「是啊。」她说。「这是我的。我在一家旧货店里淘来的，离这儿不远。」

「摄影术，」他发表宏论，带着那种使他显得对样样东西都一片热忱的临时性诚恳，「是福祸相半的东西。它把复制名画变得如此容易、如此便宜，以致昔日安分守己、忙着镌刻名家画作的蹩脚艺术家们，如今都腾出了手，放手去制造他们自己的蹩脚原作。」这些话，他对自己说，实在太不涉私人，太离题。他在节节败退。他必须下一剂猛药把它扳回来。可下什么药呢？

她来救了他。「同一回我还买了另一幅。」她说。「《圣哲罗姆的最后圣餐》（The Last Communion of St. Jerome），作者是——谁来着？我忘了。」

===Original===
“Ah, you mean Domenichino’s *St. Jerome*?” The Complete Man was afloat again. “Poussin’s favourite picture. Mine too, very nearly. I’d like to see that.”

“It’s in my room, I’m afraid. But if you don’t mind.”

He bowed. “If *you* don’t.”

She smiled graciously to him and got up. “This way,” she said, and opened the door.

“It’s a lovely picture,” Gumbril went on, loquaciously now, behind her, as they walked down the dark corridor. “And besides, I have a sentimental attachment to it. There used to be a copy of an engraving of it at home, when I was a child. And I remember wondering and wondering﻿—oh, it went on for years﻿—every time I saw the picture; wondering why on earth that old bishop (for I did know it was a bishop) should be handing the naked old man a five-shilling piece.”

===Chinese===
「啊，您是说多梅尼基诺（Domenichino）的那幅圣哲罗姆？」完人又浮起来了。「普桑（Poussin）最爱的一幅画。也差不多是我最爱的。我很想看一看。」

「它在我的卧室里，恐怕。不过，您要是不介意的话。」

他一躬身。「是您不介意才对。」

她对他嫣然一笑，站起身。「这边走。」说着开了门。

「那是一幅可爱的画。」冈布里尔接着说，此刻话又多了起来，跟在她身后，走在黑黢黢的过道里。「何况我对它还有一段感情的瓜葛。我小时候，家里有一幅它的版画的摹本。我记得每逢看见那画，我就纳闷呀、纳闷呀——哦，这么纳闷了好几年——纳闷那位老主教（因为我确实知道那是位主教）究竟为什么，要递给那个光身子的老头一枚五先令的银币。」

===Original===
She opened a door; they were in her very pink room. Grave in its solemn and subtly harmonious beauty, the picture hung over the mantelpiece, hung there, among the photographs of the little friends of her own age, like some strange object from another world. From within that chipped gilt frame all the beauty, all the grandeur of religion looked darkly out upon the pink room. The little friends of her own age, all deliciously nubile, sweetly smiled, turned up their eyes, clasped Persian cats or stood jauntily, feet apart, hand in the breeches pocket of the land-girl’s uniform; the pink roses on the wallpaper, the pink and white curtains, the pink bed, the strawberry-coloured carpet, filled all the air with the rosy reflections of nakedness and life.

And utterly remote, absorbed in their grave, solemn ecstasy, the robed and mitred priest held out, the dying saint yearningly received, the body of the Son of God. The ministrants looked gravely on, the little angels looped in the air above a gravely triumphant festoon, the lion slept at the saint’s feet, and through the arch beyond, the eye travelled out over a quiet country of dark trees and hills.

“There it is,” she waved towards the mantelpiece.

But Gumbril had taken it all in long ago. “You see what I mean by the five-shilling piece.” And stepping up to the picture, he pointed to the round bright wafer which the priest holds in his hand and whose averted disk is like the essential sun at the centre of the picture’s harmonious universe. “Those were the days of five-shilling pieces,” he went on. “You’re probably too young to remember those large, lovely things. They came my way occasionally, and consecrated wafers didn’t. So you can understand how much the picture puzzled me. A bishop giving a naked old man five shillings in a church, with angels fluttering overhead, and a lion sleeping in the foreground. It was obscure, it was horribly obscure.” He turned away from the picture and confronted his hostess, who was standing a little way behind him smiling enigmatically and invitingly.

===Chinese===
她推开一扇门；两人进了她那间粉红透顶的闺房。那幅画挂在壁炉上方，以它庄严而微妙和谐的美丽，凝然垂视；它挂在那里，夹在她那些同龄小女友的照片中间，像一件来自另一世界的异物。从那道磕碰过的镀金画框里，宗教全部的美、全部的尊严，幽幽地俯瞰着这间粉红的屋子。那些同龄的小女友，个个妙龄含苞，甜甜地微笑着，翻着媚眼，怀里抱着波斯猫，或者英姿飒爽地叉腿而立，一只手插在那身土地服务队姑娘制服（land-girl）的裤袋里；墙纸上的粉红玫瑰、粉白相间的窗帘、粉红的床、草莓色的地毯，把满屋的空气都浸透了赤裸与生命的玫瑰色反光。

而全然遥远地、沉浸在他们那凝重而神圣的迷狂里，身穿法衣、头戴法冠的祭司递出，垂死的圣者渴慕地接住，上帝之子的圣体。侍立的僧众肃然旁观，小天使们凌空连成一环，缀成一道凝重而凯旋的花彩，狮子睡在圣者脚边，而在远处的拱门之外，目光一路望去，掠过一片幽暗树木与山峦的静穆乡野。

「喏，就是它。」她朝壁炉那边摆了摆手。

可是冈布里尔早已把这一切尽收眼底。「您明白我说五先令银币的意思了吧。」说着他走到画前，指着祭司手中托着的那块圆圆发亮的圣体饼——那背转过去的圆面，恰似这幅画和谐宇宙中央的一轮本源太阳。「那是五先令银币通行的年月。」他接着说。「您多半太年轻，记不得那些大而可爱的物事了。它们偶尔落到我手里，祝圣过的圣体饼可从来不曾。所以您就能明白，这幅画当年多么让我犯难。一位主教在教堂里递给一个光身子的老头五先令，头顶上有天使翻飞，前景里睡着一头狮子。这费解，这费解得厉害。」他从画前转过身来，面对着女主人；她立在他身后不远的地方，谜一样地、引人地微笑着。

===Original===
“Obscure,” he repeated. “But so is everything. So is life in general. And you,” he stepped towards her, “you in particular.”

“Am I?” she lifted her limpid eyes at him. Oh, how her heart was beating, how hard it was to be the fastidious lady, calmly satisfying her caprice. How difficult it was to be accustomed to this sort of thing. What was going to happen next?

What happened next was that the Complete Man came still closer, put his arms round her, as though he were inviting her to the foxtrot, and began kissing her with a startling violence. His beard tickled her neck; shivering a little, she brought down the magnolia petals across her eyes. The Complete Man lifted her up, walked across the room carrying the fastidious lady in his arms and deposited her on the rosy catafalque of the bed. Lying there with her eyes shut, she did her best to pretend she was dead.

===Chinese===
「费解。」他重复了一遍。「可样样东西都费解。生活在总体上费解。而你，」他朝她跨近一步，「你尤其费解。」

「我吗？」她抬起那双清湛的眼睛望他。哦，她的心跳得多厉害；做挑剔的贵妇，从容不迫地遂一任性，多么难啊。把这一套习以为常，说起来轻巧，做起来多难。接下来会发生什么呢？

接下来发生的是：完人又凑得更近，一把搂住她，仿佛在邀她跳狐步舞，随即骇人地狂吻起来。他的胡子扎得她脖子发痒；她微微一颤，放下木兰花瓣遮住了眼睛。完人把她抱了起来，抱着挑剔的贵妇穿过房间，把她安放在那张玫瑰色的、灵柩台似的床上。她躺在那里，闭着眼睛，竭尽全力装死。

===Original===
Gumbril had looked at his wrist watch and found that it was six o’clock. Already? He prepared himself to take his departure. Wrapped in a pink kimono, she came out into the hall to wish him farewell.

“When shall I see you again, Rosie?” He had learnt that her name was Rosie.

She had recovered her great lady’s equanimity and detachment, and was able to shrug her shoulders and smile. “How should I know?” she asked, implying that she could not foresee what her caprice might be an hour hence.

“May I write then, and ask one of these days if you do know?”

===Chinese===
冈布里尔看了看腕表，发现已是六点钟。这就六点了？他整装准备告辞。她裹在一件粉色和服式晨衣里，出来到门厅送他。

「我什么时候能再见到你，罗茜（Rosie）？」他已探得她的名字是罗茜。

她已恢复了她的贵妇的镇定与超然，能耸耸肩、笑一笑了。「我怎么会知道？」她问，言下之意是，连一个钟头之后她的兴致会转向哪里，她也无法预见。

「那么我可以写信吗？过些天写信来问问，您到底知道了没有？」

===Original===
She put her head on one side and raised her eyebrows, doubtfully. At last nodded. “Yes, you can write,” she permitted.

“Good,” said the Complete Man, and picked up his wide hat. She held out her hand to him with stateliness, and with a formal gallantry he kissed it. He was just closing the front door behind him, when he remembered something. He turned round. “I say,” he called after the retreating pink kimono. “It’s rather absurd. But how can I write? I don’t know your name. I can’t just address it ‘Rosie.’ ”

The great lady laughed delightedly. This had the real *capriccio* flavour. “Wait,” she said, and she ran into the sitting-room. She was back again in a moment with an oblong of pasteboard. “There,” she said, and dropped it into his greatcoat pocket. Then blowing a kiss she was gone.

The Complete Man closed the door and descended the stairs. Well, well, he said to himself; well, well. He put his hand in his coat pocket and took out the card. In the dim light of the staircase he read the name on it with some difficulty. Mrs. James﻿—but no, but no. He read again, straining his eyes; there was no question of it. Mrs. James Shearwater.

===Chinese===
她把头一歪，扬起眉毛，将信将疑。终于点了点头。「行，你可以写。」她恩准道。

「好。」完人说，拾起他那顶宽檐帽。她端庄地向他伸出手，他以正规的骑士风度吻了它。他正要关上身后的前门，忽然想起一件事。他回过头去。「我说，」他冲着那件渐渐远去的粉红晨衣喊道，「这事未免荒唐。可我怎么写呢？我不知道您的名字。总不能光写『罗茜』收吧。」

大贵妇欢喜地笑了。这才真正有几分随想曲（capriccio）的韵味。「等着。」她说着跑进客厅。转眼她又回来了，手里拿着一张长方形的纸板。「给。」她说，把它丢进他的大衣口袋。然后飞了个吻，人就不见了。

完人关上门，走下楼去。好啊，好啊，他自言自语；好啊，好啊。他把手伸进大衣口袋，取出那张名片。在楼梯昏暗的光线里，他颇费了些劲才认出上面的名字。詹姆斯太太——不，不会吧，不会吧。他又读了一遍，眯细了眼睛；错不了了。詹姆斯·希尔沃特太太（Mrs. James Shearwater）。

===Original===
Mrs. James Shearwater.

That was why he had vaguely known the name of Bloxam Gardens.

Mrs. James Shear﻿—. Step after step he descended, ponderously. “Good Lord,” he said out loud. “Good Lord.”

But why had he never seen her? Why did Shearwater never produce her? Now he came to think of it, he hardly ever spoke of her.

===Chinese===
詹姆斯·希尔沃特太太。

难怪布洛克萨姆花园这地名他依稀耳熟。

詹姆斯·希尔沃——。他一步一步、沉甸甸地走下楼去。「天哪，」他出声地说，「天哪。」

可他怎么从来没见过她？希尔沃特（Shearwater）怎么从来不带她出来见人？如今细想，他几乎从来不提她。

===Original===
Why had she said the flat wasn’t theirs? It was; he had heard Shearwater talk about it.

Did she make a habit of this sort of thing!

Could Shearwater be wholly unaware of what she was really like? But, for that matter, what *was* she really like?

===Chinese===
她为什么说这房子不是他们的？明明是；他听希尔沃特说起过。

这种事，她难道是做惯了的！

她真正是什么样的人，希尔沃特可能全然不知么？不过话又说回来，她真正是什么样的人呢？

===Original===
He was halfway down the last flight, when with a rattle and a squeak of hinges the door of the house, which was only separated by a short lobby from the foot of the stairs, opened, revealing, on the doorstep, Shearwater and a friend, eagerly talking.

“… I take my rabbit,” the friend was saying﻿—he was a young man with dark, protruding eyes, and staring, doggy nostrils; very eager, lively and loud. “I take my rabbit and I inject into it the solution of eyes, pulped eyes of another dead rabbit. You see?”

Gumbril’s first instinct was to rush up the stairs and hide in the first likely-looking corner. But he pulled himself together at once. He was a Complete Man, and Complete Men do not hide; moreover, he was sufficiently disguised to be quite unrecognizable. He stood where he was, and listened to the conversation.

“The rabbit,” continued the young man, and with his bright eyes and staring, sniffing nose, he looked like a poacher’s terrier ready to go barking after the first white tail that passed his way; “the rabbit naturally develops the appropriate resistance, develops a specific anti-eye to protect itself. I then take some of its anti-eye serum and inject it into my female rabbit; I then immediately breed from her.” He paused.

===Chinese===
他正下到最后一段楼梯的半腰，楼下的门哗啦一声、铰链吱呀一响，开了——那扇门同楼梯脚只隔着一小截门道——门阶上现出希尔沃特和一个朋友，正谈得起劲。

「……我拿出我的兔子，」那朋友正说着——他是个黑眼珠凸出的年轻人，鼻孔张得老大，一副狗儿相；又热切，又活泼，嗓门又大。「我拿出我的兔子，往它身上注射一种眼睛的溶液——另一只死兔的眼睛捣成的浆。明白吗？」

冈布里尔的第一个本能是冲上楼去，就近找个像样的角落躲起来。但他立刻定住了神。他是完人，完人不兴躲藏；况且他化装得足够严实，断无被认出之虞。他站在原地，听那场谈话。

「那兔子，」年轻人接着说——他那双亮眼睛和那张张着、嗅着的鼻子，活像一条偷猎人的猎梗，只等第一条白尾巴打眼前溜过，就要吠着追上去——「那兔子自然而然生出相应的抵抗力，生出一种特异的『抗眼』来保护自己。我再取它的一点抗眼血清，注射进我那只母兔身上；然后立刻让它配种。」他顿了顿。

===Original===
“Well?” asked Shearwater, in his slow, ponderous way. He lifted his great round head inquiringly and looked at the doggy young man from under his bushy eyebrows.

The doggy young man smiled triumphantly. “The young ones,” he said, emphasizing his words by striking his right fist against the extended palm of his left hand, “the young ones are born with defective sight.”

Thoughtfully Shearwater pulled at his formidable moustache. “H’m,” he said slowly. “Very remarkable.”

“You realize the full significance of it?” asked the young man. “We seem to be effecting the germ-plasm directly. We have found a way of making acquired characteristics.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「后来呢？」希尔沃特问，照他那慢吞吞、沉甸甸的样子。他探询地抬起那颗滚圆的大脑袋，从两道浓眉底下望着狗儿脸的年轻人。

狗儿脸的年轻人得意地笑了。「生下来的小兔，」他说，右拳一记一记捶在摊开的左掌上，给话加重分量，「生下来视力就有缺陷。」

希尔沃特若有所思地捻着他那部威风的髭须。「唔。」他慢慢地说。「非常了不起。」

「您意识到这件事的全部意义了吗？」年轻人问。「我们似乎在直接作用于种质（germ-plasm）。我们找到了一条制造获得性性状的路子。……」

===Original===
“Pardon me,” said Gumbril. He had decided that it was time to be gone. He ran down the stairs and across the tiled hall, he pushed his way firmly but politely between the talkers.

“… heritable,” continued the young man, imperturbably eager, speaking through and over and round the obstacle.

“Damn!” said Shearwater. The Complete Man had trodden on his toe. “Sorry,” he added, absentmindedly apologizing for the injury he had received.

Gumbril hurried off along the street. “If we really have found out a technique for influencing the germ-plasm directly﻿ ﻿…” he heard the doggy young man saying; but he was already too far away to catch the rest of the sentence. There are many ways, he reflected, of spending an afternoon.

===Chinese===
「借光。」冈布里尔说。他断定该走了。他奔下楼梯，跑过铺砖的门厅，坚定而不失礼貌地从两位谈话人中间挤了过去。

「……可遗传的，」年轻人接着说，热切不为所动，隔着这个障碍物、越过它、绕着它照讲不误。

「该死！」希尔沃特说。完人踩了他的脚趾。「对不起。」他又补一句，心不在焉地为自己受到的伤害道了歉。

冈布里尔匆匆沿街走远了。「倘若我们当真找到了一种直接影响种质的技术……」他听见那狗儿脸的年轻人还在说；可他已走得太远，听不见后半句了。打发一个下午，他想，法子可真多。

===Original===
The doggy young man refused to come in, he had to get in his game of tennis before dinner. Shearwater climbed the stairs alone. He was taking off his hat in the little hall of his own apartment, when Rosie came out of the sitting-room with a trayful of tea-things.

“Well?” he asked, kissing her affectionately on the forehead. “Well? People to tea?”

“Only one,” Rosie replied. “I’ll go and make you a fresh cup.”

She glided off, rustling in her pink kimono towards the kitchen.

===Chinese===
狗儿脸的年轻人不肯进屋，他得赶在晚饭前打上他那场网球。希尔沃特独自上了楼。他正在自家那套房间的小门厅里摘帽子，罗茜端着一托盘茶具从客厅里出来。

「怎么样？」他问，亲亲热热地吻她的额头。「怎么样？有人来喝茶？」

「就一位。」罗茜答。「我去给你沏杯新的。」

她滑步而去，窸窸窣窣地裹着粉红晨衣，奔厨房去了。

===Original===
Shearwater sat down in the sitting-room. He had brought home with him from the library the fifteenth volume of the *Biochemical Journal*. There was something in it he wanted to look up. He turned over the pages. Ah, here it was. He began reading. Rosie came back again.

“Here’s your tea,” she said.

He thanked her without looking up. The tea grew cold on the little table at his side.

Lying on the sofa, Rosie pondered and remembered. Had the events of the afternoon, she asked herself, really happened? They seemed very improbable and remote, now, in this studious silence. She couldn’t help feeling a little disappointed. Was it only this? So simple and obvious? She tried to work herself up into a more exalted mood. She even tried to feel guilty; but there she failed completely. She tried to feel rapturous; but without much more success. Still, he certainly had been a most extraordinary man. Such impudence, and at the same time such delicacy and tact.

===Chinese===
希尔沃特在客厅里坐下。他从图书馆带回了《生化杂志》（Biochemical Journal）第十五卷。里面有一处他想查一查。他翻动书页。啊，在这儿。他读了起来。罗茜又回来了。

「你的茶。」她说。

他道了声谢，头也没抬。茶在他手边的小桌上渐渐凉了。

罗茜躺在沙发上，回味着，追忆着。下午那些事，她问自己，当真发生过么？此刻，在这片用功的寂静里，它们显得那样渺远、那样难以置信。她不由得有点失望。就这样而已？这样简单、这样直白？她试着把自己煽动进一种更昂扬的心境。她甚至试着觉得内疚；可这一项她全盘失败。她试着觉得狂喜；成效也不见得多大。不过话说回来，他确实是一位顶顶了不得的人物。那样的放肆，同时又那样的细腻、那样的得体。

===Original===
It was a pity she couldn’t afford to change the furniture. She saw now that it wouldn’t do at all. She would go and tell Aunt Aggie about the dreadful middle-classness of her Art and Craftiness.

She ought to have an Empire *chaise longue*. Like Madame Récamier. She could see herself lying there, dispensing tea. “Like a delicious pink snake.” He had called her that.

Well, really, now she came to think of it all again, it had been too queer, too queer.

“What’s a hedonist?” she suddenly asked.

===Chinese===
可惜她拿不出钱来换家具。她如今看明白了，这些家伙根本要不得。她要去告诉艾吉姨妈，她那套工艺美术趣味里那股可怕的中产阶级气。

她该有一张帝国式躺椅（chaise longue）。像雷卡米耶夫人（Madame Récamier）那样。她看得见自己躺在那儿斟茶待客的模样。「像一条美妙的粉红蛇。」他这样唤过她。

唉，真的，如今再把这一切从头想起，那真是太古怪了，太古怪了。

「什么叫享乐主义者？」她突然问。

===Original===
Shearwater looked up from the *Journal of Biochemistry*. “What?” he said.

“A hedonist.”

“A man who holds that the end of life is pleasure.”

A “conscientious hedonist”﻿—ah, that was good.

===Chinese===
希尔沃特从《生物化学杂志》（Journal of Biochemistry）上抬起头。「什么？」他说。

「享乐主义者。」

「一个认为人生的目的就是快乐的人。」

「恪尽职守的享乐主义者」——啊，这个说法妙。

===Original===
“This tea is cold,” Shearwater remarked.

“You should have drunk it before,” she said. The silence renewed and prolonged itself.

Rosie was getting much better, Shearwater reflected, as he washed his hands before supper, about not interrupting him when he was busy. This evening she had really not disturbed him at all, or at most only once, and that not seriously. There had been times in the past when the child had really made life almost impossible. There were those months at the beginning of their married life, when she had thought she would like to study physiology herself and be a help to him. He remembered the hours he had spent trying to teach her elementary facts about the chromosomes. It had been a great relief when she abandoned the attempt. He had suggested she should go in for stencilling patterns on Government linen. Such pretty curtains and things one could make like that. But she hadn’t taken very kindly to the idea. There had followed a long period when she seemed to have nothing to do but prevent him from doing anything. Ringing him up at the laboratory, invading his study, sitting on his knee, or throwing her arms round his neck, or pulling his hair, or asking ridiculous questions when he was trying to work.

Shearwater flattered himself that he had been extremely patient. He had never got cross. He had just gone on as though she weren’t there. As though she weren’t there.

===Chinese===
「这茶凉了。」希尔沃特说。

「你早该趁热喝的。」她说。寂静重新聚拢，又延长开去。

罗茜大有长进，希尔沃特晚饭前洗手时想道，懂得不在他忙的时候打搅他了。今晚她真可以说一晚上没搅扰他，至多一回，还不算厉害。从前有些时候，这孩子真把日子弄得几乎没法过。刚结婚那几个月，她一度以为自己想学生理学、好当他的帮手。他记得自己花掉的那些钟头，绞尽脑汁教她染色体的入门知识。等她放弃这一尝试，真是莫大的解脱。他提议过，她不如去搞搞在公家亚麻布上镂印花样的营生。那样可以做出多漂亮的窗帘和种种物件啊。可她对这主意不怎么领情。随后是一段漫长的时期，她仿佛除了妨碍他做任何事之外，再没有别的事可做：往他实验室打电话，闯进他的书房，坐上他的膝头，或者搂住他的脖子，或者拽他的头发，或者在他想用功的时候，问些荒唐问题。

希尔沃特自诩一向耐心到了家。他从不发火。他只管照旧干他的，只当她不在。只当她不在。

===Original===
“Hurry up,” he heard her calling. “The soup’s getting cold.”

“Coming,” he shouted back, and began to dry his large, blunt hands.

She seemed to have been improving lately. And tonight, tonight she had been a model of nonexistence.

He came striding heavily into the dining-room. Rosie was sitting at the head of the table, ladling out the soup. With her left hand she held back the flowing pink sleeve of her kimono so that it should not trail in the plates or the tureen. Her bare arm showed white and pearly through the steam of lentils.

===Chinese===
「快点呀，」他听见她喊，「汤要凉了。」

「来了。」他扬声应道，开始擦干他那双宽大的、骨节粗钝的手。

她近来似乎一直在进步。而今晚，今晚她简直是一位「不存在」的模范。

他沉甸甸地大步走进饭厅。罗茜坐在桌首，正在舀汤。她左手拢起晨衣那截飘垂的粉袖，免得拖进盘碟或汤盆里。她裸着的那条胳膊，在扁豆汤腾起的热气里，泛出珍珠似的白。

===Original===
How pretty she was! He could not resist the temptation, but coming up behind her bent down and kissed her, rather clumsily, on the back of her neck.

Rosie drew away from him. “Really, Jim,” she said, disapprovingly. “At mealtimes!” The fastidious lady had to draw the line at these ill-timed, tumbling familiarities.

“And what about work-times?” Shearwater asked laughing. “Still, you were wonderful this evening, Rosie, quite wonderful.” He sat down and began eating his soup. “Not a sound all the time I was reading; or, at any rate, only one sound, so far as I remember.”

The great lady said nothing, but only smiled﻿—a little contemptuously and with a touch of pity. She pushed away the plate of soup unfinished and planted her elbows on the table. Slipping her hands under the sleeves of her kimono, she began, lightly, delicately, with the tips of her fingers, to caress her own arms.

How smooth they were, how soft and warm and how secret under the sleeves. And all her body was as smooth and warm, was as soft and secret, still more secret beneath the pink folds. Like a warm serpent hidden away, secretly, secretly.

===Chinese===
她多好看啊！他抵不住这个诱惑，绕到她身后，俯下身去，相当笨拙地吻了吻她的后颈。

罗茜从他身边缩开。「真是的，吉姆（Jim）。」她不以为然地说。「吃饭的时候也来这一套！」这类不合时宜的、跌跌撞撞的亲昵，挑剔的贵妇不能不划下一道界线。

「那上工的时候呢？」希尔沃特笑着反问。「不过，你今晚真是好样的，罗茜，好极了。」他坐下，喝起汤来。「我念书那阵子，一声也没有；或者说，至多一声，就我记忆所及。」

大贵妇一言不发，只微笑着——带一点轻蔑，带一点怜悯。她推开没喝完的那盘汤，双肘往桌上一拄。她把双手滑进晨衣的袖筒，用指尖轻轻地、细细地，抚摩起自己的手臂来。

多么光滑啊，多么柔软、温暖，在袖筒底下多么隐秘。而她的整个身子也一样光滑、温暖，一样柔软、隐秘，在粉色衣褶底下更加隐秘。像一条藏起来的暖蛇，秘密地，秘密地。

## X / X

===Original===
Mr. Boldero liked the idea of the Patent Small-Clothes. He liked it immensely, he said, immensely.

"There's money in it," he said.

Mr. Boldero was a small dark man of about forty-five, active as a bird and with a bird's brown, beady eyes, a bird's sharp nose. He was always busy, always had twenty different irons in the fire at once, was always fresh, clearheaded, never tired. He was also always unpunctual, always untidy. He had no sense of time or of order. But he got away with it, as he liked to say. He delivered the goods—or rather the goods, in the convenient form of cash, delivered themselves, almost miraculously it always seemed, to him.

===Chinese===
博尔德罗先生（Mr. Boldero）喜欢专利充气裤（Patent Small-Clothes）这个点子。他喜欢得不得了，他说，不得了。

「这东西里头有钱赚。」他说。

博尔德罗先生四十五岁上下，矮小黝黑，敏捷得像只鸟，长着一双鸟儿般的棕色圆眼睛，一只鸟儿般的尖鼻子。他永远忙碌，永远同时张罗着二十桩事，却永远精神饱满、头脑清明、不知疲倦。他也永远不守时，永远不整洁。他全无时间观念，也全无条理观念。但他总能蒙混过关——这话是他自己爱说的。他总是交货——或者说，货总是以现钱这种最便利的形式，差不多像奇迹一般，自己送上门来给他。

===Original===
He was like a bird in appearance. But in mind, Gumbril found, after having seen him once or twice, he was like a caterpillar: he ate all that was put before him, he consumed a hundred times his own mental weight every day. Other people's ideas, other people's knowledge—they were his food. He devoured them and they were at once his own. All that belonged to other people he annexed without a scruple or a second thought, quite naturally, as though it were already his own. And he absorbed it so rapidly and completely, he laid public claim to it so promptly that he sometimes deceived people into believing that he had really anticipated them in their ideas, that he had known for years and years the things they had just been telling him, and which he would at once airily repeat to them with the perfect assurance of one who knows—knows by instinct, as it were, by inheritance.

At their first luncheon he had asked Gumbril to tell him all about modern painting. Gumbril had given him a brief lecture; before the savoury had appeared on the table, Mr. Boldero was talking with perfect familiarity of Picasso and Derain. He almost made it understood that he had a fine collection of their works in his drawing-room at home. Being a trifle deaf, however, he was not very good at names, and Gumbril's all-too-tactful corrections were lost on him. He could not be induced to abandon his Bacosso in favour of any other version of the Spaniard's name. Bacosso—why, he had known all about Bacosso since he was a schoolboy! Bacosso was an old master, already.

Mr. Boldero was very severe with the waiters and knew so well how things ought to be done at a good restaurant, that Gumbril felt sure he must recently have lunched with some meticulous gormandizer of the old school. And when the waiter made as though to serve them with brandy in small glasses, Mr. Boldero was so passionately indignant that he sent for the manager.

===Chinese===
外表上他像只鸟。可是论头脑，冈布里尔见过他一两面之后便发现，他像条毛毛虫：凡是摆到眼前的他一概吃下，每天要消耗掉百倍于自身精神体重的食料。别人的想法、别人的知识——就是他的食物。他吞下它们，它们立刻就成了他的。凡是属于别人的东西，他一概毫无顾忌、想也不想地吞并过来，自然得很，仿佛本来就是他的。而且他吸收得那么快、那么彻底，那么迅速地公然宣示主权，以至于有时能骗得人相信：这些想法他真是早已想到了；人家刚刚讲给他听的东西，他已经知道了多年多年，还能立刻以一个「知道者」十足的把握，轻飘飘地复述给人家听——仿佛是凭本能知道的，凭遗传知道的。

头一回共进午餐，他请冈布里尔把现代绘画的一切讲给他听。冈布里尔作了个简短的演讲；还没等末道的咸食上桌，博尔德罗先生已经满口熟稔地大谈毕加索（Picasso）和德兰（Derain）了。他几乎让人领会到：他家客厅里就藏有他们的一批精品。只是他耳朵略有些背，人名上便不甚高明，冈布里尔那些过于委婉的纠正全属徒劳。任你怎么劝，他也不肯放弃他的巴科索（Bacosso），不肯改用那位西班牙人名字的任何别的拼法。巴科索——嘿，他打学生时代就对巴科索了如指掌！巴科索早就是一位老大师了。

博尔德罗先生对侍者极严厉，对上等馆子里诸事该当如何办理又烂熟于胸，弄得冈布里尔确信，他近来必定同某位老派而一丝不苟的老饕共进过午餐。待到侍者作出要用小杯子给他们上白兰地的样子，博尔德罗先生义愤填膺，竟叫人去把经理请了来。

===Original===
"Do you mean to tell me," he shouted in a perfect frenzy of righteous anger, "that you don't yet know how brandy ought to be drunk?"

Perhaps it was only last week that he himself, Gumbril reflected, had learned to aerate his cognac in Gargantuan beakers.

Meanwhile, of course, the Patent Small-Clothes were not neglected. As soon as he had been told about the things, Mr. Boldero began speaking of them with a perfect and practised familiarity. They were already his, mentally his. And it was only Mr. Boldero's generosity that prevented him from making the Small-Clothes more effectively his own.

===Chinese===
「你是要告诉我，」他以一场正义之怒的狂乱嘶声喊道，「你们到现在还不知道白兰地该怎么喝么？」

冈布里尔暗想，说不定就在上礼拜，他本人才学会用高康大式（Gargantuan，拉伯雷笔下的巨人）的大杯来醒他的科涅克白兰地。

当然，专利充气裤也没有受冷落。一听说过这东西，博尔德罗先生谈起来便已带着十足而老练的熟稔。它们已经是他的了，精神上已经是他的了。若不是博尔德罗先生慷慨为怀，他早把充气裤更彻底地据为己有了。

===Original===
"If it weren't for the friendship and respect which I feel for your father, Mr. Gumbril," he said, twinkling genially over the brandy, "I'd just annex your Small-Clothes. Bag and baggage. Just annex them."

"Ah, but they're my patent," said Gumbril. "Or at least they're in process of being patented. The agents are at work."

Mr. Boldero laughed. "Do you suppose that would trouble me if I wanted to be unscrupulous? I'd just take the idea and manufacture the article. You'd bring an action. I'd have it defended with all the professional erudition that could be brought. You'd find yourself let in for a case that might cost thousands. And how would you pay for it? You'd be forced to come to an agreement out of court, Mr. Gumbril. That's what you'd have to do. And a damned bad agreement it would be for you, I can tell you." Mr. Boldero laughed very cheerfully at the thought of the badness of this agreement. "But don't be alarmed," he said. "I shan't do it, you know."

Gumbril was not wholly reassured. Tactfully, he tried to find out what terms Mr. Boldero was prepared to offer. Mr. Boldero was nebulously vague.

===Chinese===
「若不是看在我对你父亲那份友谊和敬重上，冈布里尔先生，」他隔着白兰地和蔼地眨着眼说，「我就干脆把你的充气裤吞并了。连锅端。干脆吞并了。」

「啊，可那是我的专利，」冈布里尔说。「至少正在办专利。代理人已经在办了。」

博尔德罗先生笑了。「你以为我要是不讲良心，这会难倒我？我只管把点子拿去，照式制造。你去告我。我便请来一切请得到的行家学问替我辩护。你会发现自己被卷进一桩官司，花销动辄数千镑。你拿什么付？你就只好庭外和解了，冈布里尔先生。你只有这条路。而且我可以告诉你，那和解对你可是糟透了。」想到这和解之糟，博尔德罗先生笑得非常快活。「不过别怕，」他说，「你知道，我不会这么干的。」

冈布里尔并未全然放心。他婉转地想探明博尔德罗先生打算开什么条件。博尔德罗先生的回答云山雾罩。

===Original===
They met again in Gumbril's rooms. The contemporary drawings on the walls reminded Mr. Boldero that he was now an art expert. He told Gumbril all about it—in Gumbril's own words. Every now and then, it was true, Mr. Boldero made a little slip. Bacosso, for example, remained unshakably Bacosso. But on the whole the performance was most impressive. It made Gumbril feel very uncomfortable, however, while it lasted. For he recognized in this characteristic of Mr. Boldero a horrible caricature of himself. He too was an assimilator; more discriminating, no doubt, more tactful, knowing better than Mr. Boldero how to turn the assimilated experience into something new and truly his own; but still a caterpillar, definitely a caterpillar. He began studying Mr. Boldero with a close and disgustful attention, as one might pore over some repulsive *memento mori*.

It was a relief when Mr. Boldero stopped talking art and consented to get down to business. Gumbril was wearing for the occasion the sample pair of Small-Clothes which Mr. Bojanus had made for him. For Mr. Boldero's benefit he put them, so to speak, through their paces. He allowed himself to drop with a bump on to the floor—arriving there bruiseless and unjarred. He sat in complete comfort for minutes at a stretch on the edge of the ornamental iron fender. In the intervals he paraded up and down before Mr. Boldero like a mannequin. "A trifle bulgy," said Mr. Boldero. "But still.…" He was, taking it all round, favourably impressed. It was time, he said, to begin thinking of details. They would have to begin by making experiments with the bladders to discover a model combining, as Mr. Boldero put it, "maximum efficiency with minimum bulge." When they had found the right thing, they would have it made in suitable quantities by any good rubber firm. As for the trousers themselves, they could rely for those on sweated female labour in the East End. "Cheap and good," said Mr. Boldero.

===Chinese===
