他们在冈布里尔的寓所又见了一面。墙上的当代素描提醒博尔德罗先生：他如今又是个艺术行家了。他把这一门的一切讲给冈布里尔听——用的正是冈布里尔自己的话。诚然，博尔德罗先生时不时也小小露一下破绽。比如巴科索，就坚不可摧地始终是巴科索。但大体而论，这场表演令人印象至深。只是表演进行之际，冈布里尔心里极不自在。因为他在博尔德罗先生的这个特点里，认出了一幅自己那可怕的漫画像。他也是个吞噬消化者；无疑更挑剔些，更得体些，比博尔德罗先生更懂得怎样把吞下的经验变成某种新的、真正属于他自己的东西；但终究是条毛毛虫，不折不扣的毛毛虫。他开始怀着一种贴近而厌恶的专注来研究博尔德罗先生，如同人俯身细看一件叫人反胃的 *memento mori*（拉丁语：勿忘你终有一死，死亡警示物）。

直到博尔德罗先生收起艺术经、同意谈正事了，冈布里尔才松了口气。为着这个场合，他穿上了博亚努斯先生（Mr. Bojanus）替他做的那条充气裤样品。可以说，为了让博尔德罗先生开开眼，他让这条裤子使出了浑身解数。他让自己咚的一声跌坐到地板上——落地时既无瘀伤，也不震荡。他又在那花饰铁炉栏的边沿上一连安坐好几分钟，舒适备至。歇场的空当，他像个时装模特儿，在博尔德罗先生面前来回走台。「鼓囊了那么一点儿，」博尔德罗先生说。「不过嘛……」总体看来，他印象颇佳。他说，是时候开始考虑细节了。他们得先拿气囊做种种试验，找出一个如博尔德罗先生所说的「以最小鼓胀收最大效能」的型号。找到了合适的东西，就交给任何一家像样的橡胶厂去适量制造。至于裤子本身，可以仰赖伦敦东区（East End）的血汗女工。「又便宜又好。」博尔德罗先生说。

===Original===
"It sounds ideal," said Gumbril.

"And then," said Mr. Boldero, "there's our advertising campaign. On that I may say," he went on with a certain solemnity, "will depend the failure or success of our enterprise. I consider it of the first importance."

"Quite," said Gumbril, nodding importantly and with intelligence.

"We must set to work," said Mr. Boldero, "sci—en—tif—ically." Gumbril nodded again.

===Chinese===
「听着真理想。」冈布里尔说。

「再有，」博尔德罗先生说，「就是咱们的广告攻势了。我可以这么说，」他带着几分庄严往下讲，「咱们这项事业，成也系于此，败也系于此。我认为这是头等重要的事。」

「正是。」冈布里尔郑重其事而又聪明地颔首道。

「咱们必须科——学——地动手。」博尔德罗先生说。冈布里尔又点了点头。

===Original===
"We have to appeal," Mr. Boldero went on so glibly that Gumbril felt sure he must be quoting somebody else's words, "to the great instincts and feelings of humanity.… They are the sources of action. They spend the money, if I may put it like that."

"That's all very well," said Gumbril. "But how do you propose to appeal to the most important of the instincts? I refer, as you may well imagine, to sex."

"I was just going to come to that," said Mr. Boldero, raising his hand as though to ask for a patient hearing. "Alas! we can't. I don't see any way of hanging our Small-Clothes on the sexual peg."

"Then we are undone," said Gumbril, too dramatically.

===Chinese===
「咱们必须诉诸，」博尔德罗先生接着说得那样顺溜，使冈布里尔断定这必定是引用别人的话，「人类那些伟大的本能与情感……它们是行动的源头。花钱的正是它们，请容我这样说。」

「这话不坏，」冈布里尔说，「可是这最重要的本能，你打算怎么个诉诸法？你所指的，不难想见，是性。」

「我正要说到这个。」博尔德罗先生举起手来，仿佛请求人家耐心听他说完。「唉！不成。我看不出有什么法子，能把咱们的充气裤挂上性这只挂钩。」

「那我们就完了。」冈布里尔说，戏剧腔过了头。

===Original===
"No, no." Mr. Boldero was reassuring. "You make the error of the Viennese. You exaggerate the importance of sex. After all, my dear Mr. Gumbril, there is also the instinct of self-preservation; there is also," he leaned forward, wagging his finger, "the social instinct, the instinct of the herd."

"True."

"Both of them as powerful as sex. What are the Professor's famous Censors but forbidding suggestions from the herd without, made powerful and entrenched by the social instinct within?"

Gumbril had no answer; Mr. Boldero continued, smiling:

"So that we shall be all right if we stick to self-preservation and the herd. Rub in the comfort and the utility, the hygienic virtues of our Small-Clothes; that will catch their self-preservatory feelings. Aim at their dread of public opinion, at their ambition to be one better than their fellows and their terror of being different—at all the ludicrous weaknesses a well-developed social instinct exposes them to. We shall get them, if we set to work scientifically." Mr. Boldero's birdlike eyes twinkled very brightly. "We shall get them," he repeated, and he laughed a happy little laugh, full of such a childlike diabolism, such an innocent gay malignity that it seemed as though a little leprechaun had suddenly taken the financier's place in Gumbril's best armchair.

===Chinese===
「不，不。」博尔德罗先生安慰道。「你犯的是维也纳人（指弗洛伊德一派）的错误。你夸大了性的重要。毕竟，我亲爱的冈布里尔先生，还有自保的本能呢；还有，」他探身向前，摇着手指，「社会本能，群居的本能。」

「不错。」

「两者都与性同样有力。那位教授（指弗洛伊德）那鼎鼎大名的『稽查员』，还不是从外界群体递进来的禁阻暗示，再教内在的社会本能养得权势赫赫、深沟高垒？」

冈布里尔无言以对；博尔德罗先生微笑着继续道：

「所以咱们只要抱住自保与群居这两条，就万无一失。把舒适和实用、把咱充气裤的种种卫生美德反复揉进去，这就抓住了他们的自保心。再瞄准他们对舆论的畏惧，瞄准他们那种要比同侪高出一头的抱负、那种害怕与众不同的恐怖——瞄准一颗发达的社会本能把他们暴露于其中的种种可笑的弱点。只要咱们科——学——地动手，就能把他们拿下。」博尔德罗先生那双鸟眼闪闪发亮。「咱们能拿下他们。」他重复道，随即快活地小小一笑，笑里满是那般孩子气的恶魔气，那般天真快活的歹毒，仿佛一个小妖精（leprechaun，爱尔兰传说中藏金子的矮妖）突然取这位金融家而代之，坐进了冈布里尔最好的一把扶手椅。

===Original===
Gumbril laughed too; for this leprechaunish mirth was infectious. "We shall get them," he echoed. "Oh, I'm sure we shall, if you set about it, Mr. Boldero."

Mr. Boldero acknowledged the compliment with a smile that expressed no false humility. It was his due, and he knew it.

"I'll give you some of my ideas about the advertising campaign," he said. "Just to give you a notion. You can think them over, quietly, and make suggestions."

"Yes, yes," said Gumbril, nodding.

===Chinese===
冈布里尔也笑了；因为这股小妖精式的欢快有传染性。「咱们能拿下他们。」他应和道。「啊，我敢肯定拿下，只要您来操办，博尔德罗先生。」

博尔德罗先生领受了这句恭维，微微一笑，笑里没有半点假谦虚。这是他应得的，他自己也知道。

「我把广告攻势的一些想法讲给你听，」他说，「只给你一点概念。你可以静静地细想，再提提建议。」

「好，好。」冈布里尔点着头说。

===Original===
Mr. Boldero cleared his throat. "We shall begin," he said, "by making the most simple elementary appeal to their instinct of self-preservation: we shall point out that the Patent Small-Clothes are comfortable; that to wear them is to avoid pain. A few striking slogans about comfort—that's all we want. Very simple indeed. It doesn't take much to persuade a man that it's pleasanter to sit on air than on wood. But while we're on the subject of hard seats we shall have to glide off subtly at a tangent to make a flank attack on the social instincts." And joining the tip of his forefinger to the tip of his thumb, Mr. Boldero moved his hand delicately sideways, as though he were sliding it along a smooth brass rail. "We shall have to speak about the glories and the trials of sedentary labour. We must exalt its spiritual dignity and at the same time condemn its physical discomforts. 'The seat of honour,' don't you know. We could talk about that. 'The Seats of the Mighty.' 'The seat that rules the office rocks the world.' All those lines might be made something of. And then we could have little historical chats about thrones; how dignified, but how uncomfortable they've been. We must make the bank clerk and the civil servant feel proud of being what they are and at the same time feel ashamed that, being such splendid people, they should have to submit to the indignity of having blistered hindquarters. In modern advertising you must flatter your public—not in the oily, abject, tradesmanlike style of the old advertisers, crawling before clients who were their social superiors; that's all over now. It's we who are the social superiors—because we've got more money than the bank clerks and the civil servants. Our modern flattery must be manly, straightforward, sincere, the admiration of equal for equal—all the more flattering as we aren't equals." Mr. Boldero laid a finger to his nose. "They're dirt and we're capitalists.…" He laughed.

Gumbril laughed too. It was the first time that he had ever thought of himself as a capitalist, and the thought was exhilarating.

===Chinese===
博尔德罗先生清了清嗓子。「咱们先从最简单、最初步的一手做起，」他说，「直接诉诸他们的自保本能：咱们要指出，专利充气裤是舒适的；穿上它就是免受疼痛。几句关于舒适的响亮口号——咱们要的就这么点。确实非常简单。要说服一个人坐在空气上比坐在木头上舒服，用不着费多大的事。可是话头既已到了硬座上，咱们就得巧妙地斜里滑开一笔，对那些社会本能来个侧翼包抄。」说着，博尔德罗先生把食指尖和拇指尖扣成一环，手腕轻巧地往旁一划，仿佛正沿着一条光滑的黄铜扶手滑过去。「咱们得谈谈案牍劳形的荣耀与磨难。咱们必须颂扬它精神上的尊严，同时谴责它肉体上的苦楚。『尊座』（the seat of honour），你懂的。这个题目大有可谈。『伟人之座』（The Seats of the Mighty）。『执掌公署之座，摇动整个世界』（The seat that rules the office rocks the world，戏拟谚语「推动摇篮的手支配世界」）。这些句子都能做出点文章。然后咱们还可以来几段关于御座的掌故闲谈：何等尊严，却又何等难受。咱们必须让银行职员和公务员为自己是这种人而自豪，同时又让他们羞愧：这般堂堂的人物，竟不得不忍受臀部起泡生茧的屈辱。做现代广告，必须奉承你的公众——但不是老派广告商那种油腻、卑屈、店小二式的奉承，在社会地位高于自己的主顾面前爬着走；那一套如今完了。如今咱们才是地位高的人——因为咱们的钱比银行职员和公务员多。咱们现代的奉承必须是有丈夫气的、直截了当的、真诚的，是平等者对平等者的钦敬——正因咱们其实并不平等，才越发奉承得人受用。」博尔德罗先生把一根手指按在鼻子上。「他们是烂泥，咱们是资本家……」他笑了。

冈布里尔也笑了。这是他生平头一回把自己想成资本家，这念头令人振奋。

===Original===
"We flatter them," went on Mr. Boldero. "We say that honest work is glorious and ennobling—which it isn't; it's merely dull and cretinizing. And then we go on to suggest that it would be finer still, more ennobling, because less uncomfortable, if they wore Gumbril's Patent Small-Clothes. You see the line?"

Gumbril saw the line.

"After that," said Mr. Boldero, "we get on to the medical side of the matter. The medical side, Mr. Gumbril—that's most important. Nobody feels really well nowadays—at any rate, nobody who lives in a big town and does the kind of loathsome work that the people we're catering for does. Keeping this fact before our eyes, we have to make it clear that only those can expect to be healthy who wear pneumatic trousers."

"That will be a little difficult, won't it?" questioned Gumbril.

===Chinese===
「咱们奉承他们，」博尔德罗先生继续说，「咱们说诚实的工作光荣而高尚——其实不然；它只是又沉闷又使人痴呆。接着咱们再暗示：倘若穿上冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤，那就更美了，更高尚了，因为不那么难受了。你看出路数了吧？」

冈布里尔看出了路数。

「再往后，」博尔德罗先生说，「咱们就进入这件事的医学一面。医学一面，冈布里尔先生——那最是要紧。如今没有谁觉得真正健康——至少，凡是住在大城市里、干着咱们主顾那一路叫人恶心的活儿的，没有一个觉得。把这个事实摆在眼前，咱们就得叫人明白：唯有穿充气裤的人，才可指望健康。」

「这怕有点难吧？」冈布里尔置疑道。

===Original===
"Not a bit of it!" Mr. Boldero laughed with an infectious confidence. "All we have to do is to talk about the great nerve centres of the spine: the shocks they get when you sit down too hard; the wearing exhaustion to which long-protracted sitting on unpadded seats subjects them. We'll have to talk very scientifically about the great lumbar ganglia—if there are such things, which I really don't pretend to know. We'll even talk almost mystically about the ganglia. You know that sort of ganglion philosophy?" Mr. Boldero went on parenthetically. "Very interesting it is, sometimes, I think. We could put in a lot about the dark, powerful sense-life, sex-life, instinct-life which is controlled by the lumbar ganglion. How important it is that that shouldn't be damaged. That already our modern conditions of civilization tend unduly to develop the intellect and the thoracic ganglia controlling the higher emotions. That we're wearing out, growing feeble, losing our balance in consequence. And that the only cure—if we are to continue our present mode of civilized life—is to be found in Gumbril's Patent Small-Clothes." Mr. Boldero brought his hand with an emphatic smack on to the table as he spoke, as he fairly shouted, these last words.

"Magnificent," said Gumbril, with genuine admiration.

===Chinese===
「一点也不难！」博尔德罗先生笑了，笑声里是富于感染力的自信。「咱们只消谈谈脊柱里那些重大的神经中枢：你坐下太猛时它们受的震动；坐在无垫的座位上旷日持久，它们所承受的磨损性疲惫。咱们得非常科——学——地大谈特谈那些了不起的腰神经节（lumbar ganglia）——到底有没有这东西，说实在的我不敢假装知道。咱们甚至要谈得近乎神秘。你知道那一套神经节哲学么？」博尔德罗先生顺势又插入一句。「有时我想，那还真有意思。咱们可以大写特写那由腰神经节管辖的黑暗而强大的感官生活、性生活、本能生活。它是何等重要，千万损伤不得。再写现代文明的条件已经趋于过度地发展智力、发展管辖高级情感的胸神经节（thoracic ganglia）。写我们因此日渐磨损、日益衰弱、失掉平衡。而唯一的解救——倘若我们还要延续眼下的文明生活方式——就在冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤里。」说到最后几句，博尔德罗先生几乎是吼出来的，手也着重地啪的一声拍在桌上。

「宏丽之至。」冈布里尔说，带着由衷的叹服。

===Original===
"This sort of medical and philosophical dope," Mr. Boldero went on, "is always very effective, if it's properly used. The public to whom we are making our appeal is, of course, almost absolutely ignorant on these, or, indeed, on almost all other subjects. It is therefore very much impressed by the unfamiliar words; particularly if they have such a good juicy sound as the word 'ganglia.'"

"There was a young man of East Anglia, whose loins were a tangle of ganglia," murmured Gumbril, *improvisatore*.

"Precisely," said Mr. Boldero. "Precisely. You see how juicy it is? Well, as I say, they're impressed. And they're also grateful. They're grateful to us for having given them a piece of abstruse, unlikely information which they can pass on to their wives, or to such friends as they know don't read the paper in which our advertisement appears—can pass on airily, don't you know, with easy erudition, as though they'd known all about ganglia from their childhood. And they'll feel such a flow of superiority as they hand on the metaphysics and the pathology, that they'll always think of us with affection. They'll buy our breeks and they'll get other people to buy. That's why," Mr. Boldero went off again on an instructive tangent, "that's why the day of secret patent medicines is really over. It's no good saying you have rediscovered some secret known only, in the past, to the Egyptians. People don't know anything about Egyptology; but they have an inkling that such a science exists. And that if it does exist, it's unlikely that patent medicine makers should have found out facts unknown to the professors at the universities. And it's much the same even with secrets that don't come from Egypt. People know there's such a thing as medical science and they again feel it's improbable that manufacturers should know things ignored by the doctors. The modern democratic advertiser is entirely aboveboard. He tells you all about it. He explains that the digestive juices acting on bismuth give rise to a disinfectant acid. He points out that lactic ferment gets destroyed before it reaches the large intestine, so that Metchnikoff's cure generally won't work. And he goes on to explain that the only way of getting the ferment there is to mix it with starch and paraffin: starch to feed the ferment on, paraffin to prevent the starch being digested before it gets to the intestine. And in consequence, he convinces you that a mixture of starch, paraffin and ferment is the only thing that's any good at all. Consequently you buy it; which you would never have done without the explanation. In the same way, Mr. Gumbril, we mustn't ask people to take our trousers on trust. We must explain scientifically why these trousers will be good for their health. And by means of the ganglia, as I've pointed out, we can even show that the trousers will be good for their souls and the whole human race at large. And as you probably know, Mr. Gumbril, there's nothing like a spiritual message to make things go. Combine spirituality with practicality and you've fairly got them. Got them, I may say, on toast. And that's what we can do with our trousers; we can put a message into them, a big, spiritual message. Decidedly," he concluded, "we shall have to work those ganglia all we can."

===Chinese===
「这类医学兼哲学的迷魂汤，」博尔德罗先生继续说，「只要用得妥当，向来非常灵验。咱们诉求的公众，在这些题目上——其实也在几乎所有别的题目上——自然是几乎绝对无知的。所以那些生僻字眼就令他们印象极深；尤其当这些字眼有着『神经节』（ganglia）这样多汁油润的好声响的时候。」

「东安格利亚（East Anglia）有一条好汉，一腰神经节乱作一团。」冈布里尔像即兴诗人（*improvisatore*，意大利语）似的低声吟道。

「正是，」博尔德罗先生说，「正是。你听听它多汁？好，就像我说的，他们被打动了。而且他们还感激。他们感激咱们给了他们一条深奥而不可信的知识，好转授给自己的太太，或者转授给他们明知不看咱广告那份报纸的朋友们——你懂的，轻飘飘地转授，带着从容不迫的博学，仿佛他们打童年起就对神经节了如指掌。等他们把这玄学和病理学传下去的时候，那股优越感的暖流会涌上来，于是他们想到咱们就总带着感情。他们会买咱的裤子，还会拉别人来买。正因为如此，」博尔德罗先生又顺势跑起一趟有益的野马，「正因为如此，秘方专利药的时代实在是一去不返了。你说你重新发现了某种往昔唯埃及人知晓的秘方——那没用。人们对埃及学一无所知；可是他们隐约晓得有这么一门学问存在。而且晓得，倘若它当真存在，专利药商居然能发现大学教授们不知道的事实，就未免不像话。就连并非来自埃及的秘方，情形也大致相同。人们知道世上有医学这门科学，他们又觉得，制造商居然知道医生们所忽略的事情，未免不太可能。现代的民主派广告商完全开诚布公。他把底细统统讲给你听。他解释说，消化液作用于铋，便生出一种消毒的酸。他指出，乳酸酵素还没到大肠就被消灭了，所以梅奇尼科夫（Metchnikoff）的疗法多半行不通。他接着又解释，要把酵素送到那里，唯一的办法是把它同淀粉和石蜡掺在一起：淀粉给酵素当粮，石蜡防止淀粉半路被消化掉。于是他就让你信了：淀粉、石蜡加酵素的混合物，是天下唯一管用的东西。于是你就买了；没有这番解释，你是断断不会买的。同理，冈布里尔先生，咱们不能指望人们凭信任来买咱的裤子。咱们必须科——学——地解释，这裤子为什么有益于他们的健康。而且，正如我指出的，靠着神经节，咱们甚至能表明，这裤子还有益于他们的灵魂，乃至全人类。你大概也知道，冈布里尔先生，再没有什么比一个灵性信息更能让货走得动了。把灵性与实用性一结合，你就稳稳把他们拿下了——可以说，是把他们夹在吐司上吃了。而这正是咱们的裤子办得到的：咱们能往裤子里装进一个信息，一个大大的、灵性的信息。断然无疑，」他总结道，「这些神经节，咱们得可着劲儿地使唤。」

===Original===
"I'll undertake to do that," said Gumbril, who felt very buoyant and self-assured. Mr. Boldero's hydrogenous conversation had blown him up like a balloon.

"And I'm sure you'll do it well," said Mr. Boldero encouragingly. "There is no better training for modern commerce than a literary education. As a practical business man, I always uphold the ancient universities, especially in their teaching of the Humanities."

Gumbril was much flattered. At the moment, it seemed supremely satisfying to be told that he was likely to make a good business man. The business man took on a radiance, began to glow, as it were, with a phosphorescent splendour.

===Chinese===
「这事交给我。」冈布里尔说，他自觉轻飘飘的，信心十足。博尔德罗先生这一席氢气成分十足的谈话，已经把他吹成了一只气球。

「而且我敢说你一定干得好。」博尔德罗先生鼓励道。「对于现代商业，再没有比文学教育更好的训练了。作为一个务实的生意人，我一贯拥护古老的大学，尤其拥护它们的人文学科教学。」

冈布里尔大为受用。此刻，听人说自己有望成为一员商界好手，似乎是无上的满足。生意人这一行当焕发出一轮光晕，可以说，开始放出磷光般的辉彩。

===Original===
"Then it's very important," continued Mr. Boldero, "to play on their snobbism; to exploit that painful sense of inferiority which the ignorant and ingenuous always feel in the presence of the knowing. We've got to make our trousers the Thing—socially right as well as merely personally comfortable. We've got to imply somehow that it's bad form not to wear them. We've got to make those who don't wear them feel rather uncomfortable. Like that film of Charlie Chaplin's, where he's the absentminded young man about town who dresses for dinner immaculately, from the waist up—white waistcoat, tail coat, stiff shirt, top-hat—and only discovers, when he gets down into the hall of the hotel, that he's forgotten to put on his trousers. We've got to make them feel like that. That's always very successful. You know those excellent American advertisements about young ladies whose engagements are broken off because they perspire too freely or have an unpleasant breath? How horribly uncomfortable those make you feel! We've got to do something of the same sort for our trousers. Or more immediately applicable would be those tailor's advertisements about correct clothes. 'Good clothes make you feel good.' You know the sort of line. And then those grave warning sentences in which you're told that a correctly cut suit may make the difference between an appointment gained and an appointment lost, an interview granted and an interview refused. But the most masterly examples I can think of," Mr. Boldero went on with growing enthusiasm, "are those American advertisements of spectacles, in which the manufacturers first assume the existence of a social law about goggles, and then proceed to invoke all the sanctions which fall on the head of the committer of a solecism upon those who break it. It's masterly. For sport or relaxation, they tell you, as though it was a social axiom, you must wear spectacles of pure tortoiseshell. For business, tortoiseshell rims and nickel earpieces lend incisive poise—incisive poise, we must remember that for our ads, Mr. Gumbril. 'Gumbril's Patent Small-Clothes lend incisive poise to business men.' For semi-evening dress, shell rims with gold earpieces and gold nose-bridge. And for full dress, gold-mounted rimless pince-nez are refinement itself, and absolutely correct. Thus we see, a social law has been created, according to which every self-respecting myope or astigmat must have four distinct pairs of glasses. Think if he should wear the all-shell sports model with full dress! Revolting solecism! The people who read advertisements like that begin to feel uncomfortable; they have only one pair of glasses, they are afraid of being laughed at, thought low-class and ignorant and suburban. And since there are few who would not rather be taken in adultery than in provincialism, they rush out to buy four new pairs of spectacles. And the manufacturer gets rich, Mr. Gumbril. Now, we must do something of the kind with our trousers. Imply somehow that they're correct, that you're undressed without, that your fiancée would break off the engagement if she saw you sitting down to dinner on anything but air." Mr. Boldero shrugged his shoulders, vaguely waved his hand.

"It may be rather difficult," said Gumbril, shaking his head.

===Chinese===
「再者，非常要紧的，」博尔德罗先生继续说，「是弹动他们的势利心弦；开发利用无知而天真的人们在懂行者面前总感到的那种痛切的自卑。咱们必须把咱的裤子捧成『正物』——不光图个人舒适，还得社交上站得住。咱们得设法暗示：不穿它便是失礼。咱们得让不穿的人觉得相当不自在。就像卓别林（Charlie Chaplin）那部电影，他演一个心不在焉的花花公子，赴晚宴装束穿得一丝不苟——上半身：白背心、燕尾服、硬胸衬衫、大礼帽——直到下了楼进了旅馆大厅，才发现忘了穿裤子。咱们就得让他们产生那种感觉。这一手向来灵验。你知道那些出色的美国广告吧：年轻小姐们婚约告吹，只因汗出得太畅，或者口气难闻？那种广告让你浑身不自在到什么地步！咱们得给咱的裤子来点同款的货色。或者更直接的现成样板，是那些裁缝店的『得体衣着』广告。『好衣裳叫你感觉好。』你知道这个路数。再有那些郑重其事的警告句：告诉你一套剪裁得体的衣服，可能正是得到与错过一个职位、获准与被拒一次会见之间的分野。不过我能想到的最大师级的典范，」博尔德罗先生越说越起劲，「是那些美国眼镜广告：制造商们先假定存在一条关于眼镜的社会法则，继而把失仪之徒应得的一切制裁，尽数援引到犯规者头上。真是大师手笔。他们告诉你——仿佛这是一条社会公理——运动或休憩，必须戴纯玳瑁的眼镜。办公呢，玳瑁框配镍镜腿，平添锋锐的从容——锋锐的从容，这一句咱们广告里得记住，冈布里尔先生。『冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤为商务人士平添锋锐的从容。』半晚装呢，玳瑁框配金镜腿、金鼻梁。而大礼服呢，金架无框夹鼻眼镜便是精致之本体，绝对得体。如此这般，一条社会法则便造出来了：凡是自尊自重的近视眼或散光眼，都必须置备四副各司其职的眼镜。想想看，他要是戴着全套玳瑁的运动款去配大礼服！何其倒人胃口的失仪！读到这种广告的人便开始心里发毛：他们只有一副眼镜，他们怕人笑话，怕被人看成品位低贱、无知、一身郊区土气。而天下滔滔，几乎人人宁肯行淫时被当场拿住，也不肯被人讥作乡气，于是他们便一窝蜂冲出去，另买四副新眼镜。于是制造商就发了财，冈布里尔先生。如今，咱们必须给咱的裤子来这么一套。设法暗示它们是得体的，不穿就等于没穿衣服，暗示你的未婚妻倘若看见你赴晚宴竟不坐在空气上、而坐在别的什么上头，她就会解除婚约。」博尔德罗先生耸了耸肩，含糊地摆了摆手。

「这恐怕相当难。」冈布里尔摇着头说。

===Original===
"It may," Mr. Boldero agreed. "But difficulties are made to be overcome. We must pull the string of snobbery and shame: it's essential. We must find out methods for bringing the weight of public opinion to bear mockingly on those who do not wear our trousers. It is difficult at the moment to see how it can be done. But it will have to be done, it will have to be done," Mr. Boldero repeated emphatically. "We might even find a way of invoking patriotism to our aid. 'English trousers filled with English air, for English men.' A little far-fetched, perhaps. But there might be something in it."

Gumbril shook his head doubtfully.

"Well, it's one of the things we've got to think about in any case," said Mr. Boldero. "We can't afford to neglect such powerful social emotions as these. Sex, as we've seen, is almost entirely out of the question. We must run the rest, therefore, as hard as we can. For instance, there's the novelty business. People feel superior if they possess something new which their neighbours haven't got. The mere fact of newness is an intoxication. We must encourage that sense of superiority, brew up that intoxication. The most absurd and futile objects can be sold because they're new. Not long ago I sold four million patent soap-dishes of a new and peculiar kind. The point was that you didn't screw the fixture into the bathroom wall; you made a hole in the wall and built the soap-dish into a niche, like a holy water stoup. My soap-dishes possessed no advantages over other kinds of soap-dishes, and they cost a fantastic amount to instal. But I managed to put them across, simply because they were new. Four million of them." Mr. Boldero smiled with satisfaction at the recollection. "We shall do the same, I hope, with our trousers. People may be shy of being the first to appear in them; but the shyness will be compensated for by the sense of superiority and elation produced by the consciousness of the newness of the things."

"Quite so," said Gumbril.

===Chinese===
「也许是难。」博尔德罗先生承认。「但困难生来就是供人克服的。咱们必须扯动势利与羞耻这两根线绳：这是根本。咱们必须想出办法，让舆论的分量讥诮地压到不穿咱裤子的人头上。眼下还看不出这事怎么做到。可是非做到不可，非做到不可。」博尔德罗先生着重地又说了一遍。「咱们甚至可以想法把爱国主义也请来帮忙。『英国裤子充着英国空气，给英国男人坐。』也许有点牵强。不过里头未必没有东西。」

冈布里尔疑惑地摇了摇头。

「反正，这是咱们无论如何都得琢磨的事之一。」博尔德罗先生说。「这么强大的社会情感，咱们可不敢怠慢。性，正如所见，几乎全然指望不上。所以其余几条，咱们就得玩命地赶。比如，还有『新奇』这门生意。人们若是拥有一件左邻右舍都没有的新东西，便自觉高人一等。单是『新』这一桩事实，本身便是一宗醉意。咱们必须助长那种优越感，酿出那宗醉意。最荒唐最无用的物件，只消它新，也卖得出去。不久前我卖出过四百万只新型特别的专利肥皂碟。妙处在乎：那托座不是拧在浴室墙上的；你要在墙上凿个洞，把肥皂碟砌进一个壁龛，活像一只圣水钵。我的肥皂碟比起别种肥皂碟毫无长处，安装起来还贵得骇人。可我照样把它们推销了出去，只因为它们新。整整四百万只。」想起往事，博尔德罗先生满意地笑了。「咱们的裤子，我希望，也能照样来一遍。头一个穿出去，人们也许害臊；可是这东西的新奇感所催生的优越与兴奋，自会把那点害臊补偿回来。」

「正是。」冈布里尔说。

===Original===
"And then, of course, there's the economy slogan. 'One pair of Gumbril's Patent Small-Clothes will outlast six pairs of ordinary trousers.' That's easy enough. So easy that it's really uninteresting." Mr. Boldero waved it away.

"We shall have to have pictures," said Gumbril, parenthetically. He had an idea.

"Oh, of course."

"I believe I know of the very man to do them," Gumbril went on. "His name's Lypiatt. A painter. You've probably heard of him."

"Heard of him!" exclaimed Mr. Boldero. He laughed. "But who hasn't heard of Lydgate."

===Chinese===
「再有，自然是省钱口号。『一条冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤，顶得上六条普通裤子。』这容易得很。容易得实在没意思。」博尔德罗先生挥手把它打发掉了。

「咱们还得配画。」冈布里尔顺势插了一句。他有了个主意。

「哦，那自然。」

「我相信我正好认得干这事的合适人选。」冈布里尔继续说。「他姓利皮亚特（Lypiatt）。画家。你多半听说过。」

「听说过！」博尔德罗先生叫道。他笑了。「谁还没听说过利德盖特（Lydgate）呢。」

===Original===
"Lypiatt."

"Lypgate, I mean, of course."

"I think he'd be the very man," said Gumbril.

"I'm certain he would," said Mr. Boldero, not a whit behindhand.

===Chinese===
「利皮亚特。」

「利普盖特（Lypgate），呃，我当然是说利普盖特。」

「我想他正是最合适的人。」冈布里尔说。

「我确定他就是。」博尔德罗先生说，半拍不落。

===Original===
Gumbril was pleased with himself. He felt he had done someone a good turn. Poor old Lypiatt; be glad of the money. Gumbril remembered also his own fiver. And remembering his own fiver, he also remembered that Mr. Boldero had as yet made no concrete suggestion about terms. He nerved himself at last to suggest to Mr. Boldero that it was time to think of this little matter. Ah, how he hated talking about money! He found it so hard to be firm in asserting his rights. He was ashamed of showing himself grasping. He always thought with consideration of the other person's point of view—poor devil, could he afford to pay? And he was always swindled and always conscious of the fact. Lord, how he hated life on these occasions! Mr. Boldero was still evasive.

"I'll write you a letter about it," he said at last.

===Chinese===
冈布里尔对自己颇为满意。他觉得自己帮人办了件好事。可怜的老利皮亚特；有了这笔钱该高兴了。冈布里尔还记起了自己那张五镑钞。记起五镑钞，他也就记起，博尔德罗先生至今对条件未作任何具体的表示。他终于鼓起勇气向博尔德罗先生提出，该是考虑这桩小事的时候了。啊，他多恨谈钱！他发现要坚持自己的权利竟这样难。他羞于显得贪心。他总是周到地为对方的处境着想——可怜虫，他付得起么？而他总是被坑，且总是心里有数。天哪，这种场合之下，他多恨生活！博尔德罗先生仍在打太极。

「我会为这个给你写封信。」他终于说。

===Original===
Gumbril was delighted. "Yes, do," he said enthusiastically, "do." He knew how to cope with letters all right. He was a devil with the fountain pen. It was these personal, hand-to-hand combats that he couldn't manage. He could have been, he always felt, such a ruthless critic and satirist, such a violent, unscrupulous polemical writer. And if ever he committed his autobiography to paper, how breath-takingly intimate, how naked—naked without so much as a healthy sunburn to colour the whiteness—how quiveringly a sensitive jelly it would be! All the things he had never told anyone would be in it. Confession at long range—if anything, it would be rather agreeable.

"Yes, do write me a letter," he repeated. "Do."

Mr. Boldero's letter came at last, and the proposals it contained were derisory. A hundred pounds down and five pounds a week when the business should be started. Five pounds a week—and for that he was to act as a managing director, writer of advertisements and promoter of foreign sales. Gumbril felt thankful that Mr. Boldero had put the terms in a letter. If they had been offered point-blank across the luncheon table, he would probably have accepted them without a murmur. He wrote a few neat, sharp phrases saying that he could not consider less than five hundred pounds down and a thousand a year. Mr. Boldero's reply was amiable; would Mr. Gumbril come and see him?

===Chinese===
冈布里尔大喜过望。「好，写吧，」他热切地说，「写吧。」信件他自有办法应付。他耍起自来水笔来简直是魔鬼。他应付不来的，是这种面对面、白刃战式的交锋。他一向觉得，自己本可以做一个何等狠辣的批评家与讽刺家，一个何等凶猛、无所顾忌的论战文字家。而倘若有一天他把自诉传付与纸墨，那会是何等令人屏息的私密，何等赤裸——赤裸得连一层健康日晒的红润都没有来给这片白着色——何等颤巍巍的一块敏感的胶冻！他从未告诉过任何人的种种，都将载于其中。隔着远距离的忏悔——非但无碍，说不定还相当惬意。

「好，就给我写信吧，」他重复道，「写吧。」

博尔德罗先生的信终于来了，所开条件可笑得近于羞辱。先付一百镑，生意开张后每周五镑。每周五镑——而他要为此充当常务董事、广告撰写人兼外销推广人。冈布里尔暗自庆幸，幸而博尔德罗先生把条件写进了信里。倘若这些条件隔着午餐桌劈面提出，他多半会一声不吭地接受下来。他回信写了几个干净利落的句子，说低于先付五百镑、年薪一千镑，他无从考虑。博尔德罗先生的答复客客气气；可否请冈布里尔先生来一见？

===Original===
See him? Well, of course, it was inevitable. He would have to see him again some time. But he would send the Complete Man to deal with the fellow. A Complete Man matched with a leprechaun—there could be no doubt as to the issue.

> "Dear Mr. Boldero," he wrote back, "I should have come to talk over matters before this. But I have been engaged during the last days in growing a beard and until this has come to maturity, I cannot, as you will easily be able to understand, leave the house. By the day after tomorrow, however, I hope to be completely presentable and shall come to see you at your office at about three o'clock, if that is convenient to you. I hope we shall be able to arrange matters satisfactorily.—Believe me, dear Mr. Boldero, yours very truly,
> Theodore Gumbril, Jr."

===Chinese===
见他？唉，当然，这是躲不过的。早晚总得再见他一面。不过，他可以派完人（the Complete Man）去对付那家伙。完人对小妖精——胜负如何，毫无悬念。

> 他回信道：「亲爱的博尔德罗先生：我本该早些前来面谈诸事。但近日我正忙于蓄须，在它长熟之前，您不难理解，我不便出门。不过到后天，我便可望完全见得人了，届时若蒙方便，当于午后三时许趋访贵办公室。望我们能把诸事安排妥帖。——请相信我，亲爱的博尔德罗先生，您非常忠实的，
> 小西奥多·冈布里尔（Theodore Gumbril, Jr.）」

===Original===
The day after tomorrow became in due course today; splendidly bearded and Rabelaisianly broad in his whipcord toga, Gumbril presented himself at Mr. Boldero's office in Queen Victoria Street.

"I should hardly have recognized you," exclaimed Mr. Boldero as he shook hands. "How it does alter you, to be sure!"

"Does it?" The Complete Man laughed with a significant joviality.

===Chinese===
后天到了日子，如期变成了今天；冈布里尔飘着一部神气的胡须，裹着斜纹呢的托加袍，一身拉伯雷式的魁伟，到维多利亚女王街（Queen Victoria Street）博尔德罗先生的办公室现身报到。

「我几乎认不出您了。」博尔德罗先生握手时叫道。「这胡子可真把您变了个人，千真万确！」

「是吗？」完人笑了一声，快活得意味深长。

===Original===
"Won't you take off your coat?"

"No, thanks," said Gumbril. "I'll keep it on."

"Well," said the leprechaun, leaning back in his chair and twinkling, birdlike, across the table.

"Well," repeated Gumbril on a different tone from behind the stooks of his corn-like beard. He smiled, feeling serenely strong and safe.

===Chinese===
「不脱下外衣么？」

「不了，谢谢，」冈布里尔说，「我穿着吧。」

「那么，」小妖精说，往椅背上一靠，隔着桌子鸟儿似的眨着眼。

「那么。」冈布里尔换了个声调，从他那一把麦束般的胡子后面应道。他微微一笑，只觉安然而强壮、安然而保险。

===Original===
"I'm sorry we should have disagreed," said Mr. Boldero.

"So am I," the Complete Man replied. "But we shan't disagree for long," he added, with significance; and as he spoke the words he brought down his fist with such a bang, that the inkpots on Mr. Boldero's very solid mahogany writing-table trembled and the pens danced, while Mr. Boldero himself started with a genuine alarm. He had not expected them. And now he came to look at him more closely, this young Gumbril was a great, hulking, dangerous-looking fellow. He had thought he would be easy to manage. How could he have made such a mistake?

Gumbril left the office with Mr. Boldero's cheque for three hundred and fifty pounds in his pocket and an annual income of eight hundred. His bruised right hand was extremely tender to the touch. He was thankful that a single blow had been enough.

===Chinese===
「很遗憾，我们竟没能谈拢。」博尔德罗先生说。

「我也遗憾。」完人答道。「不过这分歧长不了。」他意味深长地补上一句；话音未落，拳头已轰然砸下，砸得博尔德罗先生那张敦敦实实的红木写字桌上的墨水瓶直打颤，钢笔乱蹦，博尔德罗先生本人也着实一惊，弹了起来。这些话他可没料到。而此刻再细细打量，这位年轻的冈布里尔竟是个高大魁伟、看着危险的家伙。他原以为此人容易拿捏。他怎么会看走这样大的眼？

冈布里尔离开了办公室，口袋里装着博尔德罗先生三百五十镑的支票，外加八百镑的年金。他砸伤的右手，碰一碰都疼得厉害。只消一拳便已足够，他为此谢天谢地。

## XI / XI

===Original===

Gumbril had spent the afternoon at Bloxam Gardens. His chin was still sore from the spirit gum with which he had attached to it the symbol of the Complete Man; he was feeling also a little fatigued. Rosie had been delighted to see him; St. Jerome had gone on solemnly communicating all the time.

His father had gone out to dine, and Gumbril had eaten his rump steak and drunk his bottle of stout alone. He was sitting now in front of the open French windows which led from his father’s workroom on to the balcony, with a block on his knee and a fountain-pen in his hand, composing advertisements for the Patent Small-Clothes. Outside, in the plane trees of the square, the birds had gone through their nightly performance. But Gumbril had paid no attention to them. He sat there, smoking, sometimes writing a word or two﻿—sunk in the quagmire of his own drowsy and comfortable body. The flawless weather of the day had darkened into a blue May evening. It was agreeable merely to be alive.

===Chinese===

冈布里尔这个下午是在布洛克萨姆花园（Bloxam Gardens）度过的。下巴还隐隐作痛——那是拿酒精胶（spirit gum）往上面粘贴「完人」的象征时落下的；他还觉得有几分疲惫。罗茜（Rosie）见到他喜出望外；画上的圣哲罗姆（St. Jerome）则自始至终在一本正经地领受着圣餐。

他父亲出门赴宴去了，冈布里尔独自吃了牛后腿肉排，喝了一瓶黑啤。此刻他坐在父亲工作间通往阳台的那扇敞开的落地长窗前，膝上一本拍纸簿，手里一支自来水笔，正在为专利充气坐垫裤撰写广告。外面，广场的悬铃木上，鸟儿们已经演完了它们每夜的例行演出。冈布里尔却没有留意它们。他坐在那儿抽烟，偶尔写下一两个词——沉浸在自己那昏昏欲睡、舒舒服服的躯体的泥沼里。白昼无瑕的天气，已经暗成蓝湛湛的五月的黄昏。光是活着，就已够惬意了。

===Original===

He sketched out two or three advertisements in the grand idealistic transatlantic style. He imagined one in particular with a picture of Nelson at the head of the page and “England expects﻿ ﻿…” printed large beneath it. “England﻿ ﻿… Duty﻿ ﻿… these are solemn words.” That was how it would begin. “These are solemn words, and we use them solemnly as men who realize what Duty is, and who do all that in them lies to perform it as Englishmen should. The Manufacturer’s is a sacred trust. The guide and ruler of the modern world, he has, like the Monarch of other days, responsibilities towards his people; he has a Duty to fulfil. He rules, but he must also serve. We realize our responsibilities, we take them seriously. Gumbril’s Patent Small-Clothes have been brought into the world that they may serve. Our Duty towards you is a Duty of Service. Our proud boast is that we perform it. But besides his Duty towards Others, every man has a duty towards Himself. What is that Duty? It is to keep himself in the highest possible state of physical and spiritual fitness. Gumbril’s Patent Small-Clothes protect the lumbar ganglia.﻿ ﻿…” After that it would be plain medical and mystical sailing.

As soon as he got to the ganglia, Gumbril stopped writing. He put down the block, sheathed his pen, and abandoned himself to the pleasures of pure idleness. He sat, he smoked his cigar. In the basement, two floors down, the cook and the house-parlourmaid were reading﻿—one the *Daily Mirror*, the other the *Daily Sketch*. For them, Her Majesty the Queen spoke kindly words to crippled female orphans; the jockeys tumbled at the jumps; Cupid was busy in Society, and the murderers who had disembowelled their mistresses were at large. Above him was the city of models, was a bedroom, a servant’s bedroom, an attic of tanks and ancient dirt, the roof and, after that, two or three hundred light-years away, a star of the fourth magnitude. On the other side of the party-wall on his right, a teeming family of Jews led their dark, compact, Jewish lives with a prodigious intensity. At this moment they were all passionately quarrelling. Beyond the wall on the left lived the young journalist and his wife. Tonight it was he who had cooked the supper. The young wife lay on the sofa, feeling horribly sick; she was going to have a baby, there could be no doubt about it now. They had meant not to have one; it was horrible. And, outside, the birds were sleeping in the trees, the invading children from the slum tumbled and squealed. Ships meanwhile were walloping across the Atlantic freighted with more cigars. Rosie at this moment was probably mending Shearwater’s socks. Gumbril sat and smoked, and the universe arranged itself in a pattern about him, like iron filings round a magnet.

===Chinese===

他拟出了两三份广告，全是那种跨大西洋的豪迈理想主义格调。他特别设想出一则：页首印纳尔逊（Nelson）的画像，底下用大字印着「英格兰期望……」。「英格兰……天职……这是庄严的字眼。」广告便这样开头。「这是庄严的字眼；我们以庄严的态度使用它们，因为我们是一群深知天职为何物的人，一群竭尽己力、以英国人的本分履行天职的人。制造商的天职，是一桩神圣的托付。他是现代世界的向导与主宰，一如往昔的君主，对自己的子民负有责任；他负有一项必须履行的天职。他统治，但他亦须服务。我们明白自己的责任，我们郑重以待。冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤来到人世，正是为了服务。我们对贵方的天职，是服务的天职。我们引以为豪的是：我们践行了它。然而，除了对他人负有的天职，每个人对自己还负有一项义务。那义务是什么？便是使自己的肉体与精神，尽可能保持在最高的健全状态。冈布里尔专利充气坐垫裤，保护您的腰神经节（lumbar ganglia）……」此后，便可顺着纯粹的医学加神秘主义，一帆风顺地写下去了。

一写到神经节，冈布里尔便住了笔。他放下拍纸簿，套上笔帽，把自己交付给纯粹无所事事的快乐。他坐着，抽他的雪茄。底下两层、在地下室里，厨娘和客厅女仆正在读报——一个读《每日镜报》（*Daily Mirror*），一个读《每日素描报》（*Daily Sketch*）。在她们眼里，女王陛下正向残疾的女孤儿们说着慈爱的话；骑师们在跳栏前纷纷坠马；丘比特在上流交际场里忙个不停；那些把情妇开膛破肚的凶手，依然逍遥法外。他头顶上，是那座模型之城，是一间卧室、一间仆人的卧室、一个堆着水箱和经年陈垢的阁楼、屋顶，再往上，隔着两三百光年，一颗四等星。右手隔墙那边，一户人丁兴旺的犹太人家，正以惊人的炽烈，过着他们幽暗、紧凑而彻头彻尾犹太式的日子。此刻他们正全家上下吵得热火朝天。左边墙外住着年轻记者和他的妻子。今晚掌勺做饭的是他。年轻的妻子躺在沙发上，恶心得厉害；她要有孩子了，如今这已毫无疑问。他们本不打算要的；真可怕。而外面，鸟儿在树上睡去，贫民窟入侵过来的孩子们翻滚着、尖叫着。与此同时，一艘艘轮船正载着更多的雪茄，颠颠簸簸地横渡大西洋。此刻，罗茜多半正在补希尔沃特（Shearwater）的袜子。冈布里尔坐着抽烟，宇宙便在他四周自行排成一个图样，如铁屑之环于磁石。

===Original===

The door opened, and the house-parlourmaid intruded Shearwater upon his lazy felicity, abruptly, in her unceremonious old way, and hurried back to the *Daily Sketch*.

“Shearwater! This is very agreeable,” said Gumbril. “Come and sit down.” He pointed to a chair.

Clumsily, filling the space that two ordinary men would occupy, Shearwater came zigzagging and lurching across the room, bumped against the worktable and the sofa as he passed, and finally sat down in the indicated chair.

It suddenly occurred to Gumbril that this was Rosie’s husband: he had not thought of that before. Could it be in the marital capacity that he presented himself so unexpectedly now? After this afternoon.﻿ ﻿… He had come home; Rosie had confessed all.﻿ ﻿… Ah! but then she didn’t know who he was. He smiled to himself at the thought. What a joke! Perhaps Shearwater had come to complain to him of the unknown Complete Man﻿—to him! It was delightful. Anon﻿—the author of all those ballads in the *Oxford Book of English Verse*: the famous Italian painter﻿—Ignoto. Gumbril was quite disappointed when his visitor began to talk of other themes than Rosie. Sunk in the quagmire of his own comfortable guts, he felt good-humouredly obscene. The dramatic scabrousness of the situation would have charmed him in his present mood. Good old Shearwater﻿—but what an ox of a man! If he, Gumbril, took the trouble to marry a wife, he would at least take some interest in her.

Shearwater had begun to talk in general terms about life. What could he be getting at, Gumbril wondered? What particulars were ambushed behind these generalizations? There were silences. Shearwater looked, he thought, very gloomy. Under his thick moustache the small, pouting, babyish mouth did not smile. The candid eyes had a puzzled, tired expression in them.

===Chinese===

门开了，客厅女仆以她那素来不知客气为何物的老做派，冷不防把希尔沃特塞进他这份慵懒的清福里，随即匆匆回去读她的《每日素描报》了。

「希尔沃特！这可真叫人高兴。」冈布里尔说。「来，请坐。」他指了指一把椅子。

希尔沃特笨手笨脚，一个人要占两个普通人的地盘，斜斜拐拐、跌跌撞撞地穿过房间，一路撞上工作台和沙发，最后在那把指定的椅子里坐了下来。

冈布里尔猛然想起：这位是罗茜的丈夫；他先前竟没想到这一层。他此刻这般出其不意地登门，莫非是以丈夫的身份来的？经过今天下午……他回了家；罗茜一股脑儿全招了……啊！可是话说回来，她并不知道那人是谁呀。想到这里，他暗自笑了。多妙的玩笑！说不定希尔沃特是来向他控诉那位身份不明的「完人」的——向他本人！实在太有趣了。Anon（无名氏）——《牛津英诗选》（*Oxford Book of English Verse*）里那些歌谣的作者；那位大名鼎鼎的意大利画家——Ignoto（无名氏）。来客竟谈起罗茜以外的话题，冈布里尔好不失望。他沉在自己舒舒服服的五脏的泥沼里，怀着一股好脾气的猥亵。以他此刻的心境，这场面那副戏剧性的有伤风化，正够叫他心花怒放。好一个老希尔沃特——可这人真是头牛！他冈布里尔若肯费神娶上一房太太，至少总会对她有点儿兴趣吧。

希尔沃特已经开腔，泛泛而谈地聊起人生。他究竟想引到哪儿去呢，冈布里尔纳闷？这些泛泛之论的背后，埋伏着怎样的具体细节？谈话时时出现沉默。希尔沃特看上去——他觉得——阴郁得很。浓密的髭须底下，那张小小的、爱撅起的、婴孩似的嘴不带笑意。那双坦诚的眼睛里，是一副困惑而倦怠的神情。

===Original===

“People are queer,” he said after one of his silences. “Very queer. One has no idea how queer they are.”

Gumbril laughed. “But I have a very clear idea of their queerness,” he said. “Everyone’s queer, and the ordinary, respectable, bourgeois people are the queerest of the lot. How do they manage to live like that? It’s astonishing. When I think of all my aunts and uncles.﻿ ﻿…” He shook his head.

“Perhaps it’s because I’m rather incurious,” said Shearwater. “One ought to be curious, I think. I’ve come to feel lately that I’ve not been curious enough about people.” The particulars began to peep, alive and individual, out of the vagueness, like rabbits; Gumbril saw them in his fancy, at the fringe of a wood.

“Quite,” he said encouragingly. “Quite.”

“I think too much of my work,” Shearwater went on, frowning. “Too much physiology. There’s also psychology. People’s minds as well as their bodies.﻿ ﻿… One shouldn’t be limited. Not too much, at any rate. People’s minds.﻿ ﻿…” He was silent for a moment. “I can imagine,” he went on at last, as in the tone of one who puts a very hypothetical case, “I can imagine one’s getting so much absorbed in somebody else’s psychology that one could really think of nothing else.” The rabbits seemed ready to come out into the open.

===Chinese===

「人们是很怪的，」他在一阵沉默之后说。「怪得很。怪到什么地步，谁也想不到。」

冈布里尔笑了。「可我对他们的怪，心里清楚得很呢。」他说。「人人皆怪，而那些普普通通、正正经经的布尔乔亚，是所有人里最怪的。他们究竟是怎么设法那样活下来的？令人惊叹。我一想起我那些叔叔伯伯、姨妈姑妈……」他摇了摇头。

「也许是因为我这人不太好奇，」希尔沃特说。「依我看，人是应当好奇的。我近来渐渐觉得，我对人一向好奇得不够。」具体的细节开始从混沌模糊里探出头来，一只只活泛灵动、各具面目，像兔子一般；冈布里尔在想象中看见它们，在一座树林的边缘上。

「正是，」他鼓劲儿地说。「正是。」

「我在自己的工作上想得太多了，」希尔沃特皱着眉，接着说。「生理学想得太多。可还有心理学呢。人的心灵，正如人的身体……人不该自囿于一隅。无论如何，不该囿得太深。人的心灵……」他沉默了片刻。「我可以想象，」他终于又开口道，用的是那种提出一个极端假设之人的口吻，「我可以想象，一个人对另一个人的心灵痴迷到了这般地步，以致当真除它之外什么也想不了。」兔子们眼看就要蹦到光天化日的空地上来了。

===Original===

“That’s a process,” said Gumbril, with middle-aged jocularity, speaking out of his private warm morass, “that’s commonly called falling in love.”

There was another silence. Shearwater broke it to begin talking about Mrs. Viveash. He had lunched with her three or four days running. He wanted Gumbril to tell him what she was really like. “She seems to me a very extraordinary woman,” he said.

“Like everybody else,” said Gumbril irritatingly. It amused him to see the rabbits scampering about at last.

“I’ve never known a woman like that before.”

Gumbril laughed. “You’d say that of any woman you happened to be interested in,” he said. “You’ve never known any women at all.” He knew much more about Rosie, already, than Shearwater did, or probably ever would.

===Chinese===

「有这么一道程序，」冈布里尔说，带着中年人的谐谑，从他那一己温热的泥沼里发话，「世人通常称之为：堕入情网。」

又是一阵沉默。希尔沃特打破沉默，开始谈起维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash）来。他已接连三四天与她共进午餐。他要冈布里尔告诉他，她究竟是个怎样的人。「依我看，她是一位非常不同寻常的女人。」他说。

「跟谁都一样。」冈布里尔说得令人冒火。看见兔子们终于满地乱窜起来，他觉得有趣。

「我从没认识过那样的女人。」

冈布里尔笑了。「随便哪位女人，只要你恰好对她有了兴趣，你都会这么说。」他说。「你根本就没认识过任何女人。」关于罗茜，他知道的已经比希尔沃特多——而且多半远过于希尔沃特此生所能知道。

===Original===

Shearwater meditated. He thought of Mrs. Viveash, her cool, pale, critical eyes; her laughter, faint and mocking; her words that pierced into the mind, goading it into thinking unprecedented thoughts.

“She interests me,” he repeated. “I want you to tell me what she’s really like.” He emphasized the word really, as though there must, in the nature of things, be a vast difference between the apparent and the real Mrs. Viveash.

Most lovers, Gumbril reflected, picture to themselves, in their mistresses, a secret reality, beyond and different from what they see every day. They are in love with somebody else﻿—their own invention. And sometimes there is a secret reality; and sometimes reality and appearance are the same. The discovery, in either case, is likely to cause a shock. “I don’t know,” he said. “How should I know? You must find out for yourself.”

“But you knew her, you know her well,” said Shearwater, almost with anxiety in his voice.

“Not so well as all that.”

===Chinese===

希尔沃特陷入沉思。他想着维维什夫人，想着她那双冷淡、苍白、挑剔的眼睛；她那若有若无、含讥带讽的笑；她那些直刺入人心智的话语，鞭策着它去想前所未想之想。

「她让我感兴趣。」他又说了一遍。「我要你告诉我，她究竟是个怎样的人。」他把「究竟」二字咬得极重，仿佛按照事理之必然，表面的维维什夫人与真实的维维什夫人之间，必定横着天壤之别。

大多数坠入情网的人，冈布里尔暗想，都会在自己的情妇身上构想出一个秘密的真实，超出他们朝夕所见，并且与之不同。他们爱着的是另外某个人——他们自己的发明。而有时候，也确有一个秘密的真实；有时候，真实与表象原本就是一回事。无论哪种情形，水落石出之时，多半都要叫人震惊。「我不知道，」他说。「我怎么会知道？你必须自己去发现。」

「可是你认识她呀，你跟她很熟的。」希尔沃特说，嗓音里几乎带着焦灼。

「也没熟到那个地步。」

===Original===

Shearwater sighed profoundly, like a whale in the night. He felt restless, incapable of concentrating. His mind was full of a horrible confusion. A violent eruptive bubbling up from below had shaken its calm clarity to pieces. All this absurd business of passion﻿—he had always thought it nonsense, unnecessary. With a little strength of will one could shut it out. Women﻿—only for half an hour out of the twenty-four. But she had laughed, and his quiet, his security had vanished. “I can imagine,” he had said to her yesterday, “I can imagine myself giving up everything, work and all, to go running round after you.” “And do you suppose I should enjoy that?” Mrs. Viveash had asked. “It would be ridiculous,” he said, “it would be almost shameful.” And she had thanked him for the compliment. “And at the same time,” he went on, “I feel that it might be worth it. It might be the only thing.” His mind was confused, full of new thoughts. “It’s difficult,” he said after a pause, “arranging things. Very difficult. I thought I had arranged them so well.﻿ ﻿…”

“I never arrange anything,” said Gumbril, very much the practical philosopher. “I take things as they come.” And as he spoke the words, suddenly he became rather disgusted with himself. He shook himself; he climbed up out of his own morass. “It would be better, perhaps, if I arranged things more,” he added.

“Render therefore unto Caesar the things which are Caesar’s,” said Shearwater, as though to himself; “and to God, and to sex, and to work.﻿ ﻿… There must be a working arrangement.” He sighed again. “Everything in proportion. In proportion,” he repeated, as though the word were magical and had power. “In proportion.”

===Chinese===

希尔沃特长叹了一声，像黑夜里的一头鲸。他心神不定，无法集中心思。脑海里充满可怕的混乱。一股自深处猛烈喷涌而上的东西，已经把它平静的清明震成了碎片。激情这一整套荒唐事——他向来认为是无稽，是多余。凭一点儿意志之力，就能把它拒之门外。女人——二十四小时里拔出半个钟头来，也就够了。可是她笑了，于是他的清静、他的安稳，登时烟消云散。「我可以想象，」昨天他对她说，「我可以想象自己抛开一切，连工作也抛开，追在你的裙后团团转。」「那你以为，我会喜欢那样么？」维维什夫人当时问。「那样会很可笑，」他说，「那几乎算得上可耻。」她倒为这句恭维谢了他。「可与此同时，」他接着说下去，「我又觉得，那也许值得。那也许是唯一有价值的事。」他的心里乱作一团，满是新起的念头。「难哪，」他顿了一顿，说，「要把诸事安排妥帖。太难了。我原以为，我已经把一切安排得那么好了……」

「我从不安排任何事情，」冈布里尔说，十足是一位讲求实际的哲学家。「事情来了，我就顺水推舟。」话一出口，他忽然对自己生出几分厌恶。他振了振精神，从自己的泥沼里爬了上来。「要是多安排安排，也许倒好些。」他又添了一句。

「凯撒的物当归给凯撒，」希尔沃特说，仿佛自言自语；「上帝的当归给上帝，性的当归给性，工作的当归给工作……总得有一个行得通的安排。」他又叹了口气。「凡事讲比例。比例，」他重复道，仿佛这个词有魔力、有神通。「比例。」

===Original===

“Who’s talking about proportion?” They turned round. In the doorway Gumbril Senior was standing, smoothing his ruffled hair and tugging at his beard. His eyes twinkled cheerfully behind his spectacles. “Poaching on my architectural ground?” he said.

“This is Shearwater,” Gumbril Junior put in, and explained who he was.

The old gentleman sat down. “Proportion,” he said﻿—“I was just thinking about it, now, as I was walking back. You can’t help thinking about it in these London streets, where it doesn’t exist. You can’t help pining for it. There are some streets﻿ ﻿… oh, my God!” And Gumbril Senior threw up his hands in horror. “It’s like listening to a symphony of cats to walk along them. Senseless discords and a horrible disorder all the way. And the one street that was really like a symphony by Mozart﻿—how busily and gleefully they’re pulling it down now! Another year and there’ll be nothing left of Regent Street. There’ll only be a jumble of huge, hideous buildings at three-quarters of a million apiece. A concert of Brobdingnagian cats. Order has been turned into a disgusting chaos. We need no barbarians from outside; they’re on the premises, all the time.”

The old man paused and pulled his beard meditatively. Gumbril Junior sat in silence, smoking; and in silence Shearwater revolved within the walls of his great round head his agonizing thoughts of Mrs. Viveash.

“It has always struck me as very curious,” Gumbril Senior went on, “that people are so little affected by the vile and discordant architecture around them. Suppose, now, that all these brass bands of unemployed ex-soldiers that blow so mournfully at all the street corners were suddenly to play nothing but a series of senseless and devilish discords﻿—why, the first policeman would move them on, and the second would put them under arrest, and the passersby would try to lynch them on their way to the police station. There would be a real spontaneous outcry of indignation. But when at these same street corners the contractors run up enormous palaces of steel and stone that are every bit as stupid and ignoble and inharmonious as ten brass bandsmen each playing a different tune in a different key, there is no outcry. The police don’t arrest the architect; the passing pedestrians don’t throw stones at the workmen. They don’t notice that anything’s wrong. It’s odd,” said Gumbril Senior. “It’s very odd.”

===Chinese===

「谁在谈比例？」两人回过身去。门口站着老冈布里尔（Gumbril Senior），正抿平弄乱了的头发，一手捋着他的胡子。镜片后面，他的双眼快活地闪着光。「在我的建筑地盘上偷猎么？」他说。

「这位是希尔沃特。」小冈布里尔（Gumbril Junior）插话，说明了来客的身份。

老先生坐了下来。「比例，」他说——「我方才走回来的路上，正想着它呢。在伦敦这些街道上，你想不去想它都不行，因为这东西在这里根本不存在。你没法不渴念它。有那么几条街……哦，我的老天！」老冈布里尔惊骇地举起双手。「沿着它们走一遭，就像听一场猫的交响曲。一路全然是无谓的不协和音与可怕的紊乱。而唯一一条真正像莫扎特（Mozart）交响曲的街——他们如今正忙得多欢地拆着它呢！再过一年，摄政街（Regent Street）就要片瓦不剩了。只剩下一堆庞然丑物，一栋一栋，造价七十五万镑。一场大人国之猫的合奏。秩序被弄成了一团令人作呕的混沌。我们用不着外来的野蛮人；他们就在这宅院之内，无时无刻不在。」

老人顿了顿，若有所思地捋着胡子。小冈布里尔默坐抽烟；希尔沃特也在沉默中，于他那颗滚圆大脑袋的四壁之间，翻来覆去地转着关于维维什夫人的煎熬念头。

「有一桩事我一直觉得非常奇怪，」老冈布里尔接着说，「人们对周遭那些卑劣刺目的建筑，竟如此无动于衷。试想一下吧，倘若街头巷尾那些吹得那样凄凉的失业退伍军人铜管乐队，忽然改吹起一串全无意义、穷凶极恶的不协和音来——嗬，头一个警察就会驱赶他们，第二个就会把他们拘起来，行人还会在他们被押往警察局的路上，就试图对他们动私刑。那将是一场真正自发的义愤呐喊。可是，在同样的街角，承包商们飞快竖起一座座钢铁与巨石的庞大宫殿，其愚蠢、卑下与刺耳的程度，丝毫不亚于十名各吹异曲、各用异调的铜管乐手，却无人呐喊。警察不逮捕建筑师；过路的行人不朝工人们扔石头。他们根本不觉得有什么不对。真是怪事，」老冈布里尔说。「怪得很。」

===Original===

“Very odd,” Gumbril Junior echoed.

“The fact is, I suppose,” Gumbril Senior went on, smiling with a certain air of personal triumph, “the fact is that architecture is a more difficult and intellectual art than music. Music﻿—that’s just a faculty you’re born with, as you might be born with a snub nose. But the sense of plastic beauty﻿—though that’s, of course, also an inborn faculty﻿—is something that has to be developed and intellectually ripened. It’s an affair of the mind; experience and thought have to draw it out. There are infant prodigies in music; but there are no infant prodigies in architecture.” Gumbril Senior chuckled with a real satisfaction. “A man can be an excellent musician and a perfect imbecile. But a good architect must also be a man of sense, a man who knows how to think and to profit by experience. Now, as almost none of the people who pass along the streets in London, or any other city of the world, do know how to think or to profit by experience, it follows that they cannot appreciate architecture. The innate faculty is strong enough in them to make them dislike discord in music; but they haven’t the wits to develop that other innate faculty﻿—the sense of plastic beauty﻿—which would enable them to see and disapprove of the same barbarism in architecture. Come with me,” Gumbril Senior added, getting up from his chair, “and I’ll show you something that will illustrate what I’ve been saying. Something you’ll enjoy, too. Nobody’s seen it yet,” he said mysteriously as he led the way upstairs. “It’s only just finished﻿—after months and years. It’ll cause a stir when they see it﻿—when I let them see it, if ever I do, that is. The dirty devils!” Gumbril Senior added good-humouredly.

On the landing of the next floor he paused, felt in his pocket, took out a key and unlocked the door of what should have been the second best bedroom. Gumbril Junior wondered, without very much curiosity, what the new toy would turn out to be. Shearwater wondered only how he could possess Mrs. Viveash.

===Chinese===

「怪得很。」小冈布里尔应声道。

「事实是，我想，」老冈布里尔接着说，面带几分个人得胜的神气，「事实是，建筑是一门比音乐更难、更智性的艺术。音乐么——那不过是一种与生俱来的官能，好比生来一副塌鼻梁。可是造型之美——虽说那当然也是一种天赋的官能——却是必须加以发展、在智性上催熟的东西。那是心智上的事；要靠经验与思想把它牵引出来。音乐有神童；建筑却出不了神童。」老冈布里尔发出一声由衷满意的轻笑。「一个人可以是一位卓越的音乐家，同时又是一个十全十美的白痴。而一位好的建筑师，则还必须是个明白人，一个懂得如何思考、如何从经验中获益的人。而伦敦街上，或世界上任何城市的街上走过的行人，既然几乎无人懂得如何思考、如何从经验中获益，那么顺理成章，他们便鉴赏不了建筑。他们身上的天赋官能，足以叫他们厌恶音乐里的不协和音；可他们没那么点头脑，去发展另一种天赋官能——造型之美——正是靠它，他们才能看见并谴责建筑里同样的野蛮。跟我来，」老冈布里尔从椅子里站起身，又添了一句，「我给你们看一样东西，正好印证我方才说的话。也是你们看了会高兴的东西。还没有一个人见过它呢。」他领路上楼时，神秘兮兮地说。「它才刚刚完工——经过了多少个月、多少个年头。等他们看见它，准会轰动一场——也就是说，等我让他们看见，如果我终究肯让他们看的话。这群脏鬼！」老冈布里尔又好脾气地补了一句。

在上一层楼的楼梯口，他停下步来，往口袋里一摸，掏出一把钥匙，打开了那间本该是次好卧室的房门。小冈布里尔心里揣度着，这件新玩具究竟会是什么名堂——好奇倒谈不上多少。希尔沃特只在一心揣度：怎样才能把维维什夫人弄到手。

===Original===

“Come on,” called Gumbril Senior from inside the room. He turned on the light. They entered.

It was a big room; but almost the whole of the floor was covered by an enormous model, twenty feet long by ten or twelve wide, of a complete city traversed from end to end by a winding river and dominated at its central point by a great dome. Gumbril Junior looked at it with surprise and pleasure. Even Shearwater was roused from his bitter ruminations of desire to look at the charming city spread out at his feet.

“It’s exquisite,” said Gumbril Junior. “What is it? The capital of Utopia, or what?”

Delighted, Gumbril Senior laughed. “Don’t you see something rather familiar in the dome?” he asked.

===Chinese===

「来吧。」老冈布里尔在房间里喊道。他开了灯。两人走了进去。

那是一间大屋子；但几乎整片地面都被一座巨大的模型占满了——长二十英尺，宽十到十二英尺，一座完整的城市，一条蜿蜒的河从这一端贯穿到那一端，正中央雄踞着一座大穹顶。小冈布里尔又惊又喜地望着它。连希尔沃特也从他那欲望的苦涩咀嚼中被唤起，低头看起了这座铺展在他脚边的妩媚之城。

「真是精美，」小冈布里尔说。「这是什么？乌托邦（Utopia）的国都么，还是别的什么？」

老冈布里尔乐得笑了。「你在这穹顶上，可看出了什么眼熟的东西？」他问。

===Original===

“Well, I had thought﻿ ﻿…” Gumbril Junior hesitated, afraid that he might be going to say something stupid. He bent down to look more closely at the dome. “I had thought it looked rather like St. Paul’s﻿—and now I see that it is St. Paul’s.”

“Quite right,” said his father. “And this is London.”

“I wish it were,” Gumbril Junior laughed.

“It’s London as it might have been if they’d allowed Wren to carry out his plans of rebuilding after the Great Fire.”

“And why didn’t they allow him to?” Shearwater asked.

===Chinese===

「呃，我方才还在想……」小冈布里尔迟疑着，生怕自己就要说出什么蠢话来。他俯下身去，细看那穹顶。「我方才想，它看着挺像圣保罗大教堂——现在我明白了，它就是圣保罗大教堂。」

「一点不错，」他父亲说。「而这就是伦敦。」

「但愿它是，」小冈布里尔笑道。

「这是伦敦本可能成为的样子——倘若当年他们肯让雷恩（Wren）在大火之后实现他重建的规划。」

「那他们为什么不肯让他实现呢？」希尔沃特问。

===Original===

“Chiefly,” said Gumbril Senior, “because, as I’ve said before, they didn’t know how to think or profit by experience. Wren offered them open spaces and broad streets; he offered them sunlight and air and cleanliness; he offered them beauty, order and grandeur. He offered to build for the imagination and the ambitious spirit of man, so that even the most bestial, vaguely and remotely, as they walked those streets, might feel that they were of the same race﻿—or very nearly﻿—as Michelangelo; that they too might feel themselves, in spirit at least, magnificent, strong and free. He offered them all these things; he drew a plan for them, walking in peril among the still smouldering ruins. But they preferred to re-erect the old intricate squalor; they preferred the medieval darkness and crookedness and beastly irregular quaintness; they preferred holes and crannies and winding tunnels; they preferred foul smells, sunless, stagnant air, phthisis and rickets; they preferred ugliness and pettiness and dirt; they preferred the wretched human scale, the scale of the sickly body, not of the mind. Miserable fools! But I suppose,” the old man continued, shaking his head, “we can’t blame them.” His hair had blown loose from its insecure anchorage; with a gesture of resignation he brushed it back into place. “We can’t blame them. We should have done the same in the circumstances﻿—undoubtedly. People offer us reason and beauty; but we will have none of them, because they don’t happen to square with the notions that were grafted into our souls in youth, that have grown there and become a part of us. *Experientia docet*﻿—nothing falser, so far as most of us are concerned, was ever said. You, no doubt, my dear Theodore, have often in the past made a fool of yourself with women.﻿ ﻿…”

Gumbril Junior made an embarrassed gesture that half denied, half admitted the soft impeachment. Shearwater turned away, painfully reminded of what, for a moment, he had half forgotten. Gumbril Senior swept on.

===Chinese===

「主要的原因，」老冈布里尔说，「就在于，正如我方才说过的，他们不懂得思考，也不会从经验中获益。雷恩献给他们开阔的空地与宽阔的街道；献给他们阳光、空气和洁净；献给他们美、秩序与宏伟。他提议为人的想象力与雄心而建造，好教即便最兽性的人，走在那些街上，也能隐隐约约、影影绰绰地感到，自己与米开朗琪罗（Michelangelo）同属一族——或者相去不远；也能感到自己——至少在精神上——同样是恢弘、强壮而自由的。他把这一切献给了他们；他为他们绘好了图纸，在犹自闷烧的废墟中间履险奔走。可他们宁愿把旧日那套繁杂交错的龌龊重新竖起来；他们宁要中世纪的黑暗与歪扭，宁要那股兽性的、参差不齐的古怪劲儿；宁要孔洞、缝隙和弯弯绕绕的地道；宁要恶臭、不见天日的滞浊空气、肺痨和佝偻病；宁要丑陋、琐屑和污秽；宁要那可悲的人的尺度——病体的尺度，而非心智的尺度。一群可怜的蠢材！不过我想，」老人摇着头，接着说，「我们也不能怪他们。」他的头发从拴不牢的锚地里散飘了出来；他做了个无可奈何的手势，把头发抿回原处。「我们不能怪他们。换了我们处他们的境地，也会干出同样的事来——毫无疑问。人家把理性与美奉给我们；我们却一概拒收，因为它们偏偏合不上那些年轻时嫁接进我们灵魂、在那里生根长大、变成我们一部分的观念。Experientia docet（经验给人教训）——对我们大多数人来说，天底下再没有比这更不实的话了。你，亲爱的西奥多，毫无疑问，过去就常常在女人身上出尽洋相……」

小冈布里尔做了个局促的手势，对这一温和的指控半推半认。希尔沃特别过脸去，一时半忘的事，又被痛楚地勾了起来。老冈布里尔则滔滔不绝地讲了下去。

===Original===

“Will that prevent you from making as great a fool of yourself again tomorrow? It will not. It will most assuredly not.” Gumbril Senior shook his head. “The inconveniences and horrors of the pox are perfectly well known to everyone; but still the disease flourishes and spreads. Several million people were killed in a recent war and half the world ruined; but we all busily go on in courses that make another event of the same sort inevitable. *Experientia docet? Experientia* doesn’t. And that is why we must not be too hard on these honest citizens of London who, fully appreciating the inconveniences of darkness, disorder and dirt, manfully resisted any attempt to alter conditions which they had been taught from childhood onwards to consider as necessary, right and belonging inevitably to the order of things. We must not be too hard. We are doing something even worse ourselves. Knowing by a century of experience how beautiful, how graceful, how soothing to the mind is an ordered piece of town-planning, we pull down almost the only specimen of it we possess and put up in its place a chaos of Portland stone that is an offence against civilization. But let us forget about these old citizens and the labyrinth of ugliness and inconvenience which we have inherited from them, and which is called London. Let us forget the contemporaries who are making it still worse than it was. Come for a walk with me through this ideal city. Look.”

And Gumbril Senior began expounding it to them.

===Chinese===

「那能阻止你明天再出一次同样大的丑吗？阻止不了。千真万确阻止不了。」老冈布里尔摇了摇头。「梅毒的麻烦与可怖，人人心知肚明；可这病照样滋蔓四方。不久前的一场战争杀了几百万人，毁掉了半个世界；可我们大家还在忙忙碌碌地照老路走下去，而那老路，注定要再引来一场同样的浩劫。*Experientia docet*？*Experientia* 偏不。正因如此，我们切不可过于苛责伦敦这些诚实的市民——他们深谙黑暗、紊乱与污秽的种种不便，却雄赳赳地抗拒一切改动之举，改动那些他们自幼便被教导要视为必需、正当、并且必然属于万物常序的东西。我们不可过于苛责。我们自己正在干着更糟的事。凭一百年的经验，我们深知一座井然有序的城市规划，何其美好，何其优雅，何其慰藉人心；我们却把它几乎仅存的样品拆将下来，在原地竖起一片波特兰石（Portland stone）的混沌——那是对文明的冒犯。不过，让我们忘掉这些旧日市民，忘掉我们从他们手里继承下来的这座丑陋而不便的迷宫吧——它名叫伦敦。让我们忘掉那些正把它弄得比原来更糟的当代人。随我到这座理想之城里散步一遭吧。看。」

于是老冈布里尔开始为他们逐一讲解起来。

===Original===

In the middle, there, of that great elliptical Piazza at the eastern end of the new City, stands, foursquare, the Royal Exchange. Pierced only with small dark windows, and built of rough ashlars of the silvery Portland stone, the ground floor serves as a massy foundation for the huge pilasters that slide up, between base and capital, past three tiers of pedimented windows. Upon them rest the cornice, the attic and the balustrade, and on every pier of the balustrade a statue holds up its symbol against the sky. Four great portals, rich with allegory, admit to the courtyard with its double tier of coupled columns, its cloister and its gallery. The statue of Charles the Martyr rides triumphantly in the midst, and within the windows one guesses the great rooms, rich with heavy garlands of plaster, panelled with carved wood.

Ten streets give on to the Piazza, and at either end of its ellipse the water of sumptuous fountains ceaselessly blows aloft and falls. Commerce, in that to the north of the Exchange, holds up her cornucopia, and from the midst of its grapes and apples the master jet leaps up; from the teats of all the ten Useful Arts, grouped with their symbols about the central figure, there spouts a score of fine subsidiary streams. The dolphins, the seahorses and the Tritons sport in the basin below. To the south, the ten principal cities of the Kingdom stand in a family round the Mother London, who pours from her urn an inexhaustible Thames.

===Chinese===

在新城东端那座巨大的椭圆形广场（Piazza）正中央，端端正正立着皇家交易所（Royal Exchange）。它只开着小小的暗窗，以银灰波特兰石的粗面方石砌成；底层为那些巨大的壁柱充当厚重的基础，壁柱从柱础到柱头一路拔升，掠过三层带三角楣的窗子。柱身上承着檐口、阁楼层和栏杆；栏杆的每一根望柱上，都有一尊雕像把自己的象征物举向天空。四座大门满布寓意雕刻，引人进入庭院——院内是双层的成对柱列，还有回廊与游廊。殉道者查理（Charles the Martyr）的雕像踌躇满志地居于正中；窗内，人们可以想见那些轩敞的大厅，缀满沉甸甸的灰泥花环，镶着雕花的木护壁。

十条街道通向这座广场；在椭圆的两端，华美喷泉的水无休无止地喷上高空，又复落下。交易所北面的那一座，商业女神（Commerce）高举着丰饶角，主水柱从满堆的葡萄与苹果当中直跃而起；十尊实用艺术（Useful Arts）女神各携象征、环拱着中央主像，从她们的乳头喷出二十股细细的副流。海豚、海马和特里同们在下面的水池中嬉戏。南面，王国的十座主要城池如一家人般环立，拱卫着母亲伦敦（Mother London）；她从手中的瓮里，倾注出不竭的泰晤士河。

===Original===

Ranged round the Piazza are the Goldsmiths’ Hall, the Office of Excise, the Mint, the Post Office. Their flanks are curved to the curve of the ellipse. Between pilasters, their windows look out on to the Exchange, and the sister statues on the balustrades beckon to one another across the intervening space.

Two master roads of ninety feet from wall to wall run westwards from the Exchange. New Gate ends the more northern vista with an Arch of Triumph, whose three openings are deep, shadowy and solemn as the entries of caverns. The Guildhall and the halls of the twelve City Companies in their livery of rose-red brick, with their lacings of white stone at the coigns and round the windows, lend to the street an air of domestic and comfortable splendour. And every two or three hundred paces the line of the houses is broken, and in the indentation of a square recess there rises, conspicuous and insular, the fantastic tower of a parish church. Spire out of dome; octagon on octagon diminishing upwards; cylinder on cylinder; round lanterns, lanterns of many sides; towers with airy pinnacles; clusters of pillars linked by incurving cornices, and above them, four more clusters and above once more; square towers pierced with pointed windows; spires uplifted on flying buttresses; spires bulbous at the base﻿—the multitude of them beckons, familiar and friendly, on the sky. From the other shore, or sliding along the quiet river, you see them all, you tell over their names; and the great dome swells up in the midst overtopping them all.

The dome of St. Paul’s.

===Chinese===

环列广场四周的，是金匠会馆（Goldsmiths' Hall）、货物税局（Office of Excise）、铸币厂（the Mint）和邮政总局（Post Office）。它们的侧翼随椭圆的弧线弯曲。壁柱之间，一扇扇窗子朝交易所眺望；栏杆上那些情同姊妹的雕像，隔着中间的空当彼此颔首相招。

两条主干道，墙到墙宽九十英尺，从交易所向西延伸。新门（New Gate）以一座凯旋门收束较北那条街的远景，凯旋门的三个门洞深邃、幽暗而庄严，一如洞穴的入口。市政厅（Guildhall）与城内十二家同业公会（City Companies）的会馆，穿一色玫瑰红砖的号衣，墙角和窗缘镶着白石的花边，给这条街平添一种家常而安逸的富丽。每隔两三百步，房屋的行列便中断一次，在一处方形凹进的缺口里，一座教区教堂的奇巧塔楼拔地而起，醒目而孤屿似的。穹顶上生出尖顶；八角叠着八角，愈上愈收；圆筒叠着圆筒；圆的灯笼塔，多面的灯笼塔；顶着空灵小尖峰的塔楼；一簇簇柱身以内弯的檐口相联，其上又有四簇，再上复有一簇；开着尖头窗的方塔；凭飞扶壁托举而起的尖顶；底下鼓成球茎的尖顶——这一大群塔尖，熟稔而亲热地，在天幕上招手。从对岸，或者顺着静静的河水滑行时，你把它们一览无余，逐一数点着它们的名字；而那大穹顶在正中鼓隆而起，凌驾于它们所有之上。

圣保罗大教堂的穹顶。

===Original===

The other master street that goes westward from the Piazza of the Exchange slants down towards it. The houses are of brick, plain-faced and square, arcaded at the base, so that the shops stand back from the street and the pedestrian walks dry-shod under the harmonious succession of the vaultings. And there at the end of the street, at the base of a triangular space formed by the coming together of this with another master street that runs eastwards to Tower Hill, there stands the Cathedral. To the north of it is the Deanery and under the arcades are the booksellers’ shops.

From St. Paul’s the main road slopes down under the swaggering Italianate arches of Ludgate, past the wide lime-planted boulevards that run north and south within and without the city wall, to the edge of the Fleet Ditch﻿—widened now into a noble canal, on whose paved banks the barges unload their freights of country stuff﻿—leaps it on a single flying arch to climb again to a round circus, a little to the east of Temple Bar, from which, in a pair of diagonally superimposed crosses, eight roads radiate: three northwards towards Holborn, three from the opposite arc towards the river, one eastward to the City, and one past Lincoln’s Inn Fields to the west. The piazza is all of brick and the houses that compose it are continuous above the ground-floor level; for the roads lead out under archways. To one who stands in the centre at the foot of the obelisk that commemorates the victory over the Dutch, it seems a smooth well of brickwork pierced by eight arched conduits at the base and diversified above by the three tiers of plain, unornamented windows.

===Chinese===

另一条从交易所广场向西的主干道，朝着它缓缓斜落。街旁房屋是砖砌的，面目朴方、齐齐整整，底层带着拱廊，于是店铺从街沿退后，行人在一连串和谐的拱顶之下，走得分毫不湿。就在街的尽头、在这条街与另一条东通塔丘（Tower Hill）的主干道交汇而成的三角形场地的底边，矗立着大教堂。教堂北面是院长居所（Deanery），拱廊底下是一家家书铺。

从圣保罗大教堂起，主干道在卢德门（Ludgate）神气活现的意大利式拱门下缓缓下行，掠过城墙内外南北向的宽阔椴树大林荫道，来到弗利特沟（Fleet Ditch）的边沿——如今它已拓宽成一条气派的运河，驳船在它铺石的堤岸上卸下一船船乡间土产——大道以一道飞拱凌空跃过，重新爬升到一座圆形广场，位于圣殿关（Temple Bar）稍东；从那里，以一对斜向叠置的十字，八条道路放射而出：三条向北通往霍尔本（Holborn），三条自对面的弧线通向河边，一条向东入城，一条经过林肯律师学院广场（Lincoln's Inn Fields）向西。广场通体是砖，围合广场的房屋在底层以上连绵不断；因为道路都从拱门底下通出去。站在广场中央、纪念战胜荷兰人的方尖碑脚下，这广场像一口光洁的砖砌深井，井脚穿通着八条拱形孔道，井身上方则以三层素净无饰的窗户缀出变化。

===Original===

Who shall describe all the fountains in the open places, all the statues and monuments? In the circus north of London Bridge, where the four roads come together, stands a pyramid of nymphs and Tritons﻿—river goddesses of Polyolbion, sea-gods of the island beaches﻿—bathing in a ceaseless tumble of white water. And here the city griffon spouts from its beak, the royal lion from between its jaws. St. George at the foot of the Cathedral rides down a dragon whose nostrils spout, not fire, but the clear water of the New River. In front of the India House, four elephants of black marble, endorsed with towers of white, blow through their upturned trunks the copious symbol of Eastern wealth. In the gardens of the Tower sits Charles the Second, enthroned among a troop of Muses, Cardinal Virtues, Graces and Hours. The tower of the Customs-House is a pharos. A great water-gate, the symbol of naval triumph, spans the Fleet at its junction with the Thames. The river is embanked from Blackfriars to the Tower, and at every twenty paces a grave stone angel looks out from the piers of the balustrade across the water.﻿ ﻿…

Gumbril Senior expounded his city with passion. He pointed to the model on the ground, he lifted his arms and turned up his eyes to suggest the size and splendour of his edifices. His hair blew wispily loose and fell into his eyes, and had to be brushed impatiently back again. He pulled at his beard; his spectacles flashed, as though they were living eyes. Looking at him, Gumbril Junior could imagine that he saw before him the passionate and gesticulating silhouette of one of those old shepherds who stand at the base of Piranesi’s ruins demonstrating obscurely the prodigious grandeur and the abjection of the human race.

===Chinese===

谁能历数空场上所有的喷泉、所有的雕像与纪念碑？在伦敦桥北面的环形广场上、四条道路交汇之处，立着一座仙女与特里同叠成的金字塔——《多福之国》（Polyolbion）里的诸河女神、海岛沙滩上的诸海神——沐浴在不息翻腾的白水之中。在这里，这座城的狮鹫从喙中喷水，王家的雄狮从齿颔间喷水。大教堂脚下的圣乔治（St. George）驰马踏倒一条龙，龙的鼻孔里喷出的不是火，而是新河（New River）的清水。印度大厦（India House）前，四头黑大理石的大象，背上驮着白色的塔楼，从扬起的长鼻里，吹喷出东方财富那丰沛的象征。伦敦塔的花园里坐着查理二世（Charles the Second），在一群缪斯、四枢德、美惠三女神与时序女神的环侍中端坐御座。海关大楼的塔楼是一座灯塔。一座巨大的水门——海上凯旋的象征——横跨弗利特河与泰晤士河的交汇处。从黑衣修士桥到伦敦塔，两岸都砌了堤岸；每隔二十步，便有一尊庄重的石天使，从栏杆的墩柱上隔水凝望……

老冈布里尔满怀激情地讲解着他的城市。他指点着地上的模型，举起双臂、翻起双眼，以状摹他那些楼宇的规模与气象。他的头发一绺绺散飘开来，落进眼睛里，只得不耐烦地一次次抹回去。他扯着胡子；他的眼镜灼灼闪光，仿佛是一双活的眼睛。望着他，小冈布里尔恍然觉得自己看见的，是皮拉内西（Piranesi）废墟底下常见的那种老牧人中的一个——激情澎湃、比手画脚的一幅剪影——正在隐晦地昭示人类那惊人的伟大，与人类的卑贱。

## XII / XII

===Original===
“You? Is it you?” She seemed doubtful.

Gumbril nodded. “It’s me,” he reassured her. “I’ve shaved; that’s all.” He had left his beard in the top right-hand drawer of the chest of drawers, among the ties and the collars.

Emily looked at him judicially. “I like you better without it,” she decided at last. “You look nicer. Oh no, I don’t mean to say you weren’t nice before,” she hastened to add. “But﻿—you know﻿—gentler﻿—” She hesitated. “It’s a silly word,” she said, “but there it is: sweeter.”

That was the unkindest cut of all. “Milder and more melancholy?” he suggested.

“Well, if you like to put it like that,” Emily agreed.

He took her hand and raised it to his lips. “I forgive you,” he said.

===Chinese===
「你？真是你么？」她将信将疑。

冈布里尔点点头。「是我，」他让她放心，「我剃了胡子；就这么点儿事。」他把胡子留在了五斗柜右上层的抽屉里，混在领带和衣领中间。

艾米莉（Emily）像法官似的打量他。「没有它我更喜欢你。」她终于裁定。「你看着更顺眼了。哦不，我不是说你从前不好，」她赶紧补了一句，「只是——你知道——更温存了——」她迟疑了。「这词儿挺傻，」她说，「可就是这么回事儿：更甜了。」

这一下真是最无情的一击（化用莎剧《恺撒大帝》「This was the unkindest cut of all」）。「更温和、也更忧郁了？」他提示道。

「唔，你愿意那么说，也行。」艾米莉认可了。

他握起她的手，举到唇边。「我原谅你。」他说。

===Original===
He could forgive her anything for the sake of those candid eyes, anything for the grave, serious mouth, anything for the short brown hair that curled﻿—oh, but never seriously, never gravely﻿—with such a hilarious extravagance round her head. He had met her, or rather the Complete Man, flushed with his commercial triumphs as he returned from his victory over Mr. Boldero, had met her at the National Gallery. “Old Masters, young mistresses;” Coleman had recommended the National Gallery. He was walking up the Venetian Room, feeling as full of swaggering vitality as the largest composition of Veronese, when he heard, gigglingly whispered just behind him his Open Sesame to new adventure, “Beaver.” He spun round on his tracks and found himself face to face with two rather startled young women. He frowned ferociously: he demanded satisfaction for the impertinence. They were both, he noticed, of gratifyingly pleasing appearance and both extremely young. One of them, the elder it seemed, and the more charming, as he had decided from the first, of the two, was dreadfully taken aback; blushed to the eyes, stammered apologetically. But the other, who had obviously pronounced the word, only laughed. It was she who made easy the forming of an acquaintance which ripened, half an hour later, over the teacups and to the strains of the most classy music on the fifth floor of Lyons’s Strand Corner House.

===Chinese===
为了那双坦率的眼睛，他什么都能原谅她；为了那副庄重认真的嘴，什么都能；为了那头棕色短发——它卷得，天哪，从来不认真、从来不严肃，只管欢天喜地、毫无节制地绕着她的脑袋打转——也什么都能。他是在国家美术馆（National Gallery）遇见她的——更确切地说，是完人遇见的；那时他刚战胜博尔德罗先生，挟着商界凯旋的余威归来。「老大师，小情妇」（Old Masters, young mistresses）——荐他去国家美术馆的，正是科尔曼（Coleman）。他正走上威尼斯厅，自觉浑身涨满招摇的生命力，不输委罗内塞最大的一幅构图；忽然听见背后有人咯咯地低声念出他通向新历险的芝麻开门：「河狸」（Beaver，俚语：大胡子）。他霍地转身，同两个吓了一跳的年轻姑娘打了个照面。他凶巴巴地皱起眉来：他要为这番唐突讨个赔礼。他注意到，两人都生得可喜地动人，都极其年轻。其中一个——看去略长几岁，而且正如他打第一眼便已裁定的，是两人中更迷人的那一个——慌得不知所措；红晕直烧到眼角，结结巴巴地赔不是。另一个，显然正是吐出那个词的，却只管笑。多亏有她，一段相识轻而易举地结成，半小时后便告熟络——就在茶杯之间，就在莱昂斯河岸街角餐厅（Lyons’s Strand Corner House）五楼最上等音乐的乐声里。

===Original===
Their names were Emily and Molly. Emily, it seemed, was married. It was Molly who let that out, and the other had been angry with her for what was evidently an indiscretion. The bald fact that Emily was married had at once been veiled with mysteries, surrounded and protected by silences; whenever the Complete Man asked a question about it, Emily did not answer and Molly only giggled. But if Emily was married and the elder of the two, Molly was decidedly the more knowledgeable about life; Mr. Mercaptan would certainly have set her down as the more civilized. Emily didn’t live in London; she didn’t seem to live anywhere in particular. At the moment she was staying with Molly’s family at Kew.

He had seen them the next day, and the day after, and the day after that; once at lunch, to desert them precipitately for his afternoon with Rosie; once at tea in Kew Gardens; once at dinner, with a theatre to follow and an extravagant taxi back to Kew at midnight. The tame decoy allays the fears of the shy wild birds; Molly, who was tame, who was frankly a flirting little wanton, had served the Complete Man as a decoy for the ensnaring of Emily. When Molly went away to stay with friends in the country, Emily was already inured and accustomed to the hunter’s presence; she accepted the playful attitude of gallantry, which the Complete Man, at the invitation of Molly’s rolling eyes and provocative giggle, had adopted from the first, as natural and belonging to the established order of things. With giggling Molly to give her a lead, she had gone in three days much further along the path of intimacy than, by herself, she would have advanced in ten times the number of meetings.

“It seems funny,” she had said the first time they met after Molly’s departure, “it seems funny to be seeing you without Molly.”

===Chinese===
她们一个叫艾米莉，一个叫莫莉（Molly）。艾米莉似乎已经结了婚。这是莫莉说漏的嘴，另一个还为此动了气，因为那分明是一句失言。「艾米莉已婚」这个光秃秃的事实，立刻被蒙上了重重神秘，被层层沉默围拢护住；完人每问起一句，艾米莉不答，莫莉只管咯咯地笑。不过，已婚的、年长的虽然数艾米莉，对人生更有见识的却断然是莫莉；换了默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan），准会把她归作更文明的那一个。艾米莉不住在伦敦；她似乎并没有个一定的住处。眼下她寄住在莫莉家里，在邱（Kew）。

第二天他见了她们，第三天又见，第四天还见；一次是共进午餐，饭后却火速撇下她们，赶去同罗茜（Rosie）消磨下午；一次在邱园（Kew Gardens）喝茶；一次吃晚饭，接着看戏，半夜里再挥霍地雇车回邱。驯顺的媒鸟最能安抚羞怯的野鸟；莫莉是驯顺的，坦坦荡荡就是个卖弄风情的小浪蹄，便给完人充当了诱捕艾米莉的媒鸟。待到莫莉下乡到朋友家小住，艾米莉已经惯了，习惯了猎人常在跟前；完人打一开头，应着莫莉流转的眼波和撩人的轻笑摆下的那副戏谑殷勤的做派，她也就当作自然而然、既定秩序里应有之物，接受了下来。有咯咯笑的莫莉在前头引路，三天工夫，她在亲昵的路上已走出这样远；若靠她自己，三十次会面也未必走得了这么远。

「真有点怪，」莫莉走后两人头一回见面，她说，「没有莫莉，倒同你见面，真有点怪。」

===Original===
“It seemed funnier with Molly,” said the Complete Man. “It wasn’t Molly I wanted to see.”

“Molly’s a very nice, dear girl,” she declared loyally. “Besides, she’s amusing and can talk. And I can’t; I’m not a bit amusing.”

It wasn’t difficult to retort to that sort of thing; but Emily didn’t believe in compliments; oh, quite genuinely not.

He set out to make the exploration of her; and now that she was inured to him, no longer too frightened to let him approach, now, moreover, that he had abandoned the jocular insolences of the Complete Man in favour of a more native mildness, which he felt instinctively was more suitable in this particular case, she laid no difficulties in his way. She was lonely, and he seemed to understand everything so well; in the unknown country of her spirit and her history she was soon going eagerly before him as his guide.

===Chinese===
「有莫莉在才怪呢。」完人说。「我想见的可不是莫莉。」

「莫莉是个非常好、非常亲爱的姑娘。」她忠心耿耿地宣称。「再说，她有趣，会说话。我就不行；我一点儿也不有趣。」

这种话原不难回敬；可是艾米莉不信恭维；哦，是货真价实地不信。

他着手对她进行勘探；如今她既已惯了他，不再怕得不敢让他近身，又何况他已抛却完人那套打趣的无礼，换上一副更本色的温存——他本能地觉得，这温存在眼下一桩事上更为合宜——她便一路不再给他设障。她是寂寞的，而他仿佛把什么都懂得那样透；在她心灵与来历的那片未知国度里，她很快便热切地抢在他前头，给他当起了向导。

===Original===
She was an orphan. Her mother she hardly remembered. Her father had died of influenza when she was fifteen. One of his business friends used to come and see her at school, take her out for treats and give her chocolates. She used to call him Uncle Stanley. He was a leather merchant, fat and jolly with a rather red face, very white teeth and a bald head that was beautifully shiny. When she was seventeen and a half he asked her to marry him, and she had said yes.

“But why?” Gumbril asked. “Why on earth?” he repeated.

“He said he’d take me round the world; it was just when the war had come to an end. Round the world, you know; and I didn’t like school. I didn’t know anything about it and he was very nice to me; he was very pressing. I didn’t know what marriage meant.”

“Didn’t know?”

She shook her head; it was quite true. “But not in the least.”

And she had been born within the twentieth century. It seemed a case for the textbooks of sexual psychology. “Mrs. Emily X., born in 1901, was found to be in a state of perfect innocence and ignorance at the time of the Armistice, 11th November 1918,” etc.

===Chinese===
她是个孤儿。母亲她几乎不记得了。父亲在她十五岁上死于流感。父亲的一位生意上的朋友，从前常到学校来看她，带她出去散心，给她买巧克力。她管他叫斯坦利叔叔（Uncle Stanley）。他是个皮革商，胖乎乎乐呵呵，脸色红红的，牙齿雪白，一颗秃头油光锃亮，漂亮极了。她十七岁半那年，他向她求婚，她答应了。

「可是为什么？」冈布里尔问。「到底为什么？」他又问了一遍。

「他说要带我环游世界；那时仗刚刚打完。环游世界呀，你知道；我也不喜欢上学。这些事我一窍不通，他又待我很好；他求得很紧。我不知道结婚是怎么回事。」

「不知道？」

她摇了摇头；千真万确。「一点儿也不知道。」

而她可是生在二十世纪里的人。这简直该收进性心理学的教科书，作一宗病例。「艾米莉·X太太，生于1901年，经查于1918年11月11日停战之日，仍处于完全天真与完全无知之状态，」云云。

===Original===
“And so you married him?”

She had nodded.

“And then?”

She had covered her face with her hands, she had shuddered. The amateur uncle, now professionally a husband, had come to claim his rights﻿—drunk. She had fought him, she had eluded him, had run away and locked herself into another room. On the second night of her honeymoon he gave her a bruise on the forehead and a bite on the left breast which had gone on septically festering for weeks. On the fourth, more determined than ever, he seized her so violently by the throat, that a blood-vessel broke and she began coughing bright blood over the bedclothes. The amateur uncle had been reduced to send for a doctor and Emily had spent the next few weeks in a nursing home. That was four years ago; her husband had tried to induce her to come back, but Emily had refused. She had a little money of her own; she was able to refuse. The amateur uncle had consoled himself with other and more docile nieces.

===Chinese===
「于是你就嫁了他？」

她点了点头。

「后来呢？」

她曾双手掩面，她曾发抖。那位业余的叔叔，如今以丈夫为业，来讨他的权利了——喝得烂醉。她同他搏斗，她躲闪他，逃出去把自己锁进另一间屋子。蜜月的第二夜，他给她额头留下一块淤伤，左乳留下一齿咬痕，那伤口溃烂发炎了好几个星期。第四夜，他愈发不肯罢休，掐她的喉咙掐得那样狠，一根血管破裂了，她对着床单咳出鲜红的血。业余叔叔没奈何，只得去请医生，此后的几个星期，艾米莉是在疗养院里度过的。那是四年前的事；她丈夫屡次劝她回去，艾米莉拒绝了。她自己有一点钱；她拒绝得起。业余叔叔另寻了别的、更听话的侄女们聊以自慰。

===Original===
“And has nobody tried to make love to you since then?” he asked.

“Oh, lots of them have tried.”

“And not succeeded?”

She shook her head. “I don’t like men,” she said. “They’re hateful, most of them. They’re brutes.”

“*Anch’ io?*”

“What?” she asked, puzzled.

===Chinese===
「从那以后，就没有人试过向你求爱么？」他问。

「哦，试的人多着呢。」

「都没成？」

她摇了摇头。「我不喜欢男人。」她说。「他们大多可恨。都是畜生。」

「*Anch’ io?*（意大利语：我也是这样么？）」

「什么？」她茫然地问。

===Original===
“Am I a brute too?” And behind his beard, suddenly, he felt rather a brute.

“No,” said Emily, after a little hesitation, “you’re different. At least I think you are; though sometimes,” she added candidly, “sometimes you do and say things which make me wonder if you really are different.”

The Complete Man laughed.

“Don’t laugh like that,” she said. “It’s rather stupid.”

“You’re perfectly right,” said Gumbril. “It is.”

===Chinese===
「我也是畜生么？」而胡子后面，他忽然觉得自己也颇像个畜生。

「不，」艾米莉略一迟疑，说，「你不一样。至少我觉得不一样；虽然有时候，」她坦白地补了一句，「有时候你做的事、说的话，叫我不免怀疑，你是不是真不一样。」

完人笑了。

「别那么笑。」她说。「那样怪蠢的。」

「你说得完全对。」冈布里尔说。「是挺蠢的。」

===Original===
And how did she spend her time? He continued the exploration.

Well, she read a lot of books; but most of the novels she got from Boots’ seemed to her rather silly.

“Too much about the same thing. Always love.”

The Complete Man gave a shrug. “Such is life.”

“Well, it oughtn’t to be,” said Emily.

===Chinese===
那么，她的日子都是怎么打发的？他继续勘探。

唔，她读很多书；可是从博姿（Boots，附设租书部的药妆连锁店）借来的那些小说，多半她都觉得挺傻。

「翻来覆去总是那一件事。老是爱情。」

完人耸了耸肩。「生活就是如此。」

「哼，就不该是那样。」艾米莉说。

===Original===
And then, when she was in the country﻿—and she was often in the country, taking lodgings here and there in little villages, weeks and months at a time﻿—she went for long walks. Molly couldn’t understand why she liked the country; but she did. She was very fond of flowers. She liked them more than people, she thought.

“I wish I could paint,” she said. “If I could, I’d be happy forever, just painting flowers. But I can’t paint.” She shook her head. “I’ve tried so often. Such dirty, ugly smudges come out on the paper; and it’s all so lovely in my head, so lovely out in the fields.”

Gumbril began talking with erudition about the flora of West Surrey: where you could find butterfly orchis and green man and the bee, the wood where there was actually wild columbine growing, the best localities for butcher’s broom, the outcrops of clay where you get wild daffodils. All this odd knowledge came spouting up into his mind from some underground source of memory. Flowers﻿—he never thought about flowers nowadays from one year’s end to the other. But his mother had liked flowers. Every spring and summer they used to go down to stay at their cottage in the country. All their walks, all their drives in the governess cart had been hunts after flowers. And naturally the child had hunted with all his mother’s ardour. He had kept books of pressed flowers, he had mummified them in hot sand, he had drawn maps of the country and coloured them elaborately with different coloured inks to show where the different flowers grew. How long ago all that was! Horribly long ago! Many seeds had fallen in the stony places of his spirit, to spring luxuriantly up into stalky plants and wither again because they had no deepness of earth; many had been sown there and had died, since his mother scattered the seeds of the wild flowers.

===Chinese===
再有，到了乡下——她常在乡下，东一个小村西一个小村地赁屋小住，一住几星期、几个月——她就作长途散步。莫莉不明白她为什么喜欢乡下；可她就是喜欢。她非常爱花。她想，她爱花胜过爱人。

「但愿我会画画。」她说。「要会的话，我就永远幸福了，光画花儿就够。可我不会画。」她摇了摇头。「我试过那么多次。纸上出来的总是些又脏又丑的涂抹；可我脑子里明明那么美，田野里也明明那么美。」

冈布里尔开始掉起书袋，大谈西萨里（West Surrey）的植物志：蝴蝶红门兰（butterfly orchis）、「绿人」红门兰（green man）和「蜜蜂」红门兰（the bee）到哪里去找，哪片树林里当真长着野生的耧斗菜，假叶树最兴旺的去处在何方，哪几处黏土露头的地方能采到野水仙。这些冷僻的知识，从记忆的某处地下泉眼里，汩汩地涌上他的心头。花——如今他一年到头，再也想不起花这回事了。可他的母亲是喜欢花的。从前每年春夏，他们都下乡住到他们的村舍里去。他们所有的散步，所有坐着家庭女教师小马车（governess cart）出游，全是寻花的围猎。孩子自然也怀着母亲的全部热忱投入围猎。他收藏过一册册压花簿，用热沙给花防腐、制成干尸，还画过乡野的地图，用各色墨水精细地着色，标明各种花各生何处。这一切，隔得有多远了！远得可怕！许多种子曾落进他心灵的石头地，蓬勃抽发，长成高秆的植株，却因土不深又枯萎了；自从母亲撒下野花的种子，还有许多种子播在那里，随即死去了。

===Original===
“And if you want sundew,” he wound up, “you’ll find it in the Punch Bowl, under Hindhead. Or round about Frensham. The Little Pond, you know, not the Big.”

“But you know all about them,” Emily exclaimed in delight. “I’m ashamed of my poor little knowledge. And you must really love them as much as I do.”

Gumbril did not deny it; they were linked henceforth by a chain of flowers.

===Chinese===
「还有，你要是想找茅膏菜，」他收尾道，「到欣德黑德（Hindhead）山下的『拳碗』（the Punch Bowl）洼地去。或者弗伦沙姆（Frensham）一带。要知道，是小塘，不是大塘。」

「可你对这些无所不知啊。」艾米莉欢喜地叫道。「我那点可怜的学问真叫我害臊。你也一定跟我一样爱花爱得深。」

冈布里尔没有否认；从此，一条花的链子把两人拴在了一起。

===Original===
But what else did she do?

Oh, of course she played the piano a great deal. Very badly; but at any rate it gave her pleasure. Beethoven: she liked Beethoven best. More or less, she knew all the sonatas, though she could never keep up anything like the right speed in the difficult parts.
