Gumbril had again shown himself wonderfully at home. “Aha!” he said. “I bet you can’t shake that low B in the last variation but one of Op. 106 so that it doesn’t sound ridiculous.”

And of course she couldn’t, and of course she was glad that he knew all about it and how impossible it was.

===Chinese===
那么，她还做点别的什么呢？

哦，钢琴她当然弹得很多。弹得很糟；可不管怎样，它给她快乐。贝多芬：她最喜欢贝多芬。那些奏鸣曲她差不多都算熟悉，只是难弹的段落里，她的速度从来追不上该有的拍子。

冈布里尔又露了一手如数家珍的本事。「啊哈！」他说。「我打赌，作品第106号倒数第二个变奏里那个低音B的颤音，你没法弹得不像个笑话。」

她当然没法，当然也为他既对这一节了如指掌、又知道它何等办不到而高兴。

===Original===
In the cab, as they drove back to Kew that evening, the Complete Man had decided it was time to do something decisive. The parting kiss﻿—more of a playful sonorous buss than a serious embracement﻿—that was already in the protocol, as signed and sealed before her departure by giggling Molly. It was time, the Complete Man considered, that this salute should take on a character less formal and less playful. One, two, three and, decisively, as they passed through Hammersmith Broadway, he risked the gesture. Emily burst into tears. He was not prepared for that, though perhaps he should have been. It was only by imploring, only by almost weeping himself, that Gumbril persuaded her to revoke her decision never, never to see him again.

===Chinese===
当晚乘车回邱的路上，完人认定，该来点决定性的动作了。临别的亲吻——与其说是郑重的拥抱，不如说是一声闹着玩的响吻——这一项早已写进仪典，是咯咯笑的莫莉在她临走之前签字画押定下的。这一礼，完人盘算着，如今该换换性质了，少些客套，少些玩笑。一，二，三——车驶过哈默史密斯百老汇（Hammersmith Broadway）的当口，他断然实施了这一举动。艾米莉放声大哭。这一着他不曾防备，虽说按说本该防备。冈布里尔百般央告，自己也险些落下泪来，才劝得她收回那个从此永、永不再同他见面的决定。

===Original===
“I had thought you were different,” she sobbed. “And now, now﻿—”

“Please, please,” he entreated. He was on the point of tearing off his beard and confessing everything there and then. But that, on second thoughts, would probably only make things worse.

“Please, I promise.”

In the end, she had consented to see him once again, provisionally, in Kew Gardens, on the following day. They were to meet at the little temple that stands on the hillock above the valley of the heathers.

And now, duly, they had met. The Complete Man had been left at home in the top right-hand drawer, along with the ties and collars. She would prefer, he guessed, the Mild and Melancholy one; he was quite right. She had thought him “sweeter” at a first glimpse.

“I forgive you,” he said, and kissed her hand. “I forgive you.”

===Chinese===
「我原以为你不一样。」她抽泣道。「可如今，如今——」

「求你，求你。」他哀告道。他差一点当场把胡子扯下来，把一切和盘托出。可转念一想，那多半只会把事情弄得更糟。

「求你了，我保证。」

到最后，她总算同意再见他一面——姑且一见——就在第二天，在邱园。会面的地点，约定在石楠谷上方小丘上立着的那座小神庙。

而现在，他们如期相会了。完人被留在了家里右上层的抽屉里，同领带和衣领们作伴。他猜，她更喜欢那位「温和而忧郁的」；他猜得一点不错。头一眼，她就觉得他「更甜了」。

「我原谅你。」他说着，吻了吻她的手。「我原谅你。」

===Original===
Hand in hand they walked down towards the valley of the heaths.

“I don’t know why you should be forgiving me,” she said, laughing. “It seems to me that I ought to be doing the forgiving. After yesterday.” She shook her head at him. “You made me so wretched.”

“Ah, but you’ve already done your forgiving.”

“You seem to take it very much for granted,” said Emily. “Don’t be too sure.”

“But I am sure,” said Gumbril. “I can see﻿—”

Emily laughed again. “I feel happy,” she declared.

===Chinese===
他们手拉着手，朝石楠谷走下去。

「我不明白干吗要你来原谅我。」她笑着说。「要原谅，也该我来原谅才是。想想昨天。」她朝他摇头。「你把我害得好苦。」

「啊，可你已经原谅过啦。」

「你倒把这看成板上钉钉了。」艾米莉说。「别太有把握。」

「可我就是有把握。」冈布里尔说。「我看得出来——」

艾米莉又笑了。「我觉得快活。」她宣称。

===Original===
“So do I.”

“How green the grass is!”

Green, green﻿—after these long damp months it glowed in the sunlight, as though it were lighted from inside.

“And the trees!”

The pale, high, clot-polled trees of the English spring; the dark, symmetrical pine trees, islanded here and there on the lawns, each with its own separate profile against the sky and its own shadow, impenetrably dark or freckled with moving lights, on the grass at its feet.

===Chinese===
「我也快活。」

「草地多绿啊！」

绿啊，绿——熬过这漫长潮湿的几个月，它在阳光里灼灼发亮，仿佛从里面透出光来。

「还有这些树！」

英国春日里那些苍白高挑、梢头结成一团团树冠的截头树；一座座草坪上星岛般散布着的深色、齐整的松树，每一棵都以自己独一份的剪影衬着天空，在自己脚下的草地上投下自己独一方浓黑不可透、或洒满游动光斑的影子。

===Original===
They walked on in silence. Gumbril took off his hat, breathed the soft air that smelt of the greenness of the garden.

“There are quiet places also in the mind,” he said meditatively. “But we build bandstands and factories on them. Deliberately﻿—to put a stop to the quietness. We don’t like the quietness. All the thoughts, all the preoccupations in my head﻿—round and round, continually.” He made a circular motion with his hand. “And the jazz bands, the music-hall songs, the boys shouting the news. What’s it for? what’s it all for? To put an end to the quiet, to break it up and disperse it, to pretend at any cost it isn’t there. Ah, but it is; it is there, in spite of everything, at the back of everything. Lying awake at night, sometimes﻿—not restlessly, but serenely, waiting for sleep﻿—the quiet reestablishes itself, piece by piece; all the broken bits, all the fragments of it we’ve been so busily dispersing all day long. It reestablishes itself, an inward quiet, like this outward quiet of grass and trees. It fills one, it grows﻿—a crystal quiet, a growing, expanding crystal. It grows, it becomes more perfect; it is beautiful and terrifying, yes, terrifying as well as beautiful. For one’s alone in the crystal and there’s no support from outside, there’s nothing external and important, nothing external and trivial to pull oneself up by or to stand on, superiorly, contemptuously, so that one can look down. There’s nothing to laugh at or feel enthusiastic about. But the quiet grows and grows. Beautifully and unbearably. And at last you are conscious of something approaching; it is almost a faint sound of footsteps. Something inexpressibly lovely and wonderful advances through the crystal, nearer, nearer. And, oh, inexpressibly terrifying. For if it were to touch you, if it were to seize and engulf you, you’d die; all the regular, habitual, daily part of you would die. There would be an end of bandstands and whizzing factories, and one would have to begin living arduously in the quiet, arduously in some strange, unheard-of manner. Nearer, nearer come the steps; but one can’t face the advancing thing. One daren’t. It’s too terrifying, it’s too painful to die. Quickly, before it is too late, start the factory wheels, bang the drum, blow up the saxophone. Think of the women you’d like to sleep with, the schemes for making money, the gossip about your friends, the last outrage of the politicians. Anything for a diversion. Break the silence, smash the crystal to pieces. There, it lies in bits; it is easily broken, hard to build up and easy to break. And the steps? Ah, those have taken themselves off, double quick. Double quick, they were gone at the first flawing of the crystal. And by this time the lovely and terrifying thing is three infinities away, at least. And you lie tranquilly on your bed, thinking of what you’d do if you had ten thousand pounds, and of all the fornications you’ll never commit.” He thought of Rosie’s pink underclothes.

“You make things very complicated,” she said, after a silence.

===Chinese===
他们默然前行。冈布里尔摘下帽子，呼吸着满含园中青翠气息的柔软空气。

「心灵里也有幽静的地方。」他若有所思地说。「可我们在上面盖起了音乐台和工厂。存心的——好叫那幽静断根。我们不喜欢幽静。我脑袋里那些念头、那些牵挂——转啊，转啊，转个不停。」他用手画了个圈。「还有爵士乐队，杂耍剧场的歌，报童喊号外。为了什么？都是为了什么？为了了结那静，把它敲碎、驱散，不惜任何代价假装它不在。啊，可它在；它就在那里，任凭什么也在，在一切的背后。夜里醒着，有时候——不是烦躁地，而是安然地，等着睡意降临——那静便一块一块地重新聚拢起来；我们忙碌了一整天驱散的那些碎片、那些零块，全都回来了。它重新聚拢，一种内里的静，就像这外面草木的静。它充满一个人，它生长——一种水晶的静，一块生长着、扩张着的水晶。它生长，它趋于完美；它美丽而可怖，是的，可怖一如它的美丽。因为在那水晶里你是孤独的，外面没有任何支撑，没有任何外部的、重大的东西，也没有任何外部的、琐屑的东西，可供你攀附，供你居高临下、带着轻蔑踩上去，好往下看。没有什么可笑的，也没有什么可热衷的。可那静越长越大。美得动人，也大得难耐。终于，你觉出有什么在逼近；那几乎是一阵隐约的脚步声。一样无法言喻地可爱而奇妙的东西，穿过水晶推进，近了，近了。而它，哦，也无法言喻地可怖。因为倘若它碰到你，倘若它攫住你、吞没你，你就会死；你身上那个规规矩矩、习以为常、日复一日的部分就会死。音乐台和嗡嗡转的工厂便就此完蛋，人就得在那静里开始艰苦地活，以某种古怪的、闻所未闻的方式艰苦地活。近了，近了，脚步来了；可人不敢迎向那逼近的东西。人不敢。太可怖了，死也太痛了。快，趁还来得及，发动工厂的轮子，擂响鼓，吹响萨克斯管。想想你巴望同床共枕的女人们，想想赚钱的妙计，朋友的闲话，政客们最新的倒行逆施。什么都行，只求岔开心思。打破寂静，把水晶砸个粉碎。瞧，它碎作一地；这东西建起来难，碎起来容易。脚步呢？啊，早开溜了，一路小跑。才起头一道裂纹，它们便跑步开溜了。到这时候，那可爱而可怖的东西，少说也退到三个无穷远之外去了。于是你安然躺在床上，想着倘有一万镑你要怎么花，想着那些你永远不会去犯的私通。」他想起了罗茜的粉色内衣。

「你把事情弄得复杂极了。」沉默了一阵之后，她说。

===Original===
Gumbril spread out his greatcoat on a green bank and they sat down. Leaning back, his hands under his head, he watched her sitting there beside him. She had taken off her hat; there was a stir of wind in those childish curls, and at the nape, at the temples, where the hair had sleaved out thin and fine, the sunlight made little misty haloes of gold. Her hands clasped round her knees, she sat quite still, looking out across the green expanses, at the trees, at the white clouds on the horizon. There was quiet in her mind, he thought. She was native to that crystal world; for her, the steps came comfortingly through the silence and the lovely thing brought with it no terrors. It was all so easy for her and simple.

Ah, so simple, so simple; like the Hire Purchase System on which Rosie had bought her pink bed. And how simple it was, too, to puddle clear waters and unpetal every flower!﻿—every wild flower, by God! one ever passed in a governess cart at the heels of a barrel-bellied pony. How simple to spit on the floors of churches! *Si prega di non sputare.* Simple to kick one’s legs and enjoy oneself﻿—dutifully﻿—in pink underclothing. Perfectly simple.

===Chinese===
冈布里尔把大衣铺在一面绿坡上，两人坐了下来。他仰面枕着双手，看她坐在自己身旁。她已摘了帽子；那一头孩子气的鬈发里有微风拂动，而在颈后、在鬓角，发丝稀稀地、纤纤地飘散出来的地方，阳光织出一圈圈朦胧的金晕。她双手抱着膝盖，一动不动地坐着，眺望着绿色的旷野，望着树，望着天边的白云。她心里是安静的，他想。她是那个水晶世界的土著；在她那里，脚步声穿过寂静而来是令人宽慰的，那可爱的东西也不随身带来任何恐怖。这对她何其容易，何其简单。

啊，这样简单，这样简单；就像罗茜买她那张粉红床所用的分期付款制（Hire Purchase System）一样简单。把清可见底的水搅浑、把每一朵花都弄得落瓣纷纷，又是何等简单！——天哪，每一朵野花！凡是你坐着家庭女教师小马车、跟在一匹桶肚小马后面曾经路过的野花，无一例外。往教堂的地板上吐口痰，又是何等简单！*Si prega di non sputare*（意大利语：请勿吐痰）。蹬着两条腿寻欢作乐——本本分分、尽着本分地——身上穿着粉色内衣，也是何等简单。简单之极。

===Original===
“It’s like the Arietta, don’t you think?” said Emily suddenly, “the Arietta of Op. 111.” And she hummed the first bars of the air. “Don’t you feel it’s like that?”

“What’s like that?”

“Everything,” said Emily. “Today, I mean. You and me. These gardens﻿—” And she went on humming.

Gumbril shook his head. “Too simple for me,” he said.

Emily laughed. “Ah, but then think how impossible it gets a little farther on.” She agitated her fingers wildly, as though she were trying to play the impossible passages. “It begins easily for the sake of poor imbeciles like me; but it goes on, it goes on, more and more fully and subtly and abstrusely and embracingly. But it’s still the same movement.”

===Chinese===
「这就像那支《小咏叹调》（Arietta，作品第111号，贝多芬第三十二钢琴奏鸣曲的末乐章），你说是不是？」艾米莉忽然说。她哼了哼那曲子的头几个小节。「你不觉得就是这样么？」

「什么就是这样？」

「一切。」艾米莉说。「我是说，今天。你和我。这些园子——」她继续哼下去。

冈布里尔摇了摇头。「对我来说太简单了。」他说。

艾米莉笑了。「啊，那你再想想，往下走一小段，它就变得多么地不可能。」她狂乱地抖动手指，仿佛正在弹那些弹不下来的乐句。「它为了我这样的可怜傻瓜起头，开得容易；可它往前走啊，往前走，越来越丰满，越来越精微，越来越深奥，越来越包罗万象。但它仍旧是同一个乐章。」

===Original===
The shadows stretched farther and farther across the lawns, and as the sun declined the level light picked out among the grasses innumerable stipplings of shadow; and in the paths, that had seemed under the more perpendicular rays as level as a table, a thousand little shadowy depressions and sun-touched mountains were now apparent. Gumbril looked at his watch.

“Good Lord!” he said, “we must fly.” He jumped up. “Quick, quick!”

“But why?”

“We shall be late.” He wouldn’t tell her for what. “Wait and see” was all that Emily could get out of him by her questioning. They hurried out of the gardens, and in spite of her protests he insisted on taking a taxi into town. “I have such a lot of unearned increment to get rid of,” he explained. The Patent Small-Clothes seemed at the moment remoter than the farthest stars.

===Chinese===
影子在草坪上越拉越远，日头西沉，平射的光在草丛间点染出无数细碎的阴影；那些在更陡直的光线下平整如桌面的园径，此刻显出千百处小小的阴影洼地和阳光抚亮的山丘。冈布里尔看了看表。

「老天！」他说，「我们得飞跑了。」他跳起身来。「快，快！」

「可为什么呀？」

「我们要迟到了。」去做什么，他不肯说。艾米莉百般盘问，只问出一句「等着瞧」。他们匆匆出了园子，不顾她的反对，他执意雇了辆出租汽车进城。「我有好大一笔不劳而获的增值（unearned increment，经济学术语，指非由劳作挣得的进项）要打发掉呢。」他解释道。此刻，专利充气裤似乎比最远的星辰还要遥远。

## XIII / XIII

===Original===
In spite of the taxi, in spite of the gobbled dinner, they were late. The concert had begun.

“Never mind,” said Gumbril. “We shall get in in time for the minuetto. It’s then that the fun really begins.”

“Sour grapes,” said Emily, putting her ear to the door. “It sounds to me simply too lovely.”

They stood outside, like beggars waiting abjectly at the doors of a banqueting-hall﻿—stood and listened to the snatches of music that came out tantalizingly from within. A rattle of clapping announced at last that the first movement was over; the doors were thrown open. Hungrily they rushed in. The Sclopis Quartet and a subsidiary viola were bowing from the platform. There was a chirrup of tuning, then preliminary silence. Sclopis nodded and moved his bow. The minuetto of Mozart’s G minor Quintet broke out, phrase after phrase, short and decisive, with every now and then a violent sforzando chord, startling in its harsh and sudden emphasis.

===Chinese===
尽管坐了出租汽车，尽管晚饭是一阵狼吞虎咽，他们还是迟到了。音乐会已经开始。

「不要紧，」冈布里尔说，「我们总还赶得上看小步舞曲。真正的乐子，到那时候才开场呢。」

「吃不着葡萄说葡萄酸。」艾米莉说着，把耳朵贴在门上。「在我听来，简直美极了。」

他们站在门外，像乞丐低声下气地守在宴会厅门口——站着，听音乐的片段一阵一阵从里面勾人地漏出来。一阵急雨似的掌声终于宣告第一乐章结束；大门敞开了。两人饿狼似的冲了进去。斯克洛皮斯四重奏团（Sclopis Quartet）和一位外请的中提琴手正在台上鞠躬。调弦声啁啾了一阵，随后是开演前的静默。斯克洛皮斯（Sclopis）点了点头，运起琴弓。莫扎特G小调五重奏的小步舞曲迸发出来，一句接一句，短促而决断，不时爆出一个狂暴的突强（sforzando）和弦，那刺耳而骤然的重击，惊心动魄。

===Original===
Minuetto﻿—all civilization, Mr. Mercaptan would have said, was implied in the delicious word, the delicate, pretty thing. Ladies and precious gentlemen, fresh from the wit and gallantry of Crebillon-haunted sofas, stepping gracefully to a pattern of airy notes. To this passion of one who cries out, to this obscure and angry argument with fate how would they, Gumbril wondered, how would they have tripped it?

How pure the passion, how unaffected, clear and without clot or pretension the unhappiness of that slow movement which followed! Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God. Pure and unsullied; pure and unmixed, unadulterated. “Not passionate, thank God; only sensual and sentimental.” In the name of earwig. Amen. Pure, pure. Worshippers have tried to rape the statues of the gods; the statuaries who made the images were generally to blame. And how deliciously, too, an artist can suffer! and, in the face of the whole Albert Hall, with what an effective gesture and grimace! But blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God. The instruments come together and part again. Long silver threads hang aerially over a murmur of waters; in the midst of muffled sobbing a cry. The fountains blow their architecture of slender pillars, and from basin to basin the waters fall; from basin to basin, and every fall makes somehow possible a higher leaping of the jet, and at the last fall the mounting column springs up into the sunlight, and from water the music has modulated up into a rainbow. Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God; they shall make God visible, too, to other eyes.

===Chinese===
小步舞曲——默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan）准会说，全部文明都涵蕴在这个可人的字眼里，涵蕴在这件精巧娇美的东西里。夫人们和娇贵的先生们，方从小克雷比永（Crébillon）阴魂萦绕的沙发上领教完机锋与风流，正优雅地踏着一段轻盈音符织成的图样举步。对这呼号者的激情，对这场与命运的晦暗而愤怒的争辩，他们会怎样跳——冈布里尔纳闷着——他们会把它踏成怎样的舞步呢？

那激情何其纯净；随后那慢乐章里的不幸，又是何等不假雕饰、澄澈，没有一点淤结或虚饰！清心的人有福了，因为他们必得见神（《马太福音》语）。纯而无玷；纯而不杂、不掺假。「谢天谢地，不是激情；不过是肉感与感伤罢了。」奉蠼螋之圣名。阿们。纯净，纯净。礼拜者曾试图奸污神祇的雕像；该怪罪的，往往是造像的雕塑师。艺术家又能受苦受得多么有滋有味！而且当着整座阿尔伯特音乐厅（Albert Hall）的面，摆出何等奏效的手势与鬼脸！可清心的人有福了，因为他们必得见神。乐器聚拢来，又分开。长长的银线悬在半空，底下是一脉流水的低语；在闷住的啜泣中央，一声呼喊。喷泉吹起它们细柱的建筑，水从一盆跌向一盆；一盆又一盆，每一次跌落，都不知怎地使水柱跳得更高成为可能；到最后一跌，那节节升腾的水柱跃入阳光，音乐也从水一路向上转调，调成了一道彩虹。清心的人有福了，因为他们必得见神；他们也必让神向别的眼睛显为可见。

===Original===
Blood beats in the ears. Beat, beat, beat. A slow drum in the darkness, beating in the ears of one who lies wakeful with fever, with the sickness of too much misery. It beats unceasingly, in the ears, in the mind itself. Body and mind are indivisible, and in the spirit blood painfully throbs. Sad thoughts droop through the mind. A small, pure light comes swaying down through the darkness, comes to rest, resigning itself to the obscurity of its misfortune. There is resignation, but blood still beats in the ears. Blood still painfully beats, though the mind has acquiesced. And then, suddenly, the mind exerts itself, throws off the fever of too much suffering and laughing, commands the body to dance. The introduction to the last movement comes to its suspended, throbbing close. There is an instant of expectation, and then, with a series of mounting trochees and a downward hurrying, step after tiny step, in triple time, the dance begins. Irrelevant, irreverent, out of key with all that has gone before. But man’s greatest strength lies in his capacity for irrelevance. In the midst of pestilences, wars and famines, he builds cathedrals; and a slave, he can think the irrelevant and unsuitable thoughts of a free man. The spirit is slave to fever and beating blood, at the mercy of an obscure and tyrannous misfortune. But irrelevantly, it elects to dance in triple measure﻿—a mounting skip, a patter of descending feet.

The G minor Quintet is at an end; the applause rattles out loudly. Enthusiasts stand up and cry bravo. And the five men on the platform rise and bow their acknowledgments. Great Sclopis himself receives his share of the plaudits with a weary condescension; weary are his poached eyes, weary his disillusioned smile. It is only his due, he knows; but he has had so much clapping, so many lovely women. He has a Roman nose, a colossal brow and, though the tawny musical mane does much to conceal the fact, no back to his head. Garofalo, the second fiddle, is black, beady-eyed and potbellied. The convex reflections of the electroliers slide back and forth over his polished bald head, as he bends, again, again, in little military salutes. Peperkoek, two metres high, bows with a sinuous politeness. His face, his hair are all of the same greyish buff colour; he does not smile, his appearance is monolithic and grim. Not so exuberant Knoedler, who sweats and smiles and embraces his cello send lays his hand to his heart and bows almost to the ground as though all this hullabaloo were directed only at him. As for poor little Mr. Jenkins, the subsidiary viola, he has slid away into the background, and feeling that this is really the Sclopis’s show and that he, a mere intruder, has no right to any of these demonstrations, he hardly bows at all, but only smiles, vaguely and nervously, and from time to time makes a little spasmodic twitch to show that he isn’t really ungrateful or haughty, as you might think, but that he feels in the circumstances﻿—the position is a little embarrassing﻿—it is hard to explain.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
血在耳中搏动。搏动，搏动，搏动。黑暗里一面缓鼓，敲在一个因热病、因愁苦过多而恶心、醒卧不起的人的耳中。它敲个不停，在耳中，在心灵本身里。身与心不可分，在精神里，血痛苦地悸动。悲哀的念头一株株垂落过心头。一小粒纯净的光，摇摇曳曳地降下黑暗，落定了，听任自己归于不幸的昏暗。有了顺从，可血仍在耳中搏动。心已经默许，血仍在痛苦地搏动。而后，突然，心灵奋起，笑着抖落过多苦难的热病，命令身体起舞。末乐章的引子走到它悬置的、悸动的收束。有一瞬的期待，随即，一串攀升的扬抑格音步，一阵下行的小碎步，一步，又一步，踏着三拍子，舞蹈开始了。全然不相干，全然不敬，与此前的一切统统不合调。可人最伟大的力量，正在于这种不相干的本领。在瘟疫、战争与饥荒之中，他建造大教堂；身为奴隶，他却能怀有自由人的那种不相干的、不合时宜的思想。精神是热病与搏动之血的奴隶，任凭一种晦暗而暴虐的不幸摆布。可它偏偏不相干地，选定以三拍子起舞——一记上扬的蹦跳，一串下行嗒嗒的足音。

G小调五重奏到了尽头；掌声哗啦啦地响成一片。狂热的听众站起身来，高呼「好啊」。台上五人起身鞠躬答谢。伟大的斯克洛皮斯本人领受他那一份喝彩，带着一种倦怠的屈尊；他那双煮蛋似的眼睛是倦的，他那个幻灭的微笑也是倦的。他知道，这本是分所应得；可他领受过的鼓掌太多了，可爱的女人也太多了。他有一副罗马鼻，一个魁伟的额头，而且——尽管那片黄褐色的音乐鬃毛极力遮掩这一事实——后脑勺是没有的。第二小提琴加罗法洛（Garofalo）黑黑的，一双圆亮如豆的小眼睛，肚子滚圆。枝形电灯的凸面反光在他擦得锃亮的秃头上来回滑过，他一躬，又一躬，行着小小的军礼。佩珀库克（Peperkoek），两米高的个子，躬身行礼，礼数蜿蜒。他的脸、他的头发全是同一种灰扑扑的土黄色；他不笑，他的模样如整块巨石，严峻逼人。克诺德勒（Knoedler）可没这么含蓄，他流着汗，笑着，一把搂住他的大提琴，又把手按上心口，躬身几乎要躬到地上，仿佛这一片喧闹全是冲着他一个人来的。至于可怜的小个子詹金斯先生（Mr. Jenkins），那位外请中提琴手，他早已溜进了后排；他觉得这终究是斯克洛皮斯们的场子，自己不过是个闯入者，这一切热烈的表示都没他的份儿，于是他几乎全不鞠躬，只管微笑，笑得惘然又不安，还不时小小地抽搐一下，好表明他并非真的不知感激或目中无人——兴许你会那么想——而是他觉得，处此境地——这处境有点难为情——一时说不清楚。……

===Original===
“Strange,” said Gumbril, “to think that those ridiculous creatures could have produced what we’ve just been hearing.”

The poached eye of Sclopis lighted on Emily, flushed and ardently applauding. He gave her, all to herself, a weary smile. He would have a letter, he guessed, tomorrow morning signed “Your little Admirer in the Third Row.” She looked a choice little piece. He smiled again to encourage her. Emily, alas! had not even noticed. She was applauding the music.

“Did you enjoy it?” he asked, as they stepped out into a deserted Bond Street.

“Did I﻿ ﻿… ?” Emily laughed expressively. “No, I didn’t enjoy,” she said. “Enjoy isn’t the word. You enjoy eating ices. It made me happy. It’s unhappy music, but it made me happy.”

Gumbril hailed a cab and gave the address of his rooms in Great Russell Street. “Happy,” he repeated, as they sat there side by side in the darkness. He, too, was happy.

===Chinese===
「奇怪，」冈布里尔说，「想想看，那几个荒唐的家伙，竟能造出我们方才听到的东西来。」

斯克洛皮斯那只煮蛋似的眼睛落到了艾米莉身上——她红着脸，正热切地鼓着掌。他单单冲她一个人，赏了一个倦怠的微笑。他猜，明天早上他会收到一封信，落款是「第三排你的小仰慕者」。她看着是件上选的小尤物。他又笑了笑，以资鼓励。可惜啊，艾米莉根本不曾留意。她鼓掌，是为那音乐。

「你喜欢么？」两人踏进空无一人的邦德街（Bond Street）时，他问。

「我么……？」艾米莉意味深长地笑了。「不，我不是『喜欢』，」她说，「『喜欢』不是那个字眼。吃冰淇淋才谈得上喜欢。它让我幸福。这是不幸的音乐，可它让我幸福。」

冈布里尔招手叫了辆出租汽车，报出他大拉塞尔街（Great Russell Street）寓所的地址。「幸福，」黑暗里两人并肩坐着，他又念了一遍这个词。他也是幸福的。

===Original===
“Where are we going?” she asked.

“To my rooms,” said Gumbril, “we shall be quiet there.” He was afraid she might object to going there﻿—after yesterday. But she made no comment.

“Some people think that it’s only possible to be happy if one makes a noise,” she said, after a pause. “I find it’s too delicate and melancholy for noise. Being happy is rather melancholy﻿—like the most beautiful landscape, like those trees and the grass and the clouds and the sunshine today.”

“From the outside,” said Gumbril, “it even looks rather dull.” They stumbled up the dark staircase to his rooms. Gumbril lit a pair of candles and put the kettle on the gas ring. They sat together on the divan sipping tea. In the rich, soft light of the candles she looked different, more beautiful. The silk of her dress seemed wonderfully rich and glossy, like the petals of a tulip, and on her face, on her bare arms and neck the light seemed to spread an impalpable bright bloom. On the wall behind them, their shadows ran up towards the ceiling, enormous and profoundly black.

===Chinese===
「我们去哪儿？」她问。

「去我住的地方，」冈布里尔说，「那儿清静。」他生怕她不肯去——想想昨天。可她一声没吭。

「有人以为，非得闹哄哄的才幸福得起来，」她顿了顿，说。「我觉得幸福这东西太精致、太忧郁，闹不得。幸福是相当忧郁的——像最美的风景，像今天那些树、那草、那云和那阳光。」

「从外面看，」冈布里尔说，「它甚至显得怪沉闷的。」两人跌跌撞撞摸上黑洞洞的楼梯，进了他的房间。冈布里尔点起一对蜡烛，把水壶坐上煤气炉圈。两人一同坐在矮榻上啜茶。烛光丰厚而柔和，她看上去换了个人，更美了。她连衣裙的绸面显得出奇地丰润流光，像郁金香的花瓣；烛光在她的脸上、在她裸着的臂膀和颈上，仿佛匀开一层摸不着的明亮粉晕。两人背后的墙上，他们的影子一路蹿向天花板，硕大无朋，黑得深不见底。

===Original===
“How unreal it is,” Gumbril whispered. “Not true. This remote secret room. These lights and shadows out of another time. And you out of nowhere and I, out of a past utterly remote from yours, sitting together here, together﻿—and being happy. That’s the strangest thing of all. Being quite senselessly happy. It’s unreal, unreal.”

“But why,” said Emily, “why? It’s here and happening now. It *is* real.”

“It all might vanish, at any moment,” he said.

Emily smiled rather sadly. “It’ll vanish in due time,” she said. “Quite naturally, not by magic; it’ll vanish the way everything else vanishes and changes. But it’s here now.”

===Chinese===
「这一切多不真实啊，」冈布里尔低声说。「不是真的。这间僻远隐秘的屋子。这些光影，来自另一个时代。你来自子虚乌有，我来自一段同你的往昔全不相干的往昔，却在这里同坐，一起——还幸福着。这是最最奇怪的事。全然没来由地幸福着。不真实，不真实。」

「可是为什么，」艾米莉说，「为什么？它就在这儿，正在发生。它*就是*真实的。」

「这一切随时都可能消失。」他说。

艾米莉笑得有些怅然。「到时候它自然会消失。」她说。「自然而然地，不靠什么魔法；它会消失，就像旁的一切事物消失、变迁一样。可它现在在这儿。」

===Original===
They gave themselves up to the enchantment. The candles burned, two shining eyes of flame, without a wink, minute after minute. But for them there were no longer any minutes. Emily leaned against him, her body held in the crook of his arm, her head resting on his shoulder. He caressed his cheek against her hair; sometimes, very gently, he kissed her forehead or her closed eyes.

“If I had known you years ago﻿ ﻿…” she sighed. “But I was a silly little idiot then. I shouldn’t have noticed any difference between you and anybody else.”

“I shall be very jealous,” Emily spoke again after another timeless silence. “There must never be anybody else, never the shadow of anybody else.”

“There never will be anybody else,” said Gumbril.

Emily smiled and opened her eyes, looked up at him. “Ah, not here,” she said, “not in this real unreal room. Not during this eternity. But there will be other rooms just as real as this.”

“Not so real, not so real.” He bent his face towards hers. She closed her eyes again, and the lids fluttered with a sudden tremulous movement at the touch of his light kiss.

===Chinese===
他们把自己交托给了这魔力。蜡烛燃着，两只亮闪闪的火焰的眼睛，一眨不眨，一分钟又一分钟。可对他们来说，早已不再有什么分钟。艾米莉偎着他，身子收在他臂弯里，头靠在他肩上。他拿脸颊偎着她的发丝摩挲；有时，极轻极轻地，吻她的额头，或她阖着的眼睛。

「要是多年前就认识你……」她叹道。「可那时候我是个傻里傻气的小呆子。你同旁人有什么两样，我是断断看不出来的。」

「我会嫉妒得厉害，」又一段没有时间的沉默之后，艾米莉开口了。「决不可以有别人，连别人的影子也不可以有。」

「永远不会有别人。」冈布里尔说。

艾米莉笑了，睁开眼睛，仰头望他。「啊，不在这儿，」她说，「不在这个真实又不真实的房间里。不在这一场永恒里。可是会有别的房间，同这一间一样真实。」

「不会那么真实的，不会。」他俯下脸去凑近她。她又阖上眼，睫毛在他轻轻一吻的触碰下，蓦地一阵颤动。

===Original===
For them there were no more minutes. But time passed, time passed flowing in a dark stream, stanchlessly, as though from some profound mysterious wound in the world’s side, bleeding, bleeding forever. One of the candles had burned down to the socket and the long, smoky flame wavered unsteadily. The flickering light troubled their eyes; the shadows twitched and stirred uneasily. Emily looked up at him.

“What’s the time?” she said.

Gumbril looked at his watch. It was nearly one o’clock. “Too late for you to get back,” he said.

“Too late?” Emily sat-up. Ah, the enchantment was breaking, was giving way, like a film of ice beneath a weight, like a web before a thrust of the wind. They looked at one another. “What shall I do?” she asked.

“You could sleep here,” Gumbril answered in a voice that came from a long way away.

She sat for a long time in silence, looking through half-closed eyes at the expiring candle flame. Gumbril watched her in an agony of suspense. Was the ice to be broken, the web-work finally and forever torn? The enchantment could still be prolonged, the eternity renewed. He felt his heart beating in his breast; he held his breath. It would be terrible if she were to go now, it would be a kind of death. The flame of the candle flickered more violently, leaping up in a thin, long, smoky flare, sinking again almost to darkness. Emily got up and blew out the candle. The other still burned calmly and steadily.

===Chinese===
对他们来说，再没有分钟了。可时间在流过，时间流过去，像一道黑色的溪流，流个不住，仿佛从世界胁下一道深邃神秘的伤口里，永远、永远地淌着血。一支蜡烛已燃到烛窝，长长的、冒着烟的火焰摇曳不定。闪烁的光扰得他们眼睛发涩；影子抽搐着，不安地骚动。艾米莉抬起头来看他。

「几点了？」她说。

冈布里尔看了看表。快一点钟了。「你赶不回去了。」他说。

「太晚了？」艾米莉坐了起来。啊，魔力正在碎裂，正在塌陷，像重物底下的一层薄冰，像疾风一冲之下的一张蛛网。两人面面相觑。「我该怎么办呢？」她问。

「你可以睡在这儿。」冈布里尔回答，声音像从很远很远的地方传来。

她久久地默默坐着，从半阖的眼缝里望着奄奄将尽的烛焰。冈布里尔在煎熬的悬心里望着她。冰就要破么？蛛网就要最终、永远地撕破么？魔力仍可延续，永恒仍可再续。他觉出心在胸膛里跳；他屏住了呼吸。她若此刻走了，那会是可怕的，那会是某种死亡。烛焰晃动得愈猛了，蹿起一道细长冒烟的火光，又沉下去，几乎沉入黑暗。艾米莉起身吹灭了蜡烛。另一支还燃得安然、平稳。

===Original===
“May I stay?” she asked. “Will you allow me?”

He understood the meaning of her question, and nodded. “Of course,” he said.

“Of course? Is it as much of course as all that?”

“When I say so.” He smiled at her. The eternity had been renewed, the enchantment prolonged. There was no need to think of anything now but the moment. The past was forgotten, the future abolished. There was only this secret room and the candlelight and the unreal, impossible happiness of being two. Now that this peril of a disenchantment had been averted, it would last forever. He got up from the couch, crossed the room, he took her hands and kissed them.

===Chinese===
「我可以留下么？」她问。「你允许么？」

他懂了她这一问的意思，点了点头。「当然。」他说。

「当然？天下有这么『当然』的事么？」

「我说『当然』，那便有这么『当然』。」他朝她微笑。永恒已经续上，魔力已经延长。此刻除了当下，什么都不必想了。过去被遗忘了，未来被废除了。只剩下这间秘密的屋子、这烛光、和两个人相守的那种不真实的、不可能的幸福。如今这场破魔之险既已躲过，它将地久天长。他从榻上起来，穿过房间，捧起她的双手，吻了吻。

===Original===
“Shall we sleep now?” she asked.

Gumbril nodded.

“Do you mind if I blow out the light?” And without waiting for his answer, Emily turned, gave a puff, and the room was in darkness. He heard the rustling of her undressing. Hastily he stripped off his own clothes, pulled back the coverlet from the divan. The bed was made and ready; he opened it and slipped between the sheets. A dim greenish light from the gas lamp in the street below came up between the parted curtains illuminating faintly the farther end of the room. Against this tempered darkness he could see her, silhouetted, standing quite still, as if hesitating on some invisible brink.

“Emily,” he whispered.

“I’m coming,” Emily answered. She stood there, unmoving, a few seconds longer, then overstepped the brink. She came silently across the room, and sat down on the edge of the low couch. Gumbril lay perfectly still, without speaking, waiting in the enchanted timeless darkness. Emily lifted her knees, slid her feet in under the sheet, then stretched herself out beside him, her body, in the narrow bed, touching his. Gumbril felt that she was trembling; trembling, a sharp involuntary start, a little shudder, another start.

“You’re cold,” he said, and slipping one arm beneath her shoulders he drew her, limp and unresisting, towards him. She lay there, pressed against him. Gradually the trembling ceased. Quite still, quite still in the calm of the enchantment. The past is forgotten, the future abolished; there is only this dark and everlasting moment. A drugged and intoxicated stupor of happiness possessed his spirit; a numbness, warm and delicious, lay upon him. And yet through the stupor he knew with a dreadful anxious certainty that the end would soon be there. Like a man on the night before his execution, he looked forward through the endless present; he foresaw the end of his eternity. And after? Everything was uncertain and unsafe.

===Chinese===
「我们现在睡么？」她问。

冈布里尔点了点头。

「我把灯吹灭，你不介意吧？」不等他回答，艾米莉转过身，噗地一吹，房间便沉入黑暗。他听见她宽衣解带的窸窣。他慌忙剥去自己的衣裳，掀开矮榻上的罩单。床已铺停当；他掀开被角，钻进了被单之间。楼下街上的煤气灯透上来一线暗绿的光，从分开的窗帘间挤进来，幽幽照亮房间的远端。衬着这片调和了的黑暗，他望得见她，一个剪影，一动不动地站着，仿佛在一道看不见的崖边前踌躇。

「艾米莉。」他低唤。

「我来了。」艾米莉应道。她又原地立了几秒，纹丝不动，随后跨过了那道崖边。她悄没声地穿过房间，在矮榻沿上坐下。冈布里尔纹丝不动地躺着，一言不发，在着了魔的、没有时间的黑暗里等着。艾米莉抬起双膝，把脚伸进被单底下，然后在他身边舒展开身子，在这张窄床上，她的身子贴着他的。冈布里尔觉出她在发抖；发着抖，一阵急促的不由自主的惊跳，一阵小小的寒噤，又一阵惊跳。

「你冷。」他说着，把一条手臂垫到她肩下，把她——软软的、全不抗拒的——拉近自己。她躺在那里，紧贴着他。颤抖渐渐止了。静静的，静静的，在魔力的安宁里。过去已被遗忘，未来已被废除；只有这个黑暗的、永驻的当下。一种如服麻醉药、如饮醇酒的幸福迷沉攫住了他的精神；一层麻木，温暖而甘美，覆在他身上。然而透过这迷沉，他以一种可怖而焦灼的确信知道：结局很快就要到了。像个临刑前夜的人，他穿过这无尽的现在向前望去；他预见了他永恒的尽头。那之后呢？一切都没个准，一切都不稳靠。

===Original===
Very gently, he began caressing her shoulder, her long slender arm, drawing his fingertips lightly and slowly over her smooth skin; slowly from her neck, over her shoulder, lingeringly round the elbow to her hand. Again, again; he was learning her arm. The form of it was part of the knowledge, now, of his fingertips; his fingers knew it as they knew a piece of music, as they knew Mozart’s Twelfth Sonata, for example. And the themes that crowd so quickly one after another at the beginning of the first movement played themselves serially, glitteringly in his mind; they became a part of the enchantment.

Through the silk of her shift he learned her curving side, her smooth straight back and the ridge of her spine. He stretched down, touched her feet, her knees. Under the smock he learned her warm body, lightly, slowly caressing. He knew her, his fingers, he felt, could build her up, a warm and curving statue in the darkness. He did not desire her; to desire would have been to break the enchantment. He let himself sink deeper and deeper into his dark stupor of happiness. She was asleep in his arms; and soon he too was asleep.

===Chinese===
极轻柔地，他开始抚爱她的肩，她那细长的手臂，指尖轻轻地、缓缓地滑过她平滑的肌肤；从她的颈边，缓缓地，越过肩头，恋恋地绕过肘弯，直到她的手。一遍，又一遍；他在研习她的手臂。它的形状，如今已是他的指尖知识的一部分；他的手指认识它，就像认识一支乐曲，比如，就像认识莫扎特的第十二奏鸣曲。第一乐章开头那些一个紧追着一个的主题，此刻在他心里鱼贯地、粼粼地自行演奏起来；它们成了魔力的一部分。

隔着衬裙的绸子，他研习了她弯曲的体侧、她平滑笔直的背和脊梁的细棱。他向下伸去，触到她的脚、她的膝。在衬裙底下，他研习她温暖的身子，轻轻地、缓缓地爱抚。他认识了她；他的手指，他觉得，能把她整个搭起来，一尊黑暗中温暖而宛曲的雕像。他并不欲求她；欲求便会破了这魔力。他任由自己愈沉愈深，沉进他那黑暗的幸福迷沉里。她在他怀抱里睡着了；不多时，他也睡着了。

## XIV / XIV

===Original===

Mrs. Viveash descended the steps into King Street, and standing there on the pavement looked dubiously first to the right and then to the left. Little and loud, the taxis rolled by on their white wheels, the long-snouted limousines passed with a sigh. The air smelt of watered dust, tempered in Mrs. Viveash’s immediate neighbourhood by those memories of Italian jasmines which were her perfume. On the opposite pavement, in the shade, two young men, looking very conscious of their grey top-hats, marched gravely along.

Life, Mrs. Viveash thought, looked a little dim this morning, in spite of the fine weather. She glanced at her watch; it was one o’clock. Soon one would have to eat some lunch. But where, and with whom? Mrs. Viveash had no engagements. All the world was before her, she was absolutely free, all day long. Yesterday, when she declined all those pressing invitations, the prospect had seemed delightful. Liberty, no complications, no contacts; a pre-Adamite empty world to do what she liked in.

But today, when it came to the point, she hated her liberty. To come out like this at one o’clock into a vacuum﻿—it was absurd, it was appalling. The prospect of immeasurable boredom opened before her. Steppes after steppes of ennui, horizon beyond horizon, forever the same. She looked again to the right and again to the left. Finally she decided to go to the left. Slowly, walking along her private knife-edge between her personal abysses, she walked towards the left. She remembered suddenly one shining day like this in the summer of 1917, when she had walked along this same street, slowly, like this, on the sunny side, with Tony Lamb. All that day, that night, it had been one long goodbye. He was going back the next morning. Less than a week later he was dead. Never again, never again: there had been a time when she could make herself cry, simply by saying those two words once or twice, under her breath. Never again, never again. She repeated them softly now. But she felt no tears behind her eyes. Grief doesn’t kill, love doesn’t kill; but time kills everything, kills desire, kills sorrow, kills in the end the mind that feels them; wrinkles and softens the body while it still lives, rots it like a medlar, kills it too at last. Never again, never again. Instead of crying, she laughed, laughed aloud. The pigeon-breasted old gentleman who had just passed her, twirling between his finger and thumb the ends of a white military moustache, turned round startled. Could she be laughing at him?

“Never again,” murmured Mrs. Viveash.

===Chinese===

维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash）走下台阶，来到国王街（King Street），站在人行道上，将信将疑地先望望右边，再望望左边。出租车小小的、吵吵的，踏着白轮子滚过去；长鼻子的豪华轿车叹着气驶过。空气里透着洒过水的尘土的气味，到了维维什夫人贴身周围，却被她的香水——意大利茉莉花的那些回忆——调和得温柔了些。对面人行道上，背荫里，两个年轻人一本正经地行进着，对自己的灰色大礼帽自觉得很。

虽说天气晴好，维维什夫人却觉得，人生在今天早晨看上去有几分黯淡。她瞥了一眼表；一点钟了。很快，总得吃点儿午饭了。可是去哪儿吃，跟谁吃呢？维维什夫人没有任何约会。整个世界摊在她面前，她一整天绝对自由。昨天，当她回绝了那一番番盛情相邀的时候，这前景还显得煞是美妙。自由，没有纠葛，没有交往；一个亚当以前的空荡荡世界，随她高兴做什么。

可是今天，事到临头，她恨透了她的自由。一点钟就这样走出来，走进一片真空——荒唐，骇人。无可计量的烦闷在她面前铺展开来。一片倦怠的草原接着一片，一重天际之外又一重，永远一色。她又望望右边，又望望左边。最后她决定往左去。她慢慢走着，走在这条属于她自己的刀刃上，两侧是她各自的深渊。她忽然记起 1917 年夏天一个同样晴亮的日子；那天她沿着这条街，就这样慢慢地、走在向阳的一侧，身边是托尼·兰姆（Tony Lamb）。那一整个白天，那一夜，是一场长长的告别。他第二天一早就要回去。不出一个星期，他死了。再也不会有了，再也不会有了：曾有一段时候，只消压着嗓子把那两个词念上一两遍，她就能让自己哭出来。再也不会有了，再也不会有了。她此刻又轻轻地念着它们。可她眼里觉不出泪水来。悲伤杀不了人，爱情也杀不了人；倒是时间杀得死一切：杀死欲望，杀死哀伤，到头来杀死那颗感受着它们的心；它在身体还活着时便叫它起皱、发软，像一枚欧楂似的把它沤烂，最后连身体也一并杀死。再也不会有了，再也不会有了。她非但没有哭，反而笑了，放声笑了。那位刚好与她擦肩而过的鸡胸老绅士，正用拇指和食指捻着两撇白色军人髭须的梢子，吃了一惊，回过头来。她莫非是在笑他？

「再也不会有了。」维维什夫人喃喃道。

===Original===

“I beg your pardon?” queried the martial gentleman, in a rich, port-winey, cigary voice.

Mrs. Viveash looked at him with such wide-eyed astonishment that the old gentleman was quite taken aback. “A thousand apologies, dear lady. Thought you were addressing﻿ ﻿… H’m, ah’m.” He replaced his hat, squared his shoulders and went off smartly, left, right, bearing preciously before him his pigeon-breast. Poor thing, he thought, poor young thing. Talking to herself. Must be cracked, must be off her head. Or perhaps she took drugs. That was more likely: that was much more likely. Most of them did nowadays. Vicious young women. Lesbians, drug-fiends, nymphomaniacs, dipsos﻿—thoroughly vicious, nowadays, thoroughly vicious. He arrived at his club in an excellent temper.

Never again, never, never again. Mrs. Viveash would have liked to be able to cry.

St. James’s Square opened before her. Romantically under its trees the statue pranced. The trees gave her an idea: she might go down into the country for the afternoon, take a cab and drive out, out, goodness only knew where! To the top of a hill somewhere. Box Hill, Leith Hill, Holmbury Hill, Ivinghoe Beacon﻿—any hill where one could sit and look out over plains. One might do worse than that with one’s liberty.

But not much worse, she reflected.

===Chinese===

「您说什么？」那位尚武的绅士问道，嗓音醇厚，透着波特酒与雪茄的味道。

维维什夫人睁大眼睛，那样惊愕地望着他，弄得老绅士着慌了。「万分抱歉，亲爱的夫人。还以为您是在同我说话……呃哼，啊——呣。」他把帽子戴正，挺起肩膀，利利索索地走开了，左，右，把他那宝贝鸡胸端端正正顶在身前。可怜的人儿，他想，可怜的年轻人儿。自言自语。准是疯了，准是脑子出了毛病。要不就是吸毒。那更像是：那可太像是了。如今她们大多数都来这一套。堕落的年轻女人。女同性恋、瘾君子、慕男狂、酒鬼——彻头彻尾地堕落，如今这年头，彻头彻尾。他心绪极佳地到了俱乐部。

再也不会有了，再也不会，再也不会有了。维维什夫人巴不得自己还能哭得出来。

圣詹姆斯广场（St. James's Square）在她面前展开。树影之下，雕像扬蹄欲跃，一派浪漫。这些树给了她一个主意：下午不妨下乡去，雇一辆车，往外开，往外开，天晓得开到哪里去！开到哪座山山顶上去。博克斯山（Box Hill）、莱斯山（Leith Hill）、霍尔姆伯里山（Holmbury Hill）、伊夫尼霍烽火山（Ivinghoe Beacon）——随便哪座山，只要能让人坐下眺望平野。拿一个人的自由这么打发，也算不得最糟。

可也糟不到哪儿去，她想。

===Original===

Mrs. Viveash had turned up towards the northern side of the square and was almost at its northwestern corner when, with a thrill of genuine delight, with a sense of the most profound relief she saw a familiar figure, running down the steps of the London Library.

“Theodore!” she hallooed faintly but penetratingly, from her inward deathbed. “Gumbril!” She waved her parasol.

Gumbril halted, looked round, came smiling to meet her. “How delightful,” he said, “but how unfortunate.”

“Why unfortunate?” asked Mrs. Viveash. “Am I of evil omen?”

“Unfortunate,” Gumbril explained, “because I’ve got to catch a train and can’t profit by this meeting.”

“Ah no, Theodore,” said Mrs. Viveash, “you’re not going to catch a train. You’re going to come and lunch with me. Providence has decreed it. You can’t say no to Providence.”

===Chinese===

维维什夫人已经折向广场北面，差不多走到西北角了，这时她看见一个熟悉的身影正跑下伦敦图书馆（London Library）的台阶——一阵真真切切的欢喜穿过她全身，一种如释重负的至深之感。

「西奥多！」她喊道，声音轻，却穿透人心，是从她内心深处的临终之榻上喊出来的。「冈布里尔！」她挥着她的阳伞。

冈布里尔停住脚，回过头，微笑着迎上来。「真叫人高兴，」他说，「可惜又这么不巧。」

「怎么个不巧法？」维维什夫人问。「我是个凶兆么？」

「不巧的是，」冈布里尔解释道，「我得赶一趟火车，没法消受这次相遇。」

「啊，不，西奥多，」维维什夫人说，「你不用去赶什么火车。你要来跟我一起吃午饭。上天已有此安排。你不能对上天说不。」

===Original===

“I must,” Gumbril shook his head. “I’ve said yes to somebody else.”

“To whom?”

“Ah!” said Gumbril, with a coy and saucy mysteriousness.

“And where are you going in your famous train?”

“Ah again,” Gumbril answered.

“How intolerably tiresome and silly you are!” Mrs. Viveash declared. “One would think you were a sixteen-year-old schoolboy going out for his first assignation with a shop girl. At your age, Gumbril!” She shook her head, smiled agonizingly and with contempt. “Who is she? What sordid pickup?”

===Chinese===

「我必须去。」冈布里尔摇了摇头。「我已经答应了别人。」

「答应了谁？」

「啊！」冈布里尔说，一副忸怩又俏皮的卖关子相。

「那你坐着你那班鼎鼎大名的火车，要去哪儿？」

「又是啊。」冈布里尔答道。

「你真是烦死人了，蠢死了！」维维什夫人宣称。「人家还当你是十六岁的男学生，头一回溜出去跟商铺女郎幽会呢。都你这把年纪了，冈布里尔！」她摇着头，苦不堪言而又满含轻蔑地笑了笑。「她是谁？搭上的什么野货，那么见不得人？」

===Original===

“Not sordid in the least,” protested Gumbril.

“But decidedly a pickup. Eh?” A banana-skin was lying, like a bedraggled starfish, in the gutter, just in front of where they were standing. Mrs. Viveash stepped forward and with the point of her parasol lifted it carefully up and offered it to her companion.

“*Merci*,” Gumbril bowed.

She tossed the skin back again into the gutter. “In any case,” she said, “the young lady can wait while we have luncheon.”

Gumbril shook his head. “I’ve made the arrangement,” he said. Emily’s letter was in his pocket. She had taken the loveliest cottage just out of Robertsbridge, in Sussex. Ah, but the loveliest imaginable. For the whole summer. He could come and see her there. He had telegraphed that he would come today, this afternoon, by the two o’clock from Charing Cross.

===Chinese===

「丝毫不见不得人。」冈布里尔抗议道。

「可分明是搭上的。嗯？」一只香蕉皮，像一只湿塌塌的海星，正躺在他俩所站处前方的阴沟里。维维什夫人上前一步，用阳伞尖把它仔仔细细挑起来，递给她的同伴。

「*Merci*（谢谢）。」冈布里尔躬身一礼。

她把香蕉皮又甩回阴沟里。「不管怎么说，」她道，「那位小姐等一等无妨，我们先吃午饭。」

冈布里尔摇了摇头。「我已经定下了。」他说。艾米莉（Emily）的信就在他口袋里。她在萨塞克斯（Sussex）境内、罗伯茨布里奇（Robertsbridge）城外，租下了一幢可爱至极的小屋。啊，简直是可爱得无以复加。租了一整个夏天。他可以到那儿去看她。他已经拍去电报，说今天就到，今天下午，乘两点钟查令十字（Charing Cross）开出的那班车。

===Original===

Mrs. Viveash took him by the elbow. “Come along,” she said. “There’s a post office in that passage going from Jermyn Street to Piccadilly. You can wire from there your infinite regrets. These things always improve with a little keeping. There will be raptures when you *do* go tomorrow.”

Gumbril allowed himself to be led along. “What an insufferable woman you are,” he said, laughing.

“Instead of being grateful to me for asking you to luncheon!”

“Oh, I am grateful,” said Gumbril. “And astonished.”

He looked at her. Mrs. Viveash smiled and fixed him for a moment with her pale, untroubled eyes.﻿ ﻿… She said nothing.

===Chinese===

维维什夫人挽住他的胳膊肘。「走吧。」她说。「杰明街（Jermyn Street）通往皮卡迪利的那条过道里有家邮局。你可以从那儿拍去你无穷无尽的歉意。这种事搁上一搁，反倒更出滋味。等你明天当真去了，自有狂喜候着你。」

冈布里尔由着她把自己领走。「你可真是个叫人吃不消的女人。」他笑着说。

「人家请你吃午饭，你不道谢也就罢了，倒说这个！」

「哦，我是感激的。」冈布里尔说。「也是惊诧的。」

他望着她。维维什夫人微笑着，用她那双苍白、无波无澜的眼睛定定看了他片刻。……她什么也没说。

===Original===

“Still,” Gumbril went on, “I must be at Charing Cross by two, you know.”

“But we’re lunching at Verrey’s.”

Gumbril shook his head.

They were at the corner of Jermyn Street. Mrs. Viveash halted and delivered her ultimatum, the more impressive for being spoken in that expiring voice of one who says *in articulo* the final and supremely important things. “We lunch at Verrey’s, Theodore, or I shall never, never speak to you again.”

===Chinese===

「不过，」冈布里尔接着说，「你知道，两点钟以前我得赶到查令十字。」

「可我们要在韦雷餐厅（Verrey's）吃午饭呀。」

冈布里尔摇了摇头。

两人走到杰明街口。维维什夫人站住，发布了她的最后通牒——用那种 *in articulo*（弥留之际）之人交代身后至要大事的垂危嗓音说出，愈发郑重有力。「我们在韦雷吃午饭，西奥多，不然我就永远、永远不再同你说话。」

===Original===

“But be reasonable, Myra,” he implored. If only he’d told her that he had a business appointment.﻿ ﻿… Imbecile, to have dropped those stupid hints﻿—in that tone!

“I prefer not to be,” said Mrs. Viveash.

Gumbril made a gesture of despair and was silent. He thought of Emily in her native quiet among the flowers; in a cottage altogether too cottagey, with honeysuckles and red ramblers and hollyhocks﻿—though, on second thoughts, none of them would be blooming yet, would they?﻿—happily, in white muslin, extracting from the cottage piano the easier sections of the Arietta. A little absurd, perhaps, when you considered her like that; but exquisite, but adorable, but pure of heart and flawless in her bright pellucid integrity, complete as a crystal in its faceted perfection. She would be waiting for him, expecting him; and they would walk through the twiddly lanes﻿—or perhaps there would be a governess cart for hire, with a fat pony like a tub on legs to pull it﻿—they would look for flowers in the woods and perhaps he would still remember what sort of noise a whitethroat makes; or even if he didn’t remember, he could always magisterially say he did. “That’s a whitethroat, Emily. Do you hear? The one that goes ‘Tweedly, weedly, weedledy dee.’ ”

===Chinese===

「可是讲点道理吧，迈拉（Myra）。」他恳求道。他要是只告诉她自己有一桩生意上的约会，就好了……蠢材，竟丢下那些愚蠢的暗示——还用那种腔调！

「我情愿不讲道理。」维维什夫人说。

冈布里尔做了个绝望的手势，沉默下来。他想着艾米莉，在她天生的娴静里，在花丛之间；在一座小屋味儿未免太足的小屋里，有金银花、攀缘的红蔷薇和蜀葵——不过转念一想，这些眼下都还没开呢，对不对？——她快快活活地，穿一身白色细纱衣裙，从小立式钢琴上抠出《小咏叹调》（Arietta）里比较容易的几段。这样想来想去，她或许有几分可笑；可是精美，可是可爱，可是心地纯净，在那明澈剔透的正直里无瑕可指，像一块切面完美的水晶那样完整自足。她会等他，会盼他；他们会走过那些弯弯绕绕的乡径——没准还能雇到一辆家庭女教师小马车（governess cart），由一匹像只长了腿的木桶似的肥矮种马拉着——他们会到树林里寻花，也许他还记得白喉莺（whitethroat）的叫声是哪一种；就算不记得了，他也尽可以摆出权威的架势，说记得。「那是只白喉莺，艾米莉。听见了吗？就是叫『唧咕哩，咕哩，唧咕哩哩嘀』的那只。」

===Original===

“I’m waiting,” said Mrs. Viveash. “Patiently, however.”

Gumbril looked at her and found her smiling like a tragic mask. After all, he reflected, Emily would still be there if he went down tomorrow. It would be stupid to quarrel with Myra about something that was really, when he came to think of it, not of enormous importance. It was stupid to quarrel with anyone about anything; and with Myra and about this, particularly so. In this white dress patterned with flowing arabesques of black she looked, he thought, more than ever enchanting. There had been times in the past.﻿ ﻿… The past leads on to the present.﻿ ﻿… No; but in any case she was excellent company.

“Well,” he said, sighing decisively, “let’s go and send my wire.”

Mrs. Viveash made no comment, and traversing Jermyn Street they walked up the narrow passage under the lee of Wren’s bald barn of St. James’s, to the post office.

===Chinese===

「我在等着呢。」维维什夫人说。「不过，颇有耐心。」

冈布里尔望着她，发现她笑得像一副悲剧面具。说到底，他想，反正明天下去，艾米莉还是在那儿的。为一件——细想之下——实在算不得天大的事，同迈拉闹翻，未免愚蠢。同任何人、为任何事闹翻都是愚蠢的；而同迈拉、为这件事闹翻，尤其愚蠢。她穿着这袭白裙，上面印满流动的黑色阿拉伯卷纹，看上去——他想——比以往任何时候都更迷人。过去也有过那样的时刻……过去牵连着现在……不；但无论如何，她是个顶好的伴儿。

「好吧，」他毅然决然叹了口气，「走，发电报去。」

维维什夫人不置一词；两人穿过杰明街，在雷恩（Wren）设计的那座秃顶谷仓般的圣詹姆斯教堂的遮护下，走上那条狭窄的过道，来到邮局。

===Original===

“I shall pretext a catastrophe,” said Gumbril, as they entered; and going to the telegraph desk he wrote: “Slight accident on way to station not serious at all but a little indisposed come same train tomorrow.” He addressed the form and handed it in.

“A little what?” asked the young lady behind the bars, as she read it through, prodding each successive word with the tip of her blunt pencil.

“A little indisposed,” said Gumbril, and he felt suddenly very much ashamed of himself. “A little indisposed,”﻿—no, really, that was too much. He’d withdraw the telegram, he’d go after all.

“Ready?” asked Mrs. Viveash, coming up from the other end of the counter where she had been buying stamps.

===Chinese===

「我要托辞一场大祸。」进门时冈布里尔说。走到电报柜台，他写道：「赴站途中小有意外毫不严重唯略感不适明日乘同班前来」。他填好单子上的地址，递了进去。

「略有……什么？」栅栏那边的年轻女士问；她把电文从头到尾读着，用秃铅笔的笔尖把一个个词逐个点过去。

「略感不适。」冈布里尔说，忽然间羞愧难当。「略感不适」——不，说真的，这太过分了。他要把电报撤回来，他到底还是要去的。

「好了么？」维维什夫人问，她刚在柜台另一头买完邮票过来。

===Original===

Gumbril pushed a florin under the bars.

“A little indisposed,” he said, hooting with laughter, and he walked towards the door leaning heavily on his stick and limping. “Slight accident,” he explained.

“What is the meaning of this clownery?” Mrs. Viveash inquired.

“What indeed?” Gumbril had limped up to the door and stood there, holding it open for her. He was taking no responsibility for himself. It was the clown’s doing, and the clown, poor creature, was *non compos*, not entirely there, and couldn’t be called to account for his actions. He limped after her towards Piccadilly.

===Chinese===

冈布里尔从栅栏底下塞进一枚弗罗林（florin）。

「略感不适。」他说，哈哈大笑，一边朝门口走去，身子重重拄着手杖，一瘸一拐。「小有意外。」他解释道。

「这番插科打诨是什么意思？」维维什夫人质问。

「可不是么。」冈布里尔已经一瘸一拐挪到门口，站在那儿替她拉住了门。他不为他自己负任何责任。方才那些都是小丑干的；而小丑这可怜虫 *non compos*（神志不全），并不全然在场，没法为他的行为被问责。他一瘸一拐地跟在她身后，朝皮卡迪利走去。

===Original===

“*Giudicato guarabile in cinque giorni*,” Mrs. Viveash laughed. “How charming that always is in the Italian papers. The fickle lady, the jealous lover, the stab, the *colpo di rivoltella*, the mere Anglo-Saxon black eye﻿—all judged by the house surgeon at the Misericordia curable in five days. And you, my poor Gumbril, are you curable in five days?”

“That depends,” said Gumbril. “There may be complications.”

Mrs. Viveash waved her parasol; a taxi came swerving to the pavement’s edge in front of them. “Meanwhile,” she said, “you can’t be expected to walk.”

===Chinese===

「*Giudicato guarabile in cinque giorni*（诊断为五日内可愈）。」维维什夫人笑了。「意大利报纸上，这句话永远那么迷人。薄情的女郎，吃醋的情人，那一刀，那一声 *colpo di rivoltella*（左轮枪响），在盎格鲁-撒克逊人不过一只乌青的眼圈——到了仁慈医院（Misericordia）外科住院医师的笔下，统统判定五日内可愈。而你呢，我可怜的冈布里尔，你五日内可愈么？」

「那要看情况。」冈布里尔说。「兴许会有并发症。」

维维什夫人挥了挥阳伞；一辆出租车嗖地拐到他们面前的路缘。「再说了，」她说，「总不能指望你走路。」

===Original===

At Verrey’s they lunched off lobsters and white wine. “Fish suppers,” Gumbril quoted jovially from the Restoration, “fish suppers will make a man hop like a flea.” Through the whole meal he clowned away in the most inimitable style. The ghost of a governess cart rolled along the twiddly lanes of Robertsbridge. But one can refuse to accept responsibility; a clown cannot be held accountable. And besides, when the future and the past are abolished, when it is only the present instant, whether enchanted or unenchanted, that counts, when there are no causes or motives, no future consequences to be considered, how can there be responsibility, even for those who are not clowns? He drank a great deal of hock, and when the clock struck two and the train had begun to snort out of Charing Cross, he could not refrain from proposing the health of Viscount Lascelles. After that he began telling Mrs. Viveash about his adventure as a Complete Man.

“You should have seen me,” he said, describing his beard.

“I should have been bowled over.”

===Chinese===

在韦雷餐厅，他们的午饭是龙虾配白葡萄酒。「鱼晚餐，」冈布里尔兴致勃勃地引用王政复辟时期的老话，「鱼晚餐，管保叫人蹦得像跳蚤。」一顿饭从头到尾，他把小丑耍得妙不可言、无人能学。一辆家庭女教师小马车的幽灵，沿着罗伯茨布里奇弯弯绕绕的乡径辘辘驶过。可是，责任是可以拒收的；一个小丑是不能被问责的。再说，当未来与过去统统废除，当算数的只有眼下这一瞬——无论它着了魔还是没着魔——当没有原因、没有动机、无须计较日后的后果，责任又从何谈起呢，哪怕对那些并非小丑的人？他喝下了大量的霍克（hock）白葡萄酒；时钟敲响两点、火车已经喷着响鼻驶出查令十字，他忍不住提议为拉塞尔斯子爵（Viscount Lascelles）阁下的健康干杯。此后，他便开始对维维什夫人讲起自己身为「完人」的那段奇遇。

「你真该见见我那时的模样。」他说着，把自己的胡须形容了一番。

「那我准会为之倾倒。」

===Original===

“You shall see me, then,” said Gumbril. “Ah, what a Don Giovanni. *La ci darem la mano, La mi dirai di si, Vieni, non e lontano, Partiam, ben mio, da qui.* And they came, they came. Without hesitation. No ‘*vorrei e non vorrei*,’ no ‘*mi trema un poco il cor*.’ Straight away.”

“*Felice, io so, sarei*,” Mrs. Viveash sang very faintly under her breath, from a remote bed of agony.

Ah, happiness, happiness; a little dull, someone had wisely said, when you looked at it from outside. An affair of duets at the cottage piano, of collecting specimens, hand in hand, for the *hortus siccus*. A matter of integrity and quietness.

“Ah, but the history of the young woman who was married four years ago,” exclaimed Gumbril with clownish rapture, “and remains to this day a virgin﻿—what an episode in my memoirs!” In the enchanted darkness he had learned her young body. He looked at his fingers; her beauty was a part of their knowledge. On the tablecloth he drummed out the first bars of the Twelfth Sonata of Mozart. “And even after singing her duet with the Don,” he continued, “she is still virgin. There are chaste pleasures, sublimated sensualities. More thrillingly voluptuous,” with the gesture of a restaurant-keeper who praises the speciality of the house, he blew a treacly kiss, “than any of the grosser deliriums.”

===Chinese===

「那你就见见吧。」冈布里尔说。「啊，好一位唐·乔万尼（Don Giovanni）。*La ci darem la mano, La mi dirai di si, Vieni, non e lontano, Partiam, ben mio, da qui*（咱们手挽着手，你会对我说愿意；来吧，路不远；我们离开这儿吧，亲爱的）。而她们来了，她们来了。毫不迟疑。没有半句『*vorrei e non vorrei*』（我想又不敢），没有半句『*mi trema un poco il cor*』（我的心儿微微发颤）。径直就来了。」

「*Felice, io so, sarei*（我知道，我将会幸福）。」维维什夫人气若游丝地低声唱着，从一张遥远的苦痛之榻上。

啊，幸福，幸福；有人明智地说过：从外面看去，它有点儿乏味。不过是在小立式钢琴上弹弹二重唱，不过是手拉手采集标本、夹进蜡叶标本册（*hortus siccus*）的营生。一桩正直与恬静的事。

「啊，还有那位四年前嫁了人的年轻太太，」冈布里尔带着丑角式的狂喜叫道，「至今却还是个处女——写进我的回忆录，何等妙的一章！」在着了魔的黑暗里，他认识了她年轻的身体。他看看自己这双手指；她的美，已经成了它们知识的一部分。他在桌布上敲出莫扎特（Mozart）第十二奏鸣曲的开头几小节。「而且，即便同那位唐唱完了她那支二重唱，」他接着说，「她依然是处女。世上有贞洁的快乐，有升华了的声色之乐。」他做出一副餐馆老板夸耀自家招牌菜的姿势，飞出一个甜腻腻的吻，「比任何粗鄙些的癫狂，都更教人酥麻。」

===Original===

“What is all this about?” asked Mrs. Viveash.

Gumbril finished off his glass. “I am talking esoterically,” he said, “for my own pleasure, not yours.”

“But tell me more about the beard,” Mrs. Viveash insisted. “I liked the beard so much.”

“All right,” said Gumbril, “let us try to be unworthy with coherence.”

They sat for a long time over their cigarettes; it was half past three before Mrs. Viveash suggested they should go.

===Chinese===

「你这一套都说的是什么呀？」维维什夫人问。

冈布里尔把杯中酒一饮而尽。「我在说秘传的话，」他说，「图我自己快活，不是图你。」

「还是多跟我讲讲那把胡子。」维维什夫人不依不饶。「我太喜欢那把胡子了。」

「好吧，」冈布里尔说，「那就让我们试试，把不成体统做得头头是道。」

两人对着香烟坐了许久；直到三点半，维维什夫人才提议该走了。

===Original===

“Almost time,” she said, looking at her watch, “to have tea. One damned meal after another. And never anything new to eat. And every year one gets bored with another of the old things. Lobster, for instance, how I used to adore lobster once! But today﻿—well, really, it was only your conversation, Theodore, that made it tolerable.”

Gumbril put his hand to his heart and bowed. He felt suddenly extremely depressed.

“And wine: I used to think Orvieto so heavenly. But this spring, when I went to Italy, it was just a bad muddy sort of Vouvray. And those soft caramels they call Fiats; I used to eat those till I was sick. I was at the sick stage before I’d finished one of them, this time in Rome.” Mrs. Viveash shook her head. “Disillusion after disillusion.”

They walked down the dark passage into the street.

===Chinese===

「差不多，」她看看表，「该喝茶了。一顿该死的饭接着一顿该死的饭。吃的东西从来没什么新鲜的。每过一年，又添一样旧的吃腻了。就说龙虾吧，我从前何等迷恋龙虾！可今天——唉，说真的，全亏你的谈话，西奥多，才勉强咽得下去。」

冈布里尔手抚胸口，躬身一礼。他忽然觉得沮丧极了。

「还有酒：我从前觉得奥尔维耶托（Orvieto）真是天上琼浆。可今年春天去意大利，它不过是一种浑浊劣等的武弗雷（Vouvray）。还有那种他们唤作『菲亚特』的软焦糖；我从前吃得直犯恶心。这回在罗马，一块还没吃完，就恶心上了。」维维什夫人摇了摇头。「幻灭接着幻灭。」

两人顺着昏暗的过道走到街上。

===Original===

“We’ll go home,” said Mrs. Viveash. “I really haven’t the spirit to do anything else this afternoon.” To the commissionaire who opened the door of the cab she gave the address of her house in St. James’s.

“Will one ever recapture the old thrills?” she asked rather fatiguedly as they drove slowly through the traffic of Regent Street.

“Not by chasing after them,” said Gumbril, in whom the clown had quite evaporated. “If one sat still enough they might perhaps come back of their own accord.﻿ ﻿…” There would be the faint sound as it were of feet approaching through the quiet.

“It isn’t only food,” said Mrs. Viveash, who had closed her eyes and was leaning back in her corner.

“So I can well believe.”

===Chinese===

「我们回家吧。」维维什夫人说。「今天下午，我实在打不起精神再做别的了。」她对着替她打开车门的门警，报出了自己圣詹姆斯宅邸的地址。

「人还能重新抓回旧日的战栗么？」她颇为疲惫地问；出租车正缓缓穿过摄政街的车流。

「追，是追不回来的。」冈布里尔说；他身上的小丑已然蒸发净尽。「人若坐得足够静，它们或许会自己回来……」那将是一种极轻的声响，仿佛有脚步正穿过寂静，渐渐走近。

「不只是吃食。」维维什夫人说；她已阖上眼睛，靠回她那一侧的角落里。

「这我很能相信。」

===Original===

“It’s everything. Nothing’s the same now. I feel it never will be.”

“Never more,” croaked Gumbril.

“Never again,” Mrs. Viveash echoed. “Never again.” There were still no tears behind her eyes. “Did you ever know Tony Lamb?” she asked.

“No,” Gumbril answered from his corner. “What about him?”

Mrs. Viveash did not answer. What, indeed, about him? She thought of his very clear blue eyes and the fair, bright hair that had been lighter than his brown face. Brown face and neck, red-brown hands; and all the rest of his skin was as white as milk. “I was very fond of him,” she said at last. “That’s all. He was killed in 1917, just about this time of the year. It seems a very long time ago, don’t you think?”

===Chinese===

「是一切。如今什么都不一样了。我觉出，永远不会一样了。」

「永不再来。」冈布里尔哑着嗓子说。

「再也不会有了。」维维什夫人应和着。「再也不会有了。」她眼里依旧没有泪水。「你从前认识托尼·兰姆么？」她问。

「不认识。」冈布里尔从他那一侧的角落里应道。「他怎么了？」

维维什夫人没有作答。是啊，他又怎么了？她想起他那双极其清亮的蓝眼睛，那头比棕色脸庞更浅亮的金发。棕色的脸和脖颈，红棕色的一双手；他周身其余的皮肤，却白得像牛奶。「我曾经很喜欢他。」她终于开口。「就是这么回事。他是 1917 年死的，差不多就是一年里的这个时候。听上去已经很久远了，你说是不是？」

===Original===

“Does it?” Gumbril shrugged his shoulders. “I don’t know. The past is abolished. *Vivamus, mea Lesbia.* If I weren’t so horribly depressed, I’d embrace you. That would be some slight compensation for my”﻿—he tapped his foot with the end of his walking-stick﻿—“my accident.”

“You’re depressed too?”

“One should never drink at luncheon,” said Gumbril. “It wrecks the afternoon. One should also never think of the past and never for one moment consider the future. These are treasures of ancient wisdom. But perhaps after a little tea﻿—” He leaned forward to look at the figures on the taximeter, for the cab had come to a standstill﻿—“after a nip of the tannin stimulant”﻿—he threw open the door﻿—“we may feel rather better.”

Mrs. Viveash smiled excruciatingly. “For me,” she said, as she stepped out on to the pavement, “even tannin has lost its virtues now.”

===Chinese===

「是么？」冈布里尔耸了耸肩。「我不知道。过去已经被废除了。*Vivamus, mea Lesbia*（我们且活着、且相爱吧，我的莱斯比亚）。要不是我沮丧得吓人，我就要拥抱你了。那样，对我这个」——他用手杖头点了点自己的脚——「对我的意外，也算一点小小的补偿。」

「你也沮丧？」

「午饭时永远不该喝酒。」冈布里尔说。「它会毁掉整个下午。人也永远不该回想过去，一刻也不该盘算将来。这些都是古老智慧的至宝。不过，也许喝上一点儿茶之后——」他俯身去看计程器上的数字，出租车已经停了下来——「抿上一口单宁兴奋剂之后」——他一把推开车门——「我们兴许会觉得好受些。」

维维什夫人苦痛难当似的笑了笑。「对我而言，」她一面跨上人行道，一面说，「如今连单宁也失了灵效。」

===Original===

Mrs. Viveash’s drawing-room was tastefully in the movement. The furniture was upholstered in fabrics designed by Dufy﻿—racehorses and roses, little tennis players clustering in the midst of enormous flowers, printed in grey and ochre on a white ground. There were a couple of lampshades by Balla. On the pale rose-stippled walls hung three portraits of herself by three different and entirely incongruous painters, a selection of the usual oranges and lemons, and a rather forbidding contemporary nude painted in two tones of green.

“And how bored I am with this room and all these beastly pictures!” exclaimed Mrs. Viveash as she entered. She took off her hat and, standing in front of the mirror above the mantelpiece, smoothed her coppery hair.

“You should take a cottage in the country,” said Gumbril, “buy a pony and a governess cart and drive along the twiddly lanes looking for flowers. After tea you open the cottage piano,” and suiting his action to the words, Gumbril sat down at the long-tailed Blüthner, “and you play, you play.” Very slowly and with parodied expressiveness he played the opening theme of the Arietta. “You wouldn’t be bored then,” he said, turning round to her, when he had finished.

“Ah, wouldn’t I!” said Mrs. Viveash. “And with whom do you propose that I should share my cottage?”

===Chinese===

维维什夫人的客厅，雅致地紧跟着新潮。家具蒙面用的织物，出自杜菲（Dufy）的手笔——赛马与玫瑰，小小的网球手们簇拥在硕大无朋的花朵中央，灰色与赭黄，印在白底子上。还有两盏巴拉（Balla）做的灯罩。微微点染着玫瑰色的浅墙上，挂着三幅她的肖像，出自三位彼此全然格格不入的画家之手；挂着几幅千篇一律的橙子与柠檬；还挂着一幅拒人千里的当代裸女，用两种色调的绿色画成。

「我对这间屋子、这满屋子可恶的画，腻烦透了！」维维什夫人一进门便叫道。她摘下帽子，站在壁炉上方那面镜子跟前，抿平她红铜色的头发。

「你该到乡下去赁一幢小屋，」冈布里尔说，「买一匹矮种马、一辆家庭女教师小马车，沿着弯弯绕绕的乡径寻花觅草。喝过茶，你打开那架小立式钢琴」——冈布里尔言出行随，在那架长尾巴的布吕特纳（Blüthner）大钢琴前坐了下来——「你就弹呀，你就弹呀。」他弹起《小咏叹调》的开头主题，弹得极慢，带着戏拟的深情。「那时候你就不会腻烦了。」弹罢，他回头对她说。

「哈，我还会腻烦不成！」维维什夫人说。「那么依你高见，我该同谁合住我的小屋呢？」

===Original===

“Anyone you like,” said Gumbril. His fingers hung, as though meditating over the keys.

“But I don’t like anyone,” cried Mrs. Viveash with a terrible vehemence from her deathbed.﻿ ﻿… Ah, now it had been said, the truth. It sounded like a joke. Tony had been dead five years now. Those bright blue eyes﻿—ah, never again. All rotted away to nothing.

“Then you should try,” said Gumbril, whose hands had begun to creep softly forward into the Twelfth Sonata. “You should try.”

“But I do try,” said Mrs. Viveash. Her elbows propped on the mantelpiece, her chin resting on her clasped hands, she was looking fixedly at her own image in the glass. Pale eyes looked unwaveringly into pale eyes. The red mouth and its reflection exchanged their smiles of pain. She had tried; it revolted her now to think how often she had tried; she had tried to like someone, anyone, as much as Tony. She had tried to recapture, to re-evoke, to revivify. And there had never been anything, really, but a disgust. “I haven’t succeeded,” she added, after a pause.

===Chinese===

「你喜欢的谁都行。」冈布里尔说。他的手指悬着，仿佛正在琴键上方沉思。

「可我谁也不喜欢。」维维什夫人喊道，从她内心的临终之榻上，带着一种骇人的激烈。……啊，现在它说出口了，这句真话。听上去倒像句玩笑话。托尼已经死了五年了。那双亮蓝的眼睛——啊，再也不会有了。全都烂尽了，烂成了一场空。

「那你就该试试。」冈布里尔说；他的双手已经开始悄悄向前，爬进第十二奏鸣曲里。「你该试试。」

「可我试了呀。」维维什夫人说。她两肘支在壁炉台上，下巴搁在交握的双手上，正定定望着镜中自己的影像。苍白的眼睛毫不游移地看进苍白的眼睛。红色的嘴唇和镜中的它，彼此交换着含痛的微笑。她试过；如今想起自己试过多少回，只觉得恶心；她试过去喜欢某个人，随便什么人，像喜欢托尼那样。她试过重新捕捉，重新召唤，重新唤回生气。而从来——真的——什么也不曾有过，只有一阵厌恶。「我没有成功。」她顿了顿，又补上一句。

===Original===

The music had shifted from F major to D minor; it mounted in leaping anapæsts to a suspended chord, ran down again, mounted once more, modulating to C minor, then, through a passage of trembling notes to A flat major, to the dominant of D flat, to the dominant of C, to C minor, and at last, to a new clear theme in the major.

“Then I’m sorry for you,” said Gumbril, allowing his fingers to play on by themselves. He felt sorry, too, for the subjects of Mrs. Viveash’s desperate experiments. She mightn’t have succeeded in liking them﻿—for their part, poor devils, they in general only too agonizingly liked her.﻿ ﻿… Only too.﻿ ﻿… He remembered the cold, damp spots on his pillow, in the darkness. Those hopeless, angry tears. “You nearly killed me once,” he said.

“Only time kills,” said Mrs. Viveash, still looking into her own pale eyes. “I have never made anyone happy,” she added, after a pause. “Never anyone,” she thought, except Tony, and Tony they had killed, shot him through the head. Even the bright eyes had rotted, like any other carrion. She too had been happy then. Never again.

===Chinese===

乐曲已经从F大调转入D小调；它以跃动的抑抑扬格音步攀升，攀上一个悬留和弦，又疾泻下来，再度攀升，转入C小调；随后，经过一段颤音的乐句，到了降A大调，到了降D大调的属音，到了C大调的属音，到了C小调，最后，来到大调上一个焕然一新的清澈主题。

「那我为你难过。」冈布里尔说，任凭手指自顾自弹下去。他也替维维什夫人那些孤注一掷的实验品们难过。她也许没能喜欢上他们——至于他们呢，这帮可怜鬼，多半倒是苦不堪言地恋着她。……苦不堪言哪。……他想起黑暗里他枕头上那几块又冷又湿的印子。那些无望的、愤怒的眼泪。「你有一回差点儿杀了我。」他说。

「只有时间才杀人。」维维什夫人说，仍旧望进她自己那双苍白的眼睛。「我从来不曾让任何人幸福过。」她顿了顿，又补上一句。「从来不曾让任何人，」她想，除了托尼；而托尼，他们把他杀了，一枪打穿了头。连那双明亮的眼睛也烂掉了，同随便哪块腐肉没有两样。那时候，她也是幸福的。再也不会有了。

===Original===

A maid came in with the tea-things.

“Ah, the tannin!” exclaimed Gumbril with enthusiasm, and broke off his playing. “The one hope of salvation.” He poured out two cups, and picking up one of them he came over to the fireplace and stood behind her, sipping slowly at the pale brewage and looking over her shoulder at their two reflections in the mirror.

“*La ci darem*,” he hummed. “If only I had my beard!” He stroked his chin and with the tip of his forefinger brushed up the drooping ends of his moustache. “You’d come trembling like Zerlina, in under its golden shadow.”

Mrs. Viveash smiled. “I don’t ask for anything better,” she said. “What more delightful part! *Felice, io so, sarei: Batti, batti, o bel Mazetto.* Enviable Zerlina!”

===Chinese===

一个女仆端着茶具进来。

「啊，单宁！」冈布里尔热切地叫出声，停了弹奏。「得救的唯一指望。」他斟出两杯茶，端起其中一杯，走到壁炉边，立在她身后，慢慢啜着那淡色的茶汤，越过她的肩头，望着镜中他们两人的影像。

「*La ci darem*（咱们手挽着手）。」他哼道。「只可惜我没有我的胡子！」他摩挲着下巴，又用食指尖把髭须下垂的两梢往上捋了捋。「你就会像泽莉娜（Zerlina）一样，哆哆嗦嗦地走进它金色的荫翳里来。」

维维什夫人微微一笑。「我不求更好的了。」她说。「还有比这更讨人喜欢的角色么！*Felice, io so, sarei: Batti, batti, o bel Mazetto*（我知道，我将会幸福：打吧，打吧，美丽的马塞托）。令人艳羡的泽莉娜！」

===Original===

The servant made another silent entry.

“A gentleman,” she said, “called Mr. Shearwater would like﻿—”

“Tell him I’m not at home,” said Mrs. Viveash, without looking round.

There was a silence. With raised eyebrows Gumbril looked over Mrs. Viveash’s shoulder at her reflection. Her eyes were calm and without expression, she did not smile or frown. Gumbril still questioningly looked. In the end he began to laugh.

===Chinese===

那女仆又悄没声儿地进来了一回。

「有位先生，」她说，「名叫希尔沃特（Shearwater）先生的，想——」

「告诉他，我不在家。」维维什夫人说，头也没回。

一阵沉默。冈布里尔扬起眉毛，越过维维什夫人的肩头，望着镜中她的影像。那双眼睛平静而无表情，不笑，也不蹙眉。冈布里尔仍旧疑问地望着。末了，他笑了起来。

## XV / XV

===Original===
They were playing that latest novelty from across the water, “What’s he to Hecuba?” Sweet, sweet and piercing, the saxophone pierced into the very bowels of compassion and tenderness, pierced like a revelation from heaven, pierced like the angel’s treacly dart into the holy Teresa’s quivering and ecstasiated flank. More ripely and roundly, with a kindly and less agonizing voluptuousness, the cello meditated those Mohammedan ecstasies that last, under the green palms of Paradise, six hundred inenarrable years apiece. Into this charged atmosphere the violin admitted refreshing draughts of fresh air, cool and thin like the breath from a still damp squirt. And the piano hammered and rattled away unmindful of the sensibilities of the other instruments, banged away all the time reminding everyone concerned, in a thoroughly businesslike way, that this was a cabaret where people came to dance the foxtrot; not a baroque church for female saints to go into ecstasies in, not a mild, happy valley of tumbling houris.

At each recurrence of the refrain the four negroes of the orchestra, or at least the three of them who played with their hands alone—for the saxophonist always blew at this point with a redoubled sweetness, enriching the passage with a warbling contrapuntal soliloquy that fairly wrung the entrails and transported the pierced heart—broke into melancholy and drawling song:

> "What's he to Hecuba?
 

 Nothing at all.
 

 That's why there'll be no wedding on Wednesday week,
 

 Way down in old Bengal."

===Chinese===
他们奏着的，正是海那边传来的那件最新花样——《他与赫卡柏何干？》（What's he to Hecuba?，曲名语出莎士比亚《哈姆雷特》）。萨克斯管甜，甜而尖利，一径刺进怜悯与柔情的脏腑最深处；刺得像一桩从天而降的启示，像天使那支甜腻的标枪，刺进圣特雷莎颤动着的、狂喜的腰侧（圣特雷莎，即伯尼尼名雕《圣特雷莎的狂喜》中被天使金矛刺中的圣女）。大提琴更圆熟、更丰润，带着一种厚道的、不那么磨折人的香艳，沉吟着那些穆罕默德天堂的狂喜——在天堂的绿棕榈底下，每一桩都要绵延上难言难述的六百年。小提琴往这团饱和的空气里放进一股股提神的新鲜气流，凉凉的、细细的，像一支还没干透的注射器（squirt）里呼出的气。钢琴却只管锤啊敲啊响个不停，全不理会其他乐器的敏感，一路敲打，用十足公事公办的口吻提醒在场诸人：这里是一家卡巴莱（cabaret，附歌舞表演的夜总会），人们来这儿跳狐步舞；不是让女圣徒们进来出神入迷的巴洛克教堂，也不是天园玉女（houris，伊斯兰天堂中的黑眼美女）们翻滚取乐的温柔快活谷。

每逢副歌重现，乐队的四个黑人——至少是其中三个光凭双手演奏的；因为萨克斯手每到此处，总是加倍甜蜜地吹，用一段啭鸣的对位独白给这一句添彩，直绞得人肺肠寸断，叫那颗被刺穿的心飞升欲举——便放开喉咙，唱起一支忧郁拖腔的歌：

> 「他与赫卡柏何干？」
 

 什么也不是。
 

 所以下星期三的婚礼办不成了，
 

 在老孟加拉的深处。」

===Original===
“What unspeakable sadness,” said Gumbril, as he stepped, stepped through the intricacies of the trot. “Eternal passion, eternal pain. *Les chants désesperés sont les chants les plus beaux, Et j’en sais d’immortels qui sont de purs sanglots.* Rum tiddle-um-tum, pom-pom. Amen. What’s he to Hecuba? Nothing at all. Nothing, mark you. Nothing, nothing.”

“Nothing,” repeated Mrs. Viveash. “I know all about that.” She sighed.

“I am nothing to you,” said Gumbril, gliding with skill between the wall and the Charybdis of a couple dangerously experimenting with a new step. “You are nothing to me. Thank God. And yet here we are, two bodies with but a single thought, a beast with two backs, a perfectly united centaur trotting, trotting.” They trotted.

“What’s he to Hecuba?” The grinning blackamoors repeated the question, reiterated the answer on a tone of frightful unhappiness. The saxophone warbled on the verge of anguish. The couples revolved, marked time, stepped and stepped with an habitual precision, as though performing some ancient and profoundly significant rite. Some were in fancy dress, for this was a gala night at the cabaret. Young women disguised as callipygous Florentine pages, blue-breeched Gondoliers, black-breeched Toreadors circulated, moon-like, round the hall, clasped sometimes in the arms of Arabs, or white clowns, or more often of untravestied partners. The faces reflected in the mirrors were the sort of faces one feels one ought to know by sight; the cabaret was “Artistic.”

===Chinese===
「说不出的悲哀啊。」冈布里尔说，一面在狐步舞错综的步法里踏着，踏着。「永恒的激情，永恒的痛苦。*Les chants désesperés sont les chants les plus beaux, Et j’en sais d’immortels qui sont de purs sanglots.*（法语，缪塞诗句：最绝望的歌才是最美的歌，我知道有些不朽的歌，纯粹是声声呜咽。）咚嘀嗒咚，砰砰。阿门。他与赫卡柏何干？什么也不是。什么都不是，请注意。什么也不是，什么也不是。」

「什么也不是。」维维什夫人应声重复。「这里头的滋味，我全知道。」她叹了口气。

「我于你，什么也不是，」冈布里尔说，一面灵巧地滑行，在墙壁与那对危险地试验新舞步的舞伴所构成的卡律布狄斯（Charybdis，希腊神话大漩涡）之间。「你于我，也什么也不是。感谢上帝。可我们这不是正在一起么：两个身子共一条心思，一头双背兽（beast with two backs，莎剧《奥赛罗》语，戏指交欢），一头配合得天衣无缝的马人，小跑着，小跑着。」他们小跑着。

「他与赫卡柏何干？」咧嘴笑着的黑摩尔人（blackamoor，旧时对黑人的称呼）们重复着这个问题，用一种不幸得吓人的腔调重申那个回答。萨克斯管在苦痛的边缘上啭鸣。一对对舞伴旋转，踏拍，迈步又迈步，带着习惯成自然的精确，仿佛在奉行某种古老而意义深远的仪式。有些人穿着化装服，因为今夜是这家卡巴莱的庆典之夜。化装成丰臀的佛罗伦萨侍童、蓝裤子的贡多拉船夫、黑裤子的斗牛士的年轻女子们，月亮似的绕着大厅巡行，时而偎进阿拉伯人或者白面小丑、而更多时候是未化装舞伴的臂弯里。镜子里映出的那些脸，都是你自觉理当认得的那一类脸；这家卡巴莱是「艺术」气派的。

===Original===
“What’s he to Hecuba?”

Mrs. Viveash murmured the response, almost piously, as though she were worshipping almighty and omnipresent Nil. “I adore this tune,” she said, “this divine tune.” It filled up a space, it moved, it jigged, it set things twitching in you, it occupied time, it gave you a sense of being alive. “Divine tune, divine tune,” she repeated with emphasis, and she shut her eyes, trying to abandon herself, trying to float, trying to give Nil the slip.

“Ravishing little Toreador, that,” said Gumbril, who had been following the black-breeched travesty with affectionate interest.

Mrs. Viveash opened her eyes. Nil was unescapable. “With Piers Cotton, you mean? Your tastes are a little common, my dear Theodore.”

===Chinese===
「他与赫卡柏何干？」

维维什夫人几乎是虔敬地喃喃应着那句答词，仿佛在礼拜全能而无所不在的零（Nil，拉丁语的「无」，维维什夫人的口头禅）。「我崇拜这支曲子，」她说，「这支神圣的曲子。」它填满一块空间，它流动，它蹦跳，它叫你身体里的什么东西抽动起来，它占据时间，它给你一种活着的感觉。「神圣的曲子，神圣的曲子。」她加重了语气反复说，随即阖上眼睛，想把自己交付出去，想漂起来，想把零甩脱。

「那位小斗牛士，真销魂。」冈布里尔说；他一直怀着温厚的兴味，注视着那个黑裤子的化装女子。

维维什夫人睁开了眼。零是逃不脱的。「你是说，同皮尔斯·科顿（Piers Cotton）？你的口味有点儿俗气了，我亲爱的西奥多。」

===Original===
“Green-eyed monster!”

Mrs. Viveash laughed. “When I was being ‘finished’ in Paris,” she said, “Mademoiselle always used to urge me to take fencing lessons. *C’est un exercice très gracieux. Et puis*,” Mrs. Viveash mimicked a passionate earnestness, “*et puis, ça dévelope le bassin*. Your Toreador, Gumbril, looks as though she must be a champion with the foils. *Quel bassin!*”

“Hush,” said Gumbril. They were abreast of the Toreador and her partner. Piers Cotton turned his long greyhound’s nose in their direction.

“How are you?” he asked across the music.

They nodded. “And you?”

===Chinese===
「绿眼的妖魔！」

维维什夫人笑了。「当年我在巴黎受『精修』（being “finished”，闺秀的最后一道教养）的时候，」她说，「Mademoiselle（法语：小姐，此处指她的法国家庭女教师）总是劝我去学击剑。*C’est un exercice très gracieux. Et puis*（法语：这是一项非常优雅的运动。而且呢）——」维维什夫人摹仿出一副热烈恳切的腔调，「*et puis, ça dévelope le bassin*（法语：而且呢，它能发达骨盆）。你那位斗牛士，冈布里尔，瞧那样子，准是花剑场上的冠军。*Quel bassin!*（法语：好一副骨盆！）」

「嘘。」冈布里尔说。他们恰好同那位斗牛士和她的舞伴并了排。皮尔斯·科顿把他那灰狗似的长鼻子朝他们转了过来。

「你好么？」他隔着乐声问。

他们点了点头。「你呢？」

===Original===
“Ah, writing such a book,” cried Piers Cotton, “such a brilliant, brilliant, flashing book.” The dance was carrying them apart. “Like a smile of false teeth,” he shouted across the widening gulf, and disappeared in the crowd.

“What’s he to Hecuba?” Lachrymosely, the hilarious blackamoors chanted their question, mournfully pregnant with its foreknown reply.

Nil, omnipresent nil, world-soul, spiritual informer of all matter. Nil in the shape of a black-breeched moon-basined Toreador. Nil, the man with the greyhound’s nose. Nil, as four blackamoors. Nil in the form of a divine tune. Nil, the faces, the faces one ought to know by sight, reflected in the mirrors of the hall. Nil this Gumbril whose arm is round one’s waist, whose feet step in and out among one’s own. Nothing at all.

That’s why there’ll be no wedding. No wedding at St. George’s, Hanover Square—oh, desperate experiment!—with Nil Viveash, that charming boy, that charming nothing at all, engaged at the moment in hunting elephants, hunting fever and carnivores among the Tikki-tikki pygmies. That’s why there’ll be no wedding on Wednesday week. For Lycidas is dead, dead ere his prime. For the light strawy hair (not a lock left), the brown face, the red-brown hands and the smooth boy’s body, milk-white, milk-warm, are nothing at all, nothing, now, at all—nil these five years—and the shining blue eyes as much nil as the rest.

===Chinese===
「啊，正写着一本那样的书呢，」皮尔斯·科顿喊道，「那样一部才华焕发、才华焕发、闪闪发光的书。」舞步正把他们带开。「像一副假牙的微笑。」他隔着越拉越宽的鸿沟喊道，随即消失在人堆里。

「他与赫卡柏何干？」那伙欢闹的黑摩尔人哭腔哭调地吟诵着他们的问题，凄凄地孕育着那预先知晓的回答。

零，无所不在的零，世界魂，向一切物质灌注灵性者。零，化作穿黑裤、骨盆圆如满月的斗牛士。零，化作生着灰狗鼻子的那个人。零，化作四个黑摩尔人。零，化作一支神圣的曲调。零，化作那些脸，那些理当认得的脸，映在大厅的镜子里。零，就是这位冈布里尔，他一条胳膊搂着人的腰，他的脚在人自己的两脚之间穿进穿出。什么也不是。

所以婚礼才办不成。在汉诺威广场圣乔治教堂（St. George's, Hanover Square，伦敦有名的婚礼教堂）——哦，孤注一掷的试验！——同零·维维什（Nil Viveash）、同那个迷人的少年、那个迷人的「什么也不是」，是成不了婚了；他此刻正在蒂基-蒂基（Tikki-tikki）俾格米人中间忙着猎大象、猎热病、猎食肉猛兽。所以下星期三是办不成婚礼了。因为利西达斯（Lycidas，弥尔顿悼亡诗中早夭的牧人）死了，未及华年便死了。因为那淡淡的草黄色头发（一绺也不剩了）、那棕色的脸、红棕色的手，还有那少年家光洁的身体——牛奶般白、牛奶般暖——如今什么也不是，什么也不是，彻头彻尾地不是——这五年来尽是零（nil）——那双明亮的蓝眼睛，也同其余的一切一样是零。

===Original===
“Always the same people,” complained Mrs. Viveash, looking round the room. “The old familiar faces. Never anyone new. Where’s the younger generation, Gumbril? We’re old, Theodore. There are millions younger than we are. Where are they?”

“I’m not responsible for them,” said Gumbril. “I’m not even responsible for myself.” He imagined a cottagey room, under the roof, with a window near the floor and a sloping ceiling where you were always bumping your head; and in the candlelight Emily’s candid eyes, her grave and happy mouth; in the darkness, the curve, under his fingers, of her firm body.

“Why don’t they come and sing for their supper?” Mrs. Viveash went on petulantly. “It’s their business to amuse us.”

“They’re probably thinking of amusing themselves,” Gumbril suggested.

===Chinese===
「老是同样这些人。」维维什夫人环顾着大厅，抱怨道。「老是那些熟识的旧面孔。从来没有个新人。年轻的一代都上哪儿去了，冈布里尔？我们老了，西奥多。比我们年轻的有千百万。他们都在哪儿？」

「他们不归我负责，」冈布里尔说。「连我自己都不归我负责。」他想象着一间村舍风味的小屋，在屋顶底下，窗子低得贴近地板，斜斜的天花板老叫人磕着脑袋；烛光里，是艾米莉那双坦率的眼睛，她那庄重而快活的嘴；黑暗里，是指头底下那具结实身子的曲线。

「他们怎么不来唱支歌换晚饭吃呢？」维维什夫人接着耍性子说。「逗我们开心，本是他们的本分。」

「他们多半在想着自己找乐子呢。」冈布里尔接口道。

===Original===
“Well, then, they should do it where we can see them.”

“What’s he to Hecuba?”

“Nothing at all,” Gumbril clownishly sang. The room, in the cottage, had nothing to do with him. He breathed Mrs. Viveash’s memories of Italian jasmines, laid his cheek for a moment against her smooth hair. “Nothing at all.” Happy clown!

Way down in old Bengal, under the green Paradisiac palms, among the ecstatic mystagogues and the saints who scream beneath the divine caresses, the music came to an end. The four negroes wiped their glistening faces. The couples fell apart. Gumbril and Mrs. Viveash sat down and smoked a cigarette.

===Chinese===
「哼，那他们也该到我们看得见的地方去乐。」

「他与赫卡柏何干？」

「什么也不是。」冈布里尔像小丑似的唱道。村舍里的那间屋子，同他毫不相干。他呼吸着维维什夫人记忆中的意大利素馨花（jasmine），把脸颊贴上她光滑的头发，贴了片刻。「什么也不是。」快活的小丑！

在老孟加拉的深处，在天堂的绿棕榈底下，在那些出神的秘仪法师（mystagogues，传授秘义者）和蒙神圣爱抚而尖叫的圣徒们中间，乐曲到了头。四个黑人擦了擦汗津津发亮的脸。一对对舞伴散开了。冈布里尔和维维什夫人坐下来，抽了支烟。

## XVI / XVI

===Original===
The blackamoors had left the platform at the end of the hall. The curtains looped up at either side had slid down, cutting it off from the rest of the room﻿—“making two worlds,” Gumbril elegantly and allusively put it, “where only one grew before﻿—and one of them a better world,” he added too philosophically, “because unreal.” There was the theatrical silence, the suspense. The curtains parted again.

===Chinese===
黑摩尔人已经离开了大厅顶头的乐台。原先拢在两侧的帷幕滑落下来，把它同厅内其余地方隔开——「这一来便造出了两个世界，」冈布里尔文雅而用典地说，「原先只长得出一个——而且其中一个还是个更好的世界，」他又添上一句，未免哲学得过了头，「因为它不真实。」剧场式的寂静与悬念降临了。帷幕重新向两边分开。

===Original===
> On a narrow bed﻿—on a bier perhaps﻿—the corpse of a woman. The husband kneels beside it. At the foot stands the doctor, putting away his instruments. In a beribboned pink cradle reposes a monstrous baby.
> The Husband
> Margaret! Margaret!
> The Doctor
> She is dead.
> The Husband
> Margaret!
> The Doctor
> Of septicæmia, I tell you.
> The Husband
> I wish that I too were dead!
> The Doctor
> But you won’t tomorrow.
> The Husband
> Tomorrow! But I don’t want to live to see tomorrow.
> The Doctor
> You will tomorrow.
> The Husband
> Margaret! Margaret! Wait for me there; I shall not fail to meet you in that hollow vale.
> The Doctor
> You will not be slow to survive her.
> The Husband
> Christ have mercy upon us!
> The Doctor
> You would do better to think of the child.

===Chinese===
> 一张狭窄的床上——也许该说灵床上——躺着一具女人的尸身。丈夫跪在床边。床脚站着医生，正在收拾器械。一只系着缎带的粉红色摇篮里，卧着一个怪物般的婴儿。
> 丈夫
> 玛格丽特！玛格丽特！
> 医生
> 她死了。
> 丈夫
> 玛格丽特！
> 医生
> 告诉你，是败血症死的。
> 丈夫
> 但愿我也死了才好！
> 医生
> 可你明天是死不成的。
> 丈夫
> 明天！可我不想活着见到明天。
> 医生
> 到了明天你就会想活了。
> 丈夫
> 玛格丽特！玛格丽特！你在那儿等我；那死荫的幽谷里，我决不会误了与你会面。
> 医生
> 你不会迟迟才活过她的。
> 丈夫
> 愿基督怜悯我们！
> 医生
> 你还是想想孩子的好。

===Original===
> The Husband
> *Rising and standing menacingly over the cradle.* Is that the monster?
> The Doctor
> No worse than others.
> The Husband
> Begotten in a night of immaculate pleasure, monster, may you live loveless, in dirt and impurity!
> The Doctor
> Conceived in lust and darkness, may your own impurity always seem heavenly, monster, in your own eyes!
> The Husband
> Murderer, slowly die all your life long!
> The Doctor
> The child must be fed.
> The Husband
> Fed? With what?
> The Doctor
> With milk.
> The Husband
> Her milk is cold in her breasts.
> The Doctor
> There are still cows.
> The Husband
> Tubercular shorthorns. *Calling.* Let Short-i’-the-horn be brought!
> Voices (off)
> Short-i’-the-horn! Short-i’-the-horn! *Fadingly.* Short-i’-the.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 丈夫
> *站起身来，气势汹汹地立在摇篮上首。* 那就是那个怪物吗？
> 医生
> 不比别的孩子更糟。
> 丈夫
> 你这在一宵无瑕的欢愉里种下的怪物，愿你活得无人爱怜，活在污垢和肮脏里！
> 医生
> 你这在情欲和黑暗里孕成的怪物，愿你自己的污秽，在你自己眼里，永远如同天堂！
> 丈夫
> 凶手，叫你一辈子慢慢地死！
> 医生
> 孩子总得喂。
> 丈夫
> 喂？拿什么喂？
> 医生
> 拿奶。
> 丈夫
> 她的奶，在她乳房里已经凉了。
> 医生
> 还有的是奶牛。
> 丈夫
> 结核的短角牛。*高声传唤。* 把短角儿（Short-i'-the-horn）牵上来！
> 众声（幕外）
> 短角儿！短角儿！*渐弱。* 短角——儿……

===Original===
> The Doctor
> In nineteen hundred and twenty-one, twenty-seven thousand nine hundred and thirteen women died in childbirth.
> The Husband
> But none of them belonged to my harem.
> The Doctor
> Each of them was somebody’s wife.
> The Husband
> Doubtless. But the people we don’t know are only characters in the human comedy. We are the tragedians.
> The Doctor
> Not in the spectator’s eyes.
> The Husband
> Do I think of the spectators? Ah, Margaret! Margaret!﻿ ﻿…
> The Doctor
> The twenty-seven thousand nine hundred and fourteenth.
> The Husband
> The only one!
> The Doctor
> But here comes the cow.

===Chinese===
> 医生
> 一九二一年，有两万七千九百一十三名妇女死于分娩。
> 丈夫
> 可她们没有一个是我后宫里的。
> 医生
> 她们每一个，都是什么人的妻子。
> 丈夫
> 自不待言。只是我们不认识的人，不过是人间喜剧里的角色罢了。我们才是演悲剧的。
> 医生
> 在看客眼里可未必。
> 丈夫
> 我几曾把看客放在心上？啊，玛格丽特！玛格丽特！……
> 医生
> 第二万七千九百一十四位。
> 丈夫
> 独一无二的一位！
> 医生
> 好了，牛来了。

===Original===
> *Short-i’-the-horn is led in by a Yokel.*
> The Husband
> Ah, good Short-i’-the-horn! *He pats the animal.* She was tested last week, was she not?
> The Yokel
> Ay, sir.
> The Husband
> And found tubercular. No?
> The Yokel
> Even in the udders, may it please you.
> The Husband
> Excellent! Milk me the cow, sir, into this dirty wash-pot.
> The Yokel
> I will, sir. *He milks the cow.*
> The Husband
> Her milk﻿—her milk is cold already. All the woman in her chilled and curdled within her breasts. Ah, Jesus! what miraculous galactagogue will make it flow again?
> The Yokel
> The wash-pot is full, sir.
> The Husband
> Then take the cow away.
> The Yokel
> Come, Short-i’-the-horn; come up, good Short-i’-the-horn. *He goes out with the cow.*
> The Husband
> *Pouring the milk into a long-tubed feeding-bottle.* Here’s for you, monster, to drink your own health in. *He gives the bottle to the child.*
> *Curtain.*

===Chinese===
> *短角儿由一名乡巴佬（Yokel）牵上场。*
> 丈夫
> 啊，好短角儿！*他拍拍那牲口。* 上礼拜检验过的，是也不是？
> 乡巴佬
> 是，老爷。
> 丈夫
> 验出来是结核。对不对？
> 乡巴佬
> 禀您老，连乳房都结核了。
> 丈夫
> 妙极！给我挤奶吧，先生，把它挤进这只脏水盆里。
> 乡巴佬
> 这就挤，老爷。*他挤牛奶。*
> 丈夫
> 她的奶——她的奶已经凉了。她身上一切属于女人的东西，都在乳房里冻住、凝成了块。啊，耶稣！要何等神奇的催乳灵药，才能叫它重新流出来？
> 乡巴佬
> 盆满啦，老爷。
> 丈夫
> 那就把牛牵走吧。
> 乡巴佬
> 来，短角儿；走起，好短角儿。*他牵着牛下场。*
> 丈夫
> *把奶倒进一只长管奶瓶。* 这是给你的，怪物，拿它祝你自己的健康。*他把奶瓶塞给孩子。*
> *幕落。*

===Original===
“A little ponderous, perhaps,” said Gumbril, as the curtain came down.
“But I liked the cow.” Mrs. Viveash opened her cigarette-case and found it empty. Gumbril offered her one of his. She shook her head. “I don’t want it in the least,” she said.
“Yes, the cow was in the best pantomime tradition,” Gumbril agreed. Ah! but it was a long time since he had been to a Christmas pantomime. Not since Dan Leno’s days. All the little cousins, the uncles and aunts on both sides of the family, dozens and dozens of them﻿—every year they filled the best part of a row in the dress circle at Drury Lane. And buns were stickily passed from hand to hand, chocolates circulated; the grownups drank tea. And the pantomime went on and on, glory after glory, under the shining arch of the stage. Hours and hours; and the grownups always wanted to go away before the harlequinade. And the children felt sick from eating too much chocolate, or wanted with such extreme urgency to go to the w.c. that they had to be led out, trampling and stumbling over everybody else’s feet﻿—and every stumble making the need more agonizingly great﻿—in the middle of the transformation scene. And there was Dan Leno, inimitable Dan Leno, dead now as poor Yorick, no more than a mere skull like anybody else’s skull. And his mother, he remembered, used to laugh at him sometimes till the tears ran down her cheeks. She used to enjoy things thoroughly, with a whole heart.

===Chinese===
「兴许有点儿滞重。」幕落下来时，冈布里尔说。
「不过我喜欢那头牛。」维维什夫人打开烟盒，发现已经空了。冈布里尔递上自己的一支。她摇了摇头。「我一点儿也不想要。」她说。
「是啊，那头牛深得圣诞哑剧的正宗嫡传。」冈布里尔表示同意。啊！不过他自己上一次看圣诞哑剧，已经是好久以前的事了。打丹·莱诺（Dan Leno）在世那会儿往后，就再没去过。满堂的小表弟小表妹，两边的叔伯姑舅姨，几十几十个——每年都要坐满德鲁里巷剧院（Drury Lane）二楼正座的一整排。小圆面包黏糊糊地从一只手传到另一只手，巧克力转着圈儿地分，大人们喝茶。哑剧一场连一场，荣耀接着荣耀，在舞台亮堂堂的拱顶底下演个不停。一个钟头又一个钟头；而大人们总在压轴滑稽戏（harlequinade）开演之前就想回家。孩子们不是巧克力吃得发了恶心，就是憋尿憋得十万火急，非得给领出去不可，一路踩着、绊着旁人的脚——每绊一下，那桩急事就越发不可收拾——偏偏都赶在换景奇观演到一半的当口。还有丹·莱诺，无与伦比的丹·莱诺，如今同可怜的约里克（Yorick，《哈姆雷特》里的死弄臣）一样死透了，只剩一颗骷髅，同任何旁人的骷髅没有两样。他还记得，他的母亲当年有时笑他，直笑得眼泪顺着腮帮子往下淌。她当年对什么都受用得十足十，用的是一整颗心。

===Original===
