“I wish they’d hurry up with the second scene,” said Mrs. Viveash. “If there’s anything that bores me, it’s entr’actes.”
“Most of one’s life is an entr’acte,” said Gumbril, whose present mood of hilarious depression seemed favourable to the enunciation of apophthegms.
“None of your cracker mottoes, please,” protested Mrs. Viveash. All the same, she reflected, what was she doing now but waiting for the curtain to go up again, waiting, with what unspeakable weariness of spirit, for the curtain that had rung down, ten centuries ago, on those blue eyes, that bright strawy hair and the weathered face?
“Thank God,” she said with an expiring earnestness, “here’s the second scene!”

===Chinese===
「真巴望他们快点儿演第二场。」维维什夫人说。「要说有什么叫我腻烦的，头一件就是幕间休息（entr'acte）。」
「人的一生，大半都是幕间休息。」冈布里尔说；他眼下这种兴高采烈的意气消沉，似乎正宜于发布警句。
「少来你那套爆竹签格言吧。」维维什夫人抗议道。话虽如此，她心里想，此刻自己做的又是什么呢？无非是等着大幕重新拉开——等着那十个世纪之前就已在那双蓝眼睛、那头明亮的草黄头发、那张风霜面孔上落下的帷幕重新拉开——而且等得心神倦怠，倦怠得无以言表。
「谢天谢地，」她带着咽气一般的恳切说，「第二场来了！」

===Original===
> The curtain went up. In a bald room stood the Monster, grown now from an infant into a frail and bent young man with bandy legs. At the back of the stage a large window giving on to a street along which people pass.
> The Monster *Solus.*
> The young girls of Sparta, they say, used to wrestle naked with naked Spartan boys. The sun caressed their skins till they were brown and transparent like amber or a flask of olive oil. Their breasts were hard, their bellies flat. They were pure with the chastity of beautiful animals. Their thoughts were clear, their minds cool and untroubled. I spit blood into my handkerchief and sometimes I feel in my mouth something slimy, soft and disgusting, like a slug﻿—and I have coughed up a shred of my lung. The rickets from which I suffered in childhood have bent my bones and made them old and brittle. All my life I have lived in this huge town, whose domes and spires are wrapped in a cloud of stink that hides the sun. The slug-dank tatters of lung that I spit out are black with the soot I have been breathing all these years. I am now come of age. Long-expected one-and-twenty has made me a fully privileged citizen of this great realm of which the owners of the *Daily Mirror*, the *News of the World* and the *Daily Express* are noble peers. Somewhere, I must logically infer, there must be other cities, built by men for men to live in. Somewhere, in the past, in the future, a very long way off.﻿ ﻿… But perhaps the only street improvement schemes that ever really improve the streets are schemes in the minds of those who live in them: schemes of love mostly. Ah! here she comes.
> *The **Young Lady** enters. She stands outside the window, in the street, paying no attention to the **Monster**; she seems to be waiting for somebody.*
> She is like a pear tree in flower. When she smiles, it is as though there were stars. Her hair is like the harvest in an eclogue, her cheeks are all the fruits of summer. Her arms and thighs are as beautiful as the soul of St. Catherine of Siena. And her eyes, her eyes are plumbless with thought and limpidly pure like the water of the mountains.
> The Young Lady
> If I wait till the summer sale, the crêpe de Chine will be reduced by at least two shillings a yard, and on six camisoles that will mean a lot of money. But the question is: can I go from May till the end of July with the underclothing I have now?
> The Monster
> If I knew her, I should know the universe!
> The Young Lady
> My present ones are so dreadfully middle-class. And if Roger should﻿ ﻿… by any chance.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 幕升起来了。一间光秃秃的屋子里站着怪物，如今他已从婴儿长成一个瘦弱佝偻的青年，两条罗圈腿。舞台后部是一扇大窗，临着一条街，街上行人来往。
> 怪物 *独自（Solus）。*
> 据说，斯巴达的少女们从前是赤身裸体地同赤身裸体的斯巴达少年角斗的。太阳爱抚她们的皮肤，直到晒成棕色、透亮，像琥珀，像一瓶橄榄油。她们的乳房结实，肚腹平坦。她们怀着美丽牲畜的那一种贞洁，纯洁无垢。她们的思想清明，头脑凉爽，无忧无虑。我往手帕里吐血，有时觉得嘴里有一团黏滑、绵软、叫人恶心的东西，像一条蛞蝓——我还咳出过一小片自己的肺。小时候害下的佝偻病弄弯了我的骨头，把它们弄成了又老又脆的老骨头。我这一辈子都住在这座大城市里，它的穹顶和尖塔裹在一团遮天蔽日的臭云里。我吐出来的那些蛞蝓般湿冷的碎肺片，叫我这些年呼吸的煤烟熏得乌黑。如今我成年了。盼望已久的二十一岁，使我成了这个伟大国度的全权公民——在这个国度里，《每日镜报》《世界新闻报》和《每日快报》的老板们都是高贵的勋爵。依理推断，在什么地方，一定还有别的城市，是人为了让人居住而盖的。在什么地方，在过去，在未来，远得不得了。……不过，真正改良了街道的街道改良方案，恐怕只有一种：就是住在街上的人心里的那些方案——多半是爱的方案。啊！她来了。
> ***少女**上场。她站在窗外的大街上，并不理会**怪物**；似乎在等什么人。*
> 她像一株开花的梨树。她微笑的时候，仿佛天上起了星星。她的头发像牧歌里的收成，她的脸庞是夏天全部的果子。她的胳膊和大腿，美得像锡耶纳的圣凯瑟琳的灵魂。还有她的眼睛，她的眼睛因思想而深不可测，又清澄纯净，像山间的水。
> 少女
> 要是等到夏季大减价，双绉（crêpe de Chine）一码至少能便宜两先令，摊到六件衬衣上就是一大笔钱。可问题是：我手上这几件内衣，能从五月撑到七月底么？
> 怪物
> 我若认识了她，我便认识了宇宙！
> 少女
> 我现在这几件，实在太中产阶级气了。再说，万一罗杰他……碰巧……

===Original===
> The Monster
> Or, rather, I should be able to ignore it, having a private universe of my own.
> The Young Lady
> If﻿—if he did﻿—well, it might be rather humiliating with these I have﻿ ﻿… like a servant’s almost.﻿ ﻿…
> The Monster
> Love makes you accept the world; it puts an end to criticism.
> The Young Lady
> His hand already.﻿ ﻿…
> The Monster
> Dare I, dare I tell her how beautiful she is?
> The Young Lady
> On the whole, I think I’d better get it now, though it will cost more.
> The Monster
> *Desperately advancing to the window as though to assault a battery.* Beautiful! beautiful!
> The Young Lady
> *Looking at him.* Ha, ha, ha!
> The Monster
> But I love you, flowering pear tree; I love you, golden harvest; I love you, fruitage of summer; I love you, body and limbs, with the shape of a saint’s thought.
> The Young Lady
> *Redoubles her laughter.* Ha, ha, ha!

===Chinese===
> 怪物
> 或者说，我就能把它一笔勾销了，因为我自己另有一个宇宙。
> 少女
> 要是——要是他真那么——那，穿着我现在这几件，未免有点儿丢脸……简直跟女用人穿的差不多了……
> 怪物
> 爱使你接受这个世界；爱叫批评寿终正寝。
> 少女
> 他的手已经……
> 怪物
> 我敢不敢，敢不敢告诉她，她有多么美？
> 少女
> 权衡下来，我看还是现在就买的好，虽然要多花些钱。
> 怪物
> *拼死命地向窗口进逼，仿佛要去强攻一座炮兵阵地。* 美啊！美啊！
> 少女
> *看了他一眼。* 哈，哈，哈！
> 怪物
> 可我爱你啊，开花的梨树；我爱你，金色的收成；我爱你，夏天的果实；我爱你，这身子这四肢，生着圣徒思想的那副形状。
> 少女
> *笑得更响了。* 哈，哈，哈！

===Original===
> The Monster
> *Taking her hand.* You cannot be cruel! *He is seized with a violent paroxysm of coughing which doubles him up, which shakes and torments him. The handkerchief he holds to his mouth is spotted with blood.*
> The Young Lady
> You disgust me! *She draws away her skirts so that they shall not come in contact with him.*
> The Monster
> But I swear to you, I love﻿—I﻿—*He is once more interrupted by his cough.*
> The Young Lady
> Please go away. *In a different voice.* Ah, Roger! *She advances to meet a snub-nosed lubber with curly hair and a face like a groom’s, who passes along the street at this moment.*
> Roger
> I’ve got the motorbike waiting at the corner.
> The Young Lady
> Let’s go, then.
> Roger
> *Pointing to the **Monster**.* What’s that?
> The Young Lady
> Oh, it’s nothing in particular.
> *Both roar with laughter. **Roger** escorts her out, patting her familiarly on the back as they walk along.*
> The Monster
> *Looking after her.* There is a wound under my left pap. She has deflowered all women. I cannot.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 怪物
> *握住她的手。* 你不会这么残忍！*一阵剧烈的咳嗽攫住了他，咳得他弯下腰直不起身，摇撼着他、折磨着他。他捂在嘴上的手帕上，斑斑点点都是血。*
> 少女
> 你真叫我恶心！*她把裙子拉开，免得沾着他。*
> 怪物
> 可我向你起誓，我爱——我——*咳嗽又一次打断了他。*
> 少女
> 请你走开。*换了一副嗓音。* 啊，罗杰！*她迎上前去，会一个狮子鼻的莽汉：鬈头发，一张马夫似的脸，此刻正打街上经过。*
> 罗杰
> 摩托车在拐角上等着呢。
> 少女
> 那就走吧。
> 罗杰
> *指着**怪物**。* 那是什么？
> 少女
> 哦，没什么特别的。
> *两人哄然大笑。**罗杰**一路熟稔地拍着她的背，送她下场。*
> 怪物
> *目送她去远。* 我左乳头底下有一处伤口。她把天下的女人都糟蹋尽了。而我不能……

===Original===
“Lord!” whispered Mrs. Viveash, “how this young man bores me!”
“I confess,” replied Gumbril, “I have rather a taste for moralities. There is a pleasant uplifting vagueness about these symbolical generalized figures which pleases me.”
“You were always charmingly simple-minded,” said Mrs. Viveash. “But who’s this? As long as the young man isn’t left alone on the stage, I don’t mind.”

===Chinese===
「天哪！」维维什夫人低声道，「这年轻人多么叫人腻烦！」
「我招认，」冈布里尔回答，「我对寓意剧（moralities，中世纪的道德剧）颇有几分嗜好。这些象征化、泛化的人物身上，有一种令人愉快、令人向上的朦胧味儿，很合我的脾胃。」
「你向来头脑简单得可爱。」维维什夫人说。「不过这位是谁？只要不至于让那年轻人独自留在台上，我都不在乎。」

===Original===
> Another female figure has appeared in the street beyond the window. It is the Prostitute. Her face, painted in two tones of red, white, green, blue and black, is the most tasteful of *nature-mortes*.
> The Prostitute
> Hullo, duckie!
> The Monster
> Hullo!
> The Prostitute
> Are you lonely?
> The Monster
> Yes.
> The Prostitute
> Would you like me to come in to see you?
> The Monster
> Very well.
> The Prostitute
> Shall we say thirty bob?
> The Monster
> As you like.
> The Prostitute
> Come along then.
> *She climbs through the window and they go off together through the door on the left of the stage. The curtains descend for a moment, then rise again. The **Monster** and the **Prostitute** are seen issuing from the door at which they went out.*

===Chinese===
> 窗外的街上又出现了一个女人的身影。是妓女。她那张脸，红、白、绿、蓝、黑，一色各敷两层，是静物画（*nature-mortes*，法语）里最有品位的一幅。
> 妓女
> 嗨，小亲亲！
> 怪物
> 嗨！
> 妓女
> 一个人，闷得慌么？
> 怪物
> 闷得慌。
> 妓女
> 要不要我进来陪陪你？
> 怪物
> 好吧。
> 妓女
> 就算三十先令，成不成？
> 怪物
> 随你。
> 妓女
> 那就走吧。
> *她从窗口爬进来，两人一同从舞台左边的门下场。幕布落下片刻，又复升起。只见**怪物**和**妓女**正从他们方才进去的那扇门里出来。*

===Original===
> The Monster
> *Taking out a chequebook and a fountain pen.* Thirty shillings.﻿ ﻿…
> The Prostitute
> Thank you. Not a cheque. I don’t want any cheques. How do I know it isn’t a dud one that they’ll refuse payment for at the bank? Ready money for me, thanks.
> The Monster
> But I haven’t got any cash on me at the moment.
> The Prostitute
> Well, I won’t take a cheque. Once bitten, twice shy, I can tell you.
> The Monster
> But I tell you I haven’t got any cash.
> The Prostitute
> Well, all I can say is, here I stay till I get it. And, what’s more, if I don’t get it quick, I’ll make a row.
> The Monster
> But this is absurd. I offer you a perfectly good cheque.﻿ ﻿…
> The Prostitute
> And I won’t take it. So there!
> The Monster
> Well then, take my watch. It’s worth more than thirty bob. *He pulls out his gold half-hunter.*
> The Prostitute
> Thank you, and get myself arrested as soon as I take it to the pop-shop! No, I want cash, I tell you.

===Chinese===
> 怪物
> *掏出支票簿和自来水笔。* 三十先令……
> 妓女
> 谢谢。支票可不行。我不要支票。我怎么知道这不是张空头支票，一到银行就叫人家拒付？我要现钱，谢谢。
> 怪物
> 可我这会儿身上没带现钱。
> 妓女
> 反正我不收支票。跟你说吧，一朝被蛇咬，十年怕井绳。
> 怪物
> 可我告诉你，我实在没带现钱。
> 妓女
> 我只有一句话：不拿到钱，我就守在这儿。而且，要是不能快点拿到，我就要大闹一场。
> 怪物
> 可这也未免太荒唐了。我给你的是一张堂堂正正的好支票……
> 妓女
> 我就是不收。就这么着！
> 怪物
> 那这样，把我的表拿去吧。它值的可不止三十先令。*他掏出他那金质的半猎表（half-hunter，盖上开窗的怀表）。*
> 妓女
> 谢谢你，我一把它拿进当铺（pop-shop，俚语）就叫人逮起来！不干，我要现钱，告诉你。

===Original===
> The Monster
> But where the devil do you expect me to get it at this time of night?
> The Prostitute
> I don’t know. But you’ve got to get it pretty quick.
> The Monster
> You’re unreasonable.
> The Prostitute
> Aren’t there any servants in this house?
> The Monster
> Yes.
> The Prostitute
> Well, go and borrow it from one of them.
> The Monster
> But really, that would be too low, too humiliating.
> The Prostitute
> All right, I’ll begin kicking up a noise. I’ll go to the window and yell till all the neighbours are woken up and the police come to see what’s up. You can borrow it from the copper then.
> The Monster
> You really won’t take my cheque? I swear to you it’s perfectly all right. There’s plenty of money to meet it.
> The Prostitute
> Oh, shut up! No more dillydallying. Get me my money at once, or I’ll start the row. One, two, three.﻿ ﻿… *She opens her mouth wide as if to yell.*

===Chinese===
> 怪物
> 可你叫我深更半夜的，上哪儿见鬼去弄钱？
> 妓女
> 我不知道。反正你得快点弄来。
> 怪物
> 你真不讲理。
> 妓女
> 这宅子里没有仆人么？
> 怪物
> 有。
> 妓女
> 那就去找其中一个借去。
> 怪物
> 可那实在也太下作、太丢人了。
> 妓女
> 好啊，那我就开嚷了。我这就到窗口去喊，喊到四邻八舍全醒过来，喊到警察进来看看出了什么事。到时候你就跟条子（copper，俚语：警察）借去吧。
> 怪物
> 你当真不收我的支票？我向你发誓，它绝无问题。账上有的是钱，足够兑付。
> 妓女
> 哦，闭嘴！别再磨蹭了。马上把钱给我拿来，不然我就开闹。一，二，三……*她张大嘴巴，作势要喊。*

===Original===
> The Monster
> All right. *He goes out.*
> The Prostitute
> Nice state of things we’re coming to, when young rips try and swindle us poor girls out of our money! Mean, stinking skunks! I’d like to slit the throats of some of them.
> The Monster
> *Coming back again.* Here you are. *He hands her money.*
> The Prostitute
> *Examining it.* Thank you, dearie. Any other time you’re lonely.﻿ ﻿…
> The Monster
> No, no!
> The Prostitute
> Where did you get it finally?
> The Monster
> I woke the cook.
> The Prostitute
> *Goes off into a peal of laughter.* Well, so long, duckie. *She goes out.*
> The Monster *Solus.*
> Somewhere there must be love like music. Love harmonious and ordered: two spirits, two bodies moving contrapuntally together. Somewhere, the stupid brutish act must be made to make sense, must be enriched, must be made significant. Lust, like Diabelli’s waltz, a stupid air, turned by a genius into three-and-thirty fabulous variations. Somewhere.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 怪物
> 好吧。*他下场。*
> 妓女
> 世道真是可以啊，小小的浪荡子还想赖掉我们穷姑娘的钱！一帮卑鄙发臭的臭鼬！真想割了他们几个的脖子。
> 怪物
> *又回来了。* 拿去。*他把钱递给她。*
> 妓女
> *验看钞票。* 谢谢，亲爱的。往后你再闷得慌，随时……
> 怪物
> 不，不！
> 妓女
> 你到底从哪儿弄来的？
> 怪物
> 我叫醒了厨娘。
> 妓女
> *爆出一串响亮的笑。* 好啦，再会，小亲亲。*她下场。*
> 怪物 *独自。*
> 在什么地方，一定有像音乐一样的爱。和谐而有法度的爱：两个心灵、两具身体，如对位法一般相偕行进。在什么地方，那桩愚蠢的、禽兽般的行为，一定会被弄出意义来，被充实起来，被变得意味深长。情欲，就像迪亚贝利（Diabelli）的圆舞曲，一支愚蠢的调子，被天才点化成三十三支奇妙的变奏。在什么地方……

===Original===
“Oh dear!” sighed Mrs. Viveash.
“Charming!” Gumbril protested.

===Chinese===
「哎呀！」维维什夫人叹道。
「迷人！」冈布里尔抗议道。

===Original===
> … love like sheets of silky flame; like landscapes brilliant in the sunlight against a background of purple thunder; like the solution of a cosmic problem; like faith.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> ……爱，像一匹一匹丝绸般的火焰；像阳光灿烂的风景，衬着紫雷滚滚的背景；像一道宇宙难题的解；像信仰。……

===Original===
“Crikey!” said Mrs. Viveash.

===Chinese===
「乖乖！」维维什夫人说。

===Original===
> … Somewhere, somewhere. But in my veins creep the maggots of the pox.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> ……在什么地方，在什么地方。可我的血管里，爬着梅毒的蛆虫……

===Original===
“Really, really!” Mrs. Viveash shook her head. “Too medical!”

===Chinese===
「真是的，真是的！」维维什夫人摇头。「太医学了！」

===Original===
> … crawling towards the brain, crawling into the mouth, burrowing into the bones. Insatiably.
> The Monster threw himself to the ground, and the curtain came down.

===Chinese===
> ……朝大脑爬，爬进嘴里，钻进骨头。永不知足。
> 怪物一头扑倒在地，幕落了下来。

===Original===
“And about time too!” declared Mrs. Viveash.
“Charming!” Gumbril stuck to his guns. “Charming! charming!”
There was a disturbance near the door. Mrs. Viveash looked round to see what was happening. “And now on top of it all,” she said, “here comes Coleman, raving, with an unknown drunk.”
“Have we missed it?” Coleman was shouting. “Have we missed all the lovely bloody farce?”
“Lovely bloody!” his companion repeated with drunken raptures, and he went into fits of uncontrollable laughter. He was a very young boy with straight dark hair and a face of Hellenic beauty, now distorted with tipsiness.
Coleman greeted his acquaintances in the hall, shouting a jovial obscenity to each. “And Bumbril-Gumbril,” he exclaimed, catching sight of him at last in the front row. “And Hetaira-Myra!” He pushed his way through the crowd, followed unsteadily by his young disciple. “So you’re here,” he said, standing over them and looking down with an enigmatic malice in his bright blue eyes. “Where’s the physiologue?”

===Chinese===
「也正是时候了！」维维什夫人宣称。
「迷人！」冈布里尔坚持己见。「迷人！迷人！」
门边起了一阵骚动。维维什夫人回头看出了什么事。「好啊，这回全齐了，」她说，「科尔曼来了，大嚷大叫，还带着个不认识的醉鬼。」
「咱们错过了没有？」科尔曼正喊着。「那出可爱他妈的滑稽戏，咱们全错过了没有？」
「可爱他妈的！」他的同伴带着醉醺醺的狂喜应道，随即发作起一阵不可收拾的大笑。他是个非常年轻的小伙子，黑头发直溜溜的，一张希腊式的俊脸，此刻叫酒气扭曲了。
科尔曼跟大厅里的相识们一一见礼，冲每个人快快活活喊一句粗话。「还有你，布姆布里尔——冈布里尔（Bumbril-Gumbril，谑把『冈』字头换成『屁股』bum 的谑称）！」他终于在头排里瞥见了冈布里尔，喊道。「还有你，海泰拉——迈拉（Hetaira-Myra，Hetaira＝古希腊的艺妓）！」他从人群里挤过来，他那年轻的门徒深一脚浅一脚地跟在后头。「原来你们在这儿。」他说，居高临下地站着，明亮的蓝眼睛里含着一团莫测的恶意。「那位生理学大家（physiologue，科尔曼生造的谑称）呢？」

===Original===
“Am I the physiologue’s keeper?” asked Gumbril. “He’s with his glands and his hormones, I suppose. Not to mention his wife.” He smiled to himself.
“Where the hormones, there moan I,” said Coleman, skidding off sideways along the slippery word. “I hear, by the way, that there’s a lovely prostitute in this play.”
“You’ve missed her,” said Mrs. Viveash.
“What a misfortune,” said Coleman. “We’ve missed the delicious trull,” he said, turning to the young man.
The young man only laughed.
“Let me introduce, by the way,” said Coleman. “This is Dante,” he pointed to the dark-haired boy; “and I am Virgil. We’re making a round tour﻿—or, rather, a descending spiral tour of hell. But we’re only at the first circle so far. These, Alighieri, are two damned souls, though not, as you might suppose, Paolo and Francesca.”

===Chinese===
「我岂是看守那位生理学大家的吗？」冈布里尔反问。「想必正同他的腺体、他的激素泡在一起呢。更别提他的太太了。」他暗自微笑。
「激素所在，即我呻吟之地。」科尔曼说，顺着这个滑溜的字眼儿打了个侧滑。「对了，听说这出戏里有个可爱的妓女。」
「你错过了她。」维维什夫人说。
「好大的不幸，」科尔曼说。「咱们错过了那可人的窑姐儿。」他转身对年轻人说。
年轻人只是笑。
「对了，容我引见。」科尔曼说。「这位是但丁。」他指着那黑头发的小伙子；「而我呢，是维吉尔。我们正作一次环游——或者说，是沿着一条下降的螺旋线游览地狱。不过眼下才游到第一层。这两位，阿利吉耶里（但丁的姓），是两个受罚的灵魂，虽然并不像你可能以为的那样，是保罗和弗兰切斯卡。」

===Original===
The boy continued to laugh, happily and uncomprehendingly.
“Another of these interminable entr’actes,” complained Mrs. Viveash. “I was just saying to Theodore here that if there’s one thing I dislike more than another, it’s a long entr’acte.” Would hers ever come to an end?
“And if there’s one thing *I* dislike more than another,” said the boy, breaking silence for the first time, with an air of the greatest earnestness, “it’s﻿ ﻿… it’s one thing more than another.”
“And you’re perfectly right in doing so,” said Coleman. “Perfectly right.”
“I know,” the boy replied modestly.

===Chinese===
那孩子接着笑，笑得快活，一窍不通。
「又是一回没完没了的幕间休息。」维维什夫人抱怨道。「我方才正跟这儿的西奥多说，要是有一样东西我讨厌它赛过别的，那就是冗长的幕间休息。」她自己的那一回，几时才有个完呢？
「要是有一样东西，我讨厌它赛过别的，」小伙子头一回开口打破沉默，带着一副顶顶诚恳的神气说，「那就是……那就是一样东西赛过另一样。」
「你这么着是完全有理的。」科尔曼说。「完全有理。」
「我知道。」小伙子谦虚地答道。

===Original===
> When the curtain rose again it was on an aged Monster, with a black patch over the left side of his nose, no hair, no teeth, and sitting harmlessly behind the bars of an asylum.
> The Monster
> Asses, apes and dogs! Milton called them that; he should have known. Somewhere there must be men, however. The variations on Diabelli prove it. Brunelleschi’s dome is more than the magnification of Cléo de Mérode’s breast. Somewhere there are men with power, living reasonably. Like our mythical Greeks and Romans. Living cleanly. The images of the gods are their portraits. They walk under their own protection. *The **Monster** climbs on to a chair and stands in the posture of a statue.* Jupiter, father of gods, a man, I bless myself, I throw bolts at my own disobedience, I answer my own prayers, I pronounce oracles to satisfy the questions I myself propound. I abolish all tetters, poxes, blood-spitting, rotting of bones. With love I recreate the world from within. Europa puts an end to squalor, Leda does away with tyranny, Danae tempers stupidity. After establishing these reforms in the social sewer, I climb, I climb, up through the manhole, out of the manhole, beyond humanity. For the manhole, even the manhole, is dark; though not so dingy as the doghole it was before I altered it. Up through the manhole, towards the air. Up, up! *And the **Monster**, suiting the action to his words, climbs up the runged back of his chair and stands, by a miraculous feat of acrobacy, on the topmost bar.* I begin to see the stars through other eyes than my own. More than dog already, I become more than man. I begin to have inklings of the shape and sense of things. Upwards, upwards I strain, I peer, I reach aloft. *The balanced **Monster** reaches, strains and peers.* And I seize, I seize! *As he shouts these words, the **Monster** falls heavily, head foremost, to the floor. He lies there quite still. After a little time the door opens and the **Doctor** of the first scene enters with a **Warder**.*
> The Warder
> I heard a crash.
> The Doctor
> *Who has by this time become immensely old and has a beard like Father Thames.* It looks as though you were right. *He examines the **Monster**.*

===Chinese===
> 幕再度升起时，台上是一个老迈的怪物：鼻子左边贴着一块黑膏药，没了头发，没了牙齿，无害地坐在疯人院的铁栏后头。
> 怪物
> 蠢驴、猿猴、狗！弥尔顿就这么称呼过他们；他理应知道。不过，在什么地方，一定还是有人的。迪亚贝利的变奏曲便是明证。布鲁内莱斯基的穹顶，也不止是克莱奥·德·梅罗德（Cléo de Mérode，当年名动欧洲的法国舞星）乳房的放大。在什么地方，有身负力量的人，合乎理性地活着。像我们神话里的希腊人、罗马人那样。活得干干净净。神像就是他们的肖像。他们行走在自己的护佑之下。*怪物爬上一把椅子，摆出雕像的姿势。* 朱庇特，众神之父，是一个人。我为自己祝福，我向自己的不服管教掷下霹雳，我应答自己的祈祷，我颁发神谕，来解答我自己提出的问题。我废除一切癣疹、梅毒、咯血、朽骨。我用爱从内部重造世界。欧罗巴扫除肮脏，勒达废除暴政，达那厄调和愚蠢。在社会这条阴沟里完成这些改革之后，我往上爬，往上爬，爬上人孔（manhole，下水道检修口），钻出人孔，越过人类。因为人孔，即便是人孔，也是黑的；虽然比起我改造之前那个狗洞来，还不算那么龌龊。穿过人孔，向着空气。向上，向上！*怪物令行动贴合着言语，爬上椅子横档的靠背，凭着一项奇迹般的杂技功夫，站上了最顶上的那根横档。* 我开始借我以外的一双眼睛看星星。已经不止于狗了，我又将不止于人。我开始隐约窥见万物的形状与意义。向上，向上，我绷紧了，我张望，我伸手抓取。*保持着平衡的怪物伸手、绷紧、张望。* 我抓住了，我抓住了！*他喊出这些字的同时，**怪物**头朝前重重摔倒在地。他躺在那里，全然不动。过了一小会儿，门开了，第一场里的那位**医生**同一个**看守**走进来。*
> 看守
> 我听见哗啦一声响。
> 医生
> *此时他已老得不成样子，一部胡须像泰晤士老人（Father Thames，泰晤士河的人格化老像）。* 看来你说得不错。*他检查**怪物**。*

===Original===
> The Warder
> He was forever climbing on to his chair.
> The Doctor
> Well, he won’t any more. His neck’s broken.
> The Warder
> You don’t say so?
> The Doctor
> I do.
> The Warder
> Well, I never!
> The Doctor
> Have it carried down to the dissecting-room.
> The Warder
> I’ll send for the porters at once.
> *Exeunt severally, and Curtain.*

===Chinese===
> 看守
> 他老是要往椅子上爬。
> 医生
> 好啦，他再也爬不成了。脖子断了。
> 看守
> 不会吧？
> 医生
> 会。
> 看守
> 哎呀，真是的！
> 医生
> 把他抬下去，送到解剖室去。
> 看守
> 我这就去叫杂役来抬。
> *二人分头下场。幕落。*

===Original===
“Well,” said Mrs. Viveash, “I’m glad that’s over.”
The music struck up again, saxophone and cello, with the thin draught of the violin to cool their ecstasies and the thumping piano to remind them of business. Gumbril and Mrs. Viveash slid out into the dancing crowd, revolving as though by force of habit.
“These substitutes for the genuine copulative article,” said Coleman to his disciple, “are beneath the dignity of hellhounds like you and me.”
Charmed, the young man laughed; he was attentive as though at the feet of Socrates. Coleman had found him in a night club, where he had gone in search of Zoe, found him very drunk in the company of two formidable women fifteen or twenty years his senior, who were looking after him, half maternally out of pure kindness of heart, half professionally; for he seemed to be carrying a good deal of money. He was incapable of looking after himself. Coleman had pounced on him at once, claimed an old friendship which the youth was too tipsy to be able to deny, and carried him off. There was something, he always thought, peculiarly interesting about the spectacle of children tobogganing down into the cesspools.
“I like this place,” said the young man.
“Tastes differ!” Coleman shrugged his shoulders. “The German professors have catalogued thousands of people whose whole pleasure consists in eating dung.”
The young man smiled and nodded, rather vaguely. “Is there anything to drink here?” he asked.

===Chinese===
「好了，」维维什夫人说，「这总算完了，我很高兴。」
乐声重新奏起，萨克斯管和大提琴，外加小提琴细细的一缕凉风，给它们的狂喜降降温，还有砰砰锤响的钢琴，提醒大家别忘了正经营生。
「这些顶替真正交媾原件的代用品，」科尔曼对他的门徒说，「你我这样的地狱恶犬，是不屑一顾的。」
年轻人听得心醉神迷，笑了；他一副专心致志的模样，仿佛坐在苏格拉底脚下。
「我喜欢这地方。」年轻人说。
「口味人人不同！」科尔曼耸耸肩。「德国的教授们已经编目归档了成千上万号人，他们全部的乐趣就在吃粪上。」
年轻人笑了笑，点了点头，有点儿茫茫然。「这儿有喝的么？」他问。

===Original===
“Too respectable,” Coleman answered, shaking his head.
“I think this is a bloody place,” said the young man.
“Ah! but some people like blood. And some like boots. And some like long gloves and corsets. And some like birch-rods. And some like sliding down slopes and can’t look at Michelangelo’s *Night* on the Medici Tombs without dying the little death, because the statue seems to be sliding. And some.﻿ ﻿…”
“But I want something to drink,” insisted the young man.
Coleman stamped his feet, waved his arms. “*À boire! à boire!*” he shouted, like the newborn Gargantua. Nobody paid any attention.

===Chinese===
「太体面了。」科尔曼摇头回答。
「我看这地方真他妈的。」年轻人说。
「啊！可偏偏有人喜欢血。有人喜欢靴子。有人喜欢长手套和紧身褡。有人喜欢桦条鞭。有人喜欢顺坡往下溜——一见着美第奇家陵墓上米开朗琪罗的《夜》，就免不了小死一番（the little death，情欲极乐的旧谑称），因为那座像看上去正在往下溜。还有人……」
「可我要喝的。」年轻人坚持道。
科尔曼跺脚挥臂。「*À boire! à boire!*（法语：喝呀！喝呀！）」他喊道，像新生的高康大。谁也不理他。

===Original===
The music came to an end. Gumbril and Mrs. Viveash reappeared.
“Dante,” said Coleman, “calls for drink. We must leave the building.”
“Yes. Anything to get out of this,” said Mrs. Viveash. “What’s the time?”
Gumbril looked at his watch. “Half-past one.”
Mrs. Viveash sighed. “Can’t possibly go to bed,” she said, “for another hour at least.”

===Chinese===
乐曲到了头。冈布里尔和维维什夫人重新露面。
「但丁，」科尔曼说，「喊着要喝。我们必须撤离这座楼。」
「是啊，只要能离开这儿，怎么都行。」维维什夫人说。「几点了？」
冈布里尔看了看表。「一点半。」
维维什夫人叹了口气。「怎么也还上不了床，」她说，「至少还得一个钟头。」

===Original===
They walked out into the street. The stars were large and brilliant overhead. There was a little wind that almost seemed to come from the country. Gumbril thought so, at any rate; he thought of the country.
“The question is, where?” said Coleman. “You can come to my bordello, if you like; but it’s a long way off and Zoe hates us all so much, she’ll probably set on us with the meat-chopper. If she’s back again, that is. Though she may be out all night. *Zoe mou, sas agapo.* Shall we risk it?”
“To me it’s quite indifferent,” said Mrs. Viveash faintly, as though wholly preoccupied with expiring.
“Or there’s my place,” Gumbril said abruptly, as though shaking himself awake out of some dream.

===Chinese===
他们走到街上。头顶的星星又大又亮。有点儿风，那风几乎像是从乡下来的。至少冈布里尔这么想；他想起了乡下。
「问题在于：去哪儿？」科尔曼说。「诸位可以光临寒舍的窑子，只要乐意；不过路远，而且佐伊恨我们恨到了骨头里，多半会抡起剁肉刀来砍我们。当然，得她回了家才算数。她也可能整宿都在外头。*Zoe mou, sas agapo.*（希腊语：我的佐伊，我爱你）咱们冒这个险么？」
「我全无所谓。」维维什夫人细声细气地说，仿佛一心一意只顾着咽气。
「要不，就到我那儿去。」冈布里尔突兀地说，仿佛从什么梦里抖擞醒来。

===Original===
“But you live still farther, don’t you?” said Coleman. “With venerable parents, and so forth. One foot in the grave and all that. Shall we mingle hornpipes with funerals?” He began to hum Chopin’s “Funeral March” at three times its proper speed, and seizing the young stranger in his arms, two-stepped two or three turns on the pavement, then released his hold and let him go reeling against the area railings.
“No, I don’t mean the family mansion,” said Gumbril. “I mean my own rooms. They’re quite near. In Great Russell Street.”
“I never knew you had any rooms, Theodore,” said Mrs. Viveash.
“Nobody did.” Why should they know now? Because the wind seemed almost a country wind? “There’s drink there,” he said.

===Chinese===
「可你住得还更远，不是么？」科尔曼说。「跟可敬的双亲大人同住，等等等等。一脚已经踏进坟墓，诸如此类。咱们要不要把号笛舞掺和进葬礼里去？」他开始用三倍于正速的调门哼起肖邦的《葬礼进行曲》，一面把那年轻生客一把搂进怀里，在人行道上跳了两三转两步舞，随即松开手，任他踉踉跄跄，一头撞上地下室的铁栏杆。
「不，我不是说家里的大宅。」冈布里尔说。「我是说我自己的屋子。就在近处。在大拉塞尔街。」
「我从不知道你还有自己的屋子，西奥多。」维维什夫人说。
「谁都不知道。」那凭什么现在就该让他们知道呢？就因为这风差不多像是乡下吹来的风？「那儿有酒。」他说。

===Original===
“Splendid!” cried the young man. They were all splendid people.
“There’s some gin,” said Gumbril.
“Capital aphrodisiac!” Coleman commented.
“Some light white wine.”
“Diuretic.”

===Chinese===
「太好了！」年轻人喊道。这些人个个都好极了。
「有些金酒。」冈布里尔说。
「上好的春药！」科尔曼评点道。
「有点儿清淡的白葡萄酒。」
「利尿剂。」

===Original===
“And some whisky.”
“The great emetic,” said Coleman. “Come on.” And he struck up the March of the Fascisti. “*Giovinezza, giovinezza, primavera di bellezza.﻿ ﻿…*” The noise went fading down the dark, empty streets.
The gin, the white wine, and even, for the sake of the young stranger, who wanted to sample everything, the emetic whisky, were produced.

===Chinese===
「还有些威士忌。」
「了不起的催吐剂。」科尔曼说。「走吧。」说罢他领唱起法西斯党的进行曲。「*Giovinezza, giovinezza, primavera di bellezza.*……（意大利语《青春颂》：青春，青春，美丽的春天……）」歌声沿着黑黢黢的空街，渐渐淡了下去。
金酒、白葡萄酒，连催吐的威士忌也都——看那位样样都要尝一尝的年轻生客的面子——端了上来。

===Original===
“I like your rooms,” said Mrs. Viveash, looking round her. “And I resent your secrecy about them, Theodore.”
“Drink, puppy!” Coleman refilled the boy’s glass.
“Here’s to secrecy,” Gumbril proposed. Shut it tightly, keep it dark, cover it up. Be silent, prevaricate, lie outright. He laughed and drank. “Do you remember,” he went on, “those instructive advertisements of Eno’s Fruit Salt they used to have when we were young? There was one little anecdote about a doctor who advised the hypochondriacal patient who had come to consult him, to go and see Grimaldi, the clown; and the patient answered, ‘I am Grimaldi.’ Do you remember?”
“No,” said Mrs. Viveash. “And why do you?”

===Chinese===
「我喜欢你这屋子。」维维什夫人环顾着四周说。「你对它守口如瓶，我可要见怪了，西奥多。」
「喝呀，小狗子！」科尔曼给孩子的杯子又斟满。
「为保密干杯。」冈布里尔举杯提议。把它关紧，把它捂黑，把它盖严。沉默，搪塞，索性撒谎。他笑了，喝了。「你们还记得么，」他接着说，「我们小时候常登的那些有益世道的伊诺果盐（Eno's Fruit Salt）广告？有一则小轶事，讲一位医生劝来求诊的疑病症病人，去看看丑角格里马尔迪（Grimaldi），病人却回答说：『我就是格里马尔迪。』记得么？」
「不记得。」维维什夫人说。「可你为什么记得？」

===Original===
“Oh, I don’t know. Or rather, I do know,” Gumbril corrected himself, and laughed again.
The young man suddenly began to boast. “I lost two hundred pounds yesterday playing *chemin de fer*,” he said, and looked round for applause.
Coleman patted his curly head. “Delicious child!” he said. “You’re positively Hogarthian.”

===Chinese===
「哦，说不上来。或者说，我其实说得上来。」冈布里尔更正道，又笑了。
年轻人忽然自夸起来。「我昨天玩*chemin de fer*（法语：铁路牌戏，近似百家乐的赌戏）输了两百镑。」他说，一面环顾四周，指望讨个喝彩。
科尔曼拍拍他的鬈发脑袋。「可口的孩子！」他说。「你简直是霍加斯（Hogarth，18世纪英国讽刺画家）笔下的人物。」

===Original===
Angrily, the boy pushed him away. “What are you doing?” he shouted; then turned and addressed himself once more to the others. “I couldn’t afford it, you know﻿—not a bloody penny of it. Not my money, either.” He seemed to find it exquisitely humorous. “And that two hundred wasn’t all,” he added, almost expiring with mirth.
“Tell Coleman how you borrowed his beard, Theodore.”
Gumbril was looking intently into his glass, as though he hoped to see in its pale mixture of gin and Sauterne visions, as in a crystal, of the future. Mrs. Viveash touched him on the arm and repeated her injunction.
“Oh, that!” said Gumbril rather irritably. “No. It isn’t an interesting story.”

===Chinese===
孩子气恼地推开他。「你干什么？」他嚷道；随即转过身，重新去应付其余的人。「你们要知道，这两百镑我可爱不起——他妈的一个子儿也赔不起。还都不是我自个儿的钱呢。」他似乎觉得这事妙不可言。「再说，那两百镑还不算完呢。」他补上一句，几乎乐得背过气去。
「西奥多，给科尔曼讲讲，你是怎么借他那副胡子的。」
冈布里尔正专心致志地凝视着自己的杯子，仿佛指望从那金酒兑苏玳（Sauterne）的浅色混合液里，像从水晶球里一样，看出未来的种种景象。
「哦，那个呀！」冈布里尔相当不耐烦地说。「不讲。那不是个有意思的故事。」

===Original===
“Oh yes, it is! I insist,” said Mrs. Viveash, commanding peremptorily from her deathbed.
Gumbril drank his gin and Sauterne. “Very well then,” he said reluctantly, and began.
“I don’t know what my governor will say,” the young man put in once or twice. But nobody paid any attention to him. He relapsed into a sulky and, it seemed to him, very dignified silence. Under the warm, jolly tipsiness he felt a chill of foreboding. He poured out some more whisky.
Gumbril warmed to his anecdote. Expiringly Mrs. Viveash laughed from time to time, or smiled her agonizing smile. Coleman whooped like a Redskin.

===Chinese===
「哦，有意思！我坚持要听。」维维什夫人说，在她的临终病榻上斩钉截铁地发号施令。
冈布里尔喝下他那杯金酒苏玳。「那么好吧。」他不情愿地说，开了头。
「不知道我老子会怎么说。」年轻人插了一两回嘴。可是谁也不理他。他重新堕入一种愠怒的——而且在他看来极有尊严的——沉默。在暖烘烘、乐陶陶的醉意底下，他觉出一阵不祥的寒意。他又给自己斟了些威士忌。
冈布里尔越讲越起劲。维维什夫人时时咽着气发笑，或者做出她那副折磨人的微笑。科尔曼像红番（Redskin，旧时对印第安人的称呼）似的怪声呐喊。

===Original===
“And after the concert to these rooms,” said Gumbril.
Well, let everything go. Into the mud. Leave it there, and let the dogs lift their hind legs over it as they pass.
“Ah! the genuine platonic fumblers,” commented Coleman.
“I am Grimaldi,” Gumbril laughed. Further than this it was difficult to see where the joke could go. There, on the couch, where Mrs. Viveash and Coleman were now sitting, she had lain sleeping in his arms.

===Chinese===
「听完音乐会，就到了这几间屋子。」冈布里尔说。
好吧，让一切都滚蛋吧。滚进泥里去。撂在那儿，让狗们路过的时候抬起后腿，从它上面撒过去。
「啊！真正柏拉图式的笨手笨脚。」科尔曼评点道。
「我就是格里马尔迪。」冈布里尔笑道。这玩笑再往前去还能走到哪儿，就很难说了。那儿，那张长榻上——此刻维维什夫人和科尔曼正坐在上面——她曾在他的怀抱里睡着过。

===Original===
“Towsing, in Elizabethan,” said Coleman.
Unreal, eternal in the secret darkness. A night that was an eternal parenthesis among the other nights and days.
“I feel I’m going to be sick,” said the young man suddenly. He had wanted to go on silently and haughtily sulking; but his stomach declined to take part in the dignified game.
“Good Lord!” said Gumbril, and jumped up. But before he could do anything effective, the young man had fulfilled his own prophecy.

===Chinese===
「搂抱轻薄（towsing），伊丽莎白时代的说法。」科尔曼说。
不真实，却在隐秘的黑暗里永恒。一个夜晚，成了其余昼夜之间的一只永恒的括号。
「我觉得我要吐了。」年轻人突然说。他本想继续默默地、高傲地生闷气；可他的胃不肯奉陪这场体面的游戏。
「天哪！」冈布里尔说着跳了起来。可不等他采取任何有效措施，年轻人已经应验了自己的预言。

===Original===
“The real charm about debauchery,” said Coleman philosophically, “is its total pointlessness, futility, and above all its incredible tediousness. If it really were all roses and exhilaration, as these poor children seem to imagine, it would be no better than going to church or studying the higher mathematics. I should never touch a drop of wine or another harlot again. It would be against my principles. I told you it was emetic,” he called to the young man.
“And what are your principles?” asked Mrs. Viveash.
“Oh, strictly ethical,” said Coleman.
“You’re responsible for this creature,” said Gumbril, pointing to the young man, who was sitting on the floor near the fireplace, cooling his forehead against the marble of the mantelpiece. “You must take him away. Really, what a bore!” His nose and mouth were all wrinkled up with disgust.

===Chinese===
「放荡真正的迷人之处，」科尔曼以哲人的口吻说，「就在它那彻头彻尾的无意义、无用处，尤其是它那叫人难以置信的乏味。倘若它真像这些可怜的孩子想象的那样，尽是玫瑰和销魂，那它就跟上教堂或者研究高等数学一样没什么可取的了。那我往后就再不沾一滴酒、再不碰一个窑姐儿了。那就违背我的原则了。我早说过，那是催吐的。」他朝年轻人那边喊道。
「那么，你的原则是什么呢？」维维什夫人问。
「哦，纯乎其纯的伦理原则。」科尔曼说。
「这个活宝归你负责。」冈布里尔说，一面指着那年轻人——他正坐在壁炉跟前的地板上，把额头贴着壁炉架的大理石取凉。「你必须把他带走。真是的，多腻烦人！」他的鼻子和嘴厌恶得皱作一团。

===Original===
“I’m sorry,” the young man whispered. He kept his eyes shut and his face was exceedingly pale.
“But with pleasure,” said Coleman. “What’s your name?” he asked the young man, “and where do you live?”
“My name is Porteous,” murmured the young man.
“Good lord!” cried Gumbril, letting himself fall on to the couch beside Mrs. Viveash. “That’s the last straw!”

===Chinese===
「对不住。」年轻人喃喃道。他一直闭着眼，脸白得吓人。
「乐意之至。」科尔曼说。「你叫什么名字？」他问那年轻人，「住在哪儿？」
「我叫波蒂厄斯。」年轻人喃喃道。
「我的老天！」冈布里尔喊道，一屁股倒进维维什夫人身边的长榻。「这真是最后一根稻草了！」

## XVII / XVII

===Original===

The two o’clock snorted out of Charing Cross, but no healths were drunk, this time, to Viscount Lascelles. A desiccating sobriety made arid the corner of the third-class carriage in which Gumbril was sitting. His thoughts were an interminable desert of sand, with not a palm in sight, not so much as a comforting mirage. Once again he fumbled in his breast-pocket, brought out and unfolded the flimsy paper. Once more he read. How many times had he read before?

===Chinese===

两点钟的那班车喷着响鼻驶出查令十字（Charing Cross）；这一回，可没有人为拉塞尔斯子爵（Viscount Lascelles）的健康举杯。一种抽干水分般的清醒，把冈布里尔所坐的三等车厢一角晒成了荒地。他的思绪是一片没有尽头的沙漠，放眼望不见一棵棕榈，连一丝聊以慰人的海市蜃楼也没有。他又一次在胸袋里摸索，掏出那页薄薄的信纸，展开来。又读了一遍。在此之前，他已经读过多少遍了？

===Original===

> “Your telegram made me very unhappy. Not merely because of the accident﻿—though it made me shudder to think that something terrible might have happened, poor darling﻿—but also, selfishly, my own disappointment. I had looked forward so much. I had made a picture of it all so clearly. I should have met you at the station with the horse and trap from the Chequers, and we’d have driven back to the cottage﻿—and you’d have loved the cottage. We’d have had tea and I’d have made you eat an egg with it after your journey. Then we’d have gone for a walk; through the most heavenly wood I found yesterday to a place where there’s a wonderful view﻿—miles and miles of it. And we’d have wandered on and on, and sat down under the trees, and the sun would have set, and the twilight would slowly have come to an end, and we’d have gone home again and found the lamps lighted and supper ready﻿—not very grand, I’m afraid, for Mrs. Vole isn’t the best of cooks. And then the piano; for there is a piano, and I had the tuner come specially from Hastings yesterday, so that it isn’t *so* bad now. And you’d have played; and perhaps I would have made my noises on it. And at last it would have been time for candles and bed. When I heard you were coming, Theodore, I told Mrs. Vole a lie about you. I said you were my husband, because she’s fearfully respectable, of course; and it would dreadfully disturb her if you weren’t. But I told myself that, too. I meant that you should be. You see, I tell you everything. I’m not ashamed. I wanted to give you everything I could, and then we should always be together, loving one another. And I should have been your slave, I should have been your property and lived inside your life. But you would always have had to love me.

> “And then, just as I was getting ready to go and call at the Chequers for the horse and trap, your telegram came. I saw the word ‘accident,’ and I imagined you all bleeding and smashed﻿—oh, dreadful, dreadful. But then, when you seemed to make rather a joke of it﻿—why did you say ‘a little indisposed?’ that seemed, somehow, so stupid, I thought﻿—and said you were coming tomorrow, it wasn’t that which upset me; it was the dreadful, dreadful disappointment. It was like a stab, that disappointment; it hurt so terribly, so unreasonably much. It made me cry and cry, so that I thought I should never be able to stop. And then, gradually, I began to see that the pain of the disappointment wasn’t unreasonably great. It wasn’t merely a question of your coming being put off for a day; it was a question of its being put off forever, of my never seeing you again. I saw that that accident had been something really arranged by Providence. It was meant to warn me and show me what I ought to do. I saw how hopelessly impracticable the happiness I had been imagining really was. I saw that you didn’t, you couldn’t love me in anything like the same way as I loved you. I was only a curious adventure, a new experience, a means to some other end. Mind, I’m not blaming you in the least. I’m only telling you what is true, what I gradually came to realize as true. If you’d come﻿—what then? I’d have given you everything, my body, my mind, my soul, my whole life. I’d have twisted myself into the threads of your life. And then, when in due course you wanted to make an end to this curious little adventure, you would have had to cut the tangle and it would have killed me; it would also have hurt you. At least I think it would. In the end, I thanked God for the accident which had prevented you coming. In this way, Providence lets us off very lightly﻿—you with a bruise or two (for I do hope it really is nothing, my precious darling), and me with a bruise inside, round the heart. But both will get well quite soon. And all our lives, we shall have an afternoon under the trees, an evening of music and in the darkness, a night, an eternity of happiness, to look back on. I shall go away from Robertsbridge at once. Goodbye, Theodore. What a long letter! The last you’ll ever get from me. The last﻿—what a dreadful hurting word that is. I shall take it to post at once, for fear, if I leave it, I may be weak enough to change my mind and let you come tomorrow. I shall take it at once, then I shall come home again and pack up and tell some new fib to Mrs. Vole. And after that, perhaps I shall allow myself to cry again. Goodbye.”

===Chinese===

> 「你的电报让我非常难过。不只是因为那场意外——虽说一想到兴许出了什么可怕的事，我就不寒而栗，可怜的宝贝——也是因为，自私地讲，我自己的失望。我原本那样地盼着。我把一切都在心里画得清清楚楚。我本要赶着从『棋盘客栈』（the Chequers）借来的马和轻便马车，到车站接你，我们就一路赶回小屋——你准会喜欢那小屋的。我们会喝下午茶，我会让你在旅途劳顿之后，就着茶吃下一个鸡蛋。然后我们会去散步；穿过我昨天发现的那片天堂也似的树林，走到一处景致绝佳的地方——几十里又几十里，望不到头。我们会一直漫游下去，在树下坐一坐，太阳会落山，暮色会慢慢地收尽，我们再回到家，会看见灯已点亮，晚饭已经备好——只怕算不得丰盛，因为沃尔太太（Mrs. Vole）算不得好厨子。然后是钢琴；屋里是有一架钢琴的，昨天我还特意从黑斯廷斯（Hastings）请来调琴师，如今它总算不至于*那么*糟了。你会弹琴；也许我也会到琴上弄出我的响动来。到最后，就到了点蜡烛、上床的时辰。西奥多，听说你要来的时候，我为你对沃尔太太撒了个谎。我说你是我丈夫，因为她这人，不用说，规矩体面得吓人；你要不是，准会把她搅得心神大乱。可这话，我也对自己说了。我是真心要你做我丈夫的。你瞧，我什么都告诉你。我不害臊。我想把我能给出的一切都给你，然后我们就永远在一起，彼此相爱。我本该做你的奴隶，做你的财产，活在你的生活里头。只是你却必须永远、永远爱我。

> 「后来，我正预备动身，到『棋盘客栈』去取马和车，你的电报到了。我看见『意外』两个字，就想象你浑身是血、摔得稀烂的模样——啊，可怕，可怕极了。可后来，见你拿它开了个不大不小的玩笑——你为什么要说『略感不适』？不知怎么，我觉得那显得多蠢——又说你明天来；叫我心乱的并不是这个，是那可怕的、可怕的失望。那失望像扎进来的一刀；疼得那么厉害，厉害得没有道理。我哭了又哭，哭得我以为再也停不住了。后来，渐渐地，我开始明白，这失望的疼并非大得没有道理。这不单是你的到来推迟一天的问题；是它永远推迟了、我再也见不到你的问题。我看出，那场意外实在是上天安排好的。它是来警告我、指给我看我该做什么的。我看出，我一直在想象的那份幸福，实在无可救药地行不通。我看出，你并不爱我，也不可能用我爱你的方式来爱我，连近似都谈不上。我不过是一场新奇的奇遇，一番新鲜的阅历，一件通向别的目的的凭借。请注意，我丝毫也没有怪你的意思。我只是把真的事告诉你，把我渐渐认清为真的事告诉你。你若是来了——又怎样呢？我会把一切都给你：我的身体，我的头脑，我的灵魂，我的整整一生。我会把自己拧进你生活的丝线里去。到那时，等时候到了，你想了结这段新奇的小小奇遇，你就不得不挥刀斩断这团乱麻，而那会杀了我；也会叫你疼的。至少我想是会的。到头来，我为那场拦住你、没让你来的意外，感谢了上帝。上天就是这样轻轻地放过了我们——你呢，带着一两处青肿（因为我真心希望实在没事，我的宝贝心肝），我呢，带着里面的一处青肿，就围着心口。不过两处都会很快见好。而我们一生一世，都将有这么一段树下的午后，一个有音乐、有黑暗的夜晚，一夜，一段永恒的幸福，可供回望。我这就离开罗伯茨布里奇（Robertsbridge）。再见了，西奥多。好长的一封信！这是你从我这里收到的最后一封了。最后一封——这个词多伤人，多可怕。我这就拿去寄，生怕搁下了，我会心软得改变主意，让你明天来。我这就去寄，然后回家来收拾行装，再对沃尔太太编个新的小瞎话。那之后，也许我会容许自己再哭一场。再见了。」

===Original===

Aridly, the desert of sand stretched out with not a tree and not even a mirage, except perhaps the vague and desperate hope that he might get there before she started, that she might conceivably have changed her mind. Ah, if only he’d read the letter a little earlier! But he hadn’t woken up before eleven, he hadn’t been down before half-past. Sitting at the breakfast-table, he had read the letter through.

The eggs and bacon had grown still colder, if that was possible, than they were. He had read it through, he had rushed to the A.B.C. There was no practicable train before the two o’clock.

If he had taken the seven-twenty-seven he would certainly have got there before she started. Ah, if only he had woken up a little earlier! But then he would have had to go to bed a little earlier. And in order to go to bed earlier, he would have had to abandon Mrs. Viveash before she had bored herself to that ultimate point of fatigue at which she did at last feel ready for repose. And to abandon Mrs. Viveash﻿—ah, that was really impossible, she wouldn’t allow herself to be left alone. If only he hadn’t gone to the London Library yesterday! A wanton, unnecessary visit it had been. For after all, the journey was short; he didn’t need a book for the train. And the *Life of Beckford*, for which he had asked, proved, of course, to be out﻿—and he had been utterly incapable of thinking of any other book, among the two or three hundred thousand on the shelves, that he wanted to read. And, in any case, what the devil did he want with a *Life of Beckford*? Hadn’t he his own life, the life of Gumbril, to attend to? Wasn’t one life enough, without making superfluous visits to the London Library in search of other lives? And then what a stroke of bad luck to have run into Mrs. Viveash at that very moment! What an abject weakness to have let himself be bullied into sending that telegram. “A little indisposed.﻿ ﻿…” Oh, my God! Gumbril shut his eyes and ground his teeth together; he felt himself blushing with a retrospective shame.

===Chinese===

干旱地，那片沙漠延展着，没有一棵树，连海市蜃楼也没有一个——除非把那点模糊而绝望的希望也算上：他也许赶在她动身之前到那里，她兴许回心转意了，也未可知。啊，要是早一点儿读到这封信就好了！可他十一点之前没醒，十一点半之前没下楼。他是坐在早餐桌上，把这封信从头到尾读完的。

鸡蛋和培根，倘若还可能更凉的话，比原先更凉了。他读完了信，便冲去查《ABC铁路指南》（the A.B.C.）。两点钟之前，没有一班可以坐的车。

他若是赶上了那班七点二十七分的车，必定在她动身之前就到了。啊，要是早醒一会儿就好了！可那样他就得早上床一会儿。而要早上床，他就得先丢下维维什夫人（Mrs. Viveash），趁她还没把自己腻烦到那终极的疲乏点——到了那一点，她才终于觉得可以就寝。而丢下维维什夫人——啊，那实在办不到，她是不容许自己被孤零零丢下的。昨天要是不去伦敦图书馆（London Library）就好了！那本是一次任性而无必要的造访。毕竟路程不长；坐这趟车根本用不着带书。而他点名要的那部《贝克福德传》（*Life of Beckford*），不用说，偏偏外借了——书架上那二三十万册书里，他又全然想不出另外哪一本是他想读的。再说，他要一部《贝克福德传》究竟见什么鬼？他自己那条命，冈布里尔的这条命，还照应不过来呢？一条性命还不够，非要多跑一趟伦敦图书馆、去搜罗别人的性命不成？偏偏又在那么个当口撞见维维什夫人，多晦气！竟由着自己被逼着拍出那么一封电报，多么下作的窝囊。「略感不适……」哦，我的上帝！冈布里尔闭紧双眼，咬紧牙关；他感到自己正因一股事后的羞惭而满面发烧。

===Original===

And of course it was quite useless taking the train, like this, to Robertsbridge. She’d be gone, of course. Still, there was always the desperate hope. There was the mirage across the desiccated plains, the mirage one knew to be deceptive and which, on a second glance, proved not even to be a mirage, but merely a few livery spots behind the eyes. Still, it was amply worth doing﻿—as a penance, and to satisfy the conscience and to deceive oneself with an illusion of action. And then the fact that he was to have spent the afternoon with Rosie and had put her off﻿—that too was highly satisfying. And not merely put her off, but﻿—ultimate clownery in the worst of deliriously bad taste﻿—played a joke on her. “Impossible come to you, meet me 213 Sloane Street, second floor, a little indisposed.” He wondered how she’d get on with Mr. Mercaptan; for it was to his rococo boudoir and Crébillon-souled sofa that he had on the spur of the clownish moment, as he dashed into the post office on the way to the station, sent her.

Aridly, the desiccated waste extended. Had she been right in her letter? Would it really have lasted no more than a little while, and ended as she prophesied, with an agonizing cutting of the tangle? Or could it be that she had held out the one hope of happiness? Wasn’t she perhaps the one unique being with whom he might have learned to await in quietness the final coming of that lovely terrible thing, from before the sound of whose secret footsteps more than once and oh! ignobly he had fled? He could not decide, it was impossible to decide until he had seen her again, till he had possessed her, mingled his life with hers. And now she had eluded him; for he knew very well that he would not find her. He sighed and looked out of the window.

===Chinese===

当然啦，像这样坐火车去罗伯茨布里奇，全然无用。她自然是走了。可绝望的希望总归还在。焦干的平原尽头有那么一泓蜃景，明知是骗人的蜃景，而且再看一眼，连蜃景也不是，不过是眼球后面几处肝色的斑点罢了。不过，这事仍然大值得做——算一场赎罪，好安抚良心，好用行动的幻觉骗一骗自己。还有，他本该今天下午同罗茜（Rosie）一起过，却把她搪塞掉了——这也叫人心满意足。而且何止搪塞——坏趣味坏到发狂地步的压轴丑角戏——还同她开了个玩笑。「今日不能前来赴晤，请来斯隆街（Sloane Street）213 号二楼见我，略感不适。」他不知道她同默卡普坦先生（Mr. Mercaptan）会处得怎样；因为赶车站途中冲进邮局的当口，他借着丑角兴头的一时冲动，把她打发去的地方，正是那位的洛可可闺房，和那张克雷比永式灵魂（Crébillon-souled）的沙发。

干旱地，那片焦干的荒原延展着。她信里的话对么？那一切当真撑不了多久，并且如她预言的那样，以一场惨痛的挥刀斩乱麻收场么？又或者，她递出的恰恰是那唯一的幸福希望？她会不会正是那独一无二的人儿，同她在一起，他或许能学会静静守候那又可爱又可怕的东西最后降临——那东西隐秘的脚步声还未响起，他就不止一次地、哦！没出息地逃走了？他无从决断；不见到她，不占有她，不把他的生命同她的生命交融，就无从决断。而如今她脱身走掉了；因为他心里明明白白：他找不到她了。他叹了口气，望向窗外。

===Original===

The train pulled up at a small suburban station. Suburban, for though London was already some way behind, the little sham half-timbered houses near the station, the newer tile and roughcast dwellings farther out on the slope of the hill proclaimed with emphasis the presence of the business man, the holder of the season ticket. Gumbril looked at them with a pensive disgust which must have expressed itself on his features; for the gentleman sitting in the corner of the carriage facing his, suddenly leaned forward, tapped him on the knee, and said, “I see you agree with me, sir, that there are too many people in the world.”

Gumbril, who up till now had merely been aware that somebody was sitting opposite him, now looked with more attention at the stranger. He was a large, square old gentleman of robust and flourishing appearance, with a face of wrinkled brown parchment and a white moustache that merged, in a handsome curve, with a pair of side whiskers, in a manner which reminded one of the photographs of the Emperor Francis Joseph.

“I perfectly agree with you, sir,” Gumbril answered. If he had been wearing his beard, he would have gone on to suggest that loquacious old gentlemen in trains are among the supernumeraries of the planet. As it was, however he spoke with courtesy, and smiled in his most engaging fashion.

“When I look at all these revolting houses,” the old gentleman continued, shaking his fist at the snuggeries of the season-ticket holders, “I am filled with indignation. I feel my spleen ready to burst, sir, ready to burst.”

“I can sympathize with you,” said Gumbril. “The architecture is certainly not very soothing.”

===Chinese===

火车在一个小小的市郊车站停了下来。市郊的，因为伦敦虽已落在身后一段路，车站近旁那些露明木架的假古董小房子，山坡更远处新起的瓦顶拉毛墙住宅，都在着力宣告着此间生意人的存在，季票持有人的存在。冈布里尔望着它们，一脸出神的厌恶；这厌恶想必已经浮上了他的眉眼；因为坐在车厢对面角落里的那位绅士，忽然探身向前，敲了敲他的膝盖，开口道：「我看出来，先生，您与我所见相同——世上的人实在太多了。」

冈布里尔此前只觉察到对面坐着个人，这时才比较留神地打量起这位陌生人来。他是个魁梧方正的老绅士，看上去硬朗旺健，一张脸像起皱的棕色羊皮纸，唇上一撇白髭须，顺着漂亮的弧线同一对连鬓颊须连成一气——那模样叫人想起弗朗茨·约瑟夫皇帝（Emperor Francis Joseph）的照片。

「我完全同意您的看法，先生。」冈布里尔答道。他若是戴着那把胡子，本会顺势提议：火车上健谈的老绅士，也在这个星球的多余人口之列。可眼下，他只是彬彬有礼地应答，笑得一副最讨人喜欢的样子。

「我一看见这些叫人作呕的房子，」老绅士接着说，朝季票住户们的小安乐窝挥着拳头，「胸中就充满义愤。我只觉得我的肝火快要胀破，先生，快要胀破了。」

「我是同情您的。」冈布里尔说。「这建筑确实不大能安神。」

===Original===

“It’s not the architecture I mind so much,” retorted the old gentleman, “that’s merely a question of art, and all nonsense so far as I’m concerned. What disgusts me is the people inside the architecture, the number of them, sir. And the way they breed. Like maggots, sir, like maggots. Millions of them, creeping about the face of the country, spreading blight and dirt wherever they go; ruining everything. It’s the people I object to.”

“Ah well,” said Gumbril, “if you will have sanitary conditions that don’t allow plagues to flourish properly; if you will tell mothers how to bring up their children, instead of allowing nature to kill them off in her natural way; if you will import unlimited supplies of corn and meat: what can you expect? Of course the numbers go up.”

The old gentleman waved all this away. “I don’t care what the causes are,” he said. “That’s all one to me. What I do object to, sir, is the effects. Why sir, I am old enough to remember walking through the delicious meadows beyond Swiss Cottage, I remember seeing the cows milked in West Hampstead, sir. And now, what do I see now, when I go there? Hideous red cities pullulating with Jews, sir. Pullulating with prosperous Jews. Am I right in being indignant, sir? Do I do well, like the prophet Jonah, to be angry?”

“You do, sir,” said Gumbril, with growing enthusiasm, “and the more so since this frightful increase in population is the world’s most formidable danger at the present time. With populations that in Europe alone expand by millions every year, no political foresight is possible. A few years of this mere bestial propagation will suffice to make nonsense of the wisest schemes of today﻿—or would suffice,” he hastened to correct himself, “if any wise schemes were being matured at the present.”

===Chinese===

「我在意的倒不全是那建筑，」老绅士反驳道，「那不过是艺术问题，依我看纯属扯淡。叫我恶心的是住在建筑里面的人，是他们的人数，先生。还有他们下崽的那个劲儿。像蛆一样，先生，像蛆一样。千百万个，在乡野的面上爬来爬去，走到哪儿就把枯败和肮脏带到哪儿；把一切都毁了。我反对的是人。」

「唉，是啊，」冈布里尔说，「您偏要有那不容瘟疫好好滋生的卫生条件；偏要教母亲们怎样带孩子，而不放任大自然按她自己的法子把孩子淘汰掉；偏要进口不限量的粮食和肉：还能指望什么呢？数目当然往上涨。」

老绅士把这一套统统挥手赶开。「我才不管什么原因不原因，」他说。「那在我全是一回事。我反对的是结果，先生。嘿，先生，我年纪大得很，还记得在瑞士小屋（Swiss Cottage）外那片可爱的草地上散步，还记得在西汉普斯特德（West Hampstead）看人挤牛奶呢，先生。如今呢，我再到那儿去，看见的是什么？红砖的丑城，滋滋蔓蔓全是犹太人，先生。滋滋蔓蔓全是兴旺发达的犹太人。我这样义愤填膺，愤慨得对么，先生？我像先知约拿（Jonah）那样发怒，发得合宜么？」

「发得合宜，先生。」冈布里尔说，热忱愈发高涨。「更何况，人口的这种可怕增长，正是当今世界最骇人的危险。光欧洲一地的人口就一年膨胀几百万，什么政治远见都无从谈起。照这种纯乎兽性的繁衍，不用几年，就够叫今天最明智的宏图统统沦为笑谈——或者说，」他忙着纠正自己，「就够叫它们统统沦为笑谈，倘若眼下真有什么明智的宏图正在酝酿的话。」

===Original===

“Very possibly, sir,” said the old gentleman, “but what I object to is seeing good cornland being turned into streets, and meadows, where cows used to graze, covered with houses full of useless and disgusting human beings. I resent seeing the country parcelled out into back gardens.”

“And is there any prospect,” Gumbril earnestly asked, “of our ever being able in the future to support the whole of our population? Will unemployment ever decrease?”

“I don’t know, sir,” the old gentleman replied. “But the families of the unemployed will certainly increase.”

===Chinese===

「很可能，先生，」老绅士说，「可我反对的是眼看上好的麦田变成街道，眼看昔日牛群吃草的草场，盖满住着无用而可憎的人类的房子。眼看乡村被瓜分成一小块一小块的后花园，我心怀愤懑。」

「那么，可有指望，」冈布里尔恳切地问，「我们将来总有一天能养活我们的全部人口么？失业总有一天会减少么？」

「我不知道，先生。」老绅士答道。「不过失业者的家庭肯定会增多。」

===Original===

“You are right, sir,” said Gumbril, “they will. And the families of the employed and the prosperous will as steadily grow smaller. It is regrettable that birth control should have begun at the wrong end of the scale. There seems to be a level of poverty below which it doesn’t seem worth while practising birth control, and a level of education below which birth control is regarded as morally wrong. Strange, how long it has taken for the ideas of love and procreation to dissociate themselves in the human mind. In the majority of minds they are still, even in this so-called twentieth century, indivisibly wedded. Still,” he continued hopefully, “progress is being made, progress is certainly, though slowly, being made. It is gratifying to find, for example, in the latest statistics, that the clergy, as a class, are now remarkable for the smallness of their families. The old jest is out of date. Is it too much to hope that these gentlemen may bring themselves in time to preach what they already practise?”

“It *is* too much to hope, sir,” the old gentleman answered with decision.

“You are probably right,” said Gumbril.

===Chinese===

「您说得对，先生。」冈布里尔说。「会增多的。而在业者和富足者的家庭，则会同样稳步地缩小。可惜节育偏偏是从标尺错误的那一端开始的。贫困似乎有一条水准线，线之下，节育似乎犯不上；教育也有一条水准线，线之下，节育被看作伤风败俗。说来奇怪，爱情与生育这两个观念，在人的头脑里分家，竟花了这么久。在大多数头脑里，哪怕在这个所谓的二十世纪，它们仍旧难分难舍地结着夫妻。不过，」他满怀希望地接着说，「进步正在进行，进步虽慢，却确乎在进行。譬如，从最新的统计里欣慰地发现，神职人员作为一个阶级，如今竟以家口之少而引人注目。那则老玩笑过时了。这些先生们迟早总肯把他们早已行惯的事拿到布道坛上去讲——这样指望，不算过分吧？」

「这样指望*就是*过分了，先生。」老绅士斩钉截铁地回答。

「您多半是对的。」冈布里尔说。

===Original===

“If we were all to preach all the things we all practise,” continued the old gentleman, “the world would soon be a pretty sort of bear-garden, I can tell you. Yes, and a monkey-house. And a wart-hoggery. As it is, sir, it is merely a place where there are too many human beings. Vice must pay its tribute to virtue, or else we are all undone.”

“I admire your wisdom, sir,” said Gumbril.

The old gentleman was delighted. “And I have been much impressed by your philosophical reflections,” he said. “Tell me, are you at all interested in old brandy?”

===Chinese===

「假使我们个个都把我们人人行着的那一套统统宣讲出来，」老绅士接着说，「这世界很快就会变成一个像模像样的斗熊场（bear-garden）了，我可以告诉您。对，还会是猴房。还会是疣猪圈。照现在这样，先生，它不过是个活人太多的地方。罪恶必须向美德纳贡，不然我们大家就都完了。」

「我钦佩您的智慧，先生。」冈布里尔说。

老绅士大为高兴。「您的哲理高论也叫我很受触动。」他说。「告诉我，您对陈年白兰地可有一点儿兴趣？」

===Original===

“Well, not philosophically,” said Gumbril. “As a mere empiric only.”

“As a mere empiric!” The old gentleman laughed. “Then let me beg you to accept a case. I have a cellar which I shall never drink dry, alas! before I die. My only wish is that what remains of it shall be distributed among those who can really appreciate it. In you, sir, I see a fitting recipient of a case of brandy.”

“You overwhelm me,” said Gumbril. “You are too kind, and, I may add, too flattering.” The train, which was a mortally slow one, came grinding for what seemed the hundredth time to a halt.

===Chinese===

「唔，从哲学上讲，谈不上。」冈布里尔说。「不过是当个纯粹的经验主义者罢了。」

「不过是当个纯粹的经验主义者！」老绅士笑了。「那就请容我求您收下一箱吧。我有一窖存酒，唉，到死也喝不干哪！我唯一的愿望，是剩下的那些能分给真正识货的人。在您身上，先生，我看见了一箱白兰地的一位合适受赠人。」

「您叫我受宠若惊。」冈布里尔说。「您太客气了，而且——容我补一句——太抬举我了。」这趟车慢得要命，磨磨蹭蹭，像是第一百次似的，又哐当一声停了下来。

===Original===

“Not at all,” said the old gentleman. “If you have a card, sir.”

Gumbril searched his pockets. “I have come without one.”

“Never mind,” said the old gentleman. “I think I have a pencil. If you will give me your name and address, I will have the case sent to you at once.”

Leisurely, he hunted for the pencil, he took out a notebook. The train gave a jerk forward.

===Chinese===

「哪里的话。」老绅士说。「您可带有名片，先生？」

冈布里尔摸遍了口袋。「我出来没带名片。」

「不妨事。」老绅士说。「我想我带着铅笔。您把姓名住址给我，我马上叫人把那箱酒给您送去。」

他从容不迫地摸找铅笔，掏出一个记事本。火车猛地朝前一挫。

===Original===

“Now, sir,” he said.

Gumbril began dictating. “Theodore,” he said slowly.

“The﻿—o﻿—dore,” the old gentleman repeated, syllable by syllable.

===Chinese===

「好了，先生。」他说。

冈布里尔开始口述。「西奥多。」他慢慢地说。

「西——奥——多。」老绅士一个音节一个音节地复述着。

===Original===

The train crept on, with slowly gathering momentum, through the station. Happening to look out of the window at this moment, Gumbril saw the name of the place painted across a lamp. It was Robertsbridge. He made a loud, inarticulate noise, flung open the door of the compartment, stepped out on to the footboard and jumped. He landed safely on the platform, staggered forward a few paces with his acquired momentum and came at last to a halt. A hand reached out and closed the swinging door of his compartment and, an instant afterwards, through the window, a face that, at a distance, looked more than ever like the face of the Emperor Francis Joseph, looked back towards the receding platform. The mouth opened and shut; no words were audible. Standing on the platform, Gumbril made a complicated pantomime, signifying his regret by shrugging his shoulders and placing his hand on his heart; urging in excuse for his abrupt departure the necessity under which he laboured of alighting at this particular station﻿—which he did by pointing at the name on the boards and lamps, then at himself, then at the village across the fields. The old gentleman waved his hand, which still held, Gumbril noticed, the notebook in which he had been writing. Then the train carried him out of sight. There went the only case of old brandy he was ever likely to possess, thought Gumbril sadly, as he turned away. Suddenly, he remembered Emily again; for a long time he had quite forgotten her.

===Chinese===

火车带着缓缓聚拢的冲力，爬行着穿过车站。恰在这时，冈布里尔往窗外一望，看见一盏灯上横漆着此地的地名。是罗伯茨布里奇。他发出一声响亮而含混的喊叫，甩开车厢门，跨上踏板，跳了下去。他稳稳落在站台上，借着方才带上的冲力踉跄抢了几步，终于站定。一只手伸出来，关上了他那节车厢来回晃荡的门；紧接着，隔着车窗，一张脸回望着渐渐退去的站台——远看越发像弗朗茨·约瑟夫皇帝的脸了。那张嘴一张一合；一个字也听不见。冈布里尔站在站台上，打了一整套复杂的哑剧：耸耸肩、手抚胸口，表示他的歉意；又指指站牌和灯上的地名，指指自己，再指指田野那头的村庄，申明自己身不由己、非在这个车站下车不可，以求为这仓促的离去开脱。老绅士挥了挥手，冈布里尔注意到，那只手里还握着方才写字用的记事本。随后，火车把他载出了视野。他这辈子唯一有望到手的一箱陈年白兰地，就这么去了，冈布里尔一边转身走开，一边黯然想道。蓦地，他又想起了艾米莉（Emily）；好半天来，他已把她忘了个干净。

===Original===

The cottage, when at last he found it, proved to be fully as picturesque as he had imagined. And Emily, of course, had gone, leaving, as might have been expected, no address. He took the evening train back to London. The aridity was now complete, and even the hope of a mirage had vanished. There was no old gentleman to make a diversion. The size of clergymen’s families, even the fate of Europe, seemed unimportant now, were indeed perfectly indifferent to him.

===Chinese===

那幢小屋，他终于找到时，果然同他想象的一样如画。而艾米莉，不用说，已经走了，并且正如意料之中，没有留下地址。他搭傍晚的火车回了伦敦。荒旱如今圆满了，连蜃楼的指望也消失了。再没有一位老绅士来岔开他的心思。神职人员家庭的规模，甚至欧洲的命运，此刻都显得无关紧要，的确，与他全然无关了。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
Two hundred and thirteen Sloane Street. The address, Rosie reflected, as she vaporized synthetic lilies of the valley over all her sinuous person, was decidedly a good one. It argued a reasonable prosperity, attested a certain distinction. The knowledge of his address confirmed her already high opinion of the bearded stranger who had so surprisingly entered her life, as though in fulfilment of all the fortune-tellers’ prophecies that ever were made; had entered, yes, and intimately made himself at home. She had been delighted, when the telegram came that morning, to think that at last she was going to find out something more about this man of mystery. For dark and mysterious he had remained, remote even in the midst of the most intimate contacts. Why, she didn’t even know his name. “Call me Toto,” he had suggested, when she asked him what it was. And Toto she had had to call him, for lack of anything more definite or committal. But today he was letting her further into his secret. Rosie was delighted. Her pink underclothing, she decided, as she looked in the long glass, was really ravishing. She examined herself, turning first one way, then the other, looking over her shoulder to see the effect from behind. She pointed a toe, bent and straightened a knee, applauding the length of her legs (“Most women,” Toto had said, “are like dachshunds”), their slenderness and plump suavity of form. In their white stockings of Milanese silk they looked delicious; and how marvellously, by the way, those Selfridge people had mended those stockings by their new patent process! Absolutely like new, and only charged four shillings. Well, it was time to dress. Goodbye, then, to the pink underclothing and the long white legs. She opened the wardrobe door. The moving glass reflected, as it swung through its half-circle, pink bed, rose-wreathed walls, little friends of her own age, and the dying saint at his last communion. Rosie selected the frock she had bought the other day at one of those little shops in Soho, there they sell such smart things so cheaply to a clientage of minor actresses and cocottes. Toto hadn’t seen it yet. She looked extremely distinguished in it. The little hat, with its inch of veil hanging like a mask, unconcealing and inviting, from the brim, suited her to perfection. One last dab of powder, one last squirt of synthetic lilies of the valley, and she was ready. She closed the door behind her. St. Jerome was left to communicate in the untenanted pinkness.
===Chinese===
斯隆街 213 号。罗茜一面往自己蜿蜒周身喷洒合成铃兰香水，一面寻思：这地址实在是好。它昭示着一份过得去的富足，证明着一种相当的气派。对那位蓄须的陌生人的既有好评，因知道了他的地址而愈发巩固——此人如此出人意料地闯进她的生活，仿佛应验了历来一切算命先生的预言；闯了进来，是的，还熟门熟路地安顿了下来。今晨电报一到，她满心欢喜，心想总算要打听到这位神秘人物的一点底细了。因为即使在最亲昵的接触之中，他依旧神秘而疏远。可不是，连他的名字她都不知道。「叫我托托（Toto）吧。」她问他名字时，他这么提议。她也只好托托托托地叫，因为再没有更明确、更担干系的称呼可用了。但今天他要让她进一步走进他的秘密。罗茜喜不自胜。照着落地长镜，她断定自己那身粉色内衣实在销魂。她前后左右打量自己，回眸看背后的效果。她绷直脚尖，屈膝又伸直，为自己的腿长喝彩（「大多数女人，」托托说过，「都像腊肠狗」），为那双腿的纤细与丰润合度喝彩。穿着米兰丝白袜，它们看着令人垂涎；顺便说一句，塞尔弗里奇那班人用新专利工艺织补的袜子，多么神妙！同新的一模一样，只收四先令。好，该穿衣裳了。那么，再会吧，粉色内衣和修长白腿。她开开衣柜门。那面移动的穿衣镜旋过半圆，映出粉色床铺、玫瑰环绕的墙壁、与她年纪相仿的小朋友，还有领最后圣餐的垂死圣徒。罗茜拣出前两天在苏活区一家小铺子买的连衣裙——那种专为二三流女演员和交际花效劳、东西时新价钱又贱的小铺子。托托还没见过它。穿上它，她显得极其出众。那顶小帽，帽檐垂下一寸面纱，像一副面具，遮不住什么，反倒邀人来看，衬她衬到了十分。最后扑一拍粉，最后喷一注合成铃兰，她便齐备了。她随手带上门。圣哲罗姆被留在无人承租的粉红里独自领圣餐。

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan sat at his writing-table﻿—an exquisitely amusing affair in papier-mâché, inlaid with floral decorations in mother-of-pearl and painted with views of Windsor Castle and Tintern in the romantic manner of Prince Albert’s later days﻿—polishing to its final and gem-like perfection one of his middle articles. It was on a splendid subject﻿—the “Jus Primæ Noctis, or Droit du Seigneur”﻿—“that delicious *droit*,” wrote Mr. Mercaptan, “on which, one likes to think, the Sovereigns of England insist so firmly in their motto, *Dieu et mon Droit﻿—de Seigneur*.” That was charming, Mr. Mercaptan thought, as he read it through. And he liked that bit which began elegiacally: “But, alas! the Right of the First Night belongs to a Middle Age as mythical, albeit happily different, as those dismal epochs invented by Morris or by Chesterton. The Lord’s right, as we prettily imagine it, is a figment of the baroque imagination of the seventeenth century. It never existed. Or at least it did exist, but as something deplorably different from what we love to picture it.” And he went on, eruditely, to refer to that Council of Carthage which, in 398, demanded of the faithful that they should be continent on their wedding-night. It was the Lord’s right﻿—the *droit* of a heavenly Seigneur. On this text of fact, Mr. Mercaptan went on to preach a brilliant sermon on that melancholy sexual perversion known as continence. How much happier we all should be if the real historical *droit du Seigneur* had in fact been the mythical right of our “pretty prurient imaginations”! He looked forward to a golden age when all should be seigneurs possessing rights that should have broadened down into universal liberty. And so on. Mr. Mercaptan read through his creation with a smile of satisfaction on his face. Every here and there he made a careful correction in red ink. Over “pretty prurient imaginations” his pen hung for a full minute in conscientious hesitation. Wasn’t it perhaps a little too strongly alliterative, a shade, perhaps, cheap? Perhaps “pretty lascivious” or “delicate prurient” would be better. He repeated the alternatives several times, rolling the sound of them round his tongue, judicially, like a tea-taster. In the end, he decided that “pretty prurient” was right. “Pretty prurient”﻿—they were the *mots justes*, decidedly, without a question.
===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生坐在他的写字台前——一件纸胎质地的雅谑绝伦的家什，嵌着螺钿花卉，按阿尔伯特亲王晚期的浪漫风格绘着温莎堡与廷腾寺的景致——正把他的一篇中栏小品打磨到最终的宝石般完美。题目辉煌——「初夜权（Jus Primæ Noctis, or Droit du Seigneur）」——「那美妙的权利（droit）」，默卡普坦先生写道，「人们乐意相信，英格兰的历代君主正是在他们的箴言里对它坚持得那样坚决：Dieu et mon Droit——de Seigneur（神与我的权利——领主的权利）。」妙极了，默卡普坦先生通读一遍，想道。他也喜欢那段哀歌式的开头：「可是，唉！初夜权属于一个同莫里斯或切斯特顿虚构的那些阴郁时代一样神话般的中世纪——虽说幸而性质不同。我们如此美妙地想象出来的领主权利，是十七世纪巴洛克想象的捏造物。它从未存在过。或者说它确实存在过，但可叹地全然不同于我们喜爱描绘的样子。」接着他掉书袋，援引公元 398 年的迦太基主教会议——那次会议要求信众在洞房之夜持守禁欲。那是主的权柄——一位天上领主的 droit。在这条事实的经文上，默卡普坦先生继续布道，就那种名为「节欲」的可悲性倒错发表了一篇华丽的讲章。倘若真实历史上的领主权当真是我们「娇俏而好色的想象」里那种神话权利，我们大家该多么幸福！他展望一个黄金时代：人人皆领主，其权利将拓宽而下垂，直到化为普遍的自由。诸如此类。默卡普坦先生面带满意的微笑通读了自己的造物。时不时地，他用红墨水细细改上一处。在「娇俏而好色的想象」上方，他的笔悬了足足一分钟，良心不安地踌躇着。这几个词的音，会不会稍嫌过重、稍嫌廉价？或许「娇俏而淫佚」或「精微而好色」更好些。他把备选各念了几遍，像品茶师一样把声音在舌上滚来滚去，斟酌裁定。末了，他认定「娇俏而好色」是对的。「娇俏而好色」——正是必然之词（mots justes），毫无疑问。

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan had just come to this decision and his poised pen was moving farther down the page, when he was disturbed by the sound of arguing voices in the corridor, outside his room.

“What is it, Mrs. Goldie?” he called irritably, for it was not difficult to distinguish his housekeeper’s loud and querulous tones. He had given orders that he was not to be disturbed. In these critical moments of correction one needed such absolute tranquillity.

But Mr. Mercaptan was to have no tranquillity this afternoon. The door of his sacred boudoir was thrown rudely open, and there strode in, like a Goth into the elegant marble vomitorium of Petronius Arbiter, a haggard and dishevelled person whom Mr. Mercaptan recognized, with a certain sense of discomfort, as Casimir Lypiatt.

“To what do I owe the *pleasure* of this unexpected﻿ ﻿… ?” Mr. Mercaptan began with an essay in offensive courtesy.

But Lypiatt, who had no feeling for the finer shades, coarsely interrupted him. “Look here, Mercaptan,” he said. “I want to have a talk with you.”

“Delighted, I’m sure,” Mr. Mercaptan replied. “And *what*, may I ask, about?” He knew, of course, perfectly well; and the prospect of the talk disturbed him.
===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生刚下完这个裁定，悬着的笔正向页面更下方移去，走廊里、他的房门外，一阵争执的人声搅扰了他。

「什么事，戈尔迪太太？」他恼怒地喊道，因为不难分辨出女管家那高亢而嘟囔的嗓门。他早有吩咐，不许打扰。改稿的这种关键时刻，人需要绝对的清静。

但这个下午默卡普坦先生注定没有清静。他那神圣闺房的门被粗暴地推开，闯进一个人来——像哥特人闯进佩特罗尼乌斯那雅致的大理石呕吐厅——形容枯槁、头发散乱，默卡普坦先生带着几分不自在认出，此人是卡齐米尔·利皮亚特。

「我有何德何能，竟享此不速之……荣幸？」默卡普坦先生先用进攻性的客套开了个头。

但利皮亚特没有细腻层次的感受力，粗鲁地打断了他。「听着，默卡普坦，」他说，「我要同你谈谈。」

「不胜荣幸，」默卡普坦先生答道，「那么请问，谈什么？」他当然心知肚明；这场谈话的前景搅得他不安。

===Original===
“About this,” said Lypiatt; and he held out what looked like a roll of paper.

Mr. Mercaptan took the roll and opened it out. It was a copy of the *Weekly World*. “Ah!” said Mr. Mercaptan, in a tone of delighted surprise, “*The World*. You have read my little article?”

“That was what I wanted to talk to you about,” said Lypiatt.

Mr. Mercaptan modestly laughed. “It hardly deserves it,” he said.

Preserving a calm of expression which was quite unnatural to him, and speaking in a studiedly quiet voice, Lypiatt pronounced with careful deliberation: “It is a disgusting, malicious, ignoble attack on me,” he said.

“Come, *come*!” protested Mr. Mercaptan. “A critic must be allowed to criticize.”
===Chinese===
「谈这个。」利皮亚特说着，递上一卷像是纸卷的东西。

默卡普坦先生接过纸卷展开。是一份《每周世界报》。「啊！」默卡普坦先生用惊喜的腔调说，「《世界报》。您读了我那篇小文章？」

「我正要同你谈它。」利皮亚特说。

默卡普坦先生谦逊地笑了。「它当不起您的抬举。」他说。

利皮亚特维持着一种全然不合他本性的表面平静，用刻意放轻的嗓音字斟句酌地宣判：「那是一篇针对我的卑劣、恶毒、下流的攻击。」他说。

「得了，得了！」默卡普坦先生抗议，「总得允许批评家批评嘛。」

===Original===
“But there are limits,” said Lypiatt.

“Oh, I *quite* agree,” Mr. Mercaptan eagerly conceded. “But, after all, Lypiatt, you can’t pretend that I have come anywhere near those limits. If I had called you a *mur*derer, or even an *adul*terer﻿—then, I admit, you would have some cause to complain. But I haven’t. There’s nothing like a personality in the whole thing.”

Lypiatt laughed derisively, and his face went all to pieces, like a pool of water into which a stone is suddenly dropped.

“You’ve merely said I was insincere, an actor, a mountebank, a quack, raving fustian, spouting mock heroics. That’s all.”
===Chinese===
「但批评有界限。」利皮亚特说。

「哦，我完全同意，」默卡普坦先生热切地让步，「不过话说回来，利皮亚特，你总不能装作我已经逼近了那些界限。倘若我骂你是杀人凶手（murderer），或者哪怕是奸夫（adulterer）——那么我承认，你尚有几分抱怨的理由。可我没有。整篇文章里没有一处像样的人身攻击。」

利皮亚特讥诮地笑了，他的脸整个碎裂开来，像忽然被投进一颗石子的水潭。

「你只不过说我不真诚、是个戏子、卖艺的、卖假药的，咆哮浮言、喷吐伪英雄气概。仅此而已。」

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan put on the expression of one who feels himself injured and misunderstood. He shut his eyes, he flapped deprecatingly with his hand. “I *merely* suggested,” he said, “that you protest *too* much. You defeat your own ends; you lose emphasis by trying to be over-emphatic. All this *folie de grandeur*, all this hankering after *terribiltà*﻿—” sagely Mr. Mercaptan shook his head, “it’s led so *many* people astray. And, in any case, you can’t *really* expect *me* to find it very sympathetic.” Mr. Mercaptan uttered a little laugh and looked affectionately round his boudoir, his retired and perfumed poutery within whose walls so much civilization had finely flowered. He looked at his magnificent sofa, gilded and carved, upholstered in white satin, and so deep﻿—for it was a great square piece of furniture, almost as broad as it was long﻿—that when you sat right back, you had of necessity to lift your feet from the floor and recline at length. It was under the white satin that Crébillon’s spirit found, in these late degenerate days, a sympathetic home. He looked at his exquisite Condor fans over the mantelpiece; his lovely Marie Laurencin of two young girls, pale-skinned and berry-eyed, walking embraced in a shallow myopic landscape amid a troop of bounding heraldic dogs. He looked at his cabinet of *bibelots* in the corner where the nigger mask and the superb Chinese phallus in sculptured rock crystal contrasted so amusingly with the Chelsea china, the little ivory Madonna, which might be a fake, but in any case was quite as good as any medieval French original, and the Italian medals. He looked at his comical writing-desk in shining black papier-mâché and mother-of-pearl; he looked at his article on the “Jus Primæ Noctis,” black and neat on the page, with the red corrections attesting his tireless search for, and his, he flattered himself, almost invariable discovery of, the inevitable word. No, really, one couldn’t expect *him* to find Lypiatt’s notions very sympathetic.
===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生摆出一副受了伤、被误解的神情。他闭起眼睛，不爱听似的摆着手。「我只不过是暗示，」他说，「你抗议得太过了。你毁掉自己的目的；想过分强调，反而失了强调。这一切自大狂（folie de grandeur），这一切对可怖性（terribiltà）的渴慕——」默卡普坦先生睿智地摇了摇头，「把许许多多人引入了歧途。况且，你总不能当真指望我觉着它有多可爱吧。」默卡普坦先生轻笑一声，钟爱地环顾他的闺房——他这退隐而熏香的安乐窝，多少文明曾在它的四壁间精巧地开放。他望着那张华贵的沙发，描金雕花、白缎蒙面，又深——那是件巨大的方形家什，宽几乎等于长——你一旦坐到深处，便不得不把两脚抬离地板，整个人躺展了去。正是在这白缎子底下，克雷比翁的精魂在这些晚近颓败的日子里找到了投契的居所。他望着壁炉上方那几柄精美的神鹰羽扇；他那幅可爱的玛丽·洛朗森：两个少女，肤色素净、一双浆果眼，在一片浅浅近视的风景里相拥而行，身边绕着一群腾跃的纹章犬。他望着墙角那柜小摆设：黑人木雕面具和那件雕工精湛的水晶中国阳物，同切尔西瓷偶、小象牙圣母——也许是个赝品，但无论如何绝不逊于任何法国中世纪的原作——还有意大利徽章摆在一处，相映成趣。他望着他那滑稽的黑亮纸胎嵌螺钿写字台；他望着他那篇《初夜权》，黑字齐整地卧在纸上，红色的改动处见证着他对必然之词的不倦搜寻——以及（他暗自得意）近乎从不落空的发现。不，说真的，总不能指望他觉得利皮亚特的那套想法有多可爱。

===Original===
“But I don’t expect you to,” said Lypiatt, “and, good God! I don’t want you to. But you call me insincere. That’s what I can’t and won’t stand. How dare you do that?” His voice was growing louder.

Once more Mr. Mercaptan deprecatingly flapped. “At the most,” he corrected, “I said that there was a certain look of insincerity about some of the pictures. Hardly avoidable, indeed, in work of this kind.”

Quite suddenly, Lypiatt lost his self-control. All the accumulated anger and bitterness of the last days burst out. His show had been a hopeless failure. Not a picture sold, a press that was mostly bad, or, when good, that had praised for the wrong, the insulting reasons. “Bright and effective work.” “Mr. Lypiatt would make an excellent stage designer.” Damn them! damn them! And then, when the dailies had all had their yelp, here was Mercaptan in the *Weekly World* taking him as a text for what was practically an essay on insincerity in art. “How dare you?” he furiously shouted. “You﻿—how dare you talk about sincerity? What can you know about sincerity, you disgusting little bug!” And avenging himself on the person of Mr. Mercaptan against the world that had neglected him, against the fate that had denied him his rightful share of talent, Lypiatt sprang up and, seizing the author of the “Jus Primæ Noctis” by the shoulders, he shook him, he bumped him up and down in his chair, he cuffed him over the head. “How can you have the impudence,” he asked, letting go of his victim, but still standing menacingly over him, “to touch anything that even attempts to be decent and big?” All these years, these wretched years of poverty and struggle and courageous hope and failure and repeated disappointment; and now this last failure, more complete than all. He was trembling with anger; at least one forgot unhappiness while one was angry.

Mr. Mercaptan had recovered from his first terrified surprise. “Really, *really*,” he repeated, “*too* barbarous. Scuffling like hobbledehoys.”

“If you knew,” Lypiatt began; but he checked himself. If you knew, he was going to say, what those things had cost me, what they meant, what thought, what passion﻿—But how could Mercaptan understand? And it would sound as though he were appealing to this creature’s sympathy. “Bug!” he shouted instead, “bug!” And he struck out again with the flat of his hand. Mr. Mercaptan put up his hands and ducked away from the slaps, blinking.

“Really,” he protested, “*really*.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
「我也没指望你觉得可爱，」利皮亚特说，「而且，老天！我也不要你觉得可爱。可你说我不真诚。这是我不能容忍、也不会容忍的。你怎么敢？」他的嗓门越来越高。

默卡普坦先生又一次不爱听地摆手。「至多，」他纠正道，「我说的是其中几幅画带一点不真诚的样子。的确，这类作品里在所难免。」

利皮亚特骤然失去了自制。连日积攒的怒火与怨苦一齐爆发。他的画展惨败得一塌糊涂。一幅没卖出，报纸一片劣评，偶有好评，也夸得阴差阳错、明褒实贬。「明快而有效的作品。」「利皮亚特先生会是一位出色的舞台设计师。」该死的！统统该死！而当日报们叫唤完毕，默卡普坦又在《每周世界报》上拿他当题面，实际上写了一篇论艺术中不真诚的文章。「你怎么敢？」他狂怒地喊，「你——你怎么敢谈真诚？你能懂得什么真诚，你这恶心的臭虫！」利皮亚特把对冷落了他的世界、对没分给他应得天赋的命运的怨恨，一齐发泄在默卡普坦先生的肉身上：他跳起来，揪住这位《初夜权》作者的双肩，摇晃他，把他连人带椅子颠得上下磕碰，抡掌扇他的头。「你怎么好意思，」他松开猎物、仍旧威逼地立在他上方，问道，「碰任何哪怕想正派一点、想大一点的东西？」这些年，这些贫穷、挣扎、勇敢的希望、失败和一再失望的可怜年头；而如今这最后一败，比历次都彻底。他气得发抖——人在发怒的时候，至少能把不幸忘掉。

默卡普坦先生已从起初吓坏了的惊愕中缓过来。「真是的，真是的，」他一迭声道，「太野蛮了。像野小子似的扭打。」

「你要是知道——」利皮亚特开口，又忍住了。你要是知道，他本要说，那些东西花了我什么代价、对我意味着什么、多少思索、多少激情——可默卡普坦怎么懂得？而且那听起来倒像在向这东西乞怜。「臭虫！」他改口喊，「臭虫！」又扬起手掌砍下去。默卡普坦先生举起双手，闪躲着掌锋，直眨眼。

「真是的，」他抗议道，「真是的……」

===Original===
Insincere? Perhaps it was half true. Lypiatt seized his man more furiously than before and shook him, shook him. “And then that vile insult about the vermouth advertisement,” he cried out. That had rankled. Those flaring, vulgar posters! “You thought you could mock me and spit at me with impunity, did you? I’ve stood it so long, you thought I’d always stand it? Was that it? But you’re mistaken.” He lifted his fist. Mr. Mercaptan cowered away, raising his arm to protect his head. “Vile bug of a coward,” said Lypiatt, “why don’t you defend yourself like a man? You can only be dangerous with words. Very witty and spiteful and cutting about those vermouth posters, wasn’t it? But you wouldn’t dare to fight me if I challenged you.”
===Chinese===
不真诚吗？也许有一半是真的。利皮亚特比先前更狂暴地揪住他的人，摇他，摇他。「还有苦艾酒广告那句下流侮辱，」他嚷道。那句最扎心。那些艳俗刺目的招贴！「你以为可以不受惩罚地嘲弄我、唾我，是吧？我忍了这么久，你以为我会一直忍下去？是不是？可你打错了主意。」他举起拳头。默卡普坦先生缩着躲开，抬起胳膊护住脑袋。「卑鄙的胆小臭虫，」利皮亚特说，「你怎么不像个男人一样自卫？你只会在字句上伤人。拿那些苦艾酒招贴开的玩笑，够俏皮、够刻薄了吧？可我要是向你挑战，你是不敢同我打的。」

===Original===
“Well, as a matter of *fact*,” said Mr. Mercaptan, peering up from under his defences, “I didn’t invent *that* particular piece of criticism. I borrowed the *apéritif*.” He laughed feebly, more canary than bull.

“You borrowed it, did you?” Lypiatt contemptuously repeated. “And who from, may I ask?” Not that it interested him in the least to know.

“Well, if you really *want* to know,” said Mr. Mercaptan, “it was from our friend Myra Viveash.”

Lypiatt stood for a moment without speaking, then putting his menacing hand in his pocket, he turned away. “Oh!” he said noncommittally, and was silent again.

Relieved, Mr. Mercaptan sat up in his chair; with the palm of his right hand he smoothed his dishevelled head.
===Chinese===
「这个嘛，说实在的，」默卡普坦先生从掩体底下窥上来，「那条批评并不是我发明的。那杯开胃酒（apéritif）是我借来的。」他干笑了一声，金丝雀的成分多，公牛的成分少。

「你借来的，是吗？」利皮亚特鄙夷地重复。「那么请问，向谁借的？」其实他毫无兴趣知道。

「好吧，你要真想知道，」默卡普坦先生说，「是向咱们的朋友迈拉·维维什。」

利皮亚特默默立了片刻，随后把那只威吓的手插进口袋，转过身去。「哦。」他不含可否地说，又沉默了。

默卡普坦先生松了口气，在椅子里坐直；用右掌抹平了蓬乱的头发。

===Original===
Airily, outside in the sunshine, Rosie walked down Sloane Street, looking at the numbers on the doors of the houses. A hundred and ninety-nine, two hundred, two hundred and one﻿—she was getting near now. Perhaps all the people who passed, strolling so easily and elegantly and disengagedly along, perhaps they all of them carried behind their eyes a secret, as delightful and amusing as hers. Rosie liked to think so; it made life more exciting. How nonchalantly distinguished, Rosie reflected, she herself must look. Would anyone who saw her now, sauntering along like this, would anyone guess that, ten houses farther down the street, a young poet, or at least very nearly a young poet, was waiting, on the second floor, eagerly for her arrival? Of course they wouldn’t and couldn’t guess! That was the fun and the enormous excitement of the whole thing. Formidable in her lighthearted detachment, formidable in the passion which at will she could give rein to and check again, the great lady swam beautifully along through the sunlight to satisfy her caprice. Like Diana, she stooped over the shepherd boy. Eagerly the starving young poet waited, waited in his garret. Two hundred and twelve, two hundred and thirteen. Rosie looked at the entrance and was reminded that the garret couldn’t after all be very sordid, nor the young poet absolutely starving. She stepped in and, standing in the hall, looked at the board with the names. Ground floor: Mrs. Budge. First floor: F. de M. Rowbotham. Second floor: P. Mercaptan.
===Chinese===
罗茜轻盈地走在屋外的阳光里，顺着斯隆街一路数着门牌。一百九十九，二百，二百零一——快到了。也许所有这些从容、优雅、了无牵挂地散步而过的人，眼睛后面都揣着一个秘密，同她的一样可爱、一样有趣。罗茜乐意这么想；这让生活更带劲。她寻思，自己这会儿看上去该是多么淡定的名媛气派。看见她这般闲逛的人，谁会猜到，再往前十户门的三层楼上，一位年轻诗人——或者至少差一点是年轻诗人——正热切地等她到来？他们当然猜不到，也不可能猜到！这正是整件事的妙处和莫大的刺激。她是无忧无虑的冷艳中显威仪、在随心放纵又随心勒住的激情中显威仪的贵妇，一路优美地游过阳光，去满足她的一时兴致。像狄安娜，俯身向牧羊少年。饥饿的年轻诗人在阁楼里热切等待，等待。二百一十二，二百一十三。罗茜望望门厅，想起这阁楼想必终究不会太寒酸，年轻诗人也绝非真的在挨饿。她跨进门，立在门厅里看那块名牌。底层：巴奇太太。二层：F. 德. M. 罗博瑟姆。三层：P. 默卡普坦。

===Original===
P. Mercaptan.﻿ ﻿… But it was a charming name, a romantic name, a real young poet’s name! Mercaptan﻿—she felt more than ever pleased with her selection. The fastidious lady could not have had a happier caprice. Mercaptan﻿ ﻿… Mercaptan.﻿ ﻿… She wondered what the P. stood for. Peter, Philip, Patrick, Pendennis even? She could hardly have guessed that Mr. Mercaptan’s father, the eminent bacteriologist, had insisted, thirty-four years ago, on calling his firstborn “Pasteur.”
===Chinese===
P. 默卡普坦。……这名字多可爱，多罗曼蒂克，一个真正年轻诗人的名字！默卡普坦——她对自己的选择愈发得意了。这位讲究的贵妇再没有过更称心的一时兴起。默卡普坦……默卡普坦……她纳闷那 P. 代表什么。彼得？菲利普？帕特里克？甚至是彭丹尼斯？她哪里猜得到，默卡普坦先生的父亲、那位著名细菌学家，三十四年前执意给头生子取名「巴斯德」。

===Original===
A little tremulous, under her outward elegant calm, Rosie mounted the stairs. Twenty-five steps to the first floor﻿—one flight of thirteen, which was rather disagreeably ominous, and one of twelve. Then two flights of eleven, and she was on the second landing, facing a front door, a bell-push like a round eye, a brass nameplate. For a great lady thoroughly accustomed to this sort of thing, she felt her heart beating rather unpleasantly fast. It was those stairs, no doubt. She halted a moment, took two deep breaths, then pushed the bell.

The door was opened by an aged servant of the most forbiddingly respectable appearance.

“Mr. Mercaptan at home?”

The person at the door burst at once into a long, rambling, angry complaint, but precisely about what Rosie could not for certain make out. Mr. Mercaptan had left orders, she gathered, that he wasn’t to be disturbed. But someone had come and disturbed him, “fairly shoved his way in, so rude and inconsiderate,” all the same. And now he’d been once disturbed, she didn’t see why he shouldn’t be disturbed again. But she didn’t know what things were coming to if people fairly shoved their way in like that. Bolshevism, she called it.

Rosie murmured her sympathies, and was admitted into a dark hall. Still querulously denouncing the Bolsheviks who came shoving in, the person led the way down a corridor and, throwing open a door, announced, in a tone of grievance: “A lady to see you, Master Paster”﻿—for Mrs. Goldie was an old family retainer, and one of the few who knew the Secret of Mr. Mercaptan’s Christian name, one of the fewer still who were privileged to employ it. Then, as soon as Rosie had stepped across the threshold, she cut off her retreat with a bang and went off, muttering all the time, towards her kitchen.
===Chinese===
罗茜表面优雅镇定，内里微微打颤，上了楼。到二层二十五级台阶——先是十三级的一梯，多少有点不祥，再是十二级的一梯。然后又是各十一级的两梯，她到了三层的平台上，面对一扇正门、一只圆眼睛似的门铃钮、一块黄铜名牌。对一位此类事早已惯熟的名媛来说，她的心跳得未免太快了些。想必是楼梯的缘故。她停了停，深吸两口气，按响了门铃。

开门的是一位老仆人，相貌体面得吓人。

「默卡普坦先生在家吗？」

门口的人顿时发作了一长串颠三倒四的愤懑，但究竟为何，罗茜实在听不真切。她大致听出来：默卡普坦先生留了话不许打扰。可偏偏有人来了，还打扰了，「简直是硬挤进来的，又粗鲁又没分寸」。如今既然已经被打扰过一回，她看不出为什么不能再打扰一回。可要是人人都这么硬往里挤，这世道还要变成什么样，她可就不知道了。她管这个叫布尔什维主义。

罗茜喃喃地附和了两句同情，被领进一间幽暗的门厅。那人一路嘟囔着谴责硬往里挤的布尔什维克，领她穿过走廊，推开一扇门，用含怨的腔调通报：「一位太太求见，帕斯特少爷。」——戈尔迪太太是家里的老仆，是知道默卡普坦先生教名之秘的少数人之一，更是有资格用它称呼他的更少数人之一。随后，等罗茜一跨进门槛，她砰地把退路截断，一路嘀咕着回她的厨房去了。

===Original===
It certainly wasn’t a garret. Half a glance, the first whiff of potpourri, the feel of the carpet beneath her feet, had been enough to prove that. But it was not the room which occupied Rosie’s attention, it was its occupants. One of them, thin, sharp-featured and, in Rosie’s very young eyes, quite old, was standing with an elbow on the mantelpiece. The other, sleeker and more genial in appearance, was sitting in front of a writing-desk near the window.
