And neither of them﻿—Rosie glanced desperately from one to the other, hoping vainly that she might have overlooked a blond beard﻿—neither of them was Toto.

The sleek man at the writing-desk got up, advanced to meet her.

“An unexpected pleasure,” he said, in a voice that alternately boomed and fluted. “*Too* delightful! But to what do I owe﻿—? *Who*, may I ask﻿—?”

He had held out his hand; automatically Rosie proffered hers. The sleek man shook it with cordiality, almost with tenderness.

“I﻿ ﻿… I think I must have made a mistake,” she said. “Mr. Mercaptan﻿ ﻿… ?”

The sleek man smiled. “I am Mr. Mercaptan.”
===Chinese===
这里当然不是什么阁楼。半眼一瞥，干花混合香的第一缕气息，脚底地毯的触感，就足够证明了。但占住罗茜注意力的不是房间，是房里的人。其中一个瘦削、棱角分明、在罗茜十分年轻的眼光里相当老——正把一只臂肘支在壁炉架上。另一个，外表更油光、更和气，坐在靠窗的写字台前。而这两个人——罗茜拼命地从这个看到那个，徒劳地巴望自己漏看了一把金色的胡子——两个都不是托托。

写字台前的油光男人起身迎上前来。

「不期之乐，」他说，嗓音在轰鸣与长笛之间交替，「太叫人高兴了！可我有何德何能——？敢问您是——？」

他伸出了手；罗茜机械地递上自己的。油光男人亲热地握了握，几乎算得上温柔。

「我……我想我准是弄错了，」她说，「默卡普坦先生……？」

油光男人微微一笑。「我正是默卡普坦先生。」

===Original===
“You live on the second floor?”

“I never laid claims to being a mathematician,” said the sleek man, smiling as though to applaud himself, “but I have always calculated that﻿ ﻿…” he hesitated﻿ ﻿… “*enfin, que ma demeure se trouve, en effet*, on the second floor. Lypiatt will bear me out, I’m sure.” He turned to the thin man, who had not moved from the fireplace, but had stood all the time motionlessly, his elbow on the mantelpiece, looking gloomily at the ground.

Lypiatt looked up. “I must be going,” he said abruptly. And he walked towards the door. Like vermouth posters, like vermouth posters!﻿—so that was Myra’s piece of mockery! All his anger had sunk like a quenched flame. He was altogether quenched, put out with unhappiness.

Politely Mr. Mercaptan hurried across the room and opened the door for him. “*Good*-bye, then,” he said airily.

Lypiatt did not speak, but walked out into the hall. The front door banged behind him.

“Well, *well*,” said Mr. Mercaptan, coming back across the room to where Rosie was still irresolutely standing. “Talk about the *furor poeticus*! But *do* sit down, I beg you. On Crébillon.” He indicated the vast white satin sofa. “I call it Crébillon,” he explained, “because the soul of that great writer undoubtedly tenants it, *undoubtedly*. You know his book, of course? You know *Le Sopha*?”
===Chinese===
「您住在三层？」

「我从没自称过数学家，」油光男人说，笑得仿佛在给自己鼓掌，「可我一向计算得出……」他顿了顿……「enfin, que ma demeure se trouve, en effet（说到底，我的居所确实坐落）在三层。利皮亚特可以作证，我确信。」他转向那个瘦子——那人没有离开壁炉边，从头到尾一动不动地立着，臂肘支在炉架上，阴郁地望着地面。

利皮亚特抬起头。「我得走了。」他唐突地说，随即朝门口走去。像苦艾酒招贴，像苦艾酒招贴！——原来那句嘲弄是迈拉的！他的怒火像被浇熄的火焰般沉了下去。他整个熄了，被不幸浇灭了。

默卡普坦先生彬彬有礼地急步穿过房间，替他开门。「那么，再见。」他轻快地说。

利皮亚特没言语，走出去进了门厅。正门在他身后砰地关上。

「哎呀，哎呀，」默卡普坦先生说着，走回房间中央罗茜还踌躇站着的地方。「这就叫诗人的狂怒（furor poeticus）！可千万请坐，我求您。坐克雷比永。」他指指那张巨大的白缎沙发。「我叫它克雷比永，」他解释道，「因为那位伟大作家的灵魂毫无疑问栖居在它里面，毫无疑问。您当然读过他的书？您知道《沙发》（Le Sopha）？」

===Original===
Sinking into Crébillon’s soft lap, Rosie had to admit that she didn’t know *Le Sopha*. She had begun to recover her self-possession. If this wasn’t *the* young poet, it was certainly *a* young poet. And a very peculiar one, too. As a great lady she laughingly accepted the odd situation.

“Not know *Le Sopha*?” exclaimed Mr. Mercaptan. “Oh! but, my dear and mysterious young lady, let me lend you a copy of it at once. *No* education can be called *complete* without a knowledge of that divine book.” He darted to the bookshelf and came back with a small volume bound in white vellum. “The hero’s soul,” he explained, handing her the volume, “passes, by the laws of metempsychosis, into a sofa. He is doomed to remain a sofa until such time as two persons consummate upon his bosom their reciprocal and equal loves. The book is the record of the poor sofa’s hopes and disappointments.”

“Dear me!” said Rosie, looking at the title-page.

“But now,” said Mr. Mercaptan, sitting down beside her on the edge of Crébillon, “won’t you please explain? To what happy *quiproquo* do I owe this sudden and altogether delightful invasion of my privacy?”

“Well,” said Rosie, and hesitated. It was really rather difficult to explain. “I was to meet a friend of mine.”

“Quite so,” said Mr. Mercaptan encouragingly.
===Chinese===
陷进克雷比永柔软的怀抱，罗茜只得承认自己不知道《沙发》。她已开始恢复镇定。纵然这不是那位年轻诗人，总归是一位年轻诗人。而且还是位很别致的。作为贵妇，她笑吟吟地接受了这古怪的局面。

「不知道《沙发》？」默卡普坦先生叫起来，「哦！可是，我年轻而神秘的女士，让我立刻借您一册。不知道那本神圣的书，任何教育都称不上完整。」他一个箭步到书架前，捧回一本白犊皮装帧的小书。「主人公的灵魂，」他递书时解释道，「依轮回转世之法，进了一只沙发。他命定要做一只沙发，直到有两个人在他胸膛上完满彼此对等的爱。这本书就是那只可怜沙发的希望与失望的记录。」

「天哪！」罗茜看着扉页说。

「不过现在，」默卡普坦先生在克雷比永边上挨着她坐下，「可否请您解释解释？是哪一段幸福的张冠李戴（quiproquo），让我有幸迎来这场突如其来、皆大欢喜的私闯？」

「这个嘛，」罗茜说，迟疑了一下。这事确实有点难解释。「我本来要见我的一位朋友。」

「正是。」默卡普坦先生鼓励地应道。

===Original===
“Who sent me a telegram,” Rosie went on.

“He sent you a telegram!” Mr. Mercaptan echoed.

“Changing the﻿—the place we had fixed and telling me to meet him at this address.”

“Here?”

Rose nodded. “On the s﻿—second floor,” she made it more precise.

“But *I* live on the second floor,” said Mr. Mercaptan. “You don’t mean to say your friend is also called Mercaptan and lives here too?”
===Chinese===
「他给我拍了个电报。」罗茜接着说。

「他给您拍了电报！」默卡普坦先生应声回响。

「改了我们原定的地方，叫我到这个地址来见他。」

「这里？」

罗茜点点头。「在三层。」她说得更确切了些。

「可我就住在三层呀，」默卡普坦先生说，「您总不是说，您的朋友也叫默卡普坦、也住在这里吧？」

===Original===
Rosie smiled. “I don’t know what he’s called,” she said with a cool ironical carelessness that was genuinely *grande dame*.

“You don’t know his name?” Mr. Mercaptan gave a roar and a squeal of delighted laughter. “But that’s *too* good,” he said.

“S﻿—second floor, he wrote in the telegram.” Rosie was now perfectly at her ease. “When I saw your name, I thought it was his name. I must say,” she added, looking sideways at Mr. Mercaptan and at once dropping the magnolia petals of her eyelids, “it seemed to me a very charming name.”

“You overwhelm me,” said Mr. Mercaptan, smiling all over his cheerful, snouty face. “As for *your* name﻿—I am too discreet a *galantuomo* to ask. And, in any case, what *does* it matter? A rose by any other name.﻿ ﻿…”

“But, as a matter of fact,” she said, raising and lowering once again her smooth, white lids, “my name does happen to be Rose; or, at any rate, Rosie.”

“So you are sweet by right!” exclaimed Mr. Mercaptan, with a pretty gallantry which he was the first to appreciate. “Let’s order tea on the strength of it.” He jumped up and rang the bell. “How I congratulate myself on this astonishing piece of good fortune!”
===Chinese===
罗茜笑了。「我不知道他姓甚名谁。」她说，带着一种当真够贵妇气派的冷漠而讥诮的满不在乎。

「您不知道他的名字？」默卡普坦先生发出一声吼叫加一声尖叫的欢喜大笑。「这可太妙了。」他说。

「三——三层，电报上是这么写的。」罗茜此刻已完全自在。「一见您的名字，我还当那就是他的名字呢。我得说，」她补了一句，斜睨了默卡普坦先生一眼，随即垂下她那木兰花瓣似的睫毛，「这名字在我看来可爱得很。」

「您叫我无地自容了，」默卡普坦先生说，那张快活的猪脸笑开了花，「至于您的芳名——我是个太知分寸的君子（galantuomo），不便动问。况且，名字又有什么要紧？换个名字，玫瑰照样……」

「不过，说巧不巧，」她说，又一次抬起又放下那光滑白皙的眼睑，「我的名字偏偏真的就叫罗丝；或者至少，叫罗茜。」

「那您是理所当然地甜！」默卡普坦先生叫道，献上一段连他自己都先为之倾倒的漂亮殷勤。「就冲这个，吩咐上茶吧。」他跳起来按铃。「我为这桩惊人的好运怎样恭喜自己才好！」

===Original===
Rosie said nothing. This Mr. Mercaptan, she thought, seemed to be even more a man of the great artistic world than Toto.

“What puzzles me,” he went on, “is why your anonymous friend should have chosen my address out of all the millions of others. He must know me, or, at any rate, know about me.”

“I should imagine,” said Rosie, “that you have a lot of friends.”

Mr. Mercaptan laughed﻿—the whole orchestra, from bassoon to piccolo. “*Des amis, des amies*﻿—with and without the mute *e*,” he declared.
===Chinese===
罗茜没言语。这位默卡普坦先生，她想，看起来比托托还更是大艺术圈子里的人物。

「让我纳闷的是，」他接着说，「您那位匿名朋友，在千千万万个地址里，怎么偏偏挑中了我的。他一定认识我，或者至少，听说过我。」

「我猜想，」罗茜说，「您的朋友一定很多。」

默卡普坦先生笑了——整个乐队，从巴松到短笛。「Des amis, des amies（男朋友，女朋友）——带不带哑音 e 的都有。」他声明。

===Original===
The aged and forbidding servant appeared at the door.

“Tea for two, Mrs. Goldie.”

Mrs. Goldie looked round the room suspiciously. “The other gentleman’s gone, has he?” she asked. And having assured herself of his absence, she renewed her complaint. “Shoving in like that,” she said. “Bolshevism, that’s what I﻿—”
===Chinese===
那位年迈而面目威严的老仆出现在门口。

「两客茶，戈尔迪太太。」

戈尔迪太太狐疑地环顾房间。「另一位先生走了，是吧？」她问。确认了人确实不在之后，她把控诉重新拾起。「就那么往里挤，」她说，「布尔什维主义，我就叫它——」

===Original===
“All right, all right, Mrs. Goldie. Let’s have our tea as quickly as possible.” Mr. Mercaptan held up his hand, authoritatively, with the gesture of a policeman controlling the traffic.

“Very well, Master Paster.” Mrs. Goldie spoke with resignation and departed.

“But tell me,” Mr. Mercaptan went on, “if it *isn’t* indiscreet﻿—what does your friend look like?”

“W﻿—well,” Rosie answered, “he’s fair, and though he’s quite young he wears a beard.” With her two hands she indicated on her own unemphatic bosom the contours of Toto’s broad blond fan.
===Chinese===
「好了好了，戈尔迪太太。咱们的茶越快越好。」默卡普坦先生举起手，威严地，用的是交警指挥车流的手势。

「好吧，帕斯特少爷。」戈尔迪太太认命地说，退出去了。

「可是请告诉我，」默卡普坦先生接着说，「如果不唐突的话——您那位朋友长相如何？」

「呃——嗯，」罗茜答道，「他头发浅色，人挺年轻，可蓄着一把大胡子。」她用双手在自己那并不雄伟的胸前，比出托托那把金色宽扇的轮廓。

===Original===
“A beard! But, good heavens,” Mr. Mercaptan slapped his thigh, “it’s Coleman, it’s obviously and undoubtedly Coleman!”

“Well, whoever it was,” said Rosie severely, “he played a very stupid sort of joke.”

“For which I thank him. *De tout mon cœur.*”

Rosie smiled and looked sideways. “All the same,” she said, “I shall give him a piece of my mind.”
===Chinese===
「一把胡子！哎呀，天哪，」默卡普坦先生一拍大腿，「这是科尔曼！明明白白，千真万确是科尔曼！」

「不管他是谁，」罗茜正色道，「这个玩笑开得可够蠢的。」

「我可要谢谢他。De tout mon cœur（发自我的全心）。」

罗茜笑着，斜眼看了看。「不过，」她说，「我还是要说他两句的。」

===Original===
Poor Aunt Aggie! Oh, poor Aunt Aggie, indeed! In the light of Mr. Mercaptan’s boudoir her hammered copper and her leadless glaze certainly did look a bit comical.

After tea Mr. Mercaptan played cicerone in a tour of inspection round the room. They visited the papier-mâché writing-desk, the Condor fans, the Marie Laurencin, the 1914 edition of *Du Côté de chez Swann*, the Madonna that probably was a fake, the nigger mask, the Chelsea figures, the Chinese object of art in sculptured crystal, the scale model of Queen Victoria in wax under a glass bell. Toto, it became clear, had been no more than a forerunner; the definitive revelation was Mr. Mercaptan’s. Yes, poor Aunt Aggie! And indeed, when Mr. Mercaptan began to read her his little middle on the “Droit du Seigneur,” it was poor everybody. Poor mother, with her absurd, old-fashioned, prudish views; poor, earnest father, with his Unitarianism, his *Hibbert Journal*, his letters to the papers about the necessity for a spiritual regeneration.

“Bravo!” she cried from the depths of Crébillon. She was leaning back in one corner, languid, serpentine, and at ease, her feet in their mottled snake’s leather tucked up under her. “Bravo!” she cried as Mr. Mercaptan finished his reading and looked up for his applause.

Mr. Mercaptan bowed.

“You express so exquisitely what we﻿—” and waving her hand in a comprehensive gesture, she pictured to herself all the other fastidious ladies, all the marchionesses of fable, reclining, as she herself at this moment reclined, on upholstery of white satin, “what we all only feel and aren’t clever enough to say.”
===Chinese===
可怜的艾吉姨妈！哦，真是可怜的艾吉姨妈！在默卡普坦先生闺房的光照之下，她那敲花铜器和无铅釉彩实在显得有几分滑稽了。

茶后，默卡普坦先生充当向导，领她巡视全屋。他们参观了纸胎写字台、神鹰羽扇、洛朗森、1914 年版的《在斯万家那边》、多半是赝品的圣母像、黑人木雕面具、切尔西瓷偶、水晶雕的中国艺术品、玻璃罩下蜡制的维多利亚女王缩像。托托，事情清楚了，不过是个先行者；最终的启示属于默卡普坦先生。是啊，可怜的艾吉姨妈！而且，当默卡普坦先生开始给她朗读他那篇《领主权》的小品时，那就是人人可怜了。可怜的妈妈，抱着一整套过时而假正经的见解；可怜的一本正经的爸爸，抱着他的一位论、他的《希伯特学报》、他写给报纸的那些论精神再生之必要的信。

「好！」她从克雷比永深处喊道。她斜倚在一角，慵懒、蜿蜒、自在，穿斑纹蛇皮鞋的双脚蜷在身下。「好！」默卡普坦先生朗读完毕、抬起头等待喝彩时，她喊道。

默卡普坦先生鞠了一躬。

「您把我们——」她把手潇洒地一挥，在心里描画出所有其余那些讲究的太太、传说中的所有侯爵夫人，正像她此刻这样，斜倚在白缎的软垫上，「把我们大家都只可意会、却不够聪明说得出的东西，表达得这样精美。」

===Original===
Mr. Mercaptan was charmed. He got up from before his writing-desk, crossed the room and sat down beside her on Crébillon. “Feeling,” he said, “is the im*port*ant thing.”

Rosie remembered that her father had once remarked, in blank verse: “The things that matter happen in the heart.”

“I quite agree,” she said.

Like movable raisins in the suet of his snouty face, Mr. Mercaptan’s brown little eyes rolled amorous avowals. He took Rosie’s hand and kissed it. Crébillon creaked discreetly as he moved a little nearer.
===Chinese===
默卡普坦先生心花怒放。他从写字台前起身，穿过房间，在克雷比永上挨她坐下。「感情，」他说，「才是重——要的东西。」

罗茜想起她父亲曾用素体诗说过一句：「要紧的事都发生在心里。」

「我完全同意。」她说。

像猪脸板油里嵌着的两粒会动的葡萄干，默卡普坦先生棕色的小眼睛滚动着爱慕的表白。他执起罗茜的手，吻了吻。他稍稍挪近时，克雷比永知趣地吱呀了一声。

===Original===
It was only the evening of the same day. Rosie lay on her sofa﻿—a poor, hire-purchase thing indeed, compared with Mr. Mercaptan’s grand affair in white satin and carved and gilded wood, but still a sofa﻿—lay with her feet on the arm of it and her long suave legs exposed, by the slipping of the kimono, to the top of her stretched stockings. She was reading the little vellum-jacketed volume of Crébillon, which Mr. Mercaptan had given her when he said “goodbye” (or rather, “*À bientôt, mon amie*”); given, not lent, as he had less generously offered at the beginning of their afternoon; given with the most graceful of allusive dedications inscribed on the flyleaf:
===Chinese===
这不过是当天晚上。罗茜躺在她的沙发上——比起默卡普坦先生白缎描金雕花的大制作来，实在是一件寒酸的分期付款货，可总归是张沙发——两脚搁在扶手上，长而柔滑的双腿因晨衣滑开而裸露到绷直的袜口。她正读着那本犊皮封面的小小克雷比翁——道别时（更确切说，「À bientôt, mon amie（回见，我的朋友）」时）默卡普坦先生送给她的；是送，不是借，尽管午后初开口时他较小气地提议的是借；扉页上还题着一句最风雅的用典献辞：

===Original===
> To 

 **By-No-Other-Name-As-Sweet**, 

 With Gratitude, 

 from 
===Chinese===
> 赠
>
> **「换个名字也没有这般甜」**，
> 谨致谢忱
> 出自
>
> 得脱樊笼的克雷比永。

===Original===
*À bientôt*﻿—she had promised to come again very soon. She thought of the essay on the “Jus Primæ Noctis”﻿—ah! what we’ve all been feeling and none of us clever enough to say. We on the sofas, ruthless, lovely and fastidious.﻿ ﻿…

“I am proud to constitute myself”﻿—Mr. Mercaptan had said of it﻿—“*l’esprit d’escalier des dames galantes.*”

Rosie was not quite sure what he meant; but it certainly sounded very witty indeed.

She read the book slowly. Her French, indeed, wasn’t good enough to permit her to read it anyhow else. She wished it were better. Perhaps it if were better she wouldn’t be yawning like this. It was disgraceful: she pulled herself together. Mr. Mercaptan had said that, it was a masterpiece.
===Chinese===
À bientôt（回见）——她答应过很快就再来。她想着那篇论《初夜权》的文章——啊！我们大家一直感觉着、却没有一个聪明得说得出的东西。我们这些沙发上的女人，无情、可爱而挑剔。……

「我自豪于自任，」默卡普坦先生曾这样评它，「l'esprit d'escalier des dames galantes（风流女士们的事后灵机）。」

罗茜不很明白他的意思；可这话听上去实在俏皮。

她读得很慢。她的法文实在不够好，想快也快不起来。她巴望法文好些。或许好些，就不会这样打哈欠了。不像话：她振作起来。默卡普坦先生说过，这是一部杰作。

===Original===
In his study, Shearwater was trying to write his paper on the regulative functions of the kidneys. He was not succeeding.

Why wouldn’t she see me yesterday? he kept wondering. With anguish he suspected other lovers; desired her, in consequence, the more. Gumbril had said something, he remembered, that night they had met her by the coffee-stall. What was it? He wished now that he had listened more attentively.

She’s bored with me. Already. It was obvious.

Perhaps he was too rustic for her. Shearwater looked at his hands. Yes, the nails *were* dirty. He took an orange stick out of his waistcoat pocket and began to clean them. He had bought a whole packet of orange sticks that morning.

Determinedly he took up his pen. “The hydrogen ion concentration in the blood﻿ ﻿…” he began a new paragraph. But he got no further than the first seven words.
===Chinese===
书房里，希尔沃特正试着写他那篇论肾脏调节机能的论文。他写不下去。

昨天她为什么不肯见我？他翻来覆去地纳闷。他痛苦地疑心另有情人；因而愈发想要她。冈布里尔说过什么话，他记得，那晚在咖啡摊旁遇见她。是什么来着？他只恨当时没听得更仔细些。

她跟我腻了。已经腻了。明摆着的。

兴许他对她来说太土了。希尔沃特看了看自己的手。是的，指甲确实脏。他从背心口袋里掏出一根橙木签，开始剔。那天早晨他买了一整包橙木签。

他毅然提笔。「血液中的氢离子浓度……」他起了个新段。但写了头七个词就写不下去了。

===Original===
If, he began thinking with a frightful confusion, if﻿—if﻿—if﻿—Past conditionals, hopelessly past. He might have been brought up more elegantly; his father, for example, might have been a barrister instead of a barrister’s clerk. He mightn’t have had to work so hard when he was young; might have been about more, danced more, seen more young women. If he had met her years ago﻿—during the war, should one say, dressed in the uniform of a lieutenant in the Guards.﻿ ﻿…

He had pretended that he wasn’t interested in women; that they had no effect on him; that, in fact, he was above that sort of thing. Imbecile! He might as well have said that he was above having a pair of kidneys. He had only consented to admit, graciously, that they were a physiological necessity.

O God, what a fool he had been!

And then, what about Rosie? What sort of a life had she been having while he was being above that sort of thing? Now he came to think of it, he really knew nothing about her, except that she had been quite incapable of learning correctly, even by heart, the simplest facts about the physiology of frogs. Having found that out, he had really given up exploring further. How could he have been so stupid?

Rosie had been in love with him, he supposed. Had he been in love with her? No. He had taken care not to be. On principle. He had married her as a measure of intimate hygiene; out of protective affection, too, certainly out of affection; and a little for amusement, as one might buy a puppy.

Mrs. Viveash had opened his eyes; seeing her, he had also begun to notice Rosie. It seemed to him that he had been a loutish cad as well as an imbecile.
===Chinese===
假如，他开始想，心里乱作一团，假如——假如——假如——过去式的虚拟语气，无可救药地过去了。他本可以被教养得更风雅些；比如他父亲本可以当出庭律师，而不只做出庭律师的文书。他年轻时本可以不必那么苦干；本可以多出去走走，多跳跳舞，多见些年轻女人。要是多年前就遇见她——比如说大战期间，穿着禁卫军中尉的制服。……

他一直假装自己对女人没兴趣；说她们对他不起作用；说实际上他超脱于那种事。蠢材！这跟说自己超脱于长一对肾脏之外没什么两样。他只肯宽宏地承认，它们是一种生理必需。

哦上帝，他多傻！

还有，罗茜呢？在他超脱于那种事的岁月里，她过着怎样的日子？如今想起来，他对她实在一无所知，只知道她连青蛙生理学的最简单事实都背不下来。发现了这一点之后，他就真的放弃进一步的探究了。他怎么能蠢到这个地步？

罗茜曾经爱过他，他估摸。他爱过她吗？没有。他小心翼翼地不去爱。出于原则。他娶她，是一项贴身的卫生措施；也出于保护性的钟爱——不错，确实是出于钟爱；还有一点是为了好玩，像买一只小狗。

维维什夫人开开了他的眼；见了她，他也才开始注意罗茜。他觉得自己一向不光是蠢材，还是个粗手笨脚的浑小子。

===Original===
What should he do about it? He sat for a long time wondering.

In the end he decided that the best thing would be to go and tell Rosie all about it, all about everything.

About Mrs. Viveash too? Yes, about Mrs. Viveash too. He would get over Mrs. Viveash more easily and more rapidly if he did. And he would begin to try and find out about Rosie. He would explore her. He would discover all the other things besides an incapacity to learn physiology that were in her. He would discover her, he would quicken his affection for her into something livelier and more urgent. And they would begin again; more satisfactorily this time; with knowledge and understanding; wise from their experience.

Shearwater got up from his chair before the writing-table, lurched pensively towards the door, bumping into the revolving bookcase and the armchair as he went, and walked down the passage to the drawing-room. Rosie did not turn her head as he came in, but went on reading without changing her position, her slippered feet still higher than her head, her legs still charmingly avowing themselves.
===Chinese===
该怎么办呢？他坐了很久，左思右想。

末了他认定，最好的办法是去对罗茜和盘托出，什么都说。

连维维什夫人也说？是的，连维维什夫人也说。这样做，他会把维维什夫人更快更容易地熬过去。而且他要开始试着去了解罗茜。他要探究她。他要发现在不懂生理学之外，她身上还有的别的一切。他要发现她，把对她的钟爱催醒成一点更活泼、更紧迫的东西。他们要从头来过；这一回更美满些；带着认识与理解；从经验里学乖。

希尔沃特从写字台前的椅子上站起来，沉思地朝门口踉跄走去，一路撞上旋转书架和扶手椅，穿过走廊进了客厅。他进来时罗茜没有回头，照旧读着，姿势不变，穿拖鞋的脚仍旧高过头顶，双腿照旧妩媚地自我招认。

===Original===
Shearwater came to a halt in front of the empty fireplace. He stood there with his back to it, as though warming himself before an imaginary flame. It was, he felt, the safest, the most strategic point from which to talk.

“What are you reading?” he asked.

“*Le Sopha*,” said Rosie.

“What’s that?”

“What’s that?” Rosie scornfully echoed. “Why, it’s one of the great French classics.”

“Who by?”
===Chinese===
希尔沃特在空壁炉前站定。他背对炉台站着，像在一团想象的火前取暖。他觉得，这是讲话最稳妥、最有战略意义的位置。

「你在读什么？」他问。

「《沙发》（Le Sopha）。」罗茜说。

「那是什么？」

「『那是什么』？」罗茜鄙夷地学舌，「嗬，这可是法国伟大古典之一。」

「谁写的？」

===Original===
“Crébillon the younger.”

“Never heard of him,” said Shearwater. There was a silence. Rosie went on reading.

“It just occurred to me,” Shearwater began again in his rather ponderous, infelicitous way, “that you mightn’t be very happy, Rosie.”

Rosie looked up at him and laughed. “What put that into your head?” she asked. “*I*’m perfectly happy.”

Shearwater was left a little at a loss. “Well, I’m very glad to hear it,” he said. “I only thought﻿ ﻿… that perhaps *you* might think﻿ ﻿… that *I* rather neglected you.”

Rosie laughed again. “What is all this about?” she said.
===Chinese===
「小克雷比翁。」

「没听说过。」希尔沃特说。一阵沉默。罗茜继续读。

「我忽然想到，」希尔沃特用他那相当笨重而不走运的方式重新开口，「你可能不太幸福，罗茜。」

罗茜抬眼看他，笑了。「哪来的这个念头？」她问，「我幸福得很。」

希尔沃特有点不知所措。「那我很高兴，」他说，「我只是想……你也许觉得……我冷落了你。」

罗茜又笑了。「这都是在说什么呀？」她说。

===Original===
“I have it rather on my conscience,” said Shearwater. “I begin to see﻿ ﻿… something has made me see﻿ ﻿… that I’ve not.﻿ ﻿… I don’t treat you very well.﻿ ﻿…”

“But I don’t n﻿—notice it, I assure you,” put in Rosie, still smiling.

“I leave you out too much,” Shearwater went on with a kind of desperation, running his fingers through his thick brown hair. “We don’t share enough together. You’re too much outside my life.”

“But after all,” said Rosie, “we are a civ﻿—vilized couple. We don’t want to live in one another’s pockets, do we?”

“No, but we’re really no more than strangers,” said Shearwater. “That isn’t right. And it’s my fault. I’ve never tried to get into touch with your life. But you did your best to understand mine﻿ ﻿… at the beginning of our marriage.”

“Oh, *then﻿—n*!” said Rosie, laughing. “You found out what a little idiot I was.”
===Chinese===
「我心里颇为过意不去，」希尔沃特说，「我开始看出……一件事让我看出来了……我没有……我待你不好……」

「可我并没——没觉出来，我保证。」罗茜插话，仍在微笑。

「我把你撇下得太多，」希尔沃特近乎绝望地接着说，手指深深插进浓密的棕发，「我们共享得太少。你太置身于我的生活之外了。」

「可是说到底，」罗茜说，「我们是一对文——文明夫妻。我们也不想钻进彼此的口袋里过日子，对吧？」

「不，可我们实际上形同陌路，」希尔沃特说，「这不对。而且是我的错。我从来没试着接触你的生活。可你倒尽力理解过我的……在我们结婚开头。」

「哦，那——那时候！」罗茜笑着说，「你发现我是个多蠢的小傻瓜。」

===Original===
“Don’t make a joke of it,” said Shearwater. “It isn’t a joke. It’s very serious. I tell you, I’ve come to see how stupid and inconsiderate and un-understanding I’ve been with you. I’ve come to see quite suddenly. The fact is,” he went on with a rush, like an uncorked fountain, “I’ve been seeing a woman recently whom I like very much, and who doesn’t like me.” Speaking of Mrs. Viveash, unconsciously he spoke her language. For Mrs. Viveash people always euphemistically “liked” one another rather a lot, even when it was a case of the most frightful and excruciating passion, the most complete abandonments. “And somehow that’s made me see a lot of things which I’d been blind to before﻿—blind deliberately, I suppose. It’s made me see, among other things, that I’ve really been to blame towards you, Rosie.”
===Chinese===
「别拿这事开玩笑，」希尔沃特说，「这不是玩笑。这非常严重。我告诉你，我算看明白了我对你多么愚蠢、多么粗心、多么不体贴。我是突然明白过来的。事实是，」他像拔了塞的泉水一样一股脑说下去，「我近来常与一位我很喜欢的女人来往，而她不喜欢我。」说起维维什夫人，他不自觉地用起了她的语言。在维维什夫人的世界里，人们永远委婉地彼此「喜欢」得厉害，哪怕碰上的是最骇人、最煎熬的激情、最彻底的沉溺。「不知怎么，这让我看清了许多从前视而不见的事——我想是故意视而不见的。它让我看清了，除别的事情外，我对你实在有亏欠，罗茜。」

===Original===
Rosie listened with an astonishment which she perfectly disguised. So James was embarking on his little affairs, was he? It seemed incredible, and also, as she looked at her husband’s face﻿—the face behind its bristlingly manly mask of a harassed baby﻿—also rather pathetically absurd. She wondered who it could be. But she displayed no curiosity. She would find out soon enough.

“I’m sorry you should have been unhappy about it,” she said.

“It’s finished now.” Shearwater made a decided little gesture.

“Ah, no!” said Rosie. “You should persevere.” She looked at him, smiling.

Shearwater was taken aback by this display of easy detachment. He had imagined the conversation so very differently, as something so serious, so painful and, at the same time, so healing and soothing, that he did not know how to go on. “But I thought,” he said hesitatingly, “that you﻿ ﻿… that we﻿ ﻿… after this experience﻿ ﻿… I would try to get closer to you.﻿ ﻿…” (Oh, it sounded ridiculous!)﻿ ﻿… “We might start again, from a different place, so to speak.”
===Chinese===
罗茜听着，心里惊异，面上丝毫不露。原来詹姆斯也闹起他的小韵事来了，是吗？这看着难以置信，而且——她望着丈夫的脸，那张藏在胡子拉碴的男子气面具后面的、被搅扰的婴儿的脸——也可怜得滑稽。她纳闷那能是谁。但她不露一点好奇。反正很快就会知道的。

「你为此不快，我很遗憾。」她说。

「现在结束了。」希尔沃特做了个干脆的小手势。

「啊，不！」罗茜说，「你该坚持。」她微笑地望着他。

这份轻松的置身事外叫希尔沃特吃了一惊。他原先把这场谈话想象得全然不同——那么严重、那么痛苦，同时又那么治愈、那么抚慰——以至于不知该怎么接下去。「可我想，」他迟疑地说，「你……我们……经过这次……我会设法贴近你……」（哦，这听着多可笑！）……「我们可以说，从一个不同的地方重新开始。」

===Original===
“But, *cher ami*,” protested Rosie, with the inflection and in the preferred tongue of Mr. Mercaptan, “you can’t seriously expect us to do the Darby and Joan business, can you? You’re distressing yourself quite unnecessarily on my account. I don’t find you neglect me or anything like it. You have your life﻿—naturally. And I have mine. We don’t get in one another’s way.”

“But do you think that’s the ideal sort of married life?” asked Shearwater.

“It’s obviously the most civ﻿—vilized,” Rosie answered, laughing.

Confronted by Rosie’s civilization, Shearwater felt helpless.
===Chinese===
「可是，cher ami（亲爱的朋友），」罗茜抗议，用的是默卡普坦先生的语调和他中意的法语，「你总不能当真指望我们演达比和琼那老夫老妻的把戏吧？你这是替我白白地自寻烦恼。我不觉得你冷落我，或诸如此类。你有你的生活——天经地义。我也有我的。咱们互不妨碍。」

「可你觉得那是理想的婚姻生活吗？」希尔沃特问。

「这明摆着是最文——文明的。」罗茜笑着回答。

面对罗茜的文明，希尔沃特束手无策。

===Original===
“Well, if you don’t want,” he said. “I’d hoped﻿ ﻿… I’d thought.﻿ ﻿…”

He went back to his study to think things over. The more he thought them over, the more he blamed himself. And incessantly the memory of Mrs. Viveash tormented him.
===Chinese===
「那要是你不愿意，」他说，「我本指望……我本想……」

他回书房去把事情想个透。他越想越怪自己。而维维什夫人的记忆无休无止地折磨着他。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
After leaving Mr. Mercaptan, Lypiatt had gone straight home. The bright day seemed to deride him. With its shining red omnibuses, its parasols, its muslin girls, its young-leaved trees, its bands at the street corners, it was too much of a garden party to be tolerable. He wanted to be alone. He took a cab back to the studio. He couldn’t afford it, of course; but what did that matter, what did that matter now?

The cab drove slowly and as though with reluctance down the dirty mews. He paid it off, opened his little door between the wide stable doors, climbed the steep ladder of his stairs and was at home. He sat down and tried to think.
===Chinese===
离开默卡普坦先生之后，利皮亚特径直回了家。明亮的白日仿佛在嘲弄他。闪亮的红色公共马车、阳伞、纱裙少女、新叶初绽的树木、街角的铜管乐队——这一切太像一场游园会，叫人无法忍受。他想要独处。他叫了辆马车回画室。这他自然是坐不起的；可那又算什么，如今那还算得了什么？

马车磨磨蹭蹭、老大不情愿似的驶下肮脏的马厩巷。他付了车钱，推开夹在两扇宽大车库门中间的小门，爬上他那段陡得像梯子的楼梯，到了家。他坐下来，试着想点事情。

===Original===
“Death, death, death, death,” he kept repeating to himself, moving his lips as though he were praying. If he said the word often enough, if he accustomed himself completely to the idea, death would come almost by itself; he would know it already, while he was still alive, he would pass almost without noticing out of life into death. Into death, he thought, into death. Death like a well. The stone falls, falls, second after second; and at last there is a sound, a far-off, horrible sound of death and then nothing more. The well at Carisbrooke, with a donkey to wind the wheel that pulls up the bucket of water, of icy water.﻿ ﻿… He thought for a long time of the well of death.

Outside in the mews a barrel-organ struck up the tune of “Where do flies go in the wintertime?” Lypiatt lifted his head to listen. He smiled to himself. “Where *do* flies go?” The question asked itself with a dramatic, a tragical appositeness. At the end of everything﻿—the last ludicrous touch. He saw it all from outside. He pictured himself sitting there alone, broken. He looked at his hand lying limp on the table in front of him. It needed only the stigma of the nail to make it the hand of a dead Christ.

There, he was making literature of it again. Even now. He buried his face in his hands. His mind was full of twisted darkness, of an unspeakable, painful confusion. It was too difficult, too difficult.
===Chinese===
「死，死，死，死。」他对自己反复念叨，嘴唇翕动，仿佛在祈祷。倘若这字眼说得够多，倘若他让自己完全习惯了这念头，死亡几乎会自己走来；他还活着就已经认得它了，几乎不知不觉地便从生跨进死。跨进死，他想，跨进死。死像一口井。石头落下去，落下去，一秒又一秒；最后有一声响，一声遥远而可怕的死亡之声，然后便再没有别的了。卡里斯布鲁克的那口井，一头驴子摇着辘轳，吊上一桶水，一桶冰凉的水。……他久久想着那口死亡之井。

外面巷子里，一架手摇风琴奏起《苍蝇冬天飞哪儿去》的调子。利皮亚特抬起头听。他自顾自笑了。「苍蝇到底飞哪儿去？」这问题问得自己带着一种戏剧性的、悲剧性的贴切。在一切的尽头——最后一个荒谬的触笔。他从外面看见了这一切。他想象自己孤零零坐在那里，垮了。他望着自己软瘫在面前桌上的那只手。只差钉痕，它便是死去基督的手了。

瞧，他又在把它变成文学了。甚至此刻。他把脸埋进双手。他心里满是扭曲的黑暗，满是说不出的痛楚的混乱。太难了，太难了。

===Original===
The inkpot, he found when he wanted to begin writing, contained nothing but a parched black sediment. He had been meaning for days past to get some more ink; and he had always forgotten. He would have to write in pencil.

“Do you remember,” he wrote, “do you remember, Myra, that time we went down into the country﻿—you remember﻿—under the Hog’s Back at that little inn they were trying to make pretentious? ‘Hotel Bull’﻿—do you remember? How we laughed over the Hotel Bull! And how we liked the country outside its doors! All the world in a few square miles. Chalk-pits and blue butterflies on the Hog’s Back. And at the foot of the hill, suddenly, the sand; the hard, yellow sand with those queer caves, dug when and by what remote villains at the edge of the Pilgrims’ Way? the fine grey sand on which the heather of Puttenham Common grows. And the flagstaff and the inscription marking the place where Queen Victoria stood to look at the view. And the enormous sloping meadows round Compton and the thick, dark woods. And the lakes, the heaths, the Scotch firs at Cutt Mill. The forests of Shackleford. There was everything. Do you remember how we enjoyed it all? I did, in any case. I was happy during those three days. And I loved you, Myra. And I thought you might, you might perhaps, some day, love me. You didn’t. And my love has only brought me unhappiness. Perhaps it has been my fault. Perhaps I ought to have known how to make you give me happiness. You remember that wonderful sonnet of Michelangelo’s, where he says that the loved woman is like a block of marble from which the artist knows how to cut the perfect statue of his dreams. If the statue turns out a bad one, if it’s death instead of love that the lover gets﻿—why, the fault lies in the artist and in the lover, not in the marble, not in the beloved.
===Chinese===
墨水瓶，等他想动笔时才发现，只剩一圈焦干的黑色沉淀。几天来他一直想着再买点墨水；却总是忘。他只好用铅笔写。

「你还记得吗，」他写道，「你还记得吗，迈拉，那一回我们下乡——你记得的——在猪背岭下面那家正被他们装点得虚张声势的小客栈？『公牛旅店』——你记得吗？我们为这公牛旅店笑了多少回！我们又是多么喜欢它门外的乡野！整个世界方圆不过几英里。猪背岭上的白垩采石场和蓝蝴蝶。山脚下，倏忽之间，是沙地；坚硬的黄沙，带着那些古怪的洞穴——是什么年月、哪代遥远的不逞之徒在朝圣路的边上挖的？普特纳姆公地上石楠生长其上的细灰沙。还有那根旗杆和那块碑志，标着维多利亚女王当年驻足观景的地方。康普顿四周倾斜的巨大草场和幽暗的密林。还有湖泊、荒原、卡特磨坊的苏格兰冷杉。沙克尔福德的森林。那里什么都有。你还记得我们怎样享用这一切的吗？反正我记得。那三天我是幸福的。而我爱你，迈拉。我还想着，你也许、你兴许有一天会爱我。你没有。而我的爱只给我带来了不幸。也许这是我的错。也许我本该懂得怎样让你给我幸福。你记得米开朗琪罗那首奇妙的十四行诗：他说钟爱的女人像一块大理石，艺术家懂得从中凿出他梦中的完美雕像。倘若凿出来的雕像是个坏作，倘若情人得到的不是爱而是死——那么，错在艺术家、在情人，不在大理石，不在被爱者。

===Original===
> *Amor dunque non ha, ne tua beltate,*
 
 

 
 *O fortuna, o durezza, o gran disdegno,*
 
 

 
 *Del mio mal colpa, o mio destino, o sorte.*
> *Se dentro del tuo cor morte e pietate*
 
 

 
 *Porti in un tempo, e ch’l mio basso ingegno*
 
 

 
 *Non sappia ardendo trarne altro che morte.*

===Chinese===
> 爱，连同你的美貌，
> 命运、冷酷，或深重的轻蔑，
> 对我的痛苦都无罪——我的命，我的运，我的宿命；
> 若你的心同时怀着
> 死亡与怜悯，而我卑微的才具
> 燃烧着，只能从中取得死亡。

===Original===
Yes, it was my *basso ingegno*: my low genius which did not know how to draw love from you, nor beauty from the materials of which art is made. Ah, now you’ll smile to yourself and say: Poor Casimir, he has come to admit that at last? Yes, yes, I have come to admit everything. That I couldn’t paint, I couldn’t write, I couldn’t make music. That I was a charlatan and a quack. That I was a ridiculous actor of heroic parts who deserved to be laughed at﻿—and *was* laughed at. But then every man is ludicrous if you look at him from outside, without taking into account what’s going on in his heart and mind. You could turn Hamlet into an epigrammatic farce with an inimitable scene when he takes his adored mother in adultery. You could make the wittiest Guy de Maupassant short story out of the life of Christ, by contrasting the mad rabbi’s pretensions with his abject fate. It’s a question of the point of view. Everyone’s a walking farce and a walking tragedy at the same time. The man who slips on a banana-skin and fractures his skull describes against the sky, as he falls, the most richly comical arabesque. And you, Myra﻿—what do you suppose the unsympathetic gossips say of you? What sort of a farce of the Boulevards is your life in their eyes? For me, Myra, you seem to move all the time through some nameless and incomprehensible tragedy. For them you are what? Merely any sort of a wanton, with amusing adventures. And what am I? A charlatan, a quack, a pretentious, boasting, rhodomontading imbecile, incapable of painting anything but vermouth posters. (Why did that hurt so terribly? I don’t know. There was no reason why you shouldn’t think so if you wanted to.) I was all that﻿—and grotesquely laughable. And very likely your laughter was justified, your judgment was true. I don’t know. I can’t tell. Perhaps I am a charlatan. Perhaps I’m insincere; boasting to others, deceiving myself. I don’t know, I tell you. Everything is confusion in my mind now. The whole fabric seems to have tumbled to pieces; it lies in a horrible chaos. I can make no order within myself. Have I lied to myself? have I acted and postured the Great Man to persuade myself that I am one? have I something in me, or nothing? have I ever achieved anything of worth, anything that rhymed with my conceptions, my dreams (for those were fine; of that, I *am* certain)? I look into the chaos that is my soul and, I tell you, I don’t know, I don’t know. But what I do know is that I’ve spent nearly twenty years now playing the charlatan at whom you all laugh. That I’ve suffered, in mind and in body too﻿—almost from hunger, sometimes﻿—in order to play it. That I’ve struggled, that I’ve exultantly climbed to the attack, that I’ve been thrown down﻿—ah, many times!﻿—that I’ve picked myself up and started again. Well, I suppose all that’s ludicrous, if you like to think of it that way. It is ludicrous that a man should put himself to prolonged inconvenience for the sake of something which doesn’t really exist at all. It’s exquisitely comic, I can see. I can see it in the abstract, so to speak. But in this particular case, you must remember I’m not a dispassionate observer. And if I am overcome now, it is not with laughter. It is with an indescribable unhappiness, with the bitterness of death itself. Death, death, death. I repeat the word to myself, again and again. I think of death, I try to imagine it, I hang over it, looking down, where the stones fall and fall and there is one horrible noise, and then silence again; looking down into the well of death. It is so deep that there is no glittering eye of water to be seen at the bottom. I have no candle to send down. It is horrible, but I do not want to go on living. Living would be worse than.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
是的，是我的卑微才具（basso ingegno）：我那不懂得从你身上引出爱、也不懂得从艺术材质中取出美的低能。啊，此刻你要独自微笑了，要说：可怜的卡齐米尔，他终于肯承认了？是的，是的，我什么都肯承认了。承认我不会画画，不会写作，不会作曲。承认我是个江湖骗子、卖假药的。承认我是个可笑的英雄戏演员，活该被人笑——也确实被人笑了。可是，若不计算一个人心里头脑里进行着什么，只从外面看，人人都是荒谬的。你能把《哈姆雷特》改成一部警句闹剧，安上一场无从摹仿的好戏：他捉奸他敬爱的母亲。你能把基督的一生编成最俏皮的莫泊桑短篇，让那疯拉比的自我期许同他卑贱的下场两相对照。这是个视角问题。每个人同时是一场行走中的闹剧和一场行走中的悲剧。踩上香蕉皮摔碎头骨的人，坠落时在天幕上描出一幅最富喜剧性的花体弧线。而你呢，迈拉——你想想那些无情的长舌妇怎么说你？在她们眼里，你的生活是林荫大道上哪一出闹剧？在我眼里，迈拉，你始终行走在一出无名而费解的悲剧之中。在她们眼里你是什么？不过是一个随便是哪一种的荡妇，有些有趣的艳遇罢了。而我是什么？一个江湖骗子、卖假药的、自负、吹牛、说大话的蠢材，除了苦艾酒招贴什么也画不出来。（为什么那句话伤得那么厉害？我不知道。你若愿意那样想，本没有什么不可以。）我全都是——而且可笑得骇人。你的笑很可能有理，你的判断很可能属实。我不知道。我说不准。也许我就是个骗子。也许我不真诚；对别人吹嘘，欺骗自己。我告诉你，我不知道。此刻我心里一团混乱。整个织物仿佛塌成了碎片；躺在可怕的混沌里。我在自身之内理不出一点秩序。我对自己撒过谎吗？我演过、摆过大人物的姿势，好说服自己就是那样的人吗？我肚子里有点东西，还是空空如也？我可曾成就过一件与我的构想、我的梦相称的事——（梦是好的；这一点我确信）？我望进我这灵魂的混沌，我告诉你，我不知道，我不知道。但我确知的是：我花了将近二十年，扮演你们人人取笑的那个骗子。为演这个角色，我在精神上、也在肉体上吃过苦——有时几乎挨饿。我挣扎过，我意气风发地扑上去攀登过，我被摔下来过——啊，多少回！——我爬起来重新开始过。好吧，只要你们愿意那样看，这一切想必都荒谬。一个人为他其实根本不存在的东西长久地吃苦，是荒谬的。精妙绝伦的滑稽，我看得见。可以说，我能在抽象中看见它。可在眼下这件事上，你得记住，我不是个冷静的旁观者。而此刻压倒我的不是笑。是一种难以描述的不幸，是死亡本身的苦味。死，死，死。我对自己一遍又一遍重复这个字眼。我想死亡，我试着想象它，我俯身悬在它上方往下看，石头落啊落，一声可怕的响，随后又是寂静；往那口死亡之井里看。它深得井底连一点水面的闪光也望不见。我没有蜡烛可以送下去。这可怕，但我不想再活。活着会比……」

===Original===
Lypiatt was reaching out for another sheet of paper when he was startled to hear the sound of feet on the stairs. He turned towards the door. His heart beat with violence. He was filled with a strange sense of apprehension. In terror he awaited the approach of some unknown and terrible being. The feet of the angel of death were on the stairs. Up, up, up. Lypiatt felt himself trembling as the sound came nearer. He knew for certain that in a few seconds he was going to die. The hangmen had already pinioned him; the soldiers of the firing squad had already raised their rifles. One, two,﻿ ﻿… he thought of Mrs. Viveash standing, bareheaded, the wind blowing in her hair, at the foot of the flagstaff from the site of which Queen Victoria had admired the distant view of Selborne; he thought of her dolorously smiling; he remembered that once she had taken his head between her two hands and kissed him: “Because you’re such a golden ass,” she had said, laughing. Three.﻿ ﻿… There was a little tap at the door. Lypiatt pressed his hand over his heart. The door opened.
===Chinese===
利皮亚特正伸手去拿另一张纸，楼梯上的脚步声吓了他一跳。他朝门口转过身。心剧烈地跳。他被一种莫名的惊惶充满。他恐惧地等待着某个未知的可怕存在的临近。死亡天使的脚步在楼梯上。上，上，上。声音越来越近，利皮亚特觉得自己在发抖。他确知再过几秒钟他就要死了。刽子手已经捆好他的双臂；行刑队的士兵已经举起步枪。一，二，……他想起飞维什夫人光着头站在那根旗杆下，风撩着她的头发——维多利亚女王正是从那里眺望过塞尔博恩的远景；他想她忧伤地微笑的样子；他记得她曾把他的头捧在两手之间吻过他：「因为你就是这么一头金毛驴。」她当时笑着说。三。……门上轻轻敲了一声。利皮亚特把一只手按在心口。门开了。

===Original===
A small, birdlike man with a long, sharp nose and eyes as round and black and shining as buttons stepped into the room.

“Mr. Lydgate, I presume?” he began. Then looked at a card on which a name and address were evidently written. “Lypiatt, I mean. A thousand pardons. Mr. Lypiatt, I presume?”

Lypiatt leaned back in his chair and shut his eyes. His face was as white as paper. He breathed hard and his temples were wet with sweat, as though he had been running.

“I found the door down below open, so I came straight up. I hope you’ll excuse.﻿ ﻿…” The stranger smiled apologetically.

“Who are you?” Lypiatt asked, reopening his eyes. His heart was still beating hard; after the storm it calmed itself slowly. He drew back from the brink of the fearful well; the time had not yet come to plunge.
===Chinese===
一个矮小、鸟一样的男人走进屋来，长而尖的鼻子，一双纽扣似的又圆又黑又亮的眼睛。

「我猜是利德盖先生吧？」他开了口。接着看了看一张显然写着姓名地址的卡片。「是利皮亚特，对不起。一千个抱歉。我猜是利皮亚特先生吧？」

利皮亚特朝椅背上一靠，闭起眼。他的脸白得像纸。他呼吸粗重，鬓角汗湿，仿佛刚跑过步。

「我见楼下门开着，就径直上来了。望您恕罪……」陌生人歉意地微笑。

「你是谁？」利皮亚特睁开眼问。他的心还在猛跳；风暴过后，它慢慢平复下来。他从那口可怕井的边上退了回来；跳下去的时辰还没有到。

===Original===
“My name,” said the stranger, “is Boldero, Herbert Boldero. Our mutual friend Mr. Gumbril, Mr. Theodore Gumbril, junior,” he made it more precise, “suggested that I might come and see you about a little matter in which he and I are interested and in which perhaps you, too, might be interested.”

Lypiatt nodded, without saying anything.

Mr. Boldero, meanwhile, was turning his bright, birdlike eyes about the studio. Mrs. Viveash’s portrait, all but finished now, was clamped to the easel. He approached it, a connoisseur.

“It reminds me very much,” he said, “of Bacosso. Very much indeed, if I may say so. Also a little of﻿ ﻿…” he hesitated, trying to think of the name of that other fellow Gumbril had talked about. But being unable to remember the unimpressive syllables of Derain he played for safety and said﻿—“of Orpen.” Mr. Boldero looked inquiringly at Lypiatt to see if that was right.

Lypiatt still spoke no word and seemed, indeed, not to have heard what had been said.
===Chinese===
「鄙姓博尔德罗，」那陌生人说，「赫伯特·博尔德罗。咱们的共同朋友冈布里尔先生——小西奥多·冈布里尔先生，」他补精确道，「建议我来见您，谈一桩他与我都关心的小事，或许您也会感兴趣。」

利皮亚特点点头，没说话。

博尔德罗先生趁这当口，把他那双亮晶晶的鸟眼在画室里四处转。维维什夫人的肖像——如今差不多画完了——夹在画架上。他行家似的凑近去看。

「它让我很想，」他说，「想起巴科索。实在非常像，恕我直言。还有一点像……」他顿住了，努力回想冈布里尔说起过的那另一位先生的名字。但德兰这几个不起眼的音节实在想不起来，他求稳起见，改口说——「像奥彭。」博尔德罗先生询问地望着利皮亚特，看说对了没有。

利皮亚特依旧一言不发，看那样子，方才的话简直没听见。

===Original===
Mr. Boldero saw that it wasn’t much good talking about modern art. This chap, he thought, looked as though something were wrong with him. He hoped he hadn’t got influenza. There was a lot of the disease about. “This little affair I was speaking of,” he pursued, in another tone, “is a little business proposition that Mr. Gumbril and I have gone into together. A matter of pneumatic trousers,” he waved his hand airily.

Lypiatt suddenly burst out laughing, an embittered Titan. Where do flies go? Where do souls go? The barrel-organ, and now pneumatic trousers! Then, as suddenly, he was silent again. More literature? Another piece of acting? “Go on,” he said, “I’m sorry.”

“Not at all, not at all,” said Mr. Boldero indulgently. “I know the idea does seem a little humorous, if I may say so, at first. But I assure you, there’s money in it, Mr. Lydgate﻿—Mr. Lypiatt. Money!” Mr. Boldero paused a moment dramatically. “Well,” he went on, “our idea was to launch the new product with a good swingeing publicity campaign. Spend a few thousands in the papers and then get it good and strong into the Underground and on the hoardings, along with Owbridge’s and John Bull and the Golden Ballot. Now, for that, Mr. Lypiatt, we shall need, as you can well imagine, a few good striking pictures. Mr. Gumbril mentioned your name and suggested I should come and see you to find out if you would perhaps be agreeable to lending us your talent for this work. And I may add, Mr. Lypiatt,” he spoke with real warmth, “that having seen this example of your work”﻿—he pointed to the portrait of Mrs. Viveash﻿—“I feel that you would be eminently capable of.﻿ ﻿…”
===Chinese===
博尔德罗先生看出，谈现代艺术没什么用。这位老兄，他想，看上去像出了什么事。他可别是得了流感。近来这病流行。「我方才说的那件小事，」他换了个调子接着说，「是冈布里尔先生和我合伙做的一桩小小的生意提议。充气裤的事。」他潇洒地一挥手。

利皮亚特突然放声大笑，一个怨毒的提坦。苍蝇飞哪儿去？灵魂飞哪儿去？手摇风琴，如今又是充气裤！随即他又骤然沉默。又是文学？又是表演？「接着说，」他说，「对不起。」

「哪儿的话，哪儿的话，」博尔德罗先生宽容地说，「我知道，恕我直言，这主意乍看确有点滑稽。可我向您担保，这里有钞票，利德盖先生——利皮亚特先生。钞票！」博尔德罗先生戏剧性地顿了顿。「是这样，」他接着说，「我们的想法是拿一场轰轰烈烈的宣传攻势来推出新产品。先在报纸上花几千镑，然后在地铁路牌和广告墙上给足给猛，同奥桥润肺糖浆、《约翰牛》和金选票并排。那么，利皮亚特先生，您想得到，为此我们需要几幅响亮的好画。冈布里尔先生提了您的大名，建议我来拜访您，看看您是否愿意把您的才华借给我们做这件事。而且我可以说，利皮亚特先生，」他的话带着真心的热忱，「看了这幅您的作品」——他指指维维什夫人的肖像——「我觉得您完全能够……」

===Original===
He did not finish the sentence; for at this moment Lypiatt leapt up from his chair and, making a shrill, inarticulate, animal noise, rushed on the financier, seized him with both hands by the throat, shook him, threw him to the floor, then picked him up again by the coat collar and pushed him towards the door, kicking him as he went. A final kick sent Mr. Boldero tobogganing down the steep stairs. Lypiatt ran down after him; but Mr. Boldero had picked himself up, had opened the front door, slipped out, slammed it behind him, and was running up the mews before Lypiatt could get to the bottom of the stairs.
===Chinese===
他没有说完这句话；因为就在这一刻，利皮亚特从椅子里跳起来，发出一声尖厉的、非言语的兽性喊叫，朝这位金融家猛扑过去，双手掐住他的喉咙，摇晃他，把他掼倒在地，又揪住衣领把他提起来往门口推，一路连踢带搡。最后一脚把博尔德罗先生顺着陡峭的楼梯雪橇似的送了下去。利皮亚特跟着追下楼去；但博尔德罗先生已经爬起来，拉开正门，闪身出去，砰地带上门，等利皮亚特下到楼梯脚，他已经跑出巷子老远了。

===Original===
Lypiatt opened the door and looked out. Mr. Boldero was already far away, almost at the Piranesian arch. He watched him till he was out of sight, then went upstairs again and threw himself face downwards on his bed.
===Chinese===
利皮亚特开门张望。博尔德罗先生已经跑远，快到那座皮拉内西式的拱门了。他望着他直到看不见，然后又上楼，扑倒在床上，脸朝下。

## XX / 二十

===Original===
Zoe ended the discussion by driving half an inch of penknife into Coleman’s left arm and running out of the flat, slamming the door behind her. Coleman was used to this sort of thing; this sort of thing, indeed, was what he was there for. Carefully he pulled out the penknife which had remained sticking in his arm. He looked at the blade and was relieved to see that it wasn’t so dirty as might have been expected. He found some cotton-wool, mopped up the blood as it oozed out, and dabbed the wound with iodine. Then he set himself to bandage it up. But to tie a bandage round one’s own left arm is not easy. Coleman found it impossible to keep the lint in place, impossible to get the bandage tight enough. At the end of a quarter of an hour he had only succeeded in smearing himself very copiously with blood, and the wound was still unbound. He gave up the attempt and contented himself with swabbing up the blood as it came out.

“And forthwith came there out blood and water,” he said aloud, and looked at the red stain on the cotton wool. He repeated the words again and again, and at the fiftieth repetition burst out laughing.
===Chinese===
佐薇用把小刀往科尔曼左臂里捅进半英寸、随后冲出公寓摔门而去的办法，结束了那场争论。科尔曼惯于这种事；说实在的，这种事正是他待在那儿的目的。他仔细拔出扎在臂上没掉的小刀，看看刀刃，宽慰地发现它没有预想的那么脏。他找出些药棉，把渗出的血揩掉，往伤口上点了碘酒，然后动手包扎。可要给自己的左臂打绷带并不容易。科尔曼发现要让绒布贴住原位不可能，要把绷带缠得够紧也不可能。一刻钟下来，他只成功地把血抹得满身都是，伤口仍敞着。他放弃了尝试，满足于血一流出来就把它揩掉。

「随即流出血和水来，」他大声说，看着药棉上的红渍。他把这字句一遍又一遍地重复，到第五十遍，放声大笑起来。

===Original===
The bell in the kitchen suddenly buzzed. Who could it be? He went to the front door and opened it. On the landing outside stood a tall slender young woman with slanting Chinese eyes and a wide mouth, elegantly dressed in a black frock piped with white. Keeping the cotton-wool still pressed to his bleeding arm, Coleman bowed as gracefully as he could.

“Do come in,” he said. “You are just in the nick of time. I am on the point of bleeding to death. And forthwith came there out blood and water. Enter, enter,” he added, seeing the young woman still standing irresolutely on the threshold.

“But I wanted to see Mr. Coleman,” she said, stammering a little and showing her embarrassment by blushing.

“I am Mr. Coleman.” He took the cotton-wool for a moment from his arm and looked with the air of a connoisseur at the blood on it. “But I shall very soon cease to be that individual unless you come and tie up my wounds.”
===Chinese===
厨房里的门铃忽然嗡嗡响起来。会是谁呢？他走到前门开门。外面平台上立着一位高挑苗条的年轻女子，一双倾斜的丹凤眼，一张阔嘴，一身黑底白滚边的连衣裙，穿戴入时。科尔曼一只手仍把药棉按在流血的胳膊上，尽量优雅地鞠了一躬。

「快请进，」他说，「您来得正是时候。我马上就要流血而死。随即流出血和水来。进，进。」他见那年轻女子还踌躇在门槛上，又补了一句。

「可我要找的是科尔曼先生。」她说，微微结巴，脸红着露出窘态。

「我就是科尔曼先生。」他把药棉从臂上拿开片刻，行家似的看上面那摊血。「可您要是不进来给我把伤口包上，我很快就不成其为那个人了。」

===Original===
“But you’re not the Mr. Coleman I thought you were,” said the young lady, still more embarrassed. “You have a beard, it is true; but.﻿ ﻿…”

“Then I must resign myself to quit this life, must I?” He made a gesture of despair, throwing out both hands, “Out, out brief Coleman. Out, damned spot,” and he made as though to close the door.

The young lady checked him. “If you really need tying up,” she said, “I’ll do it of course. I passed my First-Aid Exam, in the war.”

Coleman reopened the door. “Saved!” he said. “Come in.”
===Chinese===
「可您不是我以为的那个科尔曼先生，」年轻女士说，更窘了。「您是留着胡子，这不错；可是……」

「那我只好认命退出此生喽？」他做了个绝望的手势，双手一甩，「熄灭吧，短促的科尔曼。出去，该死的血斑。」他作势要关门。

年轻女士拦住了他。「您要是真需要包扎，」她说，「我当然可以。大战时我考过急救考试。」

科尔曼重新开了门。「得救了！」他说，「进来吧。」

===Original===
It had been Rosie’s original intention yesterday to go straight on from Mr. Mercaptan’s to Toto’s. She would see him at once, she would ask him what he meant by playing that stupid trick on her. She would give him a good talking to. She would even tell him that she would never see him again. But, of course, if he showed himself sufficiently contrite and reasonably explanatory, she would consent﻿—oh, very reluctantly﻿—to take him back into favour. In the free, unprejudiced circles in which she now moved, this sort of joke, she imagined, was a mere trifle. It would be absurd to quarrel seriously about it. But still, she was determined to give Toto a lesson.

When, however, she did finally leave Mr. Mercaptan’s delicious boudoir, it was too late to think of going all the way to Pimlico, to the address which Mr. Mercaptan had given her. She decided to put it off till the next day.
===Chinese===
罗茜昨天的本意，是从默卡普坦先生家直接去托托那里。她要立刻见到他，质问他开那种蠢玩笑是什么意思。她要好好训他一顿。她甚至要告诉他，再也不见他了。不过当然啦，倘若他显得够悔罪、解释得还算合理，她会答应——哦，极其勉强地——重新把他纳入恩宠。在她如今活动的那个自由不拘的圈子里，这种玩笑，她想，不过是小事一桩。为它认真吵架未免可笑。不过，她还是打定主意要给托托一个教训。

然而等她终于离开默卡普坦先生那间妙不可言的闺房，已经太晚，来不及再一路赶到皮姆利科去那个默卡普坦先生给她的地址了。她决定推迟到第二天。

===Original===
And so the next day, duly, she had set out for Pimlico﻿—to Pimlico, and to see a man called Coleman! It seemed rather dull and second-rate after Sloane Street and Mr. Mercaptan. Poor Toto!﻿—the sparkle of Mr. Mercaptan had made him look rather tarnished. That essay on the “Jus Primæ Noctis”﻿—ah! Walking through the unsavoury mazes of Pimlico, she thought of it, and, thinking of it, smiled. Poor Toto! And also, she mustn’t forget, stupid, malicious, idiotic Toto! She had made up her mind exactly what she should say to him; she had even made up her mind what Toto would say to her. And when the scene was over they would go and dine at the Café Royal﻿—upstairs, where she had never been. And she would make him rather jealous by telling him how much she had liked Mr. Mercaptan; but not too jealous. Silence is golden, as her father used to say when she used to fly into tempers and wanted to say nasty things to everybody within range. Silence, about some things, is certainly golden.
===Chinese===
于是第二天，她如期出发去了皮姆利科——去皮姆利科，去见一个姓科尔曼的人！在斯隆街和默卡普坦先生之后，这显得怪乏味、怪二流。可怜的托托！——默卡普坦先生的机智把他衬得黯然失色。那篇论《初夜权》的文章——啊！穿过皮姆利科污浊的迷宫时，她想着它，想着想着便微笑。可怜的托托！而且——这可不能忘——愚蠢、恶毒、白痴的托托！她已经一字一句打定主意要对他说什么；甚至连托托会怎么回答她都打好了腹稿。等这出戏演完，他们要去王家咖啡馆吃饭——上楼，她还没上过楼。她要讲她多么喜欢默卡普坦先生，惹他吃点醋；但不可太甚。沉默是金，她父亲从前常说——每当她大发脾气、想对射程之内的每个人说难听话的时候。在某些事情上，沉默确是金。

===Original===
In the rather gloomy little turning off Lupus Street to which she had been directed, Rosie found the number, found, in the row of bells and cards, the name. Quickly and decidedly she mounted the stairs.

“Well,” she was going to say as soon as she saw him, “I thought you were a civilized being.” Mr. Mercaptan had dropped a hint that Coleman wasn’t really civilized; a hint was enough for Rosie. “But I see,” she would go on, “that I was mistaken. I don’t like to associate with boors.” The fastidious lady had selected him as a young poet, not as a ploughboy.

Well rehearsed, Rosie rang the bell. And then the door had opened on this huge bearded Cossack of a man, who smiled, who looked at her with bright, dangerous eyes, who quoted the Bible and who was bleeding like a pig. There was blood on his shirt, blood on his trousers, blood on his hands, bloody fingermarks on his face; even the blond fringe of his beard, she noticed, was dabbled here and there with blood. It was too much, at first, even for her aristocratic equanimity.
===Chinese===
在她被指到的那条卢普斯街旁颇为阴郁的小拐巷里，罗茜找到了门牌，在一排门铃和名片中间找到了那个名字。她干脆利落地上楼去了。

「好啊，」她打算一见面就说，「我还当您是个文明人。」默卡普坦先生漏过一点口风，说科尔曼算不得真文明；一点口风对罗茜就足够了。「可我如今明白了，是我弄错了。我不喜欢同粗人为伍。」讲究的贵妇挑中他，挑的是一位年轻诗人，不是一个庄稼汉。

排练停当，罗茜按响门铃。接着门开了，出来这么一个大胡子哥萨克，笑着，用亮而危险的眼睛打量她，引着圣经，还像猪一样流着血。他衬衫上是血，裤子上是血，手上是血，脸上带着血指印；连他胡子的金色须缘，她也注意到，也血迹斑斑。这一下起初连她贵族式的镇定也有点吃不消。

===Original===
In the end, however, she followed him across a little vestibule into a bright, whitewashed room empty of all furniture but a table, a few chairs and a large box-spring and mattress, which stood like an island in the middle of the floor and served as bed or sofa as occasion required. Over the mantelpiece was pinned a large photographic reproduction of Leonardo’s study of the anatomy of love. There were no other pictures on the walls.

“All the apparatus is here,” said Coleman, and he pointed to the table. “Lint, bandages, cotton-wool, iodine, gauze, oiled silk. I have them all ready in preparation for these little accidents.”

“But do you often manage to cut yourself in the arm?” asked Rosie. She took off her gloves and began to undo a fresh packet of lint.

“One gets cut,” Coleman explained. “Little differences of opinion, you know. If your eye offend you, pluck it out; love your neighbour as yourself. Argal: if his eye offend you﻿—you see? We live on Christian principles here.”
===Chinese===
不过末了她还是跟着他穿过一个小门厅，进了一间粉刷得雪亮的屋子，家什只有一张桌子、几把椅子，还有一张大弹簧垫床垫，像一座岛立在地板中央，按需要权当床或沙发。壁炉上方钉着一大幅达·芬奇爱情解剖习作的摄影复制品。墙上再没有别的画。

「家伙什都在这儿，」科尔曼指着桌子说，「绒布、绷带、药棉、碘酒、纱布、油绸。这类小小不测，我都备着应急。」

「可您常有办法让胳膊挨刀？」罗茜问。她脱下手套，开始拆一卷新绒布。

「总有挨刀的时候，」科尔曼解释，「小小的意见分歧嘛，您知道。眼睛犯了罪就剜掉它；爱邻如己。Argal（所以说）：邻人的眼睛犯了罪——明白吗？我们这儿是按基督教原则过活的。」

===Original===
“But who are ‘we’?” asked Rosie, giving the cut a last dressing of iodine and laying a big square of lint over it.

“Merely myself and﻿—how shall I put it?﻿—my helpmate,” Coleman answered. “Ah! you’re wonderfully skilful at this business,” he went on. “You’re the real hospital nurse type; all maternal instincts. When pain and anguish wring the brow, an interesting mangle thou, as we used to say in the good old days when the pun and the Spoonerismus were in fashion.”

Rosie laughed. “Oh, I don’t spend all my time tying up wounds,” she said, and turned her eyes for an instant from the bandage. After the first surprise she was feeling her cool self again.

“Brava!” cried Coleman. “You make them too, do you? Make them first and cure them afterwards in the grand old homœopathic way. Delightful! You see what Leonardo has to say about it.” With his free hand he pointed to the photograph over the mantelpiece.
===Chinese===
「『我们』是谁？」罗茜问，给伤口上了最后一道碘酒，覆上一大块方绒布。

「不过是我自己和——怎么说好呢？——我的内助，」科尔曼答道。「啊！这活儿您干得熟极了，」他接着说，「您是真正的医院护士型；一身的母性本能。疼痛与悲苦拧皱蛾眉时，你是一台有趣的绞干机——从前景气、双关语和斯普纳首音互换流行的年月，我们常这么说。」

罗茜笑了。「哦，我可没把时间都花在包扎伤口上。」她说，眼睛从绷带上抬了一瞬。最初的惊愕过后，她又恢复了冷静的自己。

「Brava（好极了）！」科尔曼喊道，「您还制造伤口，是吗？先制造，再照老祖宗顺势疗法的老样子治疗。妙极了！您看看达·芬奇对此怎么说。」他用闲着的那只手指了指壁炉上方的照片。

===Original===
Rosie, who had noticed the picture when she came into the room, preferred not to look at it too closely a second time. “I think it’s rather revolting,” she said, and was very busy with the bandage.

“Ah! but that’s the point, that’s the whole point,” said Coleman, and his clear blue eyes were alive with dancing lights. “That’s the beauty of the grand passion. It *is* revolting. You read what the Fathers of the Church have to say about love. They’re the men. It was Odo of Cluny, wasn’t it, who called woman a *saccus stercoris*, a bag of muck. *Si quis enim considerat quae intra nares et quae intra fauces et quae intra ventrem lateant, sordes ubique reperiet.*” The Latin rumbled like eloquent thunder in Coleman’s mouth. “*Et si nec extremis digitis flegma vel stercus tangere patimur, quomodo ipsum stercoris saccum amplecti desideramus.*” He smacked his lips. “Magnificent!” he said.

“I don’t understand Latin,” said Rosie, “and I’m glad of it. And your bandage is finished. Look.”
===Chinese===
罗茜进屋时就注意到了那幅画，这会儿宁可不再细看。「我觉得它相当恶心。」她说，只顾忙着缠绷带。

「啊！这正是关键，全部的关键，」科尔曼说，他清亮的蓝眼睛里跳动着光彩。「这便是伟大激情的美。它就是恶心。您去读读教父们论爱情的那些话。那才是汉子。是克吕尼的奥多吧，把女人叫做 saccus stercoris，一袋粪污。Si quis enim considerat quae intra nares et quae intra fauces et quae intra ventrem lateant, sordes ubique reperiet（但凡想一想鼻腔里、喉咙里、肚腹里各藏着什么，便处处见污秽）。」拉丁文在科尔曼口里滚动，如雄辩的雷。他咂咂嘴。「妙极！」他说。

「我不懂拉丁文，」罗茜说，「我为此高兴。您的绷带缠好了。看。」

===Original===
“Interesting mangle!” Coleman smiled his thanks. “But Bishop Odo, I fear, wouldn’t even have spared you; not even for your good works. Still less for your good looks, which would only have provoked him to dwell with the more insistency on the visceral secrets which they conceal.”

“Really,” Rosie protested. She would have liked to get up and go away, but the Cossack’s blue eyes glittered at her with such a strange expression and he smiled so enigmatically, that she found herself still sitting where she was, listening with a disgusted pleasure to his quick talk, his screams of deliberate and appalling laughter.

“Ah!” he exclaimed, throwing up his hands, “what sensualists these old fellows were! What a real voluptuous feeling they had for dirt and gloom and sordidness and boredom, and all the horrors of vice. They pretended they were trying to dissuade people from vice by enumerating its horrors. But they were really only making it more spicy by telling the truth about it. *O esca vermium, O massa pulveris!* What nauseating embracements! To conjugate the copulative verb, boringly, with a sack of tripes﻿—what could be more exquisitely and piercingly and deliriously vile?” And he threw back his head and laughed; the blood-dabbled tips of his blond beard shook. Rosie looked at them, fascinated with disgust.

“There’s blood on your beard,” she felt compelled to say.
===Chinese===
「有趣的绞干机！」科尔曼笑纳了她的谢意。「不过奥多主教，我怕是连您也不会放过；哪怕是看在您的善行上。更不会看在您的美貌上——那只会招他把那美貌所遮掩的内脏秘密，讲得越发执拗。」

「真的，」罗茜抗议。她本想起身就走，可那哥萨克的蓝眼睛带着那么古怪的神情灼灼地盯着她，他笑得那么莫测，她发现自己仍旧坐在原处，怀着厌恶的快意听他滔滔而谈，听他一声声蓄意的、骇人的大笑。

「啊！」他双手一扬，喊道，「这些老家伙多好色啊！他们对污秽、幽暗、腌臜、无聊，对罪恶的一切恐怖，怀着一股多么地道的声色之乐！他们装作历数罪恶的恐怖来劝人勿犯。其实不过是说了实话，把它变得更够味。O esca vermium, O massa pulveris（蛆虫之食啊，尘土之聚啊）！何等令人作呕的拥抱！同大肠 copulative 动词无聊地变位——还有什么比这更精微、更刺骨、更癫狂的邪劣？」他仰头大笑；金胡子血渍斑斑的须尖直颤。罗茜望着它们，厌恶得入了迷。

「您胡子上有血。」她忍不住说。

===Original===
“What of it? Why shouldn’t there be?” Coleman asked.

Confused, Rosie felt herself blushing. “Only because it’s rather unpl﻿—leasant. I don’t know why. But it is.”

“What a reason for immediately falling into my arms!” said Coleman. “To be kissed by a beard is bad enough at any time. But by a bloody beard﻿—imagine!”

Rosie shuddered.
===Chinese===
「那又如何？为什么不该有？」科尔曼反问。

罗茜发窘，觉得脸红了。「只因为那相当不愉——愉快。我说不清为什么。可就是那样。」

「多么好的扑进我怀抱的理由！」科尔曼说，「被胡子亲一亲，无论何时都够糟的了。被血胡子——想想看！」

罗茜打了个寒噤。

===Original===
“After all,” he said, “what interest or amusement is there in doing the ordinary things in the obvious way? Life *au naturel*.” He shook his head. “You must have garlic and saffron. Do you believe in God?”

“Not m﻿—much,” said Rosie, smiling.

“I pity you. You must find existence dreadfully dull. As soon as you do, everything becomes a thousand times life-size. Phallic symbols five hundred feet high,” he lifted his hand. “A row of grinning teeth you could run the hundred yards on.” He grinned at her through his beard. “Wounds big enough to let a coach-and-six drive into their purulent recesses. Every slightest act eternally significant. It’s only when you believe in God, and especially in hell, that you can really begin enjoying life. For instance, when in a few moments you surrender yourself to the importunities of my bloody beard, how prodigiously much more you’d enjoy it if you could believe you were committing the sin against the Holy Ghost﻿—if you kept thinking calmly and dispassionately all the time the affair was going on: All this is not only a horrible sin, it is also ugly, grotesque, a mere defæcation, a﻿—”

Rosie held up her hand. “You’re really horrible,” she said. Coleman smiled at her. Still, she did not go.
===Chinese===
「说到底，」他说，「照老样子做寻常事，一板一眼，还有什么趣味？赤裸裸的生活（au naturel）。」他摇摇头。「得有大蒜和番红花。您信上帝吗？」

「不——不太信。」罗茜笑着说。

「我替您惋惜。您一定觉得存在乏味得要命。一旦信了，样样东西都放大一千倍。五百英尺高的男根象征，」他举起手，「一排龇着的牙，能在上面跑百码。」他从胡子里冲她龇牙一笑。「大得能让六驾马车驶进其化脓深处的伤口。最微小的举动都有永恒的意义。只有信了上帝、尤其信了地狱，你才真正开始享受生活。比方说，几分钟后您就要屈从于我这血胡子的纠缠了，倘若你能相信你正在犯亵渎圣灵之罪——倘若这场好事进行的全程，你都平心静气地不断想：这一切不但是可怕的罪，而且丑陋、怪诞，不过是场排泄，一场——」

罗茜举手。「您真是可怕。」她说。科尔曼对她微笑。可她还是没有走。

===Original===
“He who is not with me is against me,” said Coleman. “If you can’t make up your mind to be with, it’s surely better to be positively against than merely negatively indifferent.”

“Nonsense!” exclaimed Rosie feebly.

“When I call my lover a nymphomaniacal dog, she runs the penknife into my arm.”

“Well, do you enjoy it?” asked Rosie.

“Piercingly,” he answered. “It is at once sordid to the last and lowest degree and infinitely and eternally significant.”
===Chinese===
「不与我相合的，就是敌我的，」科尔曼说。「您若下不了决心相合，那么积极为敌，总比消极冷漠好些。」

「胡说！」罗茜有气无力地喝道。

「我管我的情人叫慕男狂的母狗，她就把小刀捅进我的胳膊。」

「那您觉得痛快吗？」罗茜问。

「刺骨地痛快，」他答，「它同时卑污到最低极限，又无限而永恒地意义重大。」

===Original===
Coleman was silent and Rosie too said nothing. Futilely she wished it *had* been Toto instead of this horrible, dangerous Cossack. Mr. Mercaptan ought to have warned her. But then, of course, he supposed that she already knew the creature. She looked up at him and found his bright eyes fixed upon her; he was silently laughing.

“Don’t you want to know who I am?” she asked. “And how I got here?”

Coleman blandly shook his head. “Not in the very least,” he said.

Rosie felt more helpless, somehow, than ever. “Why not?” she asked as bravely and impertinently as she could.

Coleman answered with another question. “Why should I?”

“It would be natural curiosity.”
===Chinese===
科尔曼沉默了，罗茜也没说话。她徒然地想，来的要是托托而不是这个可怕而危险的哥萨克该多好。默卡普坦先生本该提醒她的。不过话说回来，他自然当她是认识这东西的。她抬头看他，正撞上他那双亮眼睛盯住她；他在无声地笑。

「您不想知道我是谁吗？」她问。「还有我怎么来的这儿？」

科尔曼温和地摇摇头。「一点也不想。」他说。

不知怎么，罗茜觉得比先前更没了主意。「为什么不想？」她尽量大胆无礼地问。

科尔曼用另一个问题作答。「凭什么想？」

「出于正常的好奇心嘛。」

===Original===
“But I know all I want to know,” he said. “You are a woman, or, at any rate, you have all the female stigmata. Not too sumptuously well-developed, let me add. You have no wooden legs. You have eyelids that flutter up and down over your eyes like a moving shutter in front of a signalling lamp, spelling out in a familiar code the letters: A.M.O.R., and not, unless I am very much mistaken, those others: C.A.S.T.I.T.A.S. You have a mouth that looks as though it knew how to taste and how to bite. You.﻿ ﻿…”

Rosie jumped up. “I’m going away,” she said.

Coleman leaned back in his chair and hallooed with laughter. “Bite, bite, bite,” he said. “Thirty-two times.” And he opened and shut his mouth as fast as he could, so that his teeth clicked against one another with a little dry, bony noise. “Every mouthful thirty-two times. That’s what Mr. Gladstone said. And surely Mr. Gladstone”﻿—he rattled his sharp, white teeth again﻿—“surely Mr. Gladstone should know.”
===Chinese===
「可我知道的都够我用了，」他说，「你是个女人，或者至少，具备女性的全部印记。让我补一句，发育得不算太丰腴。你没有木腿。你有一副眼睑，在眼睛上扑闪扑闪，像信号灯前的活动百叶，用熟稔的电码拼出那几个字母：A.M.O.R.（爱），而——除非我大错特错——不是另外那几个：C.A.S.T.I.T.A.S.（贞）。你有一张嘴，看着就懂得怎样品尝、怎样咬人。你……」

罗茜跳起来。「我走了。」她说。

科尔曼朝椅背上一仰，高声大笑。「咬，咬，咬，」他说，「三十二下。」他把嘴尽可能快地一开一合，牙齿相互磕碰，发出细小干燥的骨响。「每一口嚼三十二下。格拉德斯通先生说的。格拉德斯通先生，」——他又把那口尖白的牙打了个响——「格拉德斯通先生总该懂吧。」

===Original===
“Goodbye,” said Rosie from the door.

“Goodbye,” Coleman called back; and immediately afterwards jumped to his feet and made a dash across the room towards her.

Rosie uttered a cry, slipped through the door and, slamming it behind her, ran across the vestibule and began fumbling with the latches of the outer door. It wouldn’t open, it wouldn’t open. She was trembling; fear made her feel sick. There was a rattling at the door behind her. There was a whoop of laughter, and then the Cossack’s hands were on her arms, his face came peering over her shoulder, and the blond beard dabbled with blood prickled against her neck and face.

“Oh, don’t, don’t, don’t!” she implored, turning away her head. Then all at once she began violently crying.

“Tears!” exclaimed Coleman in rapture, “genuine tears!” He bent eagerly forward to kiss them away, to drink them as they fell. “What an intoxication,” he said, looking up to the ceiling like a chicken that has taken a sip of water; he smacked his lips.
===Chinese===
「再见。」罗茜在门口说。

「再见，」科尔曼回了一声；紧接着他一跃而起，横穿房间朝她扑去。

罗茜喊了一声，闪身出门，砰地关上门，穿过门厅，慌手慌脚去摸外门的门闩。门不开，门不开。她发着抖；恐惧使她想吐。身后门上一阵乱响。一声大笑的呼哨，随即哥萨克的两只手已扣上她的胳膊，他的脸从她肩头探过来，血渍斑斑的金胡子扎上她的脖颈和脸。

「哦，别，别，别！」她把头扭开，哀求道。忽然，她放声大哭起来。

「眼泪！」科尔曼狂喜地喊道，「真眼泪！」他急切地俯身去吻干它们，去接饮它们。「何等的沉醉，」他说，像喝了一口水的小鸡那样仰头望着天花板；他咂了咂嘴。

===Original===
Sobbing uncontrollably, Rosie had never in all her life felt less like a great, fastidious lady.
===Chinese===
罗茜抽噎个不停，有生以来从不曾这么不像一位伟大而讲究的贵妇。

## XXI / 二十一

===Original===
“Well,” said Gumbril, “here I am again.”

“Already?” Mrs. Viveash had been reduced, by the violence of her headache, to coming home after her luncheon with Piers Cotton for a rest. She had fed her hungry pain on Pyramidon and now she was lying down on the Dufy-upholstered sofa at the foot of her full-length portrait by Jacques-Emile Blanche. Her head was not much better, but she was bored. When the maid had announced Gumbril, she had given word that he was to be let in. “I’m very ill,” she went on expiringly. “Look at me,” she pointed to herself, “and me again.” She waved her hand towards the sizzling brilliance of the portrait. “Before and after. Like the advertisements, you know. Every picture tells a story.” She laughed faintly, then made a little grimace and, sucking in the breath between her lips, she put her hand to her forehead.
===Chinese===
「好啦，」冈布里尔说，「我又来了。」

「这就回来了？」同皮尔斯·科顿吃过午饭，维维什夫人被剧烈的头痛逼回家里歇息。她用金字塔隆喂过她那饥饿的疼痛，此刻正躺在杜菲布面的沙发上，沙发摆在雅克-埃米尔·布朗什给她画的那幅等身肖像的脚下。头不见得好多少，可是她闷了。女仆通报冈布里尔来访时，她吩咐放他进来。「我病得很重，」她接着说，声气奄奄的。「看看我，」她指指自己，「再看看又一个我。」她把手朝那幅肖像灼灼逼人的光彩一挥。「美容前后。像广告一样，你知道。Every picture tells a story（每幅画都讲一个故事）。」她浅浅一笑，随即做了个小鬼脸，倒抽一口凉气，把手按上额头。

===Original===
“My poor Myra.” Gumbril pulled up a chair to the sofa and sat there like a doctor at his patient’s bedside. “But before and after what?” he asked, almost professionally.

Mrs. Viveash gave an all but imperceptible shrug. “I don’t know,” she said.
===Chinese===
「我可怜的迈拉。」冈布里尔把一把椅子拖到沙发旁坐下，像个守在病人床边的医生。「可是美容前后什么？」他近乎职业化地问。

维维什夫人几乎察觉不出地耸了耸肩。「我不知道。」她说。

===Original===
“Not influenza, I hope?”

“No, I don’t think so.”

“Not love, by any chance?”

Mrs. Viveash did not venture another laugh; she contented herself with smiling agonizingly.
===Chinese===
「不是流感吧，我希望？」

「不，我想不是。」

「不会是相思吧？」

维维什夫人不敢再笑；她只痛楚地微笑了一下，聊作应付。

===Original===
“That would have been a just retribution,” Gumbril went on, “after what you’ve done to me.”

“What have I done to you?” Mrs. Viveash asked, opening wide her pale-blue eyes.

“Merely wrecked my existence.”

“But you’re being childish, Theodore. Say what you mean without these grand, silly phrases.” The dying voice spoke with impatience.
===Chinese===
「那倒是报应得刚刚好，」冈布里尔接着说，「考虑到你对我做的事。」

「我对你做了什么？」维维什夫人问，淡蓝色的眼睛睁得老大。

「不过是把我的存在毁了个干净。」

「你可真孩子气，西奥多。说话别用这些盛大而愚蠢的词句。」那垂死的声音不耐烦地说。

===Original===
“Well, what I mean,” said Gumbril, “is merely this. You prevented me from going to see the only person I ever really wanted to see in my life. And yesterday, when I tried to see her, she was gone. Vanished. And here am I left in the vacuum.”

Mrs. Viveash shut her eyes. “We’re all in the vacuum,” she said. “You’ll still have plenty of company, you know.” She was silent for a moment. “Still, I’m sorry,” she added. “Why didn’t you tell me? And why didn’t you just pay no attention to me and go all the same?”
===Chinese===
「好吧，我的意思是，」冈布里尔说，「就这么简单。你拦着我，不许我去见我此生唯一真正想见的人。而昨天，等我设法去见她时，她走了。无影无踪。我给撇在真空里了。」

维维什夫人闭上眼。「我们都在真空里，」她说，「你知道，你照样会有不少同伴。」她沉默了片刻。「不过，对不起，」她补了一句，「你为什么不告诉我？又为什么不听我的、照样去呢？」

===Original===
“I didn’t tell you,” Gumbril answered, “because, then, I didn’t know. And I didn’t go because I didn’t want to quarrel with you.”

“Thank you,” said Mrs. Viveash, and patted his hand, “But what are you going to do about it now? Not quarrelling with me is only a rather negative satisfaction, I’m afraid.”

“I propose to leave the country tomorrow morning,” said Gumbril.

“Ah, the classical remedy.﻿ ﻿… But not to shoot big game, I hope?” She thought of Viveash among the Tikki-tikkis and the tsetses. He was a charming creature; charming, but﻿ ﻿… but what?
===Chinese===
「我没告诉你，」冈布里尔回答，「是因为那时候我还不知道。我没去，是因为我不想同你吵架。」

「谢谢你，」维维什夫人说着，拍了拍他的手，「可你现在打算怎么办呢？不同我吵架，恐怕只是个相当消极的安慰。」

「我打算明早出国。」冈布里尔说。

「啊，经典疗法。……可我希望，不是去打大型猎物吧？」她想起提基提基人和采采蝇中间的维维什。他是个可爱的人；可爱，可是……可是什么呢？

===Original===
“Good heavens!” exclaimed Gumbril. “What do you take me for? Big game!” He leaned back in his chair and began to laugh, heartily, for the first time since he had returned from Robertsbridge, yesterday evening. He had felt then as though he would never laugh again. “Do you see me in a pith helmet, with an elephant gun?”

Mrs. Viveash put her hand to her forehead. “I see you, Theodore,” she said, “but I try to think you would look quite normal; because of my head.”

“I go to Paris first,” said Gumbril. “After that, I don’t know. I shall go wherever I think people will buy pneumatic trousers. I’m travelling on business.”

This time, in spite of her head, Mrs. Viveash laughed.
===Chinese===
「天哪！」冈布里尔叫起来，「你把我当什么人了？大型猎物！」他朝椅背上一仰，笑了起来，打昨天傍晚从罗伯茨布里奇回来后第一次笑得这样畅快。那时他觉得自己只怕再不会笑了。「你看得见我戴木髓盔、扛象枪的样子？」

维维什夫人把手按上额头。「我看得见，西奥多，」她说，「可我竭力想着你看上去会相当正常；为了我这条命的缘故。」

「我先去巴黎，」冈布里尔说，「之后，不知道。哪儿有人会买充气裤，我就去哪儿。我这是出差。」

这一回，不顾她的头，维维什夫人笑出了声。

===Original===
“I thought of giving myself a farewell banquet,” Gumbril went on. “We’ll go round before dinner, if you’re feeling well enough, that is, and collect a few friends. Then, in profoundest gloom, we’ll eat and drink. And in the morning, unshaved, exhausted and filled with disgust, I shall take the train from Victoria, feeling thankful to get out of England.”

“We’ll do it,” said Mrs. Viveash faintly and indomitably from the sofa that was almost genuinely a deathbed. “And, meanwhile, we’ll have a second brew of tea and you shall talk to me.”

The tannin was brought in. Gumbril settled down to talk and Mrs. Viveash to listen﻿—to listen and from time to time to dab her brows with eau de cologne, to take a sniff of hartshorn.
===Chinese===
「我想给自己办一场告别宴，」冈布里尔接着说，「要是你身子撑得住，我们先在晚饭前兜一圈，收罗几位朋友。然后，在最深沉的阴郁里，我们大吃大喝。明早，我没刮脸、筋疲力尽、满怀厌恶，从维多利亚车站上车，庆幸总算逃出了英格兰。」

「就这么办。」维维什夫人从那张几乎真算得上灵柩台的沙发上，微弱而不屈地说。「这会儿呢，我们先沏第二壶茶，你给我说话。」

丹宁茶端上来了。冈布里尔安顿下来开讲，维维什夫人听着——一面听，一面时不时往额上扑花露水，闻一闻鹿角精。

===Original===
Gumbril talked. He talked of the marriage ceremonies of octopuses, of the rites intricately consummated in the submarine green grottos of the Indian Ocean. Given a total of sixteen arms, how many permutations and combinations of caresses? And in the middle of each bunch of arms a mouth like the beak of a macaw.
===Chinese===
冈布里尔讲开了。他讲章鱼的婚礼，讲印度洋海底绿色洞窟里千回百转完成的仪礼。总共十六条腕，爱抚的排列组合能有多少种？而每束腕的中央，一张鹦鹉喙似的嘴。

===Original===
On the backside of the moon, his friend Umbilikoff, the mystic, used to assure him, the souls of the dead in the form of little bladders﻿—like so much swelled sago﻿—are piled up and piled up till they squash and squeeze one another with an excruciating and ever-growing pressure. In the exoteric world this squeezing on the moon’s backside is known, erroneously, as hell. And as for the constellation, Scorpio﻿—he was the first of all constellations to have a proper sort of backbone. For by an effort of the will he ingurgitated his external armour, he compressed and rebuilt it within his body and so became the first vertebrate. This, you may well believe, was a notable day in cosmic history.
===Chinese===
在月亮的背面，他的朋友、神秘主义者乌比利科夫过去常向他担保，死者的灵魂化身为一只只小泡——像发了的西米——越堆越高，越堆越高，直到彼此压挤，承受着与日俱增、痛彻的压强。在显教的世界里，月亮背面这番压挤被误称为地狱。至于天蝎宫——他是众星座中第一个长出像样脊椎的。凭一股意志之力，他把外部的甲壳吞进了肚，在体内把它压扁重塑，就此成了第一只脊椎动物。这，你们尽可以相信，是宇宙史上值得大书一笔的日子。

===Original===
The rents in these new buildings in Regent Street and Piccadilly run to as much as three or four pounds a square foot. Meanwhile, all the beauty imagined by Nash has departed, and chaos and barbarism once more reign supreme, even in Regent Street. The ghost of Gumbril Senior stalked across the room.
===Chinese===
摄政街和皮卡迪利这些新楼房的租金高达每平方英尺三四镑。与此同时，纳什构想的全部美都不见了，混乱与野蛮重新一统天下，连摄政街也不能幸免。老冈布里尔的鬼魂大步穿过房间。

===Original===
Who lives longer: the man who takes heroin for two years and dies, or the man who lives on roast beef, water and potatoes till ninety-five? One passes his twenty-four months in eternity. All the years of the beefeater are lived only in time. “I can tell you all about heroin,” said Mrs. Viveash.
===Chinese===
谁活得更久：吸两年海洛因而死的人，还是靠烤牛肉、水和土豆活到九十五的人？前者把他的二十四个月活在了永恒里。吃牛肉的人所有的年月，都只活在时间里。「海洛因的事我可以跟你讲个透彻。」维维什夫人说。

===Original===
Lady Capricorn, he understood, was still keeping open bed. How Rubens would have admired those silk cushions, those gigantic cabbage roses, those round pink pearls of hers, vaster than those that Captain Nemo discovered in the immemorial oyster! And the warm dry rustle of flesh over flesh as she walks, moving first one leg, then advancing the other.
===Chinese===
他听说，摩羯夫人仍旧门户大开、床笫常设。鲁本斯会多么艳羡那些丝绒软垫、那些巨大的包心菜玫瑰、她那些浑圆的粉红珍珠——比尼摩船长在太古牡蛎里采到的还大！还有她走路时皮肉相摩那温爽的窸窣，先挪一条腿，再递上另一条。

===Original===
Talking of octopuses, the swim-bladders of deep-sea fishes are filled with almost absolutely pure oxygen. *C’est la vie*﻿—Gumbril shrugged his shoulders.

In Alpine pastures the grasshoppers start their flight, whizzing like clockwork grasshoppers. And these brown invisible ones reveal themselves suddenly as they skim above the flowers﻿—a streak of blue lightning, a trailing curve of scarlet. Then the overwing shuts down over the coloured wing below and they are once more invisible fiddlers rubbing their thighs, like Lady Capricorn, at the foot of the towering flowers.
===Chinese===
说到章鱼，深水鱼的鳔里充满的几乎是绝对纯的氧气。C'est la vie（这就是生活）——冈布里尔耸耸肩。

在高山牧场上，蚱蜢起飞，呼呼作响像上了发条的蚱蜢。这些隐了形的褐色家伙掠过花顶时蓦地现形——一道蓝色闪电，一条拖曳的猩红弧线。随后覆翅合拢，盖住下面彩色的翅，它们又是看不见的提琴手了，在巍然的花脚下摩擦大腿——像摩羯夫人一样。

===Original===
Forgers give patina to their medieval ivories by lending them to stout young Jewesses to wear for a few months hanging, like an amulet, between their breasts.

In Italian cemeteries the family vaults are made of glass and iron, like greenhouses.

Sir Henry Griddle has finally married the hog-faced gentlewoman.

Piero della Francesca’s fresco of the Resurrection at San Sepolcro is the most beautiful picture in the world, and the hotel there is far from bad. Scriabine = *le* Tschaikovsky *de nos jours*. The dullest landscape painter is Marchand. The best poet.﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
伪造者们给中世纪象牙上包浆的办法，是把它们借给丰腴的年轻犹太女郎挂上几个月，像挂护身符一样，垂在双乳之间。

意大利墓园里的家族墓窖用玻璃和铁造，像温室。

亨利·格里德尔爵士终于娶了那位猪脸淑女。

皮耶罗·德拉·弗朗切斯卡在圣塞波尔克罗的《复活》壁画是世界上最美的画，那儿的那家旅馆也远不算差。斯克里亚宾＝我们这个时代的柴可夫斯基。最乏味的风景画家是马尚。最好的诗人。……

===Original===
“You bore me,” said Mrs. Viveash.

“Must I talk of love, then?” asked Gumbril.

“It looks like it,” Mrs. Viveash answered, and closed her eyes.
===Chinese===
「你叫我腻烦。」维维什夫人说。

「那我必须谈谈爱情了？」冈布里尔问。

「看来是。」维维什夫人答道，闭上了眼睛。

===Original===
Gumbril told the anecdote about Jo Peters, Connie Asticot and Jim Baum. The anecdote of Lola Knopf and the Baroness Gnomon. Of Margherita Radicofani, himself, and the Pastor Meyer. Of Lord Cavey and little Toby Nobes. When he had finished these, he saw that Mrs. Viveash had gone to sleep.

He was not flattered. But a little sleep would do her headache, he reflected, a world of good. And knowing that if he ceased to speak, she would probably be woken by the sudden blankness of the silence, he went on quietly talking to himself.
===Chinese===
冈布里尔讲了乔·彼得斯、康妮·阿斯蒂科和吉姆·鲍姆的轶事。讲了萝拉·诺普夫和格诺蒙男爵夫人的轶事。讲了玛格丽塔·拉迪科法尼、他自己，还有迈耶牧师的。讲了凯维勋爵和小托比·诺布斯的。讲完这些，他看见维维什夫人睡着了。

他不觉得受宠若惊。不过小睡一场，他想，对她的头痛大有好处。而且他知道，自己若是一住口，她多半会被那突然的沉默之空白惊醒，于是他继续轻声自言自语。

===Original===
“When I’m abroad this time,” he soliloquized, “I shall really begin writing my autobiography. There’s nothing like a hotel bedroom to work in.” He scratched his head thoughtfully and even picked his nose, which was one of his bad habits, when he was alone. “People who know me,” he went on, “will think that what I write about the governess cart and my mother and the flowers and so on is written merely because I know in here,” he scratched his head a little harder to show himself that he referred to his brain, “that that’s the sort of thing one ought to write about. They’ll think I’m a sort of dingy Romain Rolland, hopelessly trying to pretend that I feel the emotions and have the great spiritual experiences, which the really important people do feel and have. And perhaps they’ll be right. Perhaps the Life of Gumbril will be as manifestly an *ersatz* as the Life of Beethoven. On the other hand, they may be astonished to find that it’s the genuine article. We shall see.” Gumbril nodded his head slowly, while he transferred two pennies from his right-hand trouser pocket to his left-hand trouser pocket. He was somewhat distressed to find that these coppers had been trespassing among the silver. Silver was for the right-hand, copper for the left. It was one of the laws which it was extremely unlucky to infringe. “I have a premonition,” he went on, “that one of these days I may become a saint. An unsuccessful flickering sort of saint, like a candle beginning to go out. As for love﻿—m’yes, m’yes. And as for the people I have met﻿—I shall point out that I have known most of the eminent men in Europe, and that I have said of all of them what I said after my first love affair: Is that all?”
===Chinese===
「这次到了国外，」他自言自语，「我真要动笔写自传了。再没有比旅馆客房更好的写作地点了。」他若有所思地挠挠头，甚至还挖了挖鼻孔——这是他独处时的坏习惯之一。「认识我的人，」他接着说，「会以为我写的那些关于双轮小马车、我母亲和花朵等等的东西，仅仅是因为我在这里头明白，」他把头挠得更用力些，好让自己知道指的是脑子，「明白那是人该写的东西。他们会把我当成一个晦气的罗曼·罗兰，无可救药地假装自己也感到那些真正重要人物确实感到的深情与伟大的灵性体验。也许他们说得对。也许《冈布里尔传》会同《贝多芬传》一样明显是件代用品。另一方面，他们也可能惊奇地发现它是真货。走着瞧。」冈布里尔缓缓点头，同时把两枚便士从右边裤袋挪进左边裤袋。他颇为不安地发现，这两枚铜板竟一直僭越待在银币中间。银币归右边，铜板归左边。这是几条犯了就大大不吉的法则之一。「我有种预感，」他接着说，「总有一天我可能成圣。一个不成器的、忽闪忽闪的圣徒，像一支将熄未熄的蜡烛。至于爱情——唔，嗯。至于我见识过的人们——我要指出，我结识过欧洲大多数名人，而我对他们个个都说过我初恋之后说的那句话：就这些？」

===Original===
“Did you really say that about your first love affair?” asked Mrs. Viveash, who had woken up again.

“Didn’t you?”

“No. I said: This *is* all﻿—everything, the universe. In love, it’s either all or nothing at all.” She shut her eyes and almost immediately went to sleep again.

Gumbril continued his lullaby-soliloquy.
===Chinese===
「你初恋之后真说过那句话？」维维什夫人问，她又醒了。

「你没说过？」

「没有。我说的是：这就是一切——一切，整个宇宙。在爱情里，要么是全部，要么压根没有。」她闭上眼，几乎立刻又睡着了。

冈布里尔继续他的摇篮曲式独白。

===Original===
“ ‘This charming little book.’﻿ ﻿… *The Scotsman*. ‘This farrago of obscenity, slander and false psychology.’﻿ ﻿… *Darlington Echo*. ‘Mr. Gumbril’s first cousin is St. Francis Xavier, his second cousin is the Earl of Rochester, his third cousin is the Man of Feeling, his fourth cousin is David Hume.’﻿ ﻿… *Court Journal*.” Gumbril was already tired of this joke. “When I consider how my light is spent,” he went on, “when I consider!﻿ ﻿… Herr Jesu, as Fraulein Nimmernein used to exclaim at the critical moment. Consider, dear cow, consider. This is not the time of year for grass to grow. Consider, dear cow, consider, consider.” He got up from his chair and tiptoed across the room to the writing-table. An Indian dagger lay next to the blotting-pad; Mrs. Viveash used it as a paper-knife. Gumbril picked it up, executed several passes with it. “Thumb on the blade,” he said, “and strike upwards. On guard. Lunge. To the hilt it penetrates. Poniard at the tip”﻿—he ran the blade between his fingers﻿—“caress by the time it reaches the hilt. Z﻿—zip.” He put down the knife and stopping for a moment to make a grimace at himself in the mirror over the mantelpiece, he went back to his chair.
===Chinese===
「『这本迷人的小书。』……《苏格兰人报》。『这堆淫秽、诽谤和错误心理学的犬杂烩。』……《达灵顿回声报》。『冈布里尔先生的堂弟是圣方济各·沙勿略，他的表弟是罗切斯特伯爵，他的三表弟是多愁善感者，四表弟是大卫·休谟。』……《宫廷杂志》。」冈布里尔已经腻了这个玩笑。「『当我思量我的光阴如何虚掷』，」他接着说，「当我思量！……Herr Jesu（主耶稣啊），尼默奈因小姐从前总在紧要关头这么喊。思量吧，亲爱的母牛，思量。现在不是长草的时节。思量吧，亲爱的母牛，思量，思量。」他从椅子上起来，踮着脚穿过房间走到写字台前。吸墨纸垫旁边躺着一把印度匕首；维维什夫人拿它当裁纸刀。冈布里尔拾起它，比划了几个剑势。「拇指压剑刃，」他说，「向上撩击。预备。直刺。到剑柄，没身而入。匕首到剑尖时」——他把剑刃在指间抽过一遭——「待到剑柄，已是爱抚。嗤——溜。」他放下刀，在壁炉上方镜子前对自己做了个鬼脸，回到椅子上。

===Original===
At seven o’clock Mrs. Viveash woke up. She shook her head to feel if the pain were still rolling about loose inside her skull.

“I really believe I’m all right,” she said. She jumped up. “Come on,” she cried. “I feel ready for anything.”

“And I feel like so much food for worms,” said Gumbril. “Still, *Versiam’ a tazza piena il generoso umor*.” He hummed the Drinking Song out of *Robert the Devil*, and to that ingenuously jolly melody they left the house.
===Chinese===
七点钟，维维什夫人醒了。她摇摇头，试试那疼痛是否还在她颅骨里松散地滚动。

「我真觉得我好了。」她说。她一跃而起。「来吧，」她喊道，「我什么都准备好了。」

「而我觉得自己不过是堆蛆的食物。」冈布里尔说，「不过，Versiam' a tazza piena il generoso umor（慷慨的琼浆又斟满了杯）。」他哼起《魔鬼罗贝尔》里的饮酒歌，踏着那支天真欢快的曲调，他们出了门。

===Original===
Their taxi that evening cost them several pounds. They made the man drive back and forth, like a shuttle, from one end of London to the other. Every time they passed through Piccadilly Circus Mrs. Viveash leant out of the window to look at the sky signs dancing their unceasing St. Vitus’s dance above the monument to the Earl of Shaftesbury.
===Chinese===
那晚他们的出租车花了好几镑。他们叫那司机像梭子一样，载着他们从伦敦这头到那头来回穿行。每过皮卡迪利圆场，维维什夫人就探出车窗，看那些天空招牌在沙夫茨伯里伯爵纪念像上方跳它们永不停歇的圣维特斯舞。

===Original===
“How I adore them!” she said the first time they passed them. “Those wheels that whizz round till the sparks fly out from under them: that rushing motor, and that lovely bottle of port filling the glass and then disappearing and reappearing and filling it again. Too lovely.”

“Too revolting,” Gumbril corrected her. “These things are the epileptic symbol of all that’s most bestial and idiotic in contemporary life. Look at those beastly things and then look at that.” He pointed to the County Fire Office on the northern side of the Circus. “There stands decency, dignity, beauty, repose. And there flickers, there gibbers and twitches﻿—what? Restlessness, distraction, refusal to think, anything for an unquiet life.﻿ ﻿…”

“What a delicious pedant you are!” She turned away from the window, put her hands on his shoulders and looked at him. “Too exquisitely ridiculous!” And she kissed him.

“You won’t force me to change my opinion.” Gumbril smiled at her. “*Eppur’ si muove*﻿—I stick to my guns like Galileo. They move and they’re horrible.”

“They’re me,” said Mrs. Viveash emphatically. “Those things are me.”
===Chinese===
「我多崇拜它们！」头一回经过时她说。「那些飞旋得火星四溅的轮子；那辆疾驰的汽车，那瓶可爱的波特酒斟满酒杯、又隐没、又重现、再斟满。可爱极了。」
